Commentary on Jeremiah

Latin source: Jerome's commentary on Jeremiah, in six books, with a prologue and a preface to each later book, all addressed to Eusebius of Cremona (written at Bethlehem about 414–416, and left unfinished at Jeremiah 32:44), from the critical text of S. Reiter, Corpus Christianorum, Series Latina 74 (Turnhout, 1960), pp. 1–347, verified against the page images and collated with Reiter's earlier edition, Corpus Scriptorum Ecclesiasticorum Latinorum 59 (Vienna, 1913), with the edition's evident misprints corrected. Each lemma gives Jeremiah in Jerome's own version from the Hebrew and then, after "LXX:", in his Latin of the Septuagint, with the renderings of Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion where he adds them; all Scripture is translated as Jerome quotes it, from his own Latin. Italics mark the lemmata and the other Scripture and authors he quotes. Jeremiah follows the Hebrew chapter order; Psalms keep the Latin (LXX) numbering, with the Hebrew number in parentheses where it differs. Greek is transliterated in italics, and Hebrew is given in Jerome's Latin transliterations, with an English gloss in square brackets where he gives none; the Origenian asterisk is kept. Reiter's supplements are read into the text, the words he brackets as spurious are omitted, and his critical apparatus is not translated. This English translation is released into the public domain.

Prologue

1. After the explanations of the twelve prophets, of Isaiah, of Daniel and of Ezekiel, I at last set my hand to Jeremiah, brother Eusebius, dedicating to you my short commentaries on him, so that you may join the man of the Gospel to Matthew the evangelist, whom I expounded many years ago, at your urging, in a compressed sense. And because the volume is very long and in most places is woven of a plain history, I advise your prudence of this: do not look in this work for a broad explanation, especially on those things which have already been said in the other prophets as well and which are clear to the understanding of themselves. And so I shall try to write by the hand of secretaries in such a way that nothing is lacking in the sense, though much is lacking in the words. 2. I shall prepare for you the warp and the weft and the leashes; you yourself must make the most beautiful garment, so that you may be able not only to listen to us but also to teach others. But the little book of Baruch, which is commonly joined to the edition of the Seventy and is not found among the Hebrews, and the pseudepigraphon [falsely ascribed] letter of Jeremiah, I judged should by no means be expounded; rather, I judged that I should set in order the arrangement of Jeremiah, confused by the error of copyists, and should fill up from the Hebrew sources the many things that are lacking, so that you may hold a prophet new out of the old, and true in place of the corrupt and falsified, making light of the rage of the detractors, who cavil not only at the words but even at the syllables of our words, thinking that they know something in this, if they disparage the works of others. 3. Just as lately an unlearned slanderer burst out, who thinks my commentaries on the letter of Paul to the Ephesians deserve censure, and, snoring in his excessive madness, does not understand the laws of commentaries, in which many opinions of various men are set down, with the names of the authors either suppressed or expressed, so that it is the reader's judgment to decide which he ought chiefly to choose; although in the first book of that same work I said in the preface that I would speak things either my own or another's, and that the commentaries themselves belong as much to the ancient writers as to us. 4. Not seeing this, his forerunner Grunnius long ago strove to carp at it, and I answered him in two books. What this man brings forth as if his own has also already been cleared away when another was slandering, to pass over the volumes against Jovinian, in which he grieves that virginity has been preferred to marriage, and marriage to a second marriage, and a second marriage to many marriages. Nor does the utter dolt, weighed down with the porridge of the Scots, remember that we said in that very work: I do not condemn the twice married, nay, not even the thrice married, and, if it can be so, the eight times married; I will add something more: I receive even a fornicator who repents; whatever is equally lawful must be weighed in an equal scale. 5. Let him read the apology for that same work, which many years ago Rome received with joy against his master, and then he will perceive that he blasphemes with another's voice, and that he is so unskilled that he does not even have curses of his own, but uses against us the rage even of enemies long since buried. But now the work proposed must be undertaken.

Book One

Jeremiah 1

I. The words of Jeremiah, the son of Hilkiah, of the priests who were in Anathoth, in the land of Benjamin. Which word of the Lord came to him in the days of Josiah, the son of Amon, king of Judah, in the thirteenth year of his reign. And it came in the days of Jehoiakim, the son of Josiah, king of Judah, until the end of the eleventh year of Zedekiah, the son of Josiah, king of Judah, until the exile of Jerusalem in the fifth month. 2. The other prophets, such as Isaiah, Hosea, Joel, were before the captivity of the ten tribes of Israel or of the two tribes of Judah and Benjamin; others after the captivity, such as Daniel, Haggai and Zechariah; but Jeremiah and Ezekiel wove their prophecy when the captivity was imminent, but one of them in the land of Judah, the other in Babylon. 3. Jeremiah, while he was still a boy, began to prophesy in the thirteenth year of Josiah, the son of Amon, king of Judah, and he prophesied under his rule for nineteen years, and afterward under Jehoiakim his son for eleven years, and under Zedekiah, who was the last of the kings of Judah, for eleven years, until the fifth month, when Jerusalem was taken by the Babylonians. But the three months each of Jehoahaz and of Jeconiah — of whom the one was led into Egypt, the other with his mother into Babylon — are counted within the years mentioned above. 4. And thus from the beginning of his prophecy until the captivity of Jerusalem, in which he himself was also taken, he prophesied for forty-one years, besides that time when he was led into Egypt. And there he prophesied in Tahpanhes, as is contained in this very volume. For 'the words of Jeremiah' the Seventy put: the word of God which came to Jeremiah, in this sense, namely, that 'the words of Jeremiah' are the word of the Lord. And he was of the family of the priests who dwelt to the north of Jerusalem at the third milestone, in the little village of Anathoth. 5. And at the same time, wonderful is the clemency of the Lord, that, with the captivity already near and the Babylonian army besieging Jerusalem, he nonetheless calls the people to repentance, preferring to save them converted rather than to destroy them as transgressors. For 'exile', which all the others translated with one accord, the Seventy put 'captivity'. And after the beginning of the prophesying of Jeremiah, in the thirty-fifth year of his prophecy, Ezekiel began to prophesy in Babylon to those who had been taken captive with him.

II. And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: Before I formed you in the womb, I knew you; and before you came forth from the womb, I sanctified you, I gave you as a prophet to the nations. Not that Jeremiah existed before his conception, as heresy surmises, but that the Lord foreknew that he would be, the Lord to whom things not yet made are already made, according to that which the Apostle says: who called the things which were not as if they were. 2. And that he is sanctified in the womb we must take according to that saying of the Apostle: but when it pleased him, who set me apart from my mother's womb and called me through his grace, to reveal his Son in me, that I might preach him among the nations. John the Baptist too is sanctified in the womb, and receives the Holy Spirit, and is moved in the womb, and speaks through his mother's mouth. 3. And as for his saying: I gave you as a prophet to the nations, he wishes this to be understood, which we shall read later in the prophet himself, that he prophesied not only to Jerusalem but also to many nations round about. Some understand this place of the Savior, who was properly the prophet of the nations and through the apostles called all the nations. For he truly, before he was formed in the virginal womb and before he came forth from his mother's womb, was sanctified in the womb and known to the Father, inasmuch as he is always in the Father, and the Father always in him.

III. And I said: Ah, ah, ah, Lord God, behold, I do not know how to speak, for I am a boy. LXX: and I said, O Lord God, I do not know how to speak, for I am young. He shrinks from an office which, because of his age, he cannot sustain, with the same modesty with which Moses too says that he is of a thin and slender voice. But Moses is rebuked as one of great and robust age; to this one pardon is granted for his boyhood, which is adorned with modesty and shame.

IV. And the Lord said to me: Do not say: I am a boy, for to all that I shall send you to, you shall go, and all things whatsoever I command you, you shall speak. Do not be afraid of their face, for I am with you to rescue you, says the Lord. 'Do not consider your age,' he says, '— for you have learned from another prophet who says: the gray hairs of a man are his wisdom —, let it only be of your will that you go forward; you will have me as companion, and with my help you will accomplish all things: open your mouth, and I will fill it. 2. And do not consider the multitude of those to whom and against whom you are to speak, but me, who am with you to rescue you, says the Lord'. But the Lord delivers, not in the sense that the prophet is free of persecutions and distresses, since we read that he suffered many things, but so that by enduring he may overcome all things and not yield to distress.

V. And the Lord put forth his hand and touched my mouth, and the Lord said to me: Behold, I have given my words in your mouth. It is to be noted that here the hand of God is put forth to touch the mouth of the prophet, and it is said to him: behold, I have given my words in your mouth, whereas in Isaiah it is written: and one of the seraphim was sent to me, and in his hand he had a coal which he had taken with tongs from the altar, and he touched my mouth and said: behold, this has touched your lips, and it will take away your iniquities and cleanse your sins. 2. For there, because he was of firm and mature age, and he himself simply confesses that he has unclean lips and dwells in the midst of a people having unclean lips, one of the seraphim is sent, to touch his mouth not with a hand but with tongs and a coal, and to take away his iniquities and cleanse his sins. But here the hand of God himself is put forth, through which he has wrought all things, and which in another place is called the arm, not to take away sins, which on account of his boyhood he had not committed in great number, but to bestow the grace of speaking. 3. Further, Ezekiel devours a book written within and without, containing both divine mysteries and the simple history. Jeremiah's mouth is touched and the words of the Lord are bestowed, that he may receive confidence in preaching. And beautifully, according to the letter, a hand is put forth, so that, seeing the likeness of human limbs, he may not dread the touch of the hand.

VI. Behold, I have set you today over the nations and over the kingdoms, to pluck up and to destroy and to ruin and to scatter and to build and to plant. This, which we have added from the Hebrew, 'scatter' or 'put down', is not found in the LXX. And it must be considered that two joyful things follow four sad ones. For good things could not be built unless evil things had been destroyed, nor the best things planted unless the worst were rooted out. 2. For every planting which the heavenly Father has not planted shall be rooted out, and the building which does not have its foundation upon the rock but has been raised upon the sand is undermined and destroyed by the word of God. And that which Jesus will consume with the spirit of his mouth and will destroy by the coming of his presence, namely every sacrilegious and perverse doctrine, he will ruin forever. 3. Further, the things which are lifted up against the knowledge of God and trust in their own wisdom, which is foolishness with God, he will scatter and put down, so that lowly things may be built in their place, and in the place of the former things, which have been destroyed and plucked up, the things which accord with the truth of the Church may be raised and planted, and that may be fulfilled which the Apostle says: you are God's building, God's husbandry. 4. Many understand this place of the person of Christ. For 'Jeremiah' means 'the exalted one of the Lord', who destroyed the kingdoms of the devil, which the devil had shown him on the height of the mountain, and who brought to ruin the adverse powers, blotting out on the cross the handwriting of errors. Of these the psalm too, after the truth of the history, speaks tropikōs [figuratively]: why have the nations raged and the peoples meditated vain things? The kings of the earth stood up, and the princes gathered together into one. 5. In place of these, plucked up, destroyed, ruined and dragged down to lower things, the Church of God is built and planted. But concerning the person of Jeremiah there is no doubt; for we read in what follows that he takes in his hand a cup full of unmixed wine and is commanded to give drink to all the nations round about.

VII. And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: What do you see, Jeremiah? And I said: I see a watching rod, and the Lord said to me: You have seen well, for I will watch over my word, to perform it. For 'watching rod' the LXX translated 'staff of nut-wood'. We must therefore take pains that the Latin reader may briefly understand the Hebrew etymologian [etymology]. 2. 'Saced' is said for 'nut', while 'watch' or 'watchful' or 'to watch' is called 'soced'. Hence also in later passages the watching leopard is set down under this name. So from the word for 'nut', because of the likeness of the word, the name has played upon the sense of 'watchful'. This indeed is written also in Daniel according to Theodotion, where from the trees schinu and prinu, namely the holm oak and the mastic, cutting asunder and sawing asunder is decreed for the adulterous elders. Besides, at the beginning of Genesis too, from 'man', which is called 'is', woman is called 'issa', as it were a kind of virago, because she was taken from man. 3. For 'staff of nut-wood' Aquila and Symmachus translated 'watching rod', but Theodotion 'of almond'. And the rod watches, considering all the sins of the people, so as to strike and rebuke the transgressors. Hence the Apostle too writes to those who sin: what do you wish? Shall I come to you with a rod, or in love and the spirit of gentleness? This is the rod or staff of which David too speaks: your rod and your staff, they have comforted me. And beautifully he put 'have comforted'; for the Lord rebukes to this end, that he may amend. 4. And just as the nut or the almond has a very bitter rind and is enclosed in a very hard shell, so that, when the harsher and hard parts are stripped away, a very sweet fruit is found, so every rebuke and the labor of continence seems bitter indeed for the present, but brings forth very sweet fruits. Hence there is also that old saying: 'the roots of letters are bitter, the fruits sweet'. Some understand the 'watching rod' and 'of nut-wood' as the Lord, of whom Isaiah says: a rod shall come forth from the root of Jesse. Hence too the rod of Aaron, which was thought dead, is related to have blossomed in the resurrection of the Lord.

VIII. And the word of the Lord came to me a second time, saying: What do you see? And I said: I see a pot set on fire, and its face is from the face of the north. And the Lord said to me: From the north evils shall be opened — or shall blaze up — upon all the inhabitants of the earth. 2. By certain degrees torments are assigned to sinners, so that they may come little by little to salvation: those who will not be amended when the rod strikes are sent into the bronze pot set on fire, of which Ezekiel writes more fully, which is kindled from the face of the north, signifying the Babylonian king and the city of Jerusalem. And beautifully it is added: from the north evils shall blaze up upon all the inhabitants of the earth — either of the land of Judea or surely of the whole earth —, of whom it is written in Revelation too: woe upon all the inhabitants of the earth. 3. For the saints are not inhabitants of the earth but strangers and sojourners, of whom one says: I am a stranger upon the earth and a sojourner, as all my fathers were, and another: few and very evil are my days, in which I sojourn on the earth. Hence Peter too writes his catholic epistle to the strangers and sojourners of Pontus, Galatia and Cappadocia. And according to the mystical senses Solomon says: the north wind is a harsh wind, but by name it is called right-handed, that is, by those who have grown stiff with its cold and have lost the warmth of faith.

IX. For behold, I will call together all the families of the kingdoms of the north, says the Lord, and they shall come, and each one shall set his throne at the entrance of the gates of Jerusalem, and upon all its walls round about, and upon all the cities of Judah. And I will speak my judgments with them concerning all the wickedness of those who have forsaken me and have poured libations to foreign gods and have worshiped the work of their own hands. 2. No one doubts that many nations, and the kings of the several nations, were subject to the Babylonian king; who, when Jerusalem was besieged, set round about, and especially at the outlets of the gates, their throne and tents, so that none of those who were shut in could break out; and that not only Jerusalem but all the cities of Judea were hemmed in by a like siege. 'And when', he says, 'the city has been taken, then I will speak to them, that my judgments were right and that each one received what he deserved, not for the other vices to which the human condition is subject, but above all for idolatry, through which, forsaking me, they worshiped the works of their own hands'. 3. Some take this place in a good sense, namely that those who have been boiled in the bronze pot through torments and purged will afterward be princes of Jerusalem, and that, after the Lord has had mercy on them, he will then reproach them because, having forsaken him, they venerated images. But this is a forced and perverse interpretation; let not an unlearned expounder raise a false charge.

X. You therefore gird up your loins, and arise, and speak to them all things which I command you. Job too is commanded to gird up his loins, and the apostles, with loins girded — which Elijah and John the Baptist mortified with girdles of skin —, to hold lamps in their hands, namely those of the preaching of the Gospel. 2. Whoever therefore is to speak the words of God ought to gird up his loins, knowing that all the strength of the devil is in the loins, and that the righteous man says in the psalms: my loins are filled with illusions. And when he has girded his loins, let him hear that which is written: arise, you who sleep, and be lifted up, and Christ will enlighten you, so that, always watching and rising up from sleep, he may speak the things which God has commanded him.

XI. Do not be afraid of their face; for I will not make you fear their countenance, or, as the Seventy and the other translators have: lest perhaps I make you fear. And the sense according to our translation is: 'do not be afraid of their face; for with me as your helper you will not be able to fear them'; according to the Seventy: 'do not be afraid of their face, have confidence in my command; for if you do not offer what you have, so as to cease to fear, I will forsake you and hand you over to fear, and I shall in a manner seem to make you fear, while I leave you to dread'. 2. And this signifies that truth is always bitter, and that the multitude of men is not to be feared, who, when rebuked, do not bear correction but lay snares for him by whom they are corrected. And what follows according to the Seventy: for I am with you to deliver you, says the Lord, is not found in the Hebrew. And the sense is: 'I will rescue you, not in this, that no one lies in wait for you, but in this, that by enduring snares you do not transgress'.

XII. For I have given you today — or behold, I have set you on this day — as a fortified city, as an iron pillar and as a bronze wall over all the land: to the kings of Judah, to its princes and to the priests and to the people of the land. And they shall fight against you and shall not prevail, for I am with you, says the Lord, to deliver you. 2. The divine word describes why the prophet ought not to fear. I, he says, have set or have given you today — that is, in the present life, while it is called today — as a very strong city, not as a single house, nor a tower or some walls, but as a whole city, which, set upon a mountain, cannot be hid, of which it is written: glorious things are spoken of you, city of God, and: I am a strong city, a city which is besieged. 3. And as a pillar, he says, of iron, of which the Apostle writes: the pillar and ground of the truth. Hence Peter and John, who were reputed pillars of the Church, gave to Paul and Barnabas the right hands of fellowship. Nor is this enough, but as a wall, he says, of bronze, which is marred by no rust, nor perishes when beaten by rains, but becomes stronger with age. 4. And you shall be such against kings and princes and people, not of any place whatever, but of the land, who savor earthly things and do not know heavenly things, who bear the image of the earthy and not of the heavenly; these, he says, shall fight against you and shall not prevail. Why, I ask? What is the cause of such great strength, that neither kings nor princes nor priests nor peoples prevail against one man? It follows: for I am with you, says the Lord, to deliver you. 5. If ever the kings of Judah, which means 'confession', and its princes and priests and peoples, that is, bishops and presbyters and deacons, and the base and ignoble common crowd, should wish to rise up against a holy man, let him have firmness of faith and cease to fear, for with the Lord's help he will overcome.

Jeremiah 2

XIII.

And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: Go and cry in the ears of Jerusalem, saying. This is not found in the Seventy, but was added under asterisks from the edition of Theodotion, who translated the Hebrew word 'carath', for which we said 'cry' or 'preach', as 'read'. For by its ambiguity it signifies reading and crying out and preaching. But the ears of Jerusalem we ought to take as the ears of its inhabitants.

XIV. Thus says the Lord: I have remembered you, pitying your youth and the love of your betrothal, when you followed me in the wilderness, in a land that is not sown. LXX: thus says the Lord: I have remembered the mercy of your youth and the love of your perfection. This is said more fully in Ezekiel, when the Lord joins Jerusalem to himself in marriage and, under the person of a wife, unites her to his embraces, or, to show a more ardent affection, calls her a girl and a young maiden and betrothed; for what we have not yet obtained, we desire the more to obtain. 2. 'When', he says, 'you followed me in the solitude, and in the manner of betrothal gifts and a dowry I distributed to you the ornaments of the law and the necklaces of words'. And all this he refers not to her merit but to his own mercy, through which love too followed. This also, which we have set down: in the wilderness, in a land that is not sown, is not found in the Seventy.

XV. Israel is holy to the Lord, the firstfruits of his crops; all who devour him shall transgress; evils shall come upon them, says the Lord. When he calls Israel the firstfruits of the Lord's crops, he shows that the people gathered from the nations comes after the firstfruits, according to that which is written in another place: Remember your congregation, which you have possessed from the beginning. But firstfruits are owed to the priests and not to enemies. 2. What follows: all who devour him shall transgress; evils shall come upon them, says the Lord, has this sense: just as those who devour the firstfruits, being in no way of priestly stock, are guilty of a crime, so those who defile Israel will be subjected to evils, according to that which the holy man says in Psalm 26 (27): While the harmful draw near against me to eat my flesh, those who trouble me, my enemies themselves have become weak and have fallen. For they will not be immune from punishment on the ground that they carry out God's sentence, and evils will come upon them; for it must needs be that offences come, but woe to him through whom offences come!

XVI. Hear the word of the Lord, house of Jacob and all the families of the house of Israel. Thus says the Lord: What iniquity did your fathers find in me, that they went far from me and walked after vanity and became vain? Another prophet too testifies to this sense: My people, what have I done to you, and how have I been troublesome to you? Answer me; for I brought you out of the land of Egypt and freed you from the house of servants. 2. Now both names — Israel too — are set down, not according to the two and ten tribes, but for the whole people, since Jacob himself too was afterwards called Israel. And he traces the offense from the fathers, not so that the sins of the fathers are imputed to the sons, but so that the sons too, having the likeness of their fathers, are punished both for their own crime and for their parents'. We often read that God has mercy on sons for the sake of holy fathers. 3. Now the fathers of the sinful people forsook God — and not briefly, but for a long time — and instead of God followed vanity, namely idols, which profit their worshipers nothing, and they became like them, according to that which is written: Let those who make them become like them, and all who trust in them.

XVII. And they did not say: Where is the Lord, who made us go up from the land of Egypt, who led us across through the wilderness, through a land uninhabitable and pathless, through a land of thirst and the image of death, through a land in which no man walked nor did a human being dwell? For 'human being' the Seventy translated 'son of man', and for 'image of death' 'shadow of death' was added from Theodotion. 2. Since this is plain according to the history, this must be considered according to anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense]: that as long as we are in this world and are being led out of Egypt, we go up little by little, and first we cross deserts and a land uninhabitable, which the holy man ought not to inhabit, and pathless, to show the difficulty of the journey; through a land of thirst, where we always desire greater things and are not content with things present, and the image or shadow of death — for we always stand in danger, and everywhere the devil sets his snares —, through a land in which no man walked, who is of the perfect age in Christ — for we shall all rise again to a perfect man, to the measure of the age of the fullness of Christ — ; nor does he who is a man of God, or a son of man, ever dwell in it, but he always hastens on to greater things. 3. From this it is clear that perfection is not on the way, but at the end of the way and in the dwelling that is prepared for the saints in the heavenly places, and to whom it is said: you who stand in the house of the Lord, in the courts of the house of our God. In vain, therefore, does the new heresy sprung from the old suppose that perfect victory is here, where there is battle and contest and an uncertain outcome of things to come.

XVIII. And I brought you into the land of Carmel, that you might eat its fruit and its good things, and having entered you defiled my land and made my inheritance an abomination. 'For the toil of a very hard journey I gave you an abundance of all things'; for this is what 'Carmelus' signifies, which in Hebrew is called 'Chermel' and in our language sounds as 'knowledge of circumcision'. 2. And as that people polluted and violated with idolatry the holy land, fertile in all things, so we, receiving the knowledge of the true circumcision, eat its fruit and, if negligence creeps in, defile the land of God and make his inheritance abominable.

XIX. The priests did not say: Where is the Lord? And those who held the law did not know me, and the shepherds transgressed against me, and the prophets prophesied by Baal and followed idols. After such great benefits they turned the privileges of their dignity into contempt, so that the priests did not seek the Lord, the teachers of the law, who ought to have taught others, did not know him, and the shepherds became transgressors through negligence, and the prophets, who discourse among the peoples, speak not for God but for an idol, and venerate their own fabrications. These words must be used against the teachers of our own order, who devour the people of God like bread for food and by their evil works do not call upon the Lord.

XX. Therefore I will still contend with you in judgment, says the Lord, and with your sons I will dispute. Lest he seem to strike through power, he contends by reason as with equals, according to that which David sings and the Apostle adopts: that you may be justified in your words and may prevail when you are judged. And in setting down 'still', he testifies that he has often done so; and in adding 'with your sons', he shows a like obstinacy also in the sons of the wicked. And covertly he signifies that their sons too, at the coming of the Lord, followed the ancient denial of God.

XXI. Pass over to the islands of Kittim and see, and send to Kedar and consider earnestly, and see whether such a thing has been done: if a nation has changed gods — or its gods — ; and surely they are not gods. But my people has changed its glory into an idol or, for that from which nothing will profit it. He makes a comparison of a thing that is incomparable, and sets the true God beside lying ones. 2. 'Go', he says, 'to the islands of Kittim', which we ought to take as those either of Italy or of the western regions, from the fact that the island of Cyprus, in which a city is called by this name, is near the land of Judea; from it too came Zeno, the chief of the Stoics. Kedar, however, is a region of the wilderness and of the Ishmaelites, whom they now call Saracens, against which a prophecy of this very prophet is woven in his final parts, and which David mentions, saying: I have dwelt with the inhabitants of Kedar; my soul has long been a sojourner. 3. And the sense is: 'Either go to the west or send into the wilderness, and see whether any nation has done what you have done. For none of them has spurned its gods, nor, by comparison with golden ones, exchanged its wooden and stone ones; but following its ancient error it has kept what it had received from its ancestors; and this surely, although none of them is a god, but they are images fashioned by the hand of men. But my people has changed the truth for a lie and preferred to me an idol, which will not be able to profit them in the time of need'. 4. We can say this also against those who follow vices with greater zeal than virtues, whom the Apostle admonishes, saying: I speak in human terms because of the weakness of your flesh. As you yielded your members to serve uncleanness and iniquity unto iniquity, so now yield your members to serve righteousness unto sanctification.

XXII. Be astonished, O heavens, at this, and, its gates, be utterly desolate, says the Lord; for my people has done two evils: they have forsaken me, the fountain of living water, and have dug for themselves broken cisterns, which cannot hold waters. LXX: the heaven was astonished at this and shuddered beyond measure and exceedingly, the rest likewise. The heaven, to which it was said: Give heed, O heaven, and I will speak and: Hear, O heaven, and give ear, O earth, seeing the precepts of God trampled underfoot, shudders and cannot hide its astonishment. For every creature groans together and grieves together over the sins of men. 2. Now the people of God has done two contrary things: first, that they forsook God, who is the fountain of life and gave the precept, saying: I am the Lord your God, who brought you out of the land of Egypt; second, what is written in the same place: You shall have no gods in my sight; instead of which it followed demons, whom he calls broken cisterns because they cannot keep the commandments of God. 3. And this must be noted, that the fountain is perpetual and has life-giving waters, but cisterns and pits are filled either from torrents or from the turbid waters of the earth and from rains. And he calls those the gates of the heavens of which it is also written in Psalm 23 (24): Lift up, O gates, your heads, and the king of glory shall enter; for which the Seventy translated: Lift up your gates, O princes; of these more will be said fully in its proper place. 4. And let it trouble no one that Aquila and Symmachus translated 'heavens', but the Seventy and Theodotion 'heaven'. For the Hebrew 'samaim' is of common number, and both 'heavens' and 'heaven' are called by the same name, like Thebes, Athens, Salonae.

XXIII. Is Israel a slave, or one born in the house? I think it was from this passage that the Jews, puffed up with pride, said to the Savior: We are the seed of Abraham and have never been slaves to anyone; how do you say: You shall be free?, not knowing that everyone who commits sin is the slave of sin, and that each one serves him by whom he is overcome. Born, then, of Abraham, the friend of God, by their own vice they became as it were sons of Ham, to whom it was said: Cursed be Canaan; he shall be a slave to his brothers.

XXIV. Why then has he become a prey? Lions have roared over him and given their voice; they have made his land a wilderness, and his cities have been burned, and there is none who dwells in them. The divine word asks so that it may itself reply. 2. And by lions he means the princes of Babylon, who made his land a wilderness and destroyed his cities by fire. Or at any rate, according to anagōgēn, let us understand the lions as the adverse powers or the leaders of the heretics, who, desolating the land of the Church, have laid waste all its cities with heretical fire, and with that fire of which it is written: All committing adultery, their hearts are like an oven. 3. For they truly give their voice, and in this same prophet, under the person of the partridge, they cry out and gather what they did not bring forth, and they make riches not with judgment. But for this reason its cities have been laid waste and destroyed, because they do not have God as their inhabitant, as Scripture says: and there is none who inhabits them.

XXV. The sons also of Memphis and Tahpanhes have debauched you even to the crown of the head. Has not this been done to you because you forsook the Lord your God at the time when he was leading you along the way? This, which we said: at the time when he was leading you along the way, is not found in the Seventy. Now he names two very great cities of Egypt, Memphis and Tahpanhes, and says that their sons debauched Israel even to the crown of the head, in that sense in which Isaiah set it down: From the sole of the foot even to the crown of the head there is no soundness in him. 2. And so great was the lust of the Egyptians, who are great of flesh, that they spared no member but debauched everything. According to the letter, refer it to the idols of the Egyptians; according to the spiritual understanding, to the teachers of perverse doctrine, who pollute the purity of the Church with their own filthiness. And these things will befall him for this reason, because he forsook the Lord his God, and that above all at the time when he ought to have followed him as his guide.

XXVI. And now what do you want on the way of Egypt, to drink the water of Sior? And what have you to do with the way of the Assyrians, to drink the water of the river? For 'Sior' we translated 'turbid', which is what the Hebrew word signifies; for it the common edition has 'Geon'. 2. Since, then, he had set down above the sons of Memphis and Tahpanhes, who debauched Israel even to the crown of the head, he now names Egypt itself more plainly. And no one doubts that the Nile has turbid waters, and that by the river of the Assyrians he signifies the Euphrates, as Scripture says that the land of promise extends from the torrent of Egypt even to the great river Euphrates. 3. But those who have forsaken Christ, the fountain of life, and have dug for themselves the pits of the heretics, which cannot hold the waters of doctrines, must needs be subjected to the lions, who reduce their land to a wilderness and destroy all the churches, and be polluted even to the crown of the head, and drink turbid waters and the streams of the Assyrian river and of the north, whence evils blaze up upon the earth.

XXVII. Your malice shall reprove you — or your transgression shall instruct you — and your turning away shall rebuke you. Know and see that it is an evil and bitter thing that you have forsaken the Lord your God and that the fear of me is not with you, says the Lord God of hosts. It must be noted that malice too, or transgression, after it has glutted the transgressor and, like the quails, has come to the point of nausea, instructs the one who does repentance; and he is commanded to see what he has forsaken, what he has followed, and how, spurning good and sweet things, he has chosen evil and bitter ones. 2. And all this happened because he forsook the Lord his God, and the fear of him is not with him. For the beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord; and because he did not have it, he is handed over to evil and bitterness.

XXVIII. From of old you have broken my yoke — or your —, you have burst my bonds — or your — and said: I will not serve. For on every lofty hill and under every leafy tree you lay prostrate as a harlot or there you poured yourself out in fornication. He speaks to Israel as to a harlot, because she broke the marriage covenant and said: I will not serve — 'the Lord' or 'the husband' is understood —, but on every lofty hill and under every leafy tree lay prostrate to idolatry; for pleasant and lofty places are always dedicated to idols. 2. This can also be said to him who, a Christian from the beginning and in part instructed in the sacred letters, afterwards, through desire for secular literature, which is signified in the hills, and for the pleasant things of eloquence, which are shown in the leafy trees, prostrates himself to demons, who, under the pretext of learning and lofty knowledge, pollute the souls of believers and make them spread their feet for every passer-by.

XXIX. But I planted you a choice vineyard, wholly a true seed; how have you been turned for me into what is crooked, a strange vineyard? LXX: But I planted you a fruitful vineyard, wholly true; how have you been turned into bitterness, a strange vine? For the 'choice' or 'fruitful' vineyard the Hebrew has 'sorec', which is set down in the song of Isaiah. Now it is a kind of the finest vine, with which slip the Lord says he planted Israel too, and he marvels how the true seed and choice vineyard has been turned into bitterness and has therefore become a strange vineyard. 2. And let no one be secure, if both the planting of the Lord and the true seed and the vineyard sorec is so changed by its own vice that through bitterness it withdraws from the Lord and becomes a strange vine. And in this the clemency of the Creator must be considered, that he who said in the Gospel: I am the true vine, granted also to his disciples and to the people believing in him to be a choice or true vine, if it wills to remain in that in which it has been planted.

XXX. If you wash yourself with nitre and multiply for yourself the herb borith, you are stained with your iniquity before me, says the Lord God. For 'herb borith', which we have rendered as it is set down in the Hebrew, the Seventy translated poan, to signify the fullers' herb, which, after the manner of the province of Palestine, grows in green and moist places and has the same power for washing away dirt as nitre does; but our 'nitre' and 'fullers' herb' is repentance. The preaching of the Church too, which reproves and rebukes and corrects the offender, has the likeness of a more biting nitre. 2. He who is stained with the light dirt of sins is cleansed by lighter admonitions. But grave sins, which drag to death, can be washed away neither by nitre nor by the herb borith, but need heavier torments. For each one's work, of what sort it is, the fire will test, and in fire it will be revealed. And beautifully he added: you are stained with your iniquity in my sight, because, even if you seem clean to men, you are not clean to me, who know the consciences of each one. Hence too in another place it is said: no one living shall be justified in your sight.

XXXI. How do you say: I am not defiled, I have not gone after the Baals? See your ways in the vale or valley, which in Hebrew is called 'ge' and by the Seventy is translated polyandrion, which in our language can be called 'the tomb of a multitude'. 'In vain', he says, 'are you unwilling to confess your crimes, and do you boast of cleanness, you who are polluted with the filth of idolatry, and shamelessly deny that you worshiped the idol of the Baals. Look back at the vale of the sons of Hinnom, which is watered by the springs of Siloam, and there you will see the shrine of Baal, whom you venerated, having forsaken God'. 2. And what is added: know what you have done, opens the closed eyes of her who denies, so that she may see what she blushes to look upon. According to tropology, let us refute by their works the shameless brow of those who will not confess their vices. For men of this kind do not walk in the strait and narrow way that leads to life, but in the broad and spacious one, by which very many enter, which leads to death. Hence too it was significantly named polyandrion, or else according to the history, because there a multitude of the people was slain and perished through the evil of idolatry.

XXXII. A light runner unfolding her ways, a wild ass accustomed to the wilderness, in the desire of her soul has drawn in the wind of her love; none shall turn her away. All who seek her shall not fail; in her monthly courses they shall find her. LXX: toward evening her voice howled, she widened her ways over the waters of the wilderness, in the desire of her soul she was borne by the spirit, she was handed over. Who will turn her back? All who seek her shall not toil; in her humiliation they will find her. In this passage the Seventy's edition disagrees greatly with the Hebrew truth; yet each has its own sense. 2. Because above she said: I am not defiled, and he was speaking as if to a woman who had behaved shamefully, he describes her fornication. 'As', he says, 'a light roe' — which we called 'runner' in the common gender, and which Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion translated more expressively dromas kouphē [a light runner, in the feminine] — 'unfolds her ways and is carried swiftly to the pastures, and as a wild ass accustomed to the wilderness draws in, in the desire of her soul, the wind or spirit of her love' — for among the Hebrews both 'wind' and 'spirit' are called by one name, 'ruha' —, 'so too Israel, or Jerusalem, was borne with all her impetus toward the desire of lust and burned with every love of idols, and there is no one who can turn her from this impetus by his warnings, not that the inability of the prophets brought this about, but the perverse malice of her who desires'. 3. Whoever, he says, seek her will not toil greatly; in her monthly courses and in her uncleanness they will find her, for which Aquila translated neomēnian, that is, 'the Kalends', Symmachus 'month', the Seventy and Theodotion 'humiliation'. But according to the Seventy this is the sense: the harlot Jerusalem, like that woman who is described in Proverbs, toward evening howled with her voice and provoked lovers to lust, opened the ways of her shamefulness, and spread her feet for every passer-by. And there was a place having the pleasantness of flowing waters, which becomes more delightful when there is solitude all around, so that no one sees her fornicating. 4. In the desire, he says, of her soul epneumatophoreito [she was borne along by a spirit], or by a perverse spirit she was led, or she drew the refreshment of love, or at any rate she sang the songs of her shamefulness. She was handed over, he says, to her vices and to lust; no one will be able to turn her back. All who wish to find her will find her in the humiliation of her shamefulness, so that she can never be sated with the love of pleasure.

XXXIII. Restrain your foot from nakedness and your throat from thirst. And you said: I have despaired, by no means will I do it; for I have loved strangers, and after them I will walk. LXX: turn back your foot from the rough way and your throat from thirst. She said: I will act manfully, because she loved strangers and walked after them. Those who are about to keep the Passover are commanded to have shoes on their feet. 2. And the Apostle proclaims shod the feet of those who are prepared by the Gospel, lest, while they walk through the wilderness of this world, they lie open to venomous creatures, which ought to be trampled and crushed by the Gospel foot. We restrain the throat from thirst when we receive the precepts of the Savior, who says: Whoever thirsts, let him come to me and drink. She, out of despair of better things, denied that she would do what the Lord commanded, and set out the reason, saying: I have loved strangers, and I will follow them, thinking by a shameless confession to escape her crimes. 3. But according to the Seventy, the way of sinners is rough, and it is turned by the Lord into a level way. Whoever follows heretics is branded by the verdict of these verses, because he has said: I have despaired — or, in an evil purpose, I will act manfully — and in my error I will be strengthened. And it is necessary that whoever follows a doctrine strange to the Church's should love strangers and follow their footsteps, either demons or the leaders of the heretics, because they are strangers to God.

XXXIV. As a thief is confounded when he is caught, so are the house of Israel confounded, they and their kings, princes and priests and their prophets. Although the face of thieves is shameless and bold, yet it blushes when it has been caught in theft. And so Israel too, saying to wood: You are my father, and to stone: You have begotten me, so as to call those his parents whom he himself had fashioned, is confounded when he has been caught in his idolatry. 2. And lest we think that he says this of the common people, he sets down kings and princes and priests and their prophets. Let us use this testimony if ever our princes, and those who are reckoned leaders in the Church, are caught in shameful sins.

XXXV. They have turned to me the back and not the face. Those who cast the words of God behind them themselves turn their back against him and not their face. For when a master gives instruction, it is a sign of one who obeys if he listens with his head bowed before him; but if he turns his back, it is a sign of one who despises, as it is written in another place: and they turned to me a withdrawing shoulder. 'So far', says God, 'have they despised my precepts that they would not even hear, but showed the swelling of their mind by the gesture of their body'.

XXXVI. And in the time of their affliction they will say: Arise and deliver us. Those who did not perceive God through benefits, let them perceive him through torments.

XXXVII. Where are your gods, which you made for yourself? Let them arise and deliver you in the time of your affliction. It is a shameless request, in time of need and distress, to seek help from him whom they despised in peace. And it is to be read with the feeling of one rebuking: 'Let your gods, which you made for yourself, deliver you; so that, since God is the creator of men but man has made a god, necessity may prove what they can do whom you formerly worshiped when you were secure.'

XXXVIII. According to the number of your cities were your gods, O Judah. The several cities worshiped either the same gods or single and different ones, so that they did not seem to have agreement even in impiety, but a superstition fighting against itself followed diverse error. And what follows: and according to the number of the streets of Jerusalem they sacrificed to Baal, was added by the Seventy.

XXXIX. Why do you wish to contend with me in judgment? You have all forsaken me, says the Lord. Human perversity is prone to excuse itself, so that whatever men suffer deservedly they appear to suffer unjustly, and they refer their own fault to the judgment of God. 'In vain', therefore, he says, 'do you put forward complaints and plead that the judge is unjust, since what you suffer comes from your impiety.' And what follows: and you have all acted unjustly against me, was likewise added by the Seventy.

XL. In vain have I struck your sons; they did not receive discipline, for which the Seventy set down: you did not receive. And the sense in the Hebrew is: 'They themselves, who were struck, were unwilling to receive discipline,' but in the Seventy: 'I struck your sons for this reason, that by their death you might be instructed. And lest perhaps you should say: You were unwilling to correct us when we sinned, learn from the blows dealt your sons that I desired to cure you with a harsher remedy.'

XLI. Your sword has devoured your prophets. 'Not my prophets but yours, and by no means my sword but yours, has devoured them, the sword which you suffered for your sins'. But the Seventy do not have 'your', but translated simply: the sword has devoured your prophets, 'so that it may be shown to be either the enemy's sword or my sword, by which I have pierced your sins.'

XLII. Like a ravaging lion is your generation. LXX: like a ravaging lion, and you did not fear. 'The sword', he says, 'which devoured your prophets' — there is no doubt that he means the soothsayers of Baal and of the idols —, 'has laid everything waste like a lion, and yet your generation, which ought to have been amended by the slaying of a few, has persevered entire in crime'. 2. But according to the Seventy this is the sense: 'Thus the sword of the Lord, which has displayed the sword of the adversaries, has devoured and torn your false prophets like a lion, which most greedily tears the prey it has found, and yet not even by the punishment of your prophets could you be turned to better things.'

XLIII. See the word of the Lord: Have I become a wilderness to Israel, or a late-yielding land? Why then has my people said: We have withdrawn, we will come to you no more? LXX: hear the word of the Lord: thus says the Lord: Have I become a wilderness to Israel, or a land full of thorns, because my people said: We will not serve, nor will we come to you? Moses too saw the voice of God, and John the apostle says that he saw and handled the word of God. 2. And he marvels how the people of Israel held God as a wilderness, while it followed idols as though they were the throng of cities. A late-yielding land is one that does not receive the rains of doctrines nor the discipline of the Gospel; and full of thorns, because it had not been cultivated. The people, then, that was once God's is the more wicked in this, that it withdrew from the Lord and is unwilling to return again to its God. It is a great offense to be unwilling to appease him whom you have offended.

XLIV. Will a virgin forget her ornament, a bride the band of her breast? But my people has forgotten me days without number. Through these words we learn that Christ is the bridegroom of the virgin Church, which has no spot or wrinkle. And if he himself is the bridegroom, the words are his, of whom John the Baptist also speaks: He who has the bride is the bridegroom. 2. He, therefore, loses his ornament who withdraws from the Lord, and he loses the understanding of doctrines, which is signified in the breast. Hence also John the Evangelist reclines upon the breast of the Lord, and for the priests, among other things, the breast of the victims is set apart. But the greater the number of the times in which we forget God, the greater the penalty of the sin, because it could not be tamed even by the length of ages.

XLV. Why do you strive to show your way good, to seek love? You who moreover have also taught your wickednesses, your ways, and on your wings — or in your hands — has been found the blood of the souls of the poor and innocent. Not in pits did I find them, but in all these or under every oak. 2. 'In vain', he says, 'do you desire to defend yourself by the art of words and to show your works as good, so as to merit love, you who besides have also taught others your ways and been an example of evil works to all. And on your wings indeed — or in your hands — has been found the blood of the innocent, whom you sacrificed to idols, or whose souls you destroyed by the likeness of sacrifices'. 3. 'The poor' we set down from the Hebrew; they are not found in the Seventy. 'But these,' he says, 'poor and innocent ones I did not find slain in pits' — which usually happens through the ambushes of robbers —, 'but in all the things which I have mentioned above, or under the oak' — which in Hebrew is called 'ella,' which indeed also signifies 'these', so that the sense is: 'in all these, or under the oak and terebinth' —, 'under whose shade and leaves, as in pleasant places, you enjoyed the crimes of idolatry'.

XLVI. And you said: I am without sin and innocent, and therefore let your fury be turned away from me. Behold, I will contend with you in judgment, because you said: I have not sinned. How exceedingly vile you have become — or since you have greatly despised — repeating your ways! These words must be used against those who will not acknowledge their sins, but in the time of affliction and distress say that they suffer unjustly what they suffer, and provoke the wrath of God the more, since it is another and greater sin not to mourn what they have done, but to put forward vain excuses for their sins. 2. In judgment, he says, I will contend with you because you said: I have not sinned, as though it were something greater than this sin to hold one thing in the conscience and to utter another in speech. Let the new heresy sprung from the old hear that it is the very greatest wrath of God to be unwilling to confess sin humbly, and instead to boast shamelessly of righteousness.

XLVII. And you shall be confounded by Egypt, as you were confounded by Assyria; for from this one too you shall go out, and your hands shall be upon your head, since the Lord has crushed your confidence — or hope — and you shall have nothing prosperous in it. To avoid the onset of the Egyptians they fled for refuge to the Assyrians, whose protection was vain; for we read that they were conquered by the Egyptians. Again, to escape the wrath of the Assyrian they relied on the help of the Egyptians, who, as history relates, were overcome by the Assyrians. 2. They are rebuked, therefore, because, abandoning hope in God, they rely on the help of men, which has all been crushed and so overthrown that they can find nothing of use in it. Hence he says: and from this one you shall go out, that is, from Egypt, as you went out from the Assyrians, and your hands shall be upon your head, and you will mourn that you vainly expected protection from the Egyptians. Let us recall the history, when Tamar, corrupted and violated by Amnon, her most wicked brother, placed her hands upon her head sprinkled with ash and so returned to her house.

Jeremiah 3

XLVIII.

It is commonly said — for which the Seventy translated only it is said — : if a man dismisses his wife, and she, departing from him, marries another man, will he return to her any more? Will she not be polluted and defiled, that woman — or that land — ? And you have committed fornication with many lovers or shepherds. For the word 'rehim', which is written with four letters, 'res, ain, ioth, mem', signifies both 'lovers' and 'shepherds'; and if we read 'rehim', it means 'lovers', if 'rohim', 'shepherds'.

XLIX. Yet return to me, says the Lord or and you used to return to me, says the Lord. In the Hebrew he even receives her as a penitent after her fornication and exhorts her to return to him; but in the Seventy he does not call her to repentance, but rebukes the shamelessness of the harlot, in that after adultery she dares to return to her husband. 2. And as to what he says: and that woman will be defiled, for which in the Hebrew we read 'land', he leaves the example and speaks more plainly of the land of Israel, which is compared to an adulterous woman. Let us use this testimony against those who, abandoning faith in the Lord and entangled in the errors of the heretics, after many fornications and the deception of souls, pretend to return to the former truth, not in order to lay aside the poisons of their breast, but in order to instill them into others.

L. Lift up your eyes straight ahead and see where you have not been prostrated; you sat in the ways waiting for them like a robber in the wilderness or like a deserted crow. In place of 'robber' and 'crow' in the Hebrew 'arabe' is written, which can also signify 'Arabs', a nation given to brigandage, which to this day raids the borders of Palestine and besets the roads for those going down from Jerusalem to Jericho, a thing the Lord too recalls in the Gospel. 2. 'Lift up, therefore, your eyes, O Jerusalem, and look around this way and that, and see where you have not been by fornication prostrated. For just as robbers are accustomed, toward evening and in deserted places, to lay ambushes for travelers, so you, like the harlot of Proverbs, sat in the ways toward evening, to kill by your intercourse the souls of those who commit fornication; on account of which the whole land is polluted with your fornications'. 3. And significantly, according to anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense], those who promise that they will abandon heretical errors are commanded to lift their eyes straight ahead. For unless they begin to see what is straight, they cannot condemn their former crookedness.

LI. And you have polluted — or killed — the land with your fornications and in your evils. Therefore the drops of the rains have been withheld, and there was no late rain or and you had many shepherds for your stumbling. The land was killed or polluted on account of the killing of those who perished by the fornication of idolatry; hence the blessing of all things was taken away, so that they suffered a drought of the word of God. Or she had shepherds through whom she offended God, so that those who ought to have been teachers, to keep others from error, became the authors of impiety.

LII. The brow of a harlot woman has become yours, you were unwilling to blush. LXX: the face of a harlot has become yours, you have become without blushing toward all. Because he had said above: I have not sinned, and she had sinned the more by denying her crimes, therefore he now rebukes her as a brazen woman of excessive shamelessness, so that she is not borne with brazen face to one man and another only, but blushes before none. Let us use this word against the conventicle of the heretics, who glory in their errors.

LIII. Therefore at least from now on call me: My father, you are the guide of my virginity; will you be angry forever, or will you persevere to the end? Let the heretics blush, who are unwilling to be turned to better things or to go back to the Father, their creator, and let them hear: at least from now on call me: my father, you are the guide of my virginity. 2. He himself has betrothed your soul to his embraces and teaches how it ought to pray and to do repentance. But the more merciful he is who shows the way of salvation after fornication, the more wretched is the harlot who is unwilling to receive healing after her wounds.

LIV. Behold, you have spoken and done evil things, and you were able. 'In place of words of repentance you have blasphemed with words of pride, and you have fulfilled your evil thought and shown your strength against your husband, so that you can do what you handled in speech.'

LV. And the Lord said to me in the days of King Josiah: Have you seen what averse Israel has done? She went off by herself upon every high mountain and under every leafy tree, and committed fornication there. And I said, when she had done all these things: Turn to me; and she did not return. 2. And her sister, Judah the transgressor, saw that, because averse Israel had committed adultery, I had dismissed her and given her a bill of divorce; and the transgressor Judah, her sister, did not fear, but went and committed fornication, she too, and by the facility of her fornication she defiled the land and committed adultery with stone and wood. And in all these things her sister, Judah the transgressor, did not return to me with her whole heart, but in falsehood, says the Lord. 3. The torments of some are the remedies of others; and when a murderer is punished, he himself indeed receives what he has done, but others are deterred from the crime. Thus, when the ten tribes, which were called Israel, had been captured by the Assyrians and carried over into Media, the two tribes, Judah and Benjamin, which ought to have feared the like and to have been turned to God with their whole mind, surpassed the crimes of the ten tribes and followed idols to such a degree that they set up a statue of Baal in the temple of God, which in Ezekiel is called the idol set up to provoke the zeal and jealousy of the Lord. 4. Now he speaks under the figure of two sisters, because they were begotten of the one stock of Abraham and Isaac and Jacob, and he calls the first the averse one, the second the transgressor. For the former became wholly averse to God at once, when the golden calves were worshipped in Dan and Bethel; but the latter, with whom were the temple and the religion of the true God, by imitating her sister gradually withdrew from the Lord, and is therefore called the transgressor. 5. But according to anagōgēn the prophecy concerns the heretics, who, while they imagine that they are pursuing with heretical subtlety the knowledge falsely so called, climb the mountain of pride and, beguiled by the pleasures of this flesh, display their fornication under every leafy and pleasant tree. And when they are handed over to the devil for the destruction of the flesh, it often happens that the house of 'Iuda', that is, of 'confession' and of the true faith, is by no means frightened by the example, but commits far greater things, and by the facility of its fornication defiles the land of the Church and commits adultery with stone and with wood, following those doctrines which are adverse to God. 6. But if a man of the Church wishes to correct the one who errs, and to cut away the rotten flesh, and to draw back to repentance those who had followed falsehood, and they nonetheless, under the appearance of the Church's truth, follow their old error, it can be said of them: in all these things the transgressor Judah has not returned to me with her whole heart, but in falsehood. Now this prophecy is made in the times of Josiah, a righteous king, under whom Jeremiah also began to prophesy.

LVI. And the Lord said to me: Averse Israel has justified her soul in comparison with the transgressor Judah. 'More righteous,' he says, 'is Israel in comparison with Judah, because the former perished at once, at the beginning, while the latter could have been corrected by the torments of the former'. Let the new heresy sprung from the old take note that Israel is said to be justified in comparison with a worse one. Nor is this surprising of sisters of one nation, since Sodom too, in comparison with Jerusalem, receives the name of righteousness, as the Lord says through Ezekiel: 'Sodom has been justified by you', and the publican becomes righteous in comparison with the Pharisee.

LVII. Go and cry — or read — these words toward the north, and you shall say: Return, averse Israel, says the Lord, and I will not turn away — or I will fix — my face from you — or upon you —, for holy — or merciful — am I, says the Lord, and I will not be angry forever. Yet know your iniquity, that you have transgressed against the Lord God — or you have acted impiously — and you have scattered — or poured out — your ways to strangers under every leafy tree, and you have not heard my voice, says the Lord. 2. The Hebrew word 'carath' is understood both as 'call' or 'cry' and as 'read'; hence Aquila and Symmachus translated 'keep crying', the Seventy and Theodotion 'read'. Now the word is directed to the north and against Babylon and the Assyrians, to the ten and to the two tribes, and their return is proclaimed. 'And I will not turn away', he says, 'my face from you, or I will not fix my face against you, so that I may receive you not with the severity of a judge but with a countenance of mercy. 3. For I am holy and merciful, so as no longer to recall your iniquity nor to remember that you withdrew from the Lord and that in his place idols delighted you, and that you committed fornication under every shady and leafy tree'. This indeed may fittingly be said also to the heretics, or to those who are negligent in the Church, who are daily called to repentance by men of the Church, and to whom this can properly be applied: 'and you have not heard my voice.' 4. And every heretic dwells in the north and has lost the warmth of faith, and cannot hear that word of the Apostle: fervent in spirit; and because he has given himself over to pleasures, he has withdrawn from the Lord and scattered his ways among alien doctrines and followed pleasure. For no heresy is built except for the sake of the gullet and the belly, in order to seduce silly women laden with sins, always learning and never arriving at the knowledge of the truth, of whom it is truly said: who devour my people like food of bread, and whom Christ marks as devouring the houses of widows. 5. 'And when,' he says, 'I have had mercy on you, do not think that you are righteous, but always remember your iniquity and know that you have committed fornication against the Lord, and bow down the neck of pride, so that you who offended through arrogance may appease God through humility'. But to that which we said above, and I will not fix my face upon you, that prophetic word is fitting: turn away your face from my sins and blot out all my iniquities.

LVIII. Be converted, returning sons — or wandering and departing —, says the Lord, for I am your husband — or I will be lord over you —, and I will take you, one from a city and two from a kindred, and I will bring you into Zion, and I will give you shepherds according to my heart, and they will feed you with knowledge and doctrine. And when you have been multiplied and have increased in the land in those days, says the Lord, they will no longer say: the ark of the covenant — or testament — of the Lord; nor will it come up upon the heart, nor will they remember it, nor will it be visited, nor will it be made any more. 2. The Jews think that this was fulfilled after the return from Babylon under Cyrus, king of the Persians, and Zerubbabel son of Shealtiel, even though not all returned; that this is what is signified by: I will take one from a city and two from a kindred. But it is better taken of the coming of Christ, when the remnant was saved, as the Apostle says and explains: unless the Lord of Sabaoth had left us seed, we would have been as Sodom and would have been like Gomorrah; then they were brought into Zion, of which it is written: glorious things are spoken of you, city of God, and shepherds were given according to his heart, the apostles and apostolic men, and they fed the multitude of believers not in Jewish ceremonies, but in the knowledge and doctrine of Christ, and with the preaching of the Gospel sown in the whole world. 3. They will by no means have confidence in the ark of the Lord, which was the guardian of the Mosaic law, but they themselves will be the temple of God; nor, like the erring Nazarenes, will they serve sacrifices that have been abolished, but they will pursue a spiritual worship. Others, however, understand this of the end of the times, when, as the fullness of the Gentiles comes in, all Israel will be saved.

LIX. At that time they will call Jerusalem the throne of the Lord, and all the nations will be gathered to it in the name of the Lord in Jerusalem, and they will not walk after the depravity of their most wicked heart. The Lord will by no means sit upon the ark of the testament and the cherubim, to whom that people formerly used to say: you who sit upon the cherubim, show yourself, but all who believe with a perfect mind will be the throne of God. 2. Or certainly the whole is better understood of the Church, when all the nations are gathered in the name of the Lord into 'Hierusalem', in which is the 'vision of peace', and by no means walk after the depravity of their most wicked heart, so as to do what they desire, nor follow their own errors, but say with the prophet: my soul has clung after you, your right hand has upheld me.

LX. In those days the house of Judah will go to the house of Israel, and they will come together from the land of the north to the land which I gave to your fathers. This is properly fulfilled at the coming of Christ, when out of the twelve tribes together they believed the Gospel, and, leaving the land of the north and of the harshest cold and withdrawing from the dominion of the devil, then received the land of promise which he had promised to their fathers, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob. I recently published a small book on the land of promise.

LXI. But I said: how or I will set you among sons and give you a desirable land, a glorious inheritance of the hosts of the nations! And I said: You will call me father, and you will not cease to walk after me. In place of 'a glorious inheritance of the hosts of the nations', which the Seventy translated: a named inheritance of God almighty of the nations, Theodotion translated more significantly: a renowned inheritance of the strength of the mightiest one of the nations, signifying Christ, who is the leader and lord of all the nations that believe in his name and passion. 2. He himself said to Israel: you will call me father, and: he who believes in me believes in the Father. He himself promised: 'I will set you among sons, that is, in the number of my sons who have believed in me from the people of the nations and to whom I have given a desirable land'. For as many as received him, he gave them power to become sons of God.

LXII. But as if a woman should despise her lover, so has the house of Israel despised me, says the Lord. It is the voice of Christ toward the people of the Jews, to whom he had said: I will set you among sons and give you a desirable land, and you will call me father and will not cease to walk after me. 2. 'As,' he says, 'a woman despises not a husband but a lover, once she has been joined with him, when she sees him serving her lust and the law of nature reversed in her regard, by which she had once been subject to a husband, as the Lord says: and toward you shall be her turning, so the house of Israel, that is, the people of the Jews, has despised the Lord, the Savior, to its own ruin.'

LXIII. A voice in the ways — or on the lips — was heard, the weeping and wailing of the sons of Israel, because they have made their way wicked, they have forgotten the Lord their God. Be converted, returning sons, and I will heal your aversions — or bruises — , for which Symmachus translated conversions. God gladly receives the penitent, and runs to meet the son worn out with want and squalor, and at once clothes him in his former garments and restores glory to the one returning, provided only that he returns with weeping and wailing. 2. For by his own fault he has made his way wicked and has forgotten the Lord, his God and Father; to such the prophetic word now speaks: be converted, returning sons. 'Whom I call sons for this reason, because, having understood your sins, you return with weeping and wailing to your parent. And when you', he says, 'have returned to the Lord, he will heal all your bruises or aversions, by which you had withdrawn from the Lord, or certainly your conversions'. 3. For although we return to the Lord by our own will, yet unless he draws us and strengthens our desire with his protection, we shall not be able to be saved. Let us understand this both of the people of the Jews returning to the Lord and of the heretics who have forsaken the Lord.

LXIV. Behold, we come to you; for you are the Lord our God. Truly the hills were liars, and the multitude — or strength — of the mountains; truly in the Lord our God is the salvation of Israel. Let the penitent say this, leaving behind all pride and the multitude or height of the mountains and hills through which he grew proud against God, and, prostrate in humility, let him speak: truly in the Lord our God is the salvation of Israel.

LXV. Confusion has devoured the labor of our fathers from our youth, their flocks and their herds, their sons and their daughters. All the labors of the heretics, of which it is written: they have failed, searching with searching, from the youth of those whom they had deceived, their sons and their daughters, who had advanced in heresy or were held only by luxury, our confusion has overwhelmed. Hence they add:

LXVI. We will sleep in our confusion, and our shame will cover us, because we have sinned against our God, we and our fathers, from our youth up to this day, and we have not heard the voice of the Lord our God. Let Israel say this, who did not hear his Lord; let every heretic say this who repents; and yet it is a part of salvation to confess and to know one's sins. 'Tell,' he says, 'your iniquities first, that you may be justified'. 2. For truly Israel forsook Christ, the Lord his God, and sinned against him not only at the time when he was seen in the flesh, but also before his coming. Hence they say: 'we and our fathers, from our youth up to this day, and we have not heard the voice of our God, who spoke to our fathers: if you believed Moses, you would believe me also; for he wrote of me'.

Jeremiah 4

LXVII.

If you return, Israel, says the Lord, to me, turn back, for which the Seventy translated: if Israel is turned, says the Lord, to me, he shall be turned. And the sense is: 'if he has returned to me, he will return from captivity', or otherwise: 'when he has offered what he has'. For to him who has, it shall be given; but from him who has not, even that which he seems to have shall be taken away. 2. But according to the Hebrew the sense is this: 'if you return to me, Israel, and once for all desire salvation, and say that you have sinned and have not listened to the voice of your Lord, turn back fully; believe in him whom you denied, and then there will be full conversion'.

LXVIII. If you take away your stumbling blocks from before my face, you shall not be moved. When, therefore, we are moved and say: but my feet were almost moved, we do not suffer this through the weakness of nature, but because we set up stumbling blocks and our idols against the Lord.

LXIX. And you shall swear: the Lord lives, in truth and in judgment and in righteousness, and the nations shall bless him and shall praise him. And how is it that the Gospel forbids us to swear? But here 'you shall swear' is said for confession and for the condemnation of the idols by which Israel used to swear. Accordingly the stumbling blocks are taken away and he swears by the Lord. 2. And as for what is said: the Lord lives, in the Old Testament it is an oath for the condemnation of the dead, by whom all idolatry swears. At the same time it must be noted that an oath has these companions: truth, judgment and righteousness; if these are lacking, it will by no means be an oath, but perjury. 'And when,' he says, 'Israel has done this too, and through the apostles has become the teacher of the nations, then all the nations shall bless, or be blessed, in him, and shall praise him, because salvation has gone forth from Israel'.

LXX. For thus says the Lord to the man of Judah and Jerusalem: break up fallow ground for yourselves, and do not sow upon thorns; be circumcised to the Lord and take away the foreskins of your hearts, men of Judah and inhabitants of Jerusalem, lest my indignation go forth like fire and be kindled, and there be none to quench it, because of the wickedness of the thoughts — or devices — of yours. 2. For what we said: be circumcised to the Lord and take away the foreskin of your hearts, Symmachus put: be purified to the Lord and take away the wickednesses of your hearts, understanding circumcision as cleansing and foreskins as vice. And this is commanded to the men of Judah and Jerusalem who follow the true faith and dwell in the Church: that they should not sow upon thorns, which the word of the Gospel signifies, thorns that choke the seed of God, but should first make fallow ground, dig up all the brambles and take away the briars, so that clean fields may receive clean seeds. 3. This is what is said in another place: do not cast your pearls before swine, and do not give what is holy to dogs. For how can a man hear the word of God and conceive the seeds and bear fruit, whose mind is full of the troubles of the world? And as for what follows: be circumcised to the Lord and take away the foreskins of your hearts, it is commanded to no one else but the man of Judah and the inhabitant of Jerusalem, that they should forsake the letter that kills and follow the Spirit that gives life. 4. 'For if you do not do this,' he says, 'my indignation will go forth like fire and will be kindled, and there will be none to quench it'. And he therefore warns and foretells beforehand so as not to be compelled to do it, as we also prove in the case of the Ninevites, to whom the sentence was foretold so that they might turn aside the impending fury by repentance. And all these evils will come because of the wickedness of our thoughts or devices. Where are those who say that there is no sin in thoughts, when, according to the truth of the Gospel, all vices proceed from the heart?

LXXI. Declare in Judah and make it heard in Jerusalem; speak, sound the trumpet in the land, cry aloud, say: gather together, and let us enter the fortified cities.
