Let 'Iudas' hear this, let 'Hierusalem' hear this, in which is the 'confession' of faith, and in which the 'peace' of Christ dwells, and to which it was said through Isaiah: go up onto a high mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion; lift up your voice, you who bring good tidings to Jerusalem; let it cry aloud and give command thus: 2. 'let us enter the fortified cities — the wars of the heretics are rising: let the fortifications of Christ hold us — ; raise the standard of the cross on the watchtower and on the height of the Church; be strong, you who fear, do not stand still, but run to the help of Christ; for evil,' he says, 'I bring from the north, and great destruction, the true Nebuchadnezzar, who is permitted by me to exist in this world for this very reason, that your strength and victory may be proved'.

LXXII. The lion has come up from his lair, and the plunderer of the nations has raised himself up; he has gone forth from his place to make your land a desolation; your cities will be laid waste, remaining without inhabitant. This is, as we have said, the true Nebuchadnezzar, of whom Peter also speaks: our adversary the devil goes about like a roaring lion. 2. And he comes up, it may be, from the abysses, into which he is to be bound again, and he begs not to be sent there, and the plunderer — or the devastator — of the nations has raised himself up, of whom it was said: he shall rule over all his enemies, and who boasts in the sight of the Lord: I have gone around the whole earth and trodden it down. For who is there whom the poisons of the devil do not touch, except him alone who can say: behold, the prince of this world comes and finds nothing in me? 3. He often makes the whole land of the Church a desolation, so that those who have gone out from the Church fight against the Church, of whom John the evangelist also speaks: they went out from us, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would surely have remained with us. The cities of the land of Judea are laid waste and the conventicles of the heretics flourish; if anyone, therefore, is a supporter of the authors of perverse doctrines, of him it can be said: the lion has come up from his lair, and the plunderer of the nations has raised himself up, and the rest.

LXXIII. For this gird yourselves with sackcloth, lament and howl, for the wrath of the fury of the Lord has not turned away from us or, as the Seventy translated, from you. In no other way can we escape the lion and most savage beast unless we do repentance and turn to the Lord, not only in mind but also in deed. For as long as he lays waste the Church and the land of Judah, and ravages Jerusalem as well, the wrath of God is manifest.

LXXIV. And it shall be in that day, says the Lord: the heart of the king shall perish, and the heart of the princes, and the priests shall be astounded, and the prophets shall be dismayed. While the plunderer lays waste the Church of the Lord and the wrath of the Lord remains against us, all help will be useless. The heart of the king shall perish, whose heart ought to be in the hand of God, and the heart of the princes, who were thought to be wise; for God has made foolish the wisdom of the world, because through it they did not know God. 2. The priests themselves too, who ought to teach the law of the Lord and to defend the peoples subject to them from the fury of the lion, made senseless by a kind of stupor, shall be turned to madness — for so the Seventy translated, expressing 'ecstasy of mind' for 'stupor' — and the prophets shall be dismayed, or, as Aquila translated the Hebrew word 'iethmau', they shall be out of their minds. For who would not go mad, who would not lose heart, when he sees the princes and kings and priests and prophets that were once his own under the lion?

LXXV. And I said: alas, alas, alas, Lord God — for which the Seventy translated: O Lord God —, have you then deceived this people and Jerusalem, saying: you shall have peace? And behold, the sword has reached even to the soul. 2. Because he had said above: at that time they shall call Jerusalem the throne of God, and all the nations shall be gathered to it in the name of the Lord in Jerusalem, and now he says: the heart of the king shall perish, and the heart of the princes, and the priests shall be astounded, and the prophets shall be dismayed, the prophet is troubled and thinks that God has lied to him, and does not understand that the former was promised for long ages after, while this would come about in the near time, in accordance with which the Apostle too says: has God rejected his people? Far from it. 3. And the sword reaches even to the soul when nothing living is left in the soul. At the same time he shows this too: that unless the sword goes before, to cut away and purge the vices of the soul, peace and the promise do not follow.

LXXVI. At that time it shall be said to this people and to Jerusalem: a burning wind — or of error — in the ways that are in the desert, the ways of the daughter of my people, not for winnowing and cleansing. A full spirit shall come to me from these. When the sword has reached even to the soul and the threshing floor has been finished, then the burning wind will come from the desert, which will not cleanse and winnow it, so that, with the chaff scattered here and there, the grain may be laid up in the granaries; but a full spirit will come, not to the people at all, but to me, so that my wheat may be scattered. 2. 'Wind' and 'spirit' are called among the Hebrews by the same name, 'ruha', and according to the nature of each passage we should take it either as 'wind' or as 'spirit'. Others expound this passage thus: that after the threshing floor has been cleansed, the remnant will be saved, whence it is also written: a spirit of fullness shall come to me, as the evangelist says: 'we have all received of his fullness, and we shall obtain the grace of the Holy Spirit'. But according to the history take the burning wind as Nebuchadnezzar, who consumes all things; according to tropology, as the adverse power, which, coming from the desert and from the wilderness, where there is no lodging of God, tries to overthrow his Church.

LXXVII. And now I — but I will speak my judgments with them. Aposiōpēsis [breaking off], after that line of Vergil: whom I — but it is better to calm the troubled waves. Being about to speak of good fortune, then, he restrains himself and joins sorrows to sorrows; for these are the judgments which God speaks with his people of old, so that they may know that they justly suffer what they suffer.

LXXVIII. Behold, he shall come up like a cloud, and like a tempest his chariots; his horses are swifter than eagles. Woe to us, for we are laid waste! He sees things to come as present and describes the Babylonian army, the din of whose chariots and wheels is compared to a most savage tempest, and the swiftness of whose horses is likened to eagles. 2. When the prophet had said this and had pointed out with his finger the enemies as though they were coming, the people groans and feels these things not as future but as already done, saying: woe to us, for we are laid waste! Refer this same thing to the Church: that daily the army of the true Nebuchadnezzar assails us, and the chariots of Pharaoh and all his cavalry outstrip the onrush of eagles. If the man of the Church understands this, believing that saying: 'when you have turned and groaned, then you shall be saved', he will say: woe to us, for we are laid waste!

LXXIX. Wash your heart from wickedness, Jerusalem, that you may be saved; how long shall harmful thoughts linger in you? To the people saying: woe to us, we are laid waste! the prophet answers, nay rather God through the prophet: wash your heart from wickedness, Jerusalem, with that water of which Isaiah too speaks: wash yourselves, be clean, the water of saving baptism, the water of repentance. 2. And he speaks to the metropolis of the Jews, so that by the city the peoples may be understood: 'how long do you lie subject to wicked thoughts, which proceed from your heart?' And in the holy Scriptures we should take the heart for the soul and the understanding.

LXXX. For the voice of one declaring from Dan, and of one making known an idol — or pain — from mount Ephraim. The divine word now speaks according to the lie of the land of Judea. For the tribe of Dan, near Mount Lebanon and the city which is now called Paneas, faces the north, from which Nebuchadnezzar is to come. 2. And he describes an idol, whether Bel, or pain, or iniquity, as coming from mount Ephraim. For after the tribe of Dan comes the land of Ephraim, through which one comes to Jerusalem. 'Dan' means 'judgment', 'Ephraim' 'abundance'. The judgment of the Lord, therefore, will come upon the land that sins against the Lord, with all the abundance of punishment.

LXXXI. Say to the nations: behold, it has been heard in Jerusalem that guards are coming from a distant land; and they have given their voice over the cities of Judah; like guards of fields they have become over her round about, because she has provoked me to anger, says the Lord. He wants all the nations round about to know the sentence of God, and all to receive correction from the scourging of Jerusalem. 2. 'It is told,' he says, 'by common report in Jerusalem that adversaries are coming from a distant land, and that the roar of a howling army is rising against her, who besiege the city so diligently and shut in the city with siegeworks, that you would think them not so much adversaries as guards of fields and vineyards. But all this has come about not by the strength of the enemies, but by the fault of Jerusalem, because she provoked God to anger'. For if the adverse powers have no power over swine, how much more is this true of men, and of men once of the city of God!

LXXXII. Your ways and your thoughts have done these things to you; this is your wickedness, because it is bitter, it has touched your heart. He makes an apostrophe to the city of Jerusalem: that her ways and thoughts, by which she sinned both in deed and in word, have made all that came upon her come, and that her wickedness, which is bitter in itself, has touched her heart and pierced the inmost parts of her soul. Whatever, therefore, befalls us befalls us through our own fault, who turn the sweet God into bitterness and force him to rage against his will.

LXXXIII. My belly, my belly, I am in pain; the senses of my heart are troubled. I will not keep silent, for my soul has heard the sound of the trumpet, the clamor of battle. Destruction upon destruction has been called, and all the land is laid waste; suddenly my tents are laid waste, in a moment my skins. Where we, following Symmachus, have put 'are troubled', and in the Hebrew is written 'homae', the Seventy and Theodotion put maimassei, a word whose meaning I do not know to this day. Aquila, however, put ochlazei, which itself also means 'tumult'. 2. Let this be said about the word, over which I know there is a great dispute among very many. And the voice is introduced of the prophet, and through the prophet of God speaking, that he grieves over the destruction of his people and his inner bowels are torn as a man's are, just as the Savior too grieved over the death of Lazarus and lamented Jerusalem, so as not to hide his grief in silence; and every blast of the trumpet and roar of the battles disturbs his feeling, while evils are heaped upon evils and the whole land of the two tribes is laid waste. 3. 'While I was not expecting it,' he says, 'my tents of old and my skins were ravaged by the raging Babylonian, and my former lodgings passed into the plunder of the enemies'. And God says this same thing when he sees the tumults of sedition and discords in the Church, and the partridge crying in his assemblies, and the rest of God turned into wars. Whence it follows:

LXXXIV. How long shall I see those fleeing, shall I hear the sound of the trumpet? Either those fleeing the Babylonian king, or those fleeing me and withdrawing from my service.

LXXXV. For my foolish people has not known me. They are unwise sons and senseless; they are wise to do evil, but to do good they have not known. The cause of the destruction, the devastation, the flight, the trumpet: that the people has become foolish, not by nature but by the bent of its will. And this very folly is proved from this, that they did not know God; and instead of wise sons they have become foolish sons and senseless. 2. For what greater folly can there be than that, while the ox knows its owner and the ass its master's crib, Israel should not know the Lord and should despise him when present, whom it always desired to see? And as for what is added: they are wise to do evil, but to do good they have not known, here wisdom is to be taken for wickedness, in accordance with which the sons of this age too are wiser than the sons of light, and the steward of iniquity is said to have done certain things wisely, and the serpent in paradise is read to have been more prudent than all the beasts. 3. That, then, is true wisdom which is joined to the fear of God; otherwise, where there are snares and evasion, it is to be called not wisdom but cunning and craftiness. For what we said: for my foolish people has not known me, the Seventy translated: for the princes of my people have not known me, so that the fault is rather the teachers' than that of the people, which does not have the knowledge of God.

LXXXVI. I looked at the earth, and behold, it was empty and nothing, and at the heavens, and there was no light in them. I saw the mountains, and behold, they were moving, and all the hills were troubled. I gazed, and there was no man, and the bird of the sky had departed. I looked, and behold, Carmel was deserted, and all its cities were destroyed before the face of the Lord and before the face of the wrath of his fury. The prophet sees in the spirit the things that are to come, so that the people, hearing, may be terrified and, having done repentance, may by no means suffer what it dreads. 2. The earth is empty, its inhabitant destroyed; the heavens have no light, since the people does not see for the greatness of its terror; the mountains themselves and the hills have unsafe hiding places, and by hyperbolēn [hyperbole] they seem to move and be troubled. He gazed and looked around here and there, and not even a bird could be found; for even the mute elements feel the wrath of God, and the irrational animals dread it. 3. That this is true the whole world now shows, in that, a multitude of men having been slain, the birds too, which are wont to follow the inhabitants, have gone away and perished. Carmel itself too, which overhangs the great sea, planted with olives and thick with shrubs and vines, will come to such desolation that it will have the waste of a wilderness; all the cities too will become deserted. 4. And the cause of all these evils is this: that through the fault of the sinning people the wrath of the Lord has been roused. Whatever we have said according to the history of Jerusalem and Judea, let us refer to the Church of God when it has offended God and has been laid waste either by vices or by persecution, so that where once there was a choir of virtues and joy, there a multitude of sins and sorrows dwells.

LXXXVII. For thus says the Lord: all the land shall be deserted, but yet I will not make a full end; the earth shall mourn and the heavens above shall grieve, because I have spoken, I have purposed, and I have not repented, nor have I turned back from it. God's mercy being mixed with wrath, all the land is deserted, but a full end is not made, so that there may be those who understand his clemency. 2. The heaven too will look sad above, and the earth itself will mourn, because the sentence of the Lord has come right to its end, and he has not repented of the things he purposed and spoke. But repentance is ascribed to God when a sentence foretold is withdrawn and raging wrath does not come right to its end. He threatened through Jonah, and the multitude of tears and groans overcame the impending sword.

LXXXVIII. At the sound of the horseman and of the one shooting the arrow — or bending the bow — every city has fled — or region — ; they have gone into the heights and climbed the rocks — and what follows: and they have gone into the thickets or caves, was added by the Seventy —, all the cities are abandoned, and no man dwells in them. 2. The divine word describes the army of the raging Babylonian: that for fear of it the whole people abandoned the city and climbed every height, and yet could not escape the wrath of the Lord. But whatever, as we said above, is understood in the history against Jerusalem, refer to the Church when it has offended God and has been handed over to adversaries, either in a time of persecution or, at any rate, through vices and sins.

LXXXIX. But you, laid waste, what will you do? For 'laid waste', which in Hebrew is said 'sadud', which Aquila alone translated so, the others translated 'wretched' and 'pitiable', through her own fault, because she has offended the merciful God. Accordingly it follows:

XC. When you clothe yourself in scarlet and are adorned with a golden necklace and paint your eyes with antimony, in vain will you adorn yourself; your lovers have despised you, they will seek your soul. He speaks under the figure of an adulterous woman: 'once you have offended God and, as it were, left your husband, the Creator, in vain do you seek out ornaments; the demons, your lovers, have despised you, and they will seek not at all the uncleanness of debauchery, but the destruction of your soul'. 2. This same thing is to be understood spiritually against those who have lost conjugal affection and the chastity of the true faith. 'If,' he says, 'you clothe yourself in scarlet — take up faith in the blood of Christ —, if you adorn yourself with a golden necklace — have meditation of the sense and of spiritual understanding — and paint your eyes with antimony — have zeal for the mysteries and for knowing the secrets of God —, in vain will you adorn yourself. For these things you had prepared for your lovers too, and therefore the narrow bed cannot hold both, nor does God accept the ornaments with which you formerly pleased your lovers'.

XCI. For I have heard a voice as of a woman in labor, anguish — or groans — as of a woman in childbirth; the voice of the daughter of Zion, swooning and spreading out her hands: woe to me, for my soul has fainted because of the slain! He describes the city of Jerusalem wailing and crying out after the likeness of a woman in childbirth, that is, one who is bearing her first offspring. 2. For as a woman giving birth, who has not yet experienced the pain of childbearing, swoons — she suffers anguish, she can scarcely breathe, and with her hands spread out she collapses —, so too the daughter of Zion, when she sees her children slain, bursts out into these words and says: woe to me, for my soul has fainted because of the slain! Two examples are compared in one passage: of a woman bearing children and of a woman mourning, so that whatever a woman suffers in childbirth or in the deaths of her children, Jerusalem may suffer in her peoples.

Jeremiah 5:1-19

XCII.

Go about the ways of Jerusalem, and look, and consider, and seek in its squares, whether you may find a man who does judgment and seeks faith, and I will be merciful to it. But if they even say: The Lord lives, this too they will swear falsely. Great is the love of righteousness: not that God should deliver the city for ten righteous men, as in Abraham's question and God's answer, but that if he finds a single one in Jerusalem, now on the very point of perishing, who does judgment and seeks faith — or, as Symmachus translated, truth —, God would nonetheless have mercy on Jerusalem. 2. And because it could happen that some would be found among the people who feigned the worship of God and swore by the Lord, he forestalls this: that God is in no way delighted by empty words, but by the truth of faith; and he says: 'I do not love them because they swear by me, and they swear in falsehood; but those whose hearts and lips agree'.

XCIII. Lord, your eyes look upon faith. You struck them, and they did not grieve; you crushed them, and they refused to receive discipline; they hardened their faces beyond rock, and they would not return. After the words of the Lord, by which he had given command, saying: Go about the ways of Jerusalem and the rest, the prophet speaks to the Lord: Lord, your eyes look upon faith, which in Hebrew is called 'emuna': not the works of the Jews, in which they exulted according to the ceremonies of the law, but the faith of Christians, through which we have been saved by grace. 2. From this passage we learn that punishments are inflicted for this reason: that vices may be corrected. Accordingly he says: You struck them, and they did not grieve; you crushed them, and they refused to receive discipline. For Jerusalem is corrected through every torment and scourge; and over and above all these they did not even feel shame for their own vices, but, hardening the impudence of their brow like a rock, they would not be converted to better things.

XCIV. But I said: Perhaps they are poor and foolish — or they could not — not knowing the way of the Lord, the judgment of their God. I will go therefore to the nobles and will speak to them; for they have known the way of the Lord, the judgment of their God. He speaks here of the poor and the nobles not in terms of want and riches; rather, he compares the people with the princes. And the sense is: 'Seeing the obstinacy of the unbelieving people, and that with hardened face it would not receive discipline, I was reasoning this out with myself: perhaps the ignoble common crowd cannot know the teaching of God and is therefore excusable, because through inexperience it is not able to know the commandments of God. 2. I will go therefore to the priests and to those who preside over the people, and will speak to them; for they have known the will of the Lord and know the judgment of their God'. He says this in the language of one who doubts, according to that saying of the Gospel: I will send my son; perhaps they will reverence him, so that from the ambiguity of the statement and the suspense of the words the free will of man may be shown.

XCV. And behold, these all the more together have broken the yoke, have burst the bonds. Therefore a lion from the forest struck them, a wolf at evening laid them waste, a leopard watching over their cities. Everyone who goes out from them will be taken, because their transgressions are multiplied, their turnings-away are strengthened. 'Those whom I thought to be teachers were found to be worse than the disciples; and the greater the authority among the rich, the greater the insolence of their sins'. 2. For they broke the yoke of the law, as the Apostle says: Now therefore why do you tempt God, to put a yoke upon the neck of the disciples, which neither our fathers nor we have been able to bear? But through the grace of the Lord Jesus we believe that we are saved, in the same way as they also; and they burst the bonds of the precepts of God, and not of the Pharisees, of whom it is said in the second Psalm: Let us burst their bonds asunder, and cast away their yoke from us. 3. Because, then, they did these things, a lion from the forest struck them, namely the Babylonian kingdom; a wolf at evening laid them waste, signifying the Medes and Persians, in place of which in the vision of Daniel a 'bear' is set down, in whose mouth there were three rows; a leopard watching over their cities, prefiguring the onslaught of Alexander and his swift course from the west all the way to India. He calls him a leopard on account of his variegation, and because he fought against the Medes and Persians with very many nations subject to him. And there were, he says, four heads on the beast, and power was given to it. 4. And because he is not prophesying of the future, but is weaving the history of the past or of things now on the very point of coming, for that reason he is silent about the Roman empire, of which perhaps it is said: Everyone who goes out from them will be taken. And he gives the causes why they suffered these things: because their transgressions are multiplied and they persevered in their transgressions. Hence it is said: and their turnings-away are strengthened. 5. What we set down at the beginning, that the Hebrew word 'sacad' signifies 'watchfulness', is shown in the present place; for where we said 'a leopard watching', in the Hebrew is written 'nemer sacad'. According to tropology: those who are thought great in the Church, because they break the yoke and burst the bonds, are for that reason handed over to the disgrace of passions, so that they do the things that are not fitting.

XCVI. On what ground shall I be able to be merciful to you? Your sons have forsaken me and swear by those who are not gods. I filled them, and they committed adultery, and in the house of the harlot they ran riot. They have become to me horses, lovers toward the females, and stallions; each one neighed after the wife of his neighbor. Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord, and on such a nation shall not my soul take vengeance? A Katalogos [a catalogue] of the sins of Jerusalem, while God says that he does not know on what ground he could have mercy on her. 2. 'The sons', he says, 'of yours have forsaken me: by no means mine, but yours, because they swear by those who are not gods. I filled them, and they committed adultery'. Let those hear this who, brooding over the riches they have received from the Lord, are slaves to luxury. They have become horses, lovers toward the females. For 'stallions' in the Hebrew is written 'mosechim', which all with one accord translated helkontes, that is, 'drawing', so that the size of the genitals may be shown, according to that saying of Ezekiel: like the flesh of asses is their flesh. 3. This is what is written in another place: they were likened to senseless beasts and became like them. And at the same time he shows so great a madness of lust that he calls it not the appetite of pleasure but chremetismon, that is, 'neighing', and keeps the metaphoran [metaphor] of horses raging for lust. 'When you have done these things', he says, 'are you not deserving of a fitting visitation?' And note that here 'visitation' is put for penalty and punishments, according to what is written: I will visit their iniquities with the rod. 4. And on such a nation shall not my soul take vengeance? After she has been bound by sins, she is no longer called the people of God, but a nation, from which the soul of God has withdrawn, according to what is written: your new moons and sabbaths and feast days my soul hates. But what in the Old Testament is said by way of affection, in the New is written as truth, as the Savior says: I have power to lay down my soul, and I have power to take it up.

XCVII. Go up upon her walls — or bulwarks — and destroy, but do not make a full end! Take away her shoots — or props —, because they are not the Lord's! For with transgression the house of Israel and the house of Judah has transgressed against me, says the Lord. 2. He commands the nations of which he had said above: a lion from the forest struck them, a wolf laid them waste, and a leopard in their cities, to go up upon the walls of Jerusalem, or its bulwarks, and destroy it, but not to make a full end, so that the remnant may be saved and there may be one to announce the glory of God among the nations; and he mingles clemency with severity. 3. And as for what he added: take away her shoots or props, he orders all the aids to be removed which she lost by her own fault, because the house of Israel and the house of Judah transgressed against God, signifying the ten tribes and the two. Let the Church hear this: that the walls and bulwarks of those who have no hope in the Lord and transgress against him are quickly destroyed, but that nevertheless a full end is not made, on account of the clemency of the judge and not on account of the merits of the offenders.

XCVIII. They have denied the Lord and said: He is not — or these things are not — nor will evil come upon us; sword and famine we shall not see. The prophets have come to wind, and an answer — or a word — was not in them. These things therefore will come upon them. 2. Because they have denied the Lord — or they have lied to the Lord — and said: 'He is not, by whose judgment all things come to pass, but all these things have happened by chance; and the things which the voices of the prophets threaten us with will not come, nor shall we see the sword, nor shall we endure the famine of a siege; and whatever the prophets spoke, they spoke to the wind, and all things have passed away void, nor did they have an answer, that is, an oracle, or the word of God was not in them', therefore they will endure what the following discourse describes. Let the Church hear this, when it is negligent and rejects the providence of God: that it will endure both sword and famine, unless it believes that the things which are spoken will come.

XCIX. Thus says the Lord God of hosts: Because you have spoken this word, behold, I give my words in your mouth into fire, and this people into wood, and it will devour them. 'You said: The prophets have come to wind, and the things they threaten will not come; therefore, O prophet, I give my words in your mouth, which are to have the power of fire, and which are to turn this people into wood, so that the unbelieving may be burned up together by your discourse and prophecy.' So God is called a consuming fire, that he may consume in us whatever, upon the foundation of Christ, we have built of hay, wood, stubble.

C. Behold, I will bring upon you a nation from afar, O house of Israel, says the Lord, a strong nation, an ancient nation, a nation whose language you will not know, nor will you understand what it says. Its quiver is like an open sepulchre; all are mighty. And it will eat your crops and your bread, it will devour your sons and your daughters, it will eat your flock and your herds, it will eat your vine and your fig tree, and it will crush with the sword your fortified cities, in which you have confidence. Nevertheless in those days, he says, I will not make you into utter destruction. 2. 'Not after a long time, nor, as you falsely believe, will the prophets speak to you into the wind; but even now I will bring upon you a nation of the Babylonians, which will come from afar, a strong nation' — for which in the Hebrew is written 'etham' —, 'an ancient nation' — over which Nimrod the giant once held dominion —, 'whose language you will not know or' — as is written in the Hebrew — 'nor will you understand what it says; for it is a solace in misfortunes if you have such enemies as you can entreat and as understand your prayers'. 3. And what follows: its quiver is like an open sepulchre, which is not said in the edition of the Seventy, signifies the Babylonian armament. Nor is there any doubt that the kingdom of the Assyrians, the Babylonians, the Medes and the Persians is most skilled in archery. And at the same time he describes the devastation of the land of Judea and the slaughter of many, the driving off of cattle, the overthrow of cities and of walls: that all things are taken by the hostile sword, and that yet amid such great evils he does not destroy them to extermination, but keeps a remnant safe, either of those who were led into Babylon and released for the cultivation of the fields of the land of Judea, or of those who, after the heat of persecution, kept the faith of the Lord either by flight or by confession.

CI. But if you say: Why has the Lord our God done all these things to us? you shall say to them: As you have forsaken me and served a foreign god — or foreign gods — in your land, so you shall serve foreigners in a land not yours. Great is the folly of not knowing why they have suffered, when they have sinned so greatly; and brief is the answer to those who are in doubt: as you have served a foreign god — that is, Baal — or foreign gods of all the nations in the land of Judea, so you shall serve foreign gods in a land not yours, no doubt the Babylonians and Chaldea. 2. 'For if a strange religion delights you, what need is there to take up an error from afar? Dwell with such people; nay rather, serve those whose gods you worship.' This can also be taken of heretics, of whom it is written: they went out from us, but they were not of us; for if they had been of us, they would surely have remained with us: that the Lord casts out of the Church the heretics who for a long time under his name have venerated the images of their own lies, so that they may worship outside what they formerly venerated inside, and the chaff may be separated from the wheat.

Book Two

I. The second book on Jeremiah, brother Eusebius, we are dictating in rapid speech, turning our ears away for a little while, so that we may not hear the judgment of blood and lament the souls of the slain: those who, from the opinion of their own virtues, daily collapse into pride and think themselves like God, so that the equality which the impious heresy of the Arians denies in the Son they attribute to all men, and set their mouth against heaven, and leave nothing more for the future blessedness of the saints. 2. To their madness we have replied as best we could, and, if the Lord grants us life, we shall reply more fully. Now we must take up again the road we have begun, the road of dictating this little commentary, neither stretching out the work with excessive length nor taking away the understanding with immoderate brevity: of these, the one burdens the minds of readers, the other cuts short the desire of the studious.

Jeremiah 5:20-31

II.

Declare this to the house of Jacob and make it heard in Judah, saying: Hear, O foolish people, who have no heart, who having eyes do not see, and ears and do not hear! In many ways he draws sinners back to salvation, and he calls foolish the people that has abandoned the author of wisdom, and he compares them to the images of which it is written: They have eyes and do not see; they have ears and do not hear. Let those who make them become like them, and all who trust in them. 2. Properly, however, he speaks to Judah and to the house of Jacob — for Israel had already for a long time been in exile among the Assyrians — and at the same time he gives us to understand that even without a precept we ought to understand by natural sense the things that are right.

III. Will you not then fear me, says the Lord, and will you not grieve before my face — or fear — ? I who set the sand as a boundary for the sea, an everlasting precept, which it will not pass. And they will be moved and will not be able — or it will be troubled and will not be able — and its waves will swell — or will sound — and will not pass over it. 2. But to this people there has come to be an unbelieving and provoking heart, and they have withdrawn and gone away, and they have not said in their heart: Let us fear the Lord our God, who gives us the early and the late rain in its season, keeping for us the fullness of the yearly harvest! He recounts his benefits in order to convict the ungrateful. 3. 'Me,' he says, 'you will not fear, I who have bestowed such great things on you? I do not ask for the love of the perfect, but for the fear of beginners, I who set the sand as a boundary for the sea, I who by my precept have reined in at the shores so mighty an element and the immense masses of the deeps, according to that which is written: He set a precept, and it will not pass. Things that have no sense of hearing hear me and perceive me; and the people once mine, made foolish by its own fault, not only despises but even provokes its sweet God. 4. They have withdrawn,' he says, 'from me, and turned their backs on me, and gone away with hurried step, nor did a silent conscience draw them back, so that they should say in their hearts: Let us fear him who gives us the early and the late rain! — through all of which I have shown good things, the fullness of the yearly harvest', for which Aquila's first edition and Symmachus translated 'weeks'; for in the Hebrew is written 'sabaoth', which, owing to the ambiguity of the word, signifies both 'weeks' and 'fullness'.

IV. Our iniquities have turned these things aside, and our sins have withheld good from us. Therefore, if at any time the sea both oversteps its boundaries and the rain is held back, by no means has the hand of the Lord been shortened, so as not to do these things; but our sins have turned aside these things as they were coming to us, so that they went on to others who had not sinned. 2. 'And they have withheld,' he says, 'the good that was already coming to us, according to that which is written according to the letter: I will command the clouds that they rain no rain upon it'. But we can take the early and the late rain as the law and the Gospel, and as the various callings from the first hour to the eleventh, in which one reward of eternal life is promised to the laborers of the vineyard.

V. For among my people there have been found ungodly men lying in wait like fowlers, setting snares and traps to catch men! As a trap full of birds, so their houses are full of deceit. That the early and the late rain might turn aside from them and all good things might not come, the causes are added: because there have been found among his people 'ungodly men'. 2. He did not say 'unjust' and 'sinners', as the new heresy would have it, but 'ungodly'. Ungodliness openly denies God; iniquity and sin, if it confesses its error, easily bends God to mercy. And for what we have said, lying in wait like fowlers, which is not found in the Seventy, Aquila and Symmachus translated the word 'iasir' as like the net of a fowler, because even he who among them seems good and upright lays snares like a fowler, while they hunt one another to death and fill their own houses with the losses and costs of others, so that that saying of the philosophers is fulfilled: Every rich man is either unjust or the heir of an unjust man. 3. And would that these things were done only by those who seem to be outside, and whom the Lord judges, and were not fulfilled in our own little assemblies by those whom avarice, the root of all evils, possesses, so that we look not at the faces of those who come to us, but at their hands!

VI. Therefore they have been magnified and enriched. They have grown fat and sleek, and they have passed over my words most wickedly. They have not judged the cause, the cause — or judgment — of the orphan they have not directed, and the judgment of the poor — or of the widow — they have not judged. Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord, or shall my soul not take vengeance on a nation such as this? 2. If I should wish to note one by one the things that have been left out in the edition of the Seventy, it will become long. 'Those who lay snares,' he says, 'and rejoice in the nakedness of others, therefore they have been magnified and enriched.' Because they did the things above, they have grown fat and sleek, according to that which is written: He grew fat and sleek, and the beloved kicked. And they have passed over my words, because in the consciousness of their riches they said that saying from the Gospel: Soul, you have many goods laid up for very many years; rest, eat, drink, feast! 3. Moreover, they passed over to their own harm, and, despising all men, they did not set the judgment of God before their eyes. They despised the orphan and the poor, for which the Seventy said 'widows', which is not found in the Hebrew; for 'hebionim' properly denotes 'poor', not 'widows'. But what follows, Shall I not visit for these things? says the Lord, or shall my soul not take vengeance on a nation such as this?, has already been discussed above.

VII. Astonishment and wonders have come to pass in the land: the prophets prophesied a lie — or wicked things — and the priests clapped with their hands, and my people loved such things! What then will you do at the last — or after these things — ? Above he had said: I will go to the chief men and will speak to them; perhaps they have known the way of the Lord. And behold, these rather have together broken the yoke, they have burst the bonds; now he describes who the chief men are, namely the prophets and the priests, some of whom foretell things to come, while others determine from the law the things to be done. 2. 'And behold,' he says, 'while those men prophesied a lie, the priests clapped with their hands.' And so that it may be shown that the people too is not without fault, which lets itself be led away by such men, it is written: and my people loved such things: once mine, but after it loved them, it ceased to be mine. What then will they do when the last time of judgment has come, or the necessity of captivity? Hence there are astonishment and wonders, because neither among the princes nor among the people was anyone found who thought rightly.

Jeremiah 6

VIII.

Be strengthened, sons of Benjamin, in the midst of Jerusalem, and in Tekoa sound the trumpet, and over Beth-haccherem raise a signal — or a standard —, for evil has been seen — or has appeared — from the north, and great destruction! No one is unaware that Jerusalem is situated in the tribe of Benjamin. That Tekoa too is a small village situated on a mountain, twelve miles distant from Jerusalem, we see daily with our own eyes. Between these is another village, which in the Syriac and Hebrew tongue is named Bethacarma, and it too is set on a mountain. What he says, therefore, is this: 2. 'Since from the north Nebuchadnezzar is now on the very point of coming and captivity is near and threatening, O inhabitants of Jerusalem, seize your arms and sound the trumpet in Tekoa and in Beth-haccherem raise the standard', he says, 'so that you may be able to resist your enemies!' Beniamin means 'son of the right hand', Thecua 'trumpet', Bethaccarem 'estate of the vineyard'. Let us refer all these things to the Church, so that, if she has transgressed and the onslaught of persecution has come, she may prepare herself to resist.

IX. I have likened the daughter of Zion to a beautiful and delicate woman. To her shepherds will come with their flocks; they have pitched — or they will pitch — their tents against her round about; each one will pasture those who are under his hand. Sanctify — or Prepare — war against her; arise, and let us go up at noon! 2. The beauty of Jerusalem is described, which is Zion itself, so that the one name denotes the whole city, the other the citadel of the city. For Sion means 'citadel', that is, watchtower, and she is compared to a beautiful woman; and as lovers come together to the one, so shepherds are said to come together to the other. And quite elegantly in the Hebrew the word that is written with four letters, 'res, ain, iod, mem', if it is read 'reim', means 'lovers', if 'roim', 'shepherds', so that either, according to the metaphoran [metaphor] of a beautiful woman, lovers are understood, or, according to the overthrow of the city, shepherds: of whom the former hasten to defile a harlot, the latter to besiege and overturn a city. 3. In the shepherds and their flocks let us perceive the princes and the army of the Chaldeans. And they will pitch their tents round about in the siege of the city, and each one will pasture those who are under his hand, that is, his own squadrons and companies. These princes or shepherds will say to their flocks: 'Sanctify against Jerusalem war, for it is the command of the Lord. Arise, and let us go up at noon: let us fight not by night and by ambush, but in full daylight; for no one can resist us'.

X. Woe to us, for the day has declined, for longer have grown — or have failed — the shadows of evening. Arise, and let us go up by night, and let us destroy the houses — or foundations — of her! Those say: 'Arise, and let us go up at noon, and let us fight in clear daylight'; these reply: woe to us, for the shadows of evening have grown longer, according to that saying of Vergil: and now far off the rooftops of the farmhouses smoke, and greater shadows fall from the high mountains. 2. And the sense is: 'If we suffer these things by day, what shall we suffer by night?' And again those who had said above: 'Sanctify war against her and arise', now rouse themselves to battle, saying: 'Arise, and let us go up by night, so that our adversaries may know that the victory belongs not to the time of day but to strength, and let us destroy the houses, which are fenced in vain by the firmness of their walls'.

XI. For thus says the Lord of hosts: Cut down her tree — or her trees —, pour out — or heap up — a mound around Jerusalem! 'Therefore', they say, 'we are secure of victory, because it is the command of the Lord, who orders the Chaldeans: cut down the trees and heap up a mound for the siegeworks to come!' By these words it is shown, before it comes to pass, that the city was not to be taken at once by an encircling ring of troops, but by a long siege, as we read afterwards.

XII. This is the city of visitation — or the lying city —, all false accusation — or oppression — is in her midst. As a cistern makes cold — or a pit — its water, so she has made her wickedness cold. The Lord commands that the trees be cut down, that mounds be cast up round about, because the time of her visitation has come, that she may receive the due for her sins, the greatest of which is sykophantia, that she oppresses the innocent by false accusation. 2. As, therefore, a cistern or pit makes its water cold, so in the midst of Jerusalem the wickedness that is in her has lost all the warmth of life. And this is to be noted, that the saints are called fervent in spirit, whereas evils are cold; whence that also is written, that in the last days, when iniquity has been multiplied, the love of many will grow cold. I think that this too means the same: I have been likened to those who go down into the pit. 3. And let the Latin reader understand this, so that it may suffice to have said it once: that 'lacus' [pit] does not mean 'pool' according to the Greeks, but 'cistern', which in the Syriac and Hebrew tongue is called 'gubba'. In the present passage, however, for 'lacus', which all translated alike, in the Hebrew it is 'bor'.

XIII. Iniquity and devastation will be heard in her; before me continually are sickness and wound. Be instructed, Jerusalem, lest perhaps my soul depart from you, lest perhaps I make you a desert, an uninhabitable land! LXX: Ungodliness and misery will be heard in her before her face. Through every pain and scourge you will be instructed, Jerusalem, lest perhaps my soul depart from you, lest I make you a trackless land, which is not inhabited. 2. Through these words we learn that the Lord scourges every son whom he receives; and for that reason Jerusalem is instructed by blows and torments, that she may be corrected, and that the soul of God may not depart from her and she be reduced to a wilderness. If ever, therefore, we lie under frequent distresses, let us recall for our consolation this little verse: through every pain and scourge you will be instructed, Jerusalem.

XIV. Thus says the Lord of hosts: Even to the last cluster they will glean, as in a vineyard, the remnant of Israel; turn back your hand like a grape-gatherer to the basket! LXX: For thus says the Lord of powers: Glean, glean as in a vineyard the remnant of Israel; return like a grape-gatherer to his basket! 2. Some take these words in a good sense, others in a bad. In the good sense: 'When Jerusalem has been laid waste, let the remnant be saved'. In the bad, thus: 'Let not even one cluster and small bunch of grapes remain in the vineyard; let everything be gathered; and whatever you find, throw into the basket like a grape-gatherer, so that, as he drags the clusters to the winepress, so you may drag the captives to Babylon'.

XV. To whom shall I speak, and whom shall I solemnly warn, that he may hear? Behold, their ears — or your ears — are uncircumcised, and they cannot hear! Behold, the word of the Lord has become to them a reproach, and they will not receive it! They cannot hear because they were unwilling to circumcise their ears; yet the inability is not free of punishment, since it comes down from contempt and unbelief. 2. If anyone, therefore, does not receive the words of God and has no understanding of his precepts, he is of uncircumcised ears. And at the same time it is to be noted that circumcision in the Scriptures is spoken of in three kinds: in the foreskin, in the heart, and in the ears; whence the Lord too says: He who has ears to hear, let him hear. It is by our own will, therefore, that we do not receive the word of God; and for that reason it becomes to us a reproach, so that what had been given for salvation is turned by our own fault into punishment.

XVI. Therefore I am full of the fury of the Lord; I have toiled in holding it in. LXX: And I have filled up my fury and held it in, and I did not consume it. According to the Hebrew it is said in the person of the prophet, that he foresees the wrath of God that is coming, and is full of the fury and anger of the Lord, and can hold it in no longer, nor dares to entreat the Lord for sinners. 2. According to the Seventy, however, a new sense is set down: that the Lord himself speaks and says: I have completed my fury, by striking the sinful people, and yet that he restrained it and did not pour it all out, so that a remnant might be saved. This seems to me to be self-contradictory; for if he completed his fury, how did he hold it in so as not to complete it?

XVII. Pour out — or He poured out — upon the little child outside and upon the council of young men together; for man with wife will be taken, the old man with him who is full of days. And their houses will pass to others, their fields and wives alike. Either the prophet in the spirit commands the coming Chaldean to pour out the fury of the Lord upon the little children and to spare not even their innocent age, or else he relates what has been done upon the council of young men together, who had seized their arms to fight back. 2. For man with wife will be taken: names most sweet to one another will alike feel captivity, the old man with him who is full of days. Therefore old age is not the last age, but that of those who are 'full of days', whom in our own speech we call 'depositos' [given up for dead] or 'decrepitos' [decrepit]. There follows: and their houses will pass to others through the evil of captivity, their fields and wives together, so that both wife and possession pass to the enemy. Whatever we understand according to the letter of Jerusalem, let us refer according to the spiritual understanding to the Church, if she has offended God.

XVIII. For I will stretch out my hand over those who inhabit the earth, says the Lord. For from the least even to the greatest, all are devoted to avarice, and from prophet even to priest all practice deceit. And they were healing the breach of the daughter of my people with disgrace, saying: Peace, peace! And there was no peace. 2. What in the Hebrew is said: I will stretch out my hand over sinners or the inhabitants of the earth, among the Seventy is always written 'I will lift up'; and each is the posture of one who strikes, according to what is written: and still the hand of the Lord is stretched out or lifted high. The inhabitants of the earth, moreover, are always in vice; whence in Revelation too it is said quite frequently: Woe to the inhabitants of the earth! 3. From the least even to the greatest, all are devoted to avarice, according to that saying of the Apostle: the root of all evils is avarice. And from prophet even to priest all practice deceit: the one by prophesying a lie, the other by perversely interpreting the law of God; for the prophet also says in another place: Seek the law from the priests. 4. 'And though', he says, 'they were doing such things, they kept announcing prosperity of every kind to my people, and as it were desired to heal the wound and the disgrace of my daughter, saying: Peace, peace, when there was no peace at all'. This is properly to be understood of the priests and teachers who promise prosperity to the rich and to those whom they see set in the highest honor, and proclaim God to be merciful, rather preparing them for punishment and wrath.

XIX. Were they confounded because they committed abomination? Nay rather, they were not confounded with confusion, and they knew not how to blush. This is to be read more closely according to the Hebrew. 'And though', he says, 'they have done such things, were they at all confounded? Did they at all blush in their crimes? Nay rather, they increased their contempt, and they knew not how to blush' — and he put 'knew not' here for 'would not' — 'or else, through excessive contempt and the vice of an ingrained evil, they could not even understand'.

XX. Therefore they will fall among those who fall; in the time of their visitation they will collapse, says the Lord. 'Since', he says, 'they knew not how to blush, and had not only not the deed but not even the knowledge and the sense of repentance, therefore those who formerly stood will fall among them; they will collapse through their own vices and, when the time of their visitation and punishment has come, they will be joined to all who fall'. And it is a great ungodliness not only not to guard against sins but not even to be willing to understand them, and to make no distinction between good and evil works.

XXI. Thus says the Lord: Stand upon the ways and see, and ask about the ancient paths, which is the good way, and walk in it! And you will find refreshment — or purification — for your souls. And they said: We will not walk — or We will not go —. And I set watchmen over you: hear the voice of the trumpet! And they said: We will not hear. 2. Therefore hear — or they heard —, O nations, and know, O congregation — or you who pasture flocks, or according to Symmachus: and know the testimony that is in them —, how great things I will do to them! Hear, O earth! The parable of the Gospel, if it is understood, will furnish the understanding of this passage; in it the good merchant is said to sell all his pearls, so that with their price he may buy the one most precious pearl: namely, that through the patriarchs and prophets we come to him who says: I am the way. 3. We must therefore stand in the prophets, and contemplate most diligently, and ask about the ancient or everlasting paths — which are worn by the footsteps of many saints, which in Greek are more expressively called triboi —, which is the good way in the Gospel, and walk in it; and that way, when it has been found, affords refreshment or purification to the souls of believers. But they, on the contrary, answered: We will not walk in the way of the Gospel, the prophet saying this properly of the faithlessness of the Jews. 4. And at once he adds: 'And I set watchmen over you' — no doubt the choir of the apostles is indicated, according to Ezekiel: Son of man, I have given you as a watchman to the house of Israel —, 'and I commanded that you hear the voice of the trumpet, either the commands of the Gospel or the teaching of the apostles, according to that saying of Isaiah: Go up onto a high mountain, you who bring good tidings to Zion; lift up your voice like a trumpet, you who announce to Jerusalem'. 5. They said: We will not hear, and when invited to the supper they would not come; therefore it is said: Hear, O nations! This the apostles too followed when they speak in Lycaonia: It was necessary that the word of God be spoken first to you; but since you thrust it away and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, behold, we turn to the Gentiles. 6. And know, O congregation: by no means the Jewish one, but that of all nations; or, you who pasture flocks: the bishops and presbyters and the whole order of the Church; or, know the testimony that is in them; for the testimony of God is faithful, giving wisdom to little ones. To them the Lord also speaks: Fear not, little flock, and: Behold, I and my children, whom God has given me. 7. 'Know, therefore, how great things I will do to the people that does not believe.' And in what is added: Hear, O earth, the whole world is called to hear, as we also read at the beginning of Isaiah: Hear, O heaven, and give ear, O earth: that is, what the Lord is going to do to the people of the Jews.

XXII. Behold, I will bring evils upon this people, the fruit of their thoughts — or of their turning away —, because they have not heard my words and have cast away my law. He calls 'evils' the punishments and penalties of those who suffer, not upon the Gentiles, who are called to the truth of the Gospel, but upon the people that answered: We will not hear. And it is going to receive the fruit of its thoughts or of its turning away, as David says: You shall eat the labors of your hands. And the cause is plain: because they did not hear the words of the Lord and cast off his law.

XXIII. To what purpose do you bring me frankincense from Sheba, and sweet-smelling calamus from a distant land? Your burnt offerings are not accepted, and your victims have not pleased me. That frankincense comes from Sheba no one doubts; whence also that line of Vergil: and a hundred altars glow with Sabaean incense. 2. And that 'calamus', which in Hebrew is called 'cane', for which the Seventy and Theodotion translated 'cinnamon', comes from a distant land, the prophetic word shows, so that we may understand India, from which many spices come by way of the Red Sea. This kind of spice physicians call kassian syringos [pipe cassia]. And the sense is: 'In vain do you bring me spices of sweetest odor for compounding the ointments that are prescribed in the law, and kindle burnt offerings, you who do not do my will in the law', according to what was said above: they have not heard my words and have cast away my law. 3. This, moreover, properly fits those who offer sacrifices from robbery and the stripping of the wretched, and think they redeem their sins by alms from iniquity, though Scripture says: The ransom of a man's soul is his own riches, which are gathered not from iniquity but from labor and righteousness.

XXIV. Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will put ruins into this people, and they will fall in them — or weakness, and they will be weakened in it —, fathers and sons together, neighbor and kinsman, and they will perish. We see fulfilled all the things with which the Lord threatened that people; for daily they fall in their blasphemies, and they have nothing strong in them, but all is weakness among them. 2. The sons follow the blasphemies of their fathers, and daily receive that imprecation: His blood be upon us and upon our children; and not only they themselves, but also their neighbors and kinsmen, all who follow the law and the prophets according to the letter that kills and not according to the spirit that gives life; and all alike will perish, because alike they have sinned.

XXV. Thus says the Lord: Behold, a people comes from the land of the north, and a great nation will rise from the ends of the earth. It will seize arrow and shield — or zibynnam [spear] —; it is cruel — or shameless — and will not have mercy; its voice will sound like the sea, and they will mount upon horses, prepared like a man for battle against you, daughter of Zion. This is properly prophesied of the Babylonians, who are to come against the people of Jerusalem; and the whole array of their armor and the onset of the combatants is described, so that, shaken by the terror of the voice, they may do repentance and appease a most merciful God. 2. Finally, he gives occasion for entreaty when he says: against you, daughter of Zion. We may apply this testimony, in a transferred sense, to a time of persecution, when the whole rage of the devil is stirred up against us, and there is no mercy, and like the waves of a most violent sea they overwhelm those who resist.

XXVI. We have heard the report of it; our hands have grown feeble; tribulation has seized us, pains as of a woman in labor. The people answers, whom the prophet, nay rather the Lord through the prophet, has threatened with the Babylonians: that before they come they have been overcome by fear, and cannot lift up their hands, and anguish has seized them as pain seizes a woman in labor; than which pain those women who have felt it affirm that nothing is more grievous.

XXVII. Do not go out into the fields, and do not walk on the road, for the sword of the enemy, terror is all around! The Gospel teaches that one must not go out into the field nor come down from the height of the housetops, but must heed that word: Save yourself on the mountain, to which in Isaiah and Micah we are commanded to run and climb. According to the letter, however, it is commanded that they not go outside, that they not desert the walls, but that they protect themselves with strong fortifications.

XXVIII. Daughter of my people, gird yourself with sackcloth and sprinkle yourself with ashes; make for yourself the mourning for an only-begotten — or a beloved —, a bitter lament — or a pitiable one —, for suddenly the destroyer will come — or misery — upon us — or upon you — ! Since he had said above: Do not go out into the fields, and do not walk on the road, for the sword of the enemy, terror is all around, and had forbidden flight as well, he teaches what they ought to do: namely, that they be converted to repentance and have this as their firmest and safest armor. 2. Where we have said: make for yourself the mourning for an only-begotten, for 'only-begotten' in the Hebrew is written 'iaid', which means 'solitary' rather than 'only-begotten'. For if it were 'beloved' or 'lovable', as the Seventy translated, 'ididia' would be set down, which is also the name God gave to Solomon. And nothing is more grievous than to lose one son, or an only son. 3. And that which we have translated: for suddenly the destroyer will come or misery upon us, the Seventy rendered upon you, whereas God has spoken far more mercifully, testifying that whatever is to come upon his people comes upon himself as well. 'The destroyer', moreover, properly signifies either Nebuchadnezzar or the devil.

XXIX. I have given you as a strong assayer among my people, and you will know and test their way. All these are princes turning aside — or disobedient —, walking deceitfully — or perversely —, bronze and iron; all of them are corrupted. The bellows has failed in the fire — which the Greeks more expressively call physētēra —, the lead is consumed, in vain has the smelter smelted — or the silversmith —; for their wickednesses have not been consumed. Call them reprobate silver, for the Lord has cast them away! 2. The prophet is given to the unbelieving people as a 'strong' assayer, which in Hebrew is called 'mabsar', which means either 'fortified', according to Aquila, or 'shut in' and 'surrounded', according to Symmachus and the Seventy, like a most strongly fortified city, so that he may fear no ambushes of the people: 'so that, when you have tested', he says, 'and known the way of the transgressing people, you may then understand that silver mixed with bronze can by no means be purified'. For as lead is mixed with metals that are adulterated and debased, so that the foreign matter may be separated, and, if perhaps it has not been purified, all the lead is consumed and reduced to nothing, so all the eloquence of teaching and the prophetic word perishes in those who scorn to hear. 3. Let us speak also of those who, like deaf asps, stop their ears so as not to hear the voices of the charmers. For in vain has the silversmith smelted or the smelter; for their wickednesses have not been consumed. Hence they are called not 'silver' but 'reprobate silver', for the Lord has cast them off. And the princes are those who withdraw from the Lord, or the disobedient, who walk perversely and deceitfully.

Jeremiah 7

XXX.

The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord, saying: Stand in the gate of the house of the Lord and proclaim — or read — there this word, and say: Hear the word of the Lord, all Judah, you who enter by these gates to worship the Lord! This is not found in the edition of the Seventy, but has been added from Theodotion out of the Hebrew. 2. Now the prophet is commanded to stand in the gate of the Lord, through which the multitude of the people enters to pray to the Lord, so that on this occasion they may be able to hear what the Lord commands. By this we understand the hardness of the people of the Jews, in that they held the prophets as liars and madmen, since it is through the occasion and the throng of the place that they are compelled to hear the words of the Lord, and not because they are the words of the Lord.

XXXI. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Make good — or direct — your ways and your pursuits — or your devices — and I will dwell with you in this place or I will make you dwell in this place. The most merciful physician desires to heal the wounded with every remedy. But when he says: make good or correct your ways, he shows that they are perverse and have nothing good in them. 2. And because it is natural that each person loves his native soil and holds nothing sweeter than his fatherland, he promises rewards to the obedient. 'I will dwell,' he says, 'with you, so that I may make you secure about your dwelling, or I will found you yourselves' — according to Symmachus — 'with a firm dwelling', who says: and I will establish you in this place.

XXXII. Do not trust in lying words, saying: The temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord it is! For if you direct well — or direct — your ways and your pursuits, if you execute judgment between a man and his neighbor, if you do not practice false accusation against the stranger and the orphan and the widow — or do not oppress them — and do not shed innocent blood in this place, and do not walk after strange gods to your own harm, I will dwell with you — or I will make you dwell — in this place, in the land which I gave to your fathers from age to age. 2. This, which the Seventy added at the beginning of this chapter: in words of falsehood, which will not profit you at all, is not found in the Hebrew. He commands both the people of the Jews then and us today, who seem to be established in the Church, not to have confidence in the splendor of buildings and in gilded ceilings and in walls clad with veneers of marble, and say: the temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord it is. For that is the temple of the Lord in which true faith dwells, holy conduct, and the choir of all the virtues. 3. Then he adds: 'if you make your ways straight, and your thought does not go off after error, and you follow righteousness and do not commit evil, and do not shed innocent blood by scandalizing every simple person, and do not walk after strange gods by worshipping perverse doctrines which you have feigned out of your own heart to your own harm, then either I will dwell with you in this place, which you call the temple of God, and in the land which I gave to your fathers, namely the apostles and apostolic men, or at any rate with a firm standing I will make you dwell from the beginning to the end.' 4. This can also suit those virgins who boast of their chastity and display purity with a shameless face, while their conscience holds something else, and who do not know that definition of virginity given by the Apostle, that she be holy in body and in spirit. For what does purity of body profit when the mind has been debauched, if she does not have the other virtues which the prophetic word has described?

XXXIII. Behold, you trust in words of falsehood — or lying words —, which will not profit you: to steal, to kill, to commit adultery, to swear falsely, to pour libations to the Baals, and to go after strange gods whom you do not know. And you came and stood before me in this house, in which my name has been called upon, and you said: We are delivered — or we have ceased — because we have done all these abominations. 2. The sins that follow show that they have confidence in the temple in vain. For what does it profit to cross boldly the threshold of the house of God, to stand with neck held high, and to have not only the heart but also the hands polluted with theft, murder, adultery, perjury, sacrilege, and the worship of those gods whom you do not know? No one doubts that this happens spiritually in the Church, when people, considering the prosperity of the present time, do not take account of their sins and think that they are hidden from God, because punishment does not follow at once; nay rather, they break out into such madness that they think themselves delivered because after their evil works they have even withdrawn from the worship of the Lord.

XXXIV. Has this house — or my house —, in which my name has been called upon, become a den of robbers in your eyes? I, I am, I have seen, says the Lord. From this passage, I think, was taken what is in the Gospel: It is written: My Father's house shall be called a house of prayer; but you have made it a den of robbers, or, as is written in another Gospel, a house of trade. 2. The Church of God is turned into a den of robbers when theft, murder, adultery, perjury, sacrilege, the invention of heresy and every crime are at home in it, when princes burn with the torches of avarice and a cheap, or at any rate not cheap, little cloak possesses what were once the riches of kings. 3. Hence he adds: 'I, I am, I have seen, says the Lord; my eyes have beheld what you think hidden; the darkness of your treasuries does not escape my knowledge'. He who, though he was rich, became poor for our sake, now blushes at our riches and says: Woe to you rich, who have your consolation!

XXXV. Go to my place in Shiloh, where my name dwelt from the beginning, and see what I did to it because of the wickedness of my people Israel! From the past he teaches the present, and to those who say: the temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord, the temple of the Lord and who rejoice in the gleam of a costly house he recalls the history of Shiloh, where the tabernacle of God first was, of which it is written in the psalm: and he rejected the tabernacle of Shiloh, so that, just as that place collapsed into ruins and ashes, so the temple too may fall, since it has been the dwelling of like sins. Therefore, just as Shiloh is an example for the temple, so is the temple for us, when the time of that testimony comes: Do you think the Son of Man, when he comes, will find faith on the earth?

XXXVI. And now, because you have done all these works, says the Lord, and I spoke to you, rising early and speaking, and you did not hear, and I called you and you did not answer, I will do to this house, in which my name has been called upon and in which you have confidence, and to the place which I gave to you and to your fathers, as I did to Shiloh, and I will cast you out from my face, as I cast out all your brothers, the whole seed of Ephraim. 2. This which we have set down: rising early and speaking, is not found in the Seventy. Now God rises early, not because there is any time for him without daybreak, but because after the rest of the night, when the strength of the body has been restored, the soul of men is more vigorous and, in no way occupied with pleasures and the desire for food, is able to hear and to do what is said. 3. Hence we also read this in the psalm: In the morning you will hear my voice; in the morning I will stand before you and will see; and in Isaiah: By night — or at daybreak — my spirit will rise to you, O God, for your precepts are light upon the earth. Hence also the apostle Paul calls them sons of light, and not of night nor of darkness, nor sleepers, as the others too have slept, who do not perceive the commandments of God. Since, therefore, God called them, rising by night, so that he might free them too from darkness, he threatens them that he will do to the temple in Jerusalem things like those he did to the place Shiloh, where the tabernacle first was, so that like sins may be struck by an equal sentence. 4. And just as the Lord cast out the seed of Ephraim, that is, the ten tribes, which were called Israel and had princes on account of Jeroboam son of Nebat, of the tribe of Ephraim, which was likewise called the tribe of Joseph, so he testifies that he will also cast away Jerusalem and the tribe of Judah with Benjamin. He cast away Shiloh, therefore, and he will cast away the temple too; he cast away the ten tribes, and he will cast away the two as well. Whatever is said to that people, let us understand it of ourselves too, if we do like things.

XXXVII. You, therefore, do not pray for this people, nor take up praise and prayer for them — or nor ask that they obtain mercy, — and do not withstand me, for I will not hear you. So that the prophet may not seem, in asking, to fail to obtain what he requests, God commands him not to intercede for a sinful people that does no repentance at all. But when he says: and do not resist me, he shows this, that the prayers of the saints can resist the wrath of God. 2. Hence the Lord also speaks to Moses: Let me go, that I may strike this people, and I will make you into a great nation; and in the Psalms it is written: And Phinehas stood and appeased, and the shaking ceased, and it was reckoned to him for righteousness. Aaron too, seizing a censer, stood in the midst between the fire and the people about to burn, and the wrath of God ceased. And lest we think God cruel, since he does not even allow himself to be entreated, he gives the reasons why he does not hear, saying:

XXXVIII. Do you not see what these are doing in the cities of Judah and in the streets of Jerusalem? The sons gather wood and the fathers kindle the fire and the women sprinkle fat, to make cakes — or chauonas — for the queen — or the host — of heaven, and to pour libations to strange gods and to provoke me to anger. Is it me they provoke to anger? says the Lord, is it not themselves, to the confusion of their own face? 2. 'Do you wish,' he says, 'to hear, prophet, why I said to you: do not pray for this people? They do these things that follow: both within and without and in the streets and in the outlets of Jerusalem the sons carry together wood and the fathers kindle the fire and the women sprinkle fat with flour to make chauonim' — which we have rendered 'cakes' or 'preparations', so that it shows every kind of sacrifice to the queen of heaven, whom we should take to be the moon, or at any rate to the host of heaven, so that we understand all the stars — 'and after this they pour libations to strange gods, not that these exist, but in that under their names they burn incense to demons, and they provoke me to anger by doing these things. 3. Nor do the wretches understand that this contention does not harm me, whom anger never changes, but themselves, to the confusion of their own face and to everlasting disgrace.' Whatever, therefore, we do, we do not harm God, who can never be harmed, but we prepare destruction for ourselves, storing up wrath for the day of wrath. And he set down the different tasks of the sons and of the fathers and of the mothers, or wives, so that there may be no age that has dissented from the impiety.

XXXIX. Therefore thus says the Lord God: Behold, my fury and my indignation is kindled — or has dripped — upon this place, upon men and upon beasts and upon the trees of the region and upon the fruits of the earth, and it will be set alight and will not be quenched. He who had said above: Is it me they provoke to anger?, how does he now say: Behold, my fury and my indignation has dripped upon this place? 2. And the sense is: 'I indeed am not angered by nature, but they act in such a way as to provoke me to anger, so that I seem to change my nature. Let them, therefore, feel me angry, which, so far as in them lies, they are trying to bring about.' And beautifully he does not say: 'has been poured out' my fury upon this place, but: has dripped, so as to signify a moderate punishment. But if in a drop of fury there is such harshness, what will happen if the whole downpour is poured out? 3. But 'kindling' too can be understood thus: that what he long did not wish to do, he is compelled to do by the multitude of sins. And when God is angry, both men and the things that are men's will feel a like destruction. 'And it will be set alight', he says — no doubt the fury of the Lord — 'and will not be quenched, because the people do not do the things through which it could be quenched.'

XL. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Add your burnt offerings to your victims and eat flesh, for I did not speak with your fathers, and I did not command them on the day that I led them out of the land of Egypt, concerning the matter of burnt offerings and victims, but this word I commanded them, saying: Hear my voice, and I will be your God and you will be my people, and walk in every way that I have commanded you, that it may be well with you. Having rejected their temple, he consequently rejects their sacrifices too, and indirectly reproves them because they sacrifice victims not out of reverence for him but out of desire for feasting. 2. But when he says: I did not speak with your fathers, and I did not command them on the day that I led them out of the land of Egypt, about the matter of the burnt offering and of victims, it is clearly understood that he first gave the Decalogue, written on tablets of stone by the finger of God, and that after the offense of idolatry and the head of the calf he afterward commanded that it be done to himself rather than to demons, taking away the pure religion of the commandments of God and granting the blood of victims and the craving for flesh.

XLI. And they did not hear nor incline their ear, but they went off in the wills — or desires — and the depravity of their evil heart, and they became backward and not forward, from the day on which their fathers came out of the land of Egypt until this day. 2. 'When I said: Hear my voice and I will be your God and the rest, they did not hear nor incline their ear, but they did the desires of their own heart, and against the judgment of the Apostle, who forgot what lay behind and stretched himself toward what lay before, they did the contrary, so as to desire the past and despise the future.' 3. And when he says: from the day on which their fathers came out of the land of Egypt until this day, he says that they spent all the time in between in offense against the Lord. Hence the grace of the Gospel was also necessary, which saved them not by their own merit but by the mercy of the Lord.

XLII. And I sent to you my servants, the prophets, by day, rising at daybreak and sending; and they did not hear me nor incline their ear, but they hardened their neck and wrought worse than their fathers. Just, therefore, is the wrath of the Lord, which has been kindled and has dripped upon a contemptuous and stiff-necked people that refuses to hear the words of God.
