But how God sends the prophets either by night or at daybreak and by day, we have said above.

XLIII. And you will speak to them all these words, and they will not hear you; and you will call them, and they will not answer you. And you will say to them: This is the nation that has not heard the voice of the Lord its God nor received discipline; faith has perished and has been taken away from their mouth. 'Do not doubt,' he says, 'that they have hardened their neck and wrought worse than their fathers; behold, I give room for repentance, and I do not speak in order that these things may happen, but because they are going to happen, therefore I foretell them. 2. At least now speak to them with my words, and yet they will not hear you; and you will call them, and they will not answer you; for they will be of such pride that, when you call them to hear, no one will deign to answer. And you will say to them: This is the nation that has not heard the voice of the Lord its God nor received discipline'. Beautifully, as I have already said before, he calls them not at all his people, but a nation. 3. And although this was in part done in the time of the prophets too, and went before in shadow and in image, yet it is more fully fulfilled in Christ, when they refused to receive discipline and despised the voice of their Lord. Hence it is fittingly added: faith has perished, which belongs properly to Christians, and has been taken away from their mouth, namely every confession of the Son of God and of the faith.

XLIV. Shear your hair and cast it away, and take up in the open — or upon the lips — a lament, for the Lord has cast off and forsaken the generation of his fury. We read that Job too, on hearing of the death of his sons and daughters, sheared his hair, and among the ancients this was the custom of all who mourned, to shear the locks; but now, on the contrary, to let the hair grow long is the sign of mourning. 2. And all the prophet's lament and lamentation is taken up for this reason, for the Lord has cast off and forsaken the generation of his fury; no doubt he signifies the people of the Jews. And this is properly to be referred to the times of Christ, when faith perished and was taken away from the mouth of the people blaspheming the Lord.

XLV. For the sons of Judah have done evil in my sight, says the Lord; they have set their stumbling blocks in the house in which my name has been called upon, to pollute it, and they have built the high places — or the altar — of Topheth, which is in the valley of the son of Hinnom, to burn their sons and their daughters with fire, which I did not command nor think of in my heart. 2. That the sons of Judah set up a statue of Baal in the temple of God we learn at the beginning of Ezekiel. Now 'high places', which in Hebrew are called 'bamath', or 'altar' of Topheth, which is in the valley of the sons of Hinnom, signifies that place which is watered by the springs of Siloam, and is pleasant and wooded, and to this day offers the delights of gardens. And this error of paganism took hold of all the provinces, so that they sacrificed victims at the heads of springs and in the loveliest groves, and the observance of every depraved superstition was kept. 3. 'Tofeth' in the Hebrew tongue means 'breadth', and it is said to be written in the book of Joshua son of Nun about this place, which is in the valley of the sons of Hinnom and in Hebrew is called 'gehennom'; for 'ge' signifies pharanga, that is, 'valley', and 'hennom' signifies either a man's name or 'grace'. And the Hebrews hand down that 'gehennam' was named from this place, because, namely, the whole people of the Jews perished there by offending God, in which place they also consecrated their sons in fire to idols or offered them as a burnt offering, which he had not commanded them nor ordered by any sanction of the law. 4. But if Jephthah offered his virgin daughter to God, it is not the sacrifice that pleases, but the mind of the one offering; for if a dog or an ass or any unclean animal whatever had first met the father as he returned from the slaughter of the enemy, he ought not to have offered it to God.

XLVI. Therefore, behold, the days will come, says the Lord, and it will no longer be called Topheth and the valley of the son of Hinnom, but the valley of killing, and they will bury in Topheth because there is no room. And the carcasses of this people will be food for the birds of heaven and the beasts of the earth, and there will be none to drive them away. He signifies the time of the siege, which they endured from the ninth year of King Zedekiah to the eleventh year, and that the valley itself will by no means be called 'gehennom', that is, 'valley of hennom', or 'of the sons of Hinnom', but, on account of the killing of very many, 'valley of slaughter'. 2. And so great will the slaughter be that in the place formerly of worship there will be innumerable tombs, and those whom they cannot bury will be torn by birds and devoured by beasts, and there will be no one to drive them away, since he fears the like and is overcome by the duties of burying. We pass quickly over what is plain, so that, wherever there is occasion, we may linger over what is obscure. For the very size of the book can cause weariness to readers; how much more if it were discussed by us at greater length!

XLVII. And I will make to cease from the cities of Judah and from the streets of Jerusalem the voice of joy and the voice of gladness, and the voice of the bridegroom and the voice of the bride; for the land will be in desolation. When the place of idolatry has been turned into tombs, so that where they had offended God, there their corpses lie unburied, all gladness will be taken away from the city of Jerusalem that once was and from the other cities that were under its dominion, and all things will be filled with sorrow and groaning and desolation.

Jeremiah 8

XLVIII.

At that time, says the Lord, they shall cast out the bones of the king of Judah and the bones of his princes and the bones of the priests and the bones of the prophets and the bones of those who dwelt in Jerusalem, from their graves. And they shall spread them out to the sun and the moon and all the host of heaven, which they have loved and which they have served and after which they have walked and which they have sought and worshiped. They shall not be gathered and shall not be buried; they shall be for a dunghill upon the face of the earth. And all who remain of this most wicked family shall choose death rather than life, in all the places that are left, to which I have cast them out, says the Lord of hosts. 2. All the things that the prophetic word describes we see to have happened in our own time, not to Jerusalem alone, which suffered these things from the Chaldeans and the Romans, but to the whole world, so that tears have dried up and everything has been filled with the bones of the dead. And because, according to ancient custom, they used to lay up gold and certain ornaments, whether of women or of men, in tombs, avarice broke these open too and dug them up, so as to expose them to the sky and the light. 3. And therefore first the bones of the kings of Judah and of its princes, of the priests also and of the prophets and of all the people who had been in Jerusalem, were brought out of their tombs, lying open to the sun and the moon and to the sight of all the stars, so that they lay open to the gaze of those whom they had served after forsaking God, and, consumed, were dissolved into a dunghill and ashes; and if anyone of the multitude had been able to escape, to whatever places he came, he would prefer death to life and think destruction the remedy of an unhappy soul.

XLIX. And you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord: Shall he who falls not rise again? And he who has turned away, shall he not return — or shall he not turn away — ? After such great evils he calls to repentance those who were able to remain, or before they come. He has threatened these things; he exhorts them to conversion and gives room for repentance. 2. But what is written according to the Hebrew: and he who has turned away, shall he not turn away, signifies this: that he who has turned away from God, if he is willing to be converted to better things, can also turn the fury of God to better things, and withstand what is coming, and escape the blows by prayers.

L. Why then has this people in Jerusalem turned away with a contentious turning away? They have laid hold of falsehood and they would not return? 'The more I,' he says, 'called them to repentance, the more they withdrew from me, not so much from eagerness to sin as from eagerness to overcome me. For they have laid hold firmly of falsehood, whether idols or whatever perverse things are contrary to truth and righteousness, and they would not return' — he did not say 'they could not' —, 'but with all eagerness they have pursued wicked things'.

LI. I attended and listened; no one speaks what is good, there is none who does repentance over his sin, saying: What have I done? All have turned to their course, like a horse going with a rush to battle. Called back to repentance, they scorn to listen; nor is it enough for them to have offended against the Lord and to have spoken blasphemy, but all, like horses rushing in hot course to battle, do not reflect on their sin nor say: what have I done? 2. By which we understand that these things are said either of the whole human race, because it is prone to vices, or of the time of the Savior, when all have turned aside, together they have become useless; there was none who did good, there was not even one. Hence he himself also cries out mystically: save me, O Lord, for the holy one has failed! But if these things are so, where are those who say that it lies within our will to be free of all sin? No one, he says, speaks what is good, because we shall render account even for an idle word on the day of judgment.

LII. The kite in the heaven has known its time; the turtledove and the swallow and the stork have kept the time of their coming; but my people has not known the judgment of the Lord. For 'kite', which Symmachus translated, the Seventy and Theodotion put the Hebrew word itself, 'asida', Aquila 'herodiona' [heron]. And again for 'swallow' Symmachus translated 'cicada', which in Hebrew is called 'sis'. And for what we have put as 'stork', both Aquila and Symmachus translated 'agur', just as it is written in the Hebrew, for which the Seventy translated 'sparrows of the field'. 2. And it is one and the same sense which is also set down at the beginning of Isaiah: the ox has known its owner and the ass the manger of its master, but Israel has not known me and my people has not understood; namely, that even small birds know their seasons and know when, hastening to warm places, they ought to escape the harshness of winter, and again at the beginning of spring come back to their accustomed regions. And here we ought to take 'heaven' for 'the air', which is above.

LIII. How do you say: We are wise, and the law of the Lord is with us? Truly the lying pen of the scribes has wrought falsehood. He speaks to the scribes and Pharisees, who boast of their knowledge of the law and, writing, write iniquity. And as for what he says:

LIV. The wise are confounded, terrified and taken, it is not that those who do these things are wise, but he calls them wise so that they may be condemned by their own judgment and, on the contrary, their wisdom may be proved to be folly, as Paul says: you who teach another, do you not teach yourself? And because they have cast away the word of the Lord, therefore there is no wisdom in them. In vain, then, do they boast of knowledge of the law who destroy their teaching by their works.

LV. Therefore I will give their women — or wives — to strangers, their fields to heirs, because from the least to the greatest all are devoted to avarice, from the prophet to the priest all practice falsehood — or iniquity —. And they healed the breaking of the daughter of my people to ignominy, saying: Peace, peace, when there was not peace. 2. They received the wages of their works, so that those who cast away the word of the Lord were themselves cast away by him. 'Their wives,' he says, 'and their possessions I will hand over to the enemy. And lest my sentence be thought cruel, let the hearer recognize the causes: from the least to the greatest all are devoted to avarice — for avarice is the root of all evils —, from the prophet to the priest. Those who ought to restrain others when they sin are the first to be given over to crimes, and all either do iniquitous things, while they plunder the goods of others, or at any rate practice falsehood, so that there is no truth in their mouth. And after these things, like good physicians, they desired to heal the wounds of others with words, they who were themselves pierced through with the wounds of every shameful deed'. 3. This we see daily in our own people too, as the Apostle says: you who preach that one must not steal, do you steal? and the rest. When these see sinners and rich men, they desire to heal the breaking of the daughter of the people of God, that is, of the Church, to the ignominy either of those who are deceived or of those who deceive others, so that they say to men covered with every crime: peace, peace, when there is no peace, while the war of their sins hangs over them.

LVI. Were they confounded, because they committed abomination? Nay rather, they were not confounded with confusion, and they did not know how to blush. This is to be read Erōtēmatikōs [as a question], so that the sense is: 'Did they blush in their crimes, did they understand the abominations which they wrought?' 'By no means; but they burst out into such madness that they neither desired to amend their vices through confusion nor to confess their crimes in hope of pardon'.

LVII. Therefore they shall fall among those who collapse; in the time of their visitation they shall collapse, says the Lord. These are the rewards of those who did not know how to blush: that those whose dignity was loftier than the peoples should be mingled in the ruins of the people. For from the least to the greatest all are devoted to avarice, and from the prophet to the priest all practice falsehood; and the time of visitation is the approaching captivity.

LVIII. Gathering I will gather them, says the Lord; there is no grape on the vines and there are no figs on the fig tree; the leaf has fallen, and I have given them things that have passed by. What the time of visitation is, in which the transgressors shall collapse, he teaches more plainly, saying: 'Gathering I will gather them' — no doubt in Jerusalem —, 'so that they may be besieged by the Chaldeans for a long time and endure the evils of famine. When,' he says, 'the seasons pass, and autumn follows summer, and in winter the leaves of the trees fall, you will see everything from afar and from them you will take no food. 2. There is no indeed grape on the vines for you, who do not take the fruit of the vines, and there are no figs on the fig tree for the besieged people, who sees the fruits of its own trees plundered by the enemy. The leaves,' he says, 'will fall when summer and autumn are past. For I have given them things which they would see pass by, so that with the greater grief they would lose the abundance of all things, which they were not permitted to touch'.

LIX. Why do we sit still? Come together, and let us enter the fortified city — or the fortified cities — and let us be silent there — or let us be cast out there —, for our Lord has cast us off — or has made us silent — and has given us water of gall to drink; for we have sinned against the Lord. We waited for peace, and there was no good, for a time of healing, and behold, dread. 2. The voice of the people is brought in, answering and confessing its vices and exhorting one another to enter the walled cities, or the one city Jerusalem; for the rest had already been taken. 'And let us be silent', he says, 'there, for our Lord has made us silent — for we have no confidence to pray —, or let us be cast away there or let us be cast out like dung. He himself has made us drink waters of gall, because we turned God, who is sweet, into bitterness. 3. And the cause why we should drink such waters is plain: for we have sinned against the Lord and we waited for peace, we who had done no good work and thought that the time of remedy was at hand for us, when all things were filled with dread and terror'. A change of persons, especially in the prophets, makes the understanding difficult; if these are restored to their own places and causes and times, the things which seemed obscure will become plain.

LX. From Dan was heard the snorting of his horses, at the sound of the neighings of his fighters — or of his cavalry — the whole land was shaken; and they came and devoured — or and they will come and will devour — the land and its fullness, the city and its inhabitants. 2. So the people did not say, as the Seventy translated above: let us enter the fortified cities, but: the fortified city, so as to signify Jerusalem. And indeed he now also adds: the city and its inhabitants. Nebuchadnezzar is described coming with his army from Dan, through Phoenicia, at which place the river Jordan rises; and what the Seventy give as things to come, the Hebrew, in accordance with the truth of the event, records as already done.

LXI. For behold, I will send among you serpents, basilisks — or most wicked or, as the Seventy translated, deadly —, for which there is no charm; and they shall bite you, says the Lord, incurably, with the pain of your failing heart. 2. Who those are who come from Dan, and whose snorting was heard, and who reduced the whole land to a wilderness, the same prophetic word shows under another figure, calling them 'serpents', 'most wicked' or 'deadly', and, as Aquila translated, 'basilisks', which are called in Hebrew 'sapphonim'; for which I do not understand what his second edition meant by saying 'watchmen', unless perhaps because of the similarity of the word. 3. For which there is no, he says, charm. For in vain do they pour out prayers to God against the ancient serpent, the twisting snake, who have scorned the commandments of God. Let us use this passage against those who, scorning the sayings of the Savior, are handed over to the adverse powers.

LXII. My pain is above pain, within me my heart is mourning. For this, as we said above, the Seventy joined what is said to the preceding sentence, so as to put: and they shall bite you, says the Lord, incurably, with the pain of your failing heart. But in the Hebrew there is not so much 'pain', which in Greek is called odynē, as meidiama, which we can translate as the 'grimace' of a mouth contracted by pain and having the likeness of a smile. And these things are to be read Emphatikōs [emphatically] as from the person of God, lamenting the overthrow of Jerusalem and not bearing her miseries.

LXIII. Behold the voice of the crying of the daughter of my people from a land far off! He describes the weeping and wailing of the city of Jerusalem when the enemy had entered.

LXIV. Is the Lord not in Zion, or is her king not in her? Either the Lord himself is the king, or else 'Lord' refers to the Father, 'king' to the Son, according to that which is written under the name of Solomon: O God, give your judgment to the king, and your righteousness to the son of the king. But why the voice of crying is in Jerusalem and the crying itself comes from a land far off, the cause is manifest: because the Lord is not in her and her king has withdrawn from her.

LXV. Why then have they provoked me to anger with their graven images and with foreign vanities? 'But the Lord has withdrawn from them,' he says, 'because they provoked me, who was their Lord and king, to anger by the worship of idols'.

LXVI. The harvest is past, the summer is ended; and we are not saved. Again the people speaks, which was shut up in Jerusalem by a long siege, saying that the seasons have changed and the circle of the year has rolled round, and all their hope has been made void and has passed away.

LXVII. Over the breaking of the daughter of my people I am broken and saddened, astonishment has taken hold of me. God answers that in the affliction of Jerusalem he himself appears afflicted, and, after the likeness of a man, he is broken by astonishment.

LXVIII. Is there no resin in Gilead, or is there no physician there? Why then has the scar of the daughter of my people not closed over? Not only in the present passage, but also in many other testimonies of the Scriptures we find the resin of Gilead put for repentance and medicine; and we find that God now wonders why the wounds of Jerusalem have by no means been healed and the scars have not yet drawn over the skin, because there are no prophets or priests by whose medicine they ought to be healed.

Jeremiah 9

LXIX.

Who will give water to my head, and to my eyes a fountain of tears? And I will weep day and night for the slain of the daughter of my people. 'If I,' he says, 'were turned wholly into weeping, and there were not drops of tears at all but the abundance of a river, still I could not worthily weep for the slain of the daughter of my people. For so great are the evils that by their greatness they surpass all grief over them'. And this can be understood as much from the person of the prophet as from that of the Lord.

LXX. Who will give me in the wilderness a lodging place of wayfarers? And I will forsake my people and withdraw from them. LXX: who would give me in the wilderness a last dwelling, that I may leave my people and withdraw from them? 'It is better,' he says, 'to dwell in the farthest wilderness than to abide among such great crimes of men'. Hence the Savior also said in the Gospel: how long shall I bear with you? And in another place it is written: at that time he who understands shall sit and be silent, for it is a most evil time.

LXXI. For they are all adulterers, an assembly of transgressors, and they have stretched out their tongue like a bow of falsehood and not of truth; they have grown strong in the land, for they have gone forth from evil to evil, and they have not known me, says the Lord. Sinners pass from evil to evil when they exchange idol for idol and go over from sins to sins, or at any rate pass from the evil of the siege to captivity. 2. And of the saints indeed it is said: they shall go from strength to strength, but of sinners: they have gone forth from evil to evil. And the cause of all their miseries is that they did not know the Lord, and that it is an assembly of men transgressing and arming their tongue like a stretched bow for blasphemy; and they have grown strong in the land, so that they deserve to hear: earth you are and into earth you shall go.

LXXII. Let each one guard himself from his neighbor, and let him not have confidence in any brother of his, for every brother supplants with supplanting, and every friend walks deceitfully, and a man will mock his brother, and they will not speak the truth; for their tongue has learned to speak falsehood — or for they have taught their tongue to speak falsehood —. They have labored to act wickedly — or they have acted wickedly and have not ceased, so as to be converted —. Your dwelling is in the midst of deceit; in deceit — or usury upon usury and deceit in deceit; — they have refused — or they would not — to know me, says the Lord. 2. This passage is to be used in a time of persecution and distress, when faith is either rare or none, when neither brother nor neighbor is to be trusted and his enemies are those of his household, when, according to the Gospel, the father will hand over the son and the son the father, and two are divided against three and three against two. And what he adds: they have taught their tongue to speak falsehood or their tongue has learned to speak falsehood, shows that the habit of lying is in a manner turned into nature, and that they act with zeal so as to do wicked things. 3. And as for what follows: your dwelling is in the midst of deceit, in deceit, the word is properly directed to the prophet, that he dwells in the midst of a lying people — or, as the Seventy translated: usury upon usury and deceit upon deceit — and that they daily increase their crimes and by no means repent of their former deed, but heap new crimes upon past ones; doing these things, they act with all eagerness so as not to know the Lord, who commanded that these things should not be done.

LXXIII. Therefore thus says the Lord of hosts: Behold, I will melt — or I will test by fire — and try them; for what else shall I do in the face of the daughter of my people? Or for I will act in the face of the wickedness of the daughter of my people. Whenever we are subject to distresses, we receive evils from God and are tested by persecutions, so that whatever adulterated matter is in us may be smelted out by the heat of tribulations and miseries: for the silver of the Lord is tested by fire, proved in the earth, purified sevenfold.

LXXIV. Their tongue is a wounding arrow, it has spoken deceit; with his mouth he speaks peace with his friend and secretly lays snares for him. Shall I not visit upon these things, says the Lord, or on such a nation shall my soul not take vengeance? 2. Every heretic who wounds the hearts of his hearers, of those who do not know what is written: keep your heart with all watchfulness, possesses a wounding arrow and speaks in deceit, and while with his mouth he promises peace to his neighbor, he secretly sets snares. As for the following verses, in which he says: shall I not visit upon these things, says the Lord, or on such a nation shall my soul not take vengeance? He makes frequent use of them in this prophet, so that, when he has enumerated the evil works one by one, he infers that he does justly what he does.

LXXV. Upon the mountains I will take up — or take up — weeping and lamentation, and over the beautiful places — or the paths — of the desert a wailing, because they are burned up — or they have failed — because there is no man — or human being — passing through. And they have not heard the voice of the possessor — or of substance —; from the bird of the heaven to the beasts they have migrated and withdrawn. 2. When the Babylonian comes upon them and lays everything waste, the desolation of the province is prophesied: that there is wailing on the mountains, weeping in the desert or in the paths of the wilderness, that all things have failed and been burned up, and there is no one to walk through the land, all having been slain, and nothing has remained that can breathe and live. Hence for 'possessor' the Seventy translated 'of substance', which in Hebrew is called 'macne'; and 'substance' here is not taken for ousia, that is, 'essence', but for 'wealth' and 'riches'. 3. And what he adds: from the bird of the heaven to the beast they have withdrawn and departed, shows this, as we have often said: that all things feel the wrath of God, and not only the birds of the air but also the fish of the water fail. According to tropology, weeping is taken up upon the mountains and lamentation over the beautiful places of the desert when the princes of the Church sin and nothing of the substance of God is found in her, nor is the voice of the Lord who possesses the Church heard through holy and apostolic men, and from the bird of the heaven to the beasts, that is, from those who can ascend on high down to the irrational and all the simpler ones, they have withdrawn from the assembly of God.

LXXVI. And I will give Jerusalem into heaps of sand — or into exile — and into lairs of dragons, and the cities of Judah I will give into desolation, because there is no inhabitant. When churchmen and all the teachers have failed, then Jerusalem is given into exile or into heaps of sand, so that heretical speech prevails in her and she is made a lair of dragons and her cities are reduced to a wilderness, and there is in her no dwelling of the divine word and of him who says: I will dwell and walk among them, and I will be their God.

LXXVII. Who is the wise man who may understand this, and to whom the word of the mouth of the Lord may come, and who may declare it: why the land has perished and is burned up like a desert, because there is none who passes through? And the Lord said: Because they have forsaken my law, which I gave them, and have not heard my voice and have not walked in it, and have gone after the perversity of their heart and after the Baals, whom they learned from their fathers. 2. The prophet asks whether he can find anyone of the wise in Jerusalem, and of those to whom the word of God comes and who can declare the will of the Lord and give the reasons why Judea has been reduced to a wilderness and, all having been slain, no one has remained to pass through it. And he brings in the Lord answering and giving the reasons: because they had forsaken his law, which he had given them, and had not heard his voice nor done what had been commanded, but had gone after the perversity of their heart. 3. Therefore we must trust not in our own will but in the Lord; for the heart in men is perverse, and from our heart come forth the worst thoughts. And after the Baals, he says, they have gone, whom they learned from their fathers. Bahal is the idol of the Sidonians and is of the singular number, but Bahalim of the plural number. Therefore neither the error of parents nor that of ancestors is to be followed, but the authority of the Scriptures and the command of God who teaches.

LXXVIII. Therefore thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Behold, I will feed them, this people, with wormwood — or with distresses — and I will give them water of gall for drink, and I will scatter them among the nations which neither they nor their fathers have known, and I will send the sword after them until they are consumed. This can be prophesied both of the near time, when they were taken captive by the Chaldeans, and properly of this present time, when they have been scattered among the nations which neither they nor their fathers had known, and, divided through the whole world, have been fed with wormwood, or with necessities and distresses. 2. And they have received water of gall for drink, which either signifies the greatness of their evils and the everlasting yoke of captivity, or else means that, through ignorance of the law of God, they are going to receive Antichrist instead of Christ. And the sword is sent after them, so that they are consumed even to destruction, or else a sword which divides them and does not allow them to be of one accord in evil, so that they perish in this, that they are evil.

LXXIX. Thus says the Lord of hosts: Consider — or understand — and call the mourning women, and let them come, and send to those who are wise, and let them hasten — or let them speak —, let them make haste and take up a lamentation over us — or over you — and let our eyes — or your — bring down tears and our eyelids — or your — flow with waters, for a voice of lamentation has been heard from Zion or in Zion. 2. On account of the coming captivity and overthrow of Jerusalem he bids the mourning women be called, who in times of grief are accustomed, with a tearful voice and beating their arms with their hands, to provoke the people to tears; for this custom remains to this day in Judea, that women with hair loosened and breasts bared, with a measured chant, stir everyone to weeping. And God joins himself to them with the feeling of one who suffers with them, or the prophet does, so as to say that whatever the people endures, he himself endures and feels. But as for what he adds: for a voice of lamentation has been heard from Zion, what this voice is follows at once.

LXXX. How are we laid waste and confounded exceedingly? Because we have forsaken the land, for our tents have been thrown down — or we have cast away —. This is the voice of those lamenting Zion: how are we laid waste and confounded exceedingly? 2. And at once they answer themselves and set out the causes of the devastation, saying: because we have forsaken the land through our own fault and sin, and our tents have been thrown down, which they once possessed as if passing by. Let the crowds of those who once believed say this too in persecution: that they have been laid waste and confounded for this reason, because they have forsaken the land of the Lord and deserted their tents.

LXXXI. Hear therefore, O women, the word of the Lord, and let your ear take up the speech of his mouth, and teach your daughters lamentation, and each woman her neighbor wailing, for death has come up through our windows, it has entered our houses, to destroy the little ones from outside, the young men from the streets! In the preceding section he had said: call the mourning women, and let them come, and send to those who are wise, and let them hasten; now he speaks as if to women present, to the condemnation of the priests and teachers and of all the men, so that, since these cease from teaching, the women may hear the word of the Lord and take up the words of his mouth and teach their daughters and their neighbors wailing and the causes of tears, for death, he says, has come up through our windows, it has entered our houses. 2. Although this can also be understood spiritually, in that the death of sins enters through all the senses to the destruction of the soul, yet it can also be understood of the onslaught of the Babylonians: that so great is their strength and speed in fighting that they do not wait to unbar the doors, but climb up through the windows and roofs to lay waste the houses of Jerusalem. And the little ones perish who are outside and go out of Jerusalem, and the young men, to whom John also writes, who do not enter by the strait and narrow way that leads to life, but walk through the streets, of which it is written: how broad and spacious is the way that leads to death!

LXXXII. Speak: Thus says the Lord: And the carcass of man shall fall — or the corpses of men — like dung upon the face of the region — or of the field — and like hay behind the back of the reaper, and there is none who gathers. The Hebrew word that is written with three letters, 'daleth, beth, res' — for it has no vowels in the middle —, if it is read, according to the context and the judgment of the reader, as 'dabar', means 'word'; if as 'deber', 'death'; if as 'dabber', 'speak'. Hence the Seventy and Theodotion joined it to the previous section, so as to say: to destroy the little ones from outside, the young men from the streets, by death. 2. But Aquila and Symmachus translated lalēson, 'speak', so that God commands the prophet to speak what follows: thus says the Lord and the rest. And the sense is: when death has come up through our windows and has entered the houses of Jerusalem, and the little ones and the young men have perished from outside and from the streets, then their carcass, or the corpses of the dead, will be as it were for a dunghill upon the face of the earth and like stubble that is left behind the back of the reapers and, being useless, is not gathered. By these things he wishes to show that there will be so great a slaughter in Jerusalem and around the city that there will be no one to bury those who fall.

LXXXIII. Thus says the Lord: Let not the wise man glory in his wisdom, and let not the strong man glory in his strength, and let not the rich man glory in his riches; but let him who glories glory in this: to know — or to understand — and to know me, that I am the Lord, who do mercy and judgment and righteousness on the earth! For these things please me — or for in these is my will —, says the Lord. 2. All the pride of men is taken away, while their wisdom and strength and wealth are reckoned as nothing, and this is the only glorying: that one should know and understand that he himself is the Lord, who does mercy and judgment and righteousness upon the earth; that all things are governed by the providence and righteousness of God, and that the things which seem to us to have no reason are full of righteousness and reason. 3. For these things alone please God, and in these is his will. Where then are those who say that man is governed by his own will, and that the power of free will was so given that the mercy and righteousness of God were taken away? Hence also the Apostle, taking up this testimony, sets down the example: let him who glories, glory in the Lord.

LXXXIV. Behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and I will visit upon everyone who have the foreskin circumcised, upon Egypt and upon Judah and upon Edom and upon the sons of Ammon and upon Moab and upon all who are shorn about the hair, dwelling in the desert; for all the nations have the foreskin, but all the house of Israel are uncircumcised in heart. 2. In part, the peoples of many nations, and especially of those that border on Judea and Palestine, are circumcised to this day, and above all the Egyptians and the Edomites, the Ammonites and the Moabites, and the whole region of the Saracens, which dwells in the wilderness and of whom it is said: upon all who are shorn about the hair, dwelling in the desert. 3. Judah, therefore, who is mingled with the nations written above, ought not to glory because he does not have the foreskin but is circumcised by the law of God, since others too do these things who do not keep the commandments of the law and do not know the God of Israel. Nor is circumcision, which was given as a sign, of any profit unless the commandments of the Lord are fulfilled; just as the long hair also signifies a nation, not strength of body and the bravery of fighters. 4. And what follows: all the nations have the foreskin, but all the house of Israel are uncircumcised in heart, has this sense: whereas, apart from the Egyptians, Edomites, Ammonites and Moabites, and the Ishmaelites dwelling in the wilderness, of whom for the most part a portion is circumcised, all the other nations in the whole world are uncircumcised in the flesh, the whole house of Israel is uncircumcised in heart, not in flesh; and this uncircumcision leads to death, for that one is of the flesh, this of the spirit.

Jeremiah 10

LXXXV.

Hear the word which the Lord has spoken concerning you, house of Israel! Thus says the Lord: Do not learn according to the ways of the nations, and do not be afraid of the signs of heaven, which the nations fear, for the laws of the peoples are vain! 2. He speaks properly against those who venerate the heavenly bodies and suppose that the human race is ruled by those which have been set as signs of years, seasons, months and days, and that earthly things are governed by the causes of heavenly ones. And in saying: the laws or the statutes of the peoples are vain, he shows that all human wisdom is futile and has no usefulness in itself.

LXXXVI. For he has cut wood from the forest, the work of the craftsman's hands with the adze; with silver and gold he has adorned it, with nails and hammers he has fastened it, that it may not come apart — or may not be moved —. They are fashioned after the likeness of a palm tree and will not speak; carried, they will be lifted, because they cannot walk. Do not therefore fear them, for they can do neither evil nor good. 2. A description of the idols which the nations venerate. He has cut wood, he says, from the forest — the material of idols, then, is cheap and perishable — the work of the craftsman's hands — since the craftsman is mortal, mortal therefore are those things too which he fashions —; with silver and gold he has adorned it, so that by the gleam of both materials he may deceive the simple. And this error has passed down even to us, so that we think religion lies in riches. 3. With nails and hammers he has fastened it, that it may not come apart or that it may not be moved. How great is the power of idols, which cannot stand by themselves unless they are fastened with nails and hammers? Fashioned after the likeness of a palm tree, they have the beauty of metals and are adorned by the art of painting, but they possess no usefulness that would yield any fruit to the craftsman. 4. And they will not speak — for they have nothing living in them, those of whom it is written: they have a mouth and will not speak; they have ears and will not hear —; carried, they will be lifted. Stronger is he who carries than the things which are carried; nay rather, in him there is sense, in this a shape without sense. 5. Do not therefore fear them, for they can do neither good nor evil. For many of the Gentiles are accustomed to worship some demons so that they may do no harm, and to entreat others so that they may grant benefits; whence also that line of Vergil: a black beast to Winter, a white one to the favoring Zephyrs. Whatever we have said of idols can be referred to all the doctrines which are contrary to the truth. 6. For they too promise mighty things and fashion out of their own heart an image of vain worship; they boast great things, and to deceive all the simple they hold fast the gaze of the unskilled with thoughts golden, as it were, and with speech gleaming with the splendor of silver, and they are raised aloft by their inventors; in them there is no usefulness, and their worship belongs properly to the nations and to those who do not know God.

LXXXVII. There is none like you, O Lord; great are you, and great is your name in might. Who will not fear you, O king of the nations? For yours is the beauty; among all the wise of the nations and in all their kingdoms there is none like you. The wise and the foolish alike shall be proved; the teaching of their vanity is wood; 2. silver wrapped — or drawn out — is brought from Tarshish, and gold from Uphaz, the work of the craftsman and of the hand of the coppersmith; blue and purple are their clothing; the work of craftsmen — or of the wise — are all these things. But the Lord is the true God; this is the living God and the everlasting king. At his indignation the earth will be shaken, and the nations will not endure his threatening. 3. These words are not found in the Seventy, but in most copies they have been added from Theodotion's edition, and although according to the letter they seem clear, according to the anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense] they hold great difficulty. For none of those gods which are fashioned by heretical art is like the true God. All will fear him who is king of the nations; for the fear of the Lord is the beginning of wisdom, and from it we advance to love. 4. Yours, he says, is the beauty; in truth there is comeliness, in falsehood ugliness. Although the heretics seem to themselves to be wise according to the wisdom of the world, which is destroyed, yet in all the kingdoms in which they tear the Church apart there is none like you, as the divine word says: I will destroy the wisdom of the wise, and the prudence of the prudent I will reject. 5. They are at once wise and foolish; their teaching, according to the quality of their talent, is either cheap and is compared to wood, or is like silver because of the charm of its eloquence; it is brought from Tarshish — either it is a region of India, as Josephus would have it, or else the whole sea is called Tarshish and has a likeness to the sky — and yet it is wrapped in the artifice of words; for if it were laid open, it could not deceive. 6. And gold from Uphaz. Gold is called by seven names among the Hebrews, of which one is called 'ophaz', which we can call 'obryzum' [refined gold], so that it may gleam on the surface of the idols, which within is wood and of cheap material. They are covered with blue and purple, so that by their surface they may deceive the eyes, while they promise themselves the color of the heavens and the heavenly kingdoms; and yet all these things are works of the wise, who are thought wise in the world, but before God are foolish. 7. But the Lord our God is the true God — all those, therefore, are lies — and he himself is the living God — so those which are fashioned are dead — and the everlasting king — the shadows of the heretics prevail for a time, but in the long run they decay —; at his indignation the earth will be shaken — those who have earthly works and fashion earthly images — and the nations will not endure: by no means the people of the Lord, but the multitude of the nations, which cannot bear God's threatening.

LXXXVIII. Thus therefore you shall say to them: The gods who did not make the heavens and the earth, let them perish from the earth and from those things which are under heaven! These words are to be said to the false gods and to those which have been put together by artifice — for they made neither the heavens nor the earth —, words which the fellow workers of Christ, who are called God's and the Lord's, for the most part frame through the Church's teaching.

LXXXIX. The Lord, who makes — or made — the earth by his might, prepares the world by his wisdom, and by his prudence stretches out the heavens. At his voice he gives a multitude of waters in the heaven, and lifts up — or brings out — the mists from the ends of the earth; he has made lightnings into rain, and brings out the wind — or the winds — from his treasuries. 2. Every man has become foolish from knowledge; every craftsman is confounded in his carved image, for what he has cast is false, and there is no spirit in them. They are vain and works worthy of laughter; in the time of their visitation they will perish. Not like these is the portion of Jacob; for he who formed all things, he it is, and Israel is the rod of his inheritance; the Lord of hosts is his name. 3. He who makes the earth by his might is God the Father. And he makes it by his might, that is, by the Lord the Savior; for Christ is the power of God and the wisdom of God. He himself is also the prudence in which he stretches out the heaven. For he himself spoke, and they were made; he commanded, and they were created, saying to the Son: Let us make man after our image and likeness! 4. At his voice he gives a multitude of waters in the heaven. For all the teaching of the Lord flows from heavenly things, as David says: A willing rain you will set apart, O God, for your inheritance, and it was weakened; but you have perfected it. And he lifts up — or brings out — the mists from the ends of the earth. The mists or clouds, which the Lord commanded not to rain a shower upon Israel, are brought out from the extremities of the earth; one of these mists said: For I think that God has displayed us apostles as last, as men destined to death, because we have been made a spectacle to this world, both to angels and to men. 5. He has made lightnings into rain. For when the shower of teachings has come from heaven and has sated the parched hearts of men, then you will find flashes and the bright lightnings of wisdom. And he brings out the winds from his treasuries, in which are hidden all the treasures of wisdom and knowledge. Every man has become foolish from knowledge. Though it be Paul, though Peter, though Moses and Abraham, in comparison with God all their wisdom will be counted as nothing; whence also the foolishness of God is wiser than men. 6. Every craftsman is confounded in his carved image, he who has fashioned images out of his own heart, and what he has cast is false. But if every man is foolish from knowledge, everything that he has made is foolish and false. And there is no spirit in them. It should be noted that in this passage too 'wind' and 'spirit' are called by one name among the Hebrews, 'ruha'; but he means the spirit of sanctification, which cannot be found in the minds of heretics. 7. They are vain and a work worthy of laughter. For who would not laugh when he has looked closely at the images of the heretics? For either what they say is rustic, and it is wood; or it is composed in fine speech, and it is found to be silver; or else it is feigned out of their own mind, and in vain they promise the image of gold. In the time of their visitation they will perish. Heresy prevails for a time, so that all the elect may become manifest and be proved; but when the visitation of God has come and his eye has looked upon foolish things, all things fall silent. 8. Not like these is the portion of Jacob, that is, of those who supplanted the Jews and daily destroy the heretics. But the portion of the saints is God, of whom the prophet says: The Lord is my portion. For he who formed all things, he it is, who made and molded all things, both in mind and in body, and Israel is the rod of his inheritance. Everyone who is 'upright in God' or 'discerns God with the mind' is the scepter of his inheritance, whose name is 'the Lord almighty' or 'of powers'; for this is what 'the Lord of hosts' means, which in the Hebrew is written 'the Lord sabaoth'.

XC. Gather out of the land your confusion, you who dwell under siege, for thus says the Lord: Behold, I will cast far away the inhabitants of the land at this turn, and I will afflict them, that they may be found. LXX: She has gathered from outside her substance, she who dwells in a stronghold, for thus says the Lord: Behold, I will supplant the inhabitants of this land and afflict them, that they may be found. 2. Jerusalem is commanded to gather whatever substance she has outside into the most strongly fortified city and to prepare provisions for a long siege; for he is threatening no longer, as before, about a future and distant time, but now about the captivity close at hand, which is about to come at any moment. 'Gather,' he says, 'both from outside and from the land, that is, from the fields, your substance into confusion. 3. For whatever you have is worthy of confusion; and although you prepare these things, learn the words of the Lord concerning them: Behold, I at this turn, that is, at this time, will cast or hurl as from a sling far away the inhabitants of the land, this one'. For this the Seventy translated: I will supplant and make fall; for this Hebrew word, that is 'cole', Aquila and Symmachus translated sphendonisō [I will sling], and the sense is: 'As from a sling I will hurl them with all force, and so I will cause them to be besieged, and so I will afflict and hem them in, that all may be found in the city and be unable to escape the evil'.

XCI. Woe is me over my crushing, my most grievous wound! LXX: Woe over your crushing, your most grievous wound! According to the Hebrew, Jerusalem herself speaks, because she is grievously afflicted and bears an incurable wound; but according to the Seventy, the prophet speaks to Jerusalem and laments her over her crushing and her own wound.

XCII. But I said: Truly this is my infirmity — or my wound — and I will bear it or it has seized me. Jerusalem herself speaks: 'Whatever I suffer, I suffer through my own fault; I understand my wound, which has seized me, or I will bear the wrath of the Lord, because I have sinned against him'.

XCIII. My tabernacle is established, all my cords are broken — or all my skins are torn apart —, my sons have gone out from me — or my sheep — and they are not; there is no one to stretch out my tent any more — or there is no more place for my tabernacle — and to raise up my skins. 2. Jerusalem laments the overthrow of her city as so easy that you would think it not the razing of the walls and all the fortifications to the ground, but the taking down of a tabernacle and a tent. 'My tabernacle,' she says, 'that is, my dwelling, has suddenly been taken away. All my cords are broken — she keeps the metaphoran [metaphor] of the tabernacle — or all my skins are torn apart. 3. My sons have gone out from me or my cattle'; this, added by the Seventy, does not stand according to the history; for in a long siege how could sheep and cattle have been carried off from Jerusalem, when, even if there had been any, famine had consumed them? 'And they do not', she says, 'subsist or they are not'; for they were not carried over into Chaldea, but a great part of them was killed and utterly destroyed. 'There is no one to stretch out my tent any more: there is no one to restore me and lay the foundations of my walls, which have been leveled to the ground'.

XCIV. For the shepherds have acted foolishly and have not sought the Lord; therefore they have not understood, and all their flock has been scattered. Through the metaphor of shepherds and sheep the guilt of the princes and the scattering of the people are described. For because the princes acted foolishly and did not seek the Lord, whom they ought to have sought with their whole heart, therefore they did not see the evils that were coming, or did not understand the Lord, and the whole multitude of Jerusalem has been scattered hither and thither.

XCV. A voice of hearing, behold, comes, and a great commotion from the land of the north, to make the cities of Judah a desolation and a dwelling of dragons — or a lair of ostriches and, as Symmachus translated, of sirens —; for this in the Hebrew 'thannim' is set down. 2. The words of the prophet: 'Behold,' he says, 'the sound and roaring of the Babylonian as he comes is heard, and a great commotion, or earthquake, from the land of the north, to reduce all the cities of Judea to desolation, their inhabitants slain, and to make dragons and all venomous creatures dwell in place of men, or ostriches'; this animal too is at home in desolation, and is born and nourished in deserts; or else we can understand sirens, certain monsters and phantoms of demons. 3. All these things, which both the past and the present discourse have described, let us refer to the times of the Church's persecution, when the tabernacles of the Lord are overthrown and every dwelling of the Church is reduced to desolation. And that all these things come to pass is the fault of the shepherds, who have acted foolishly and have not sought the Lord nor understood him; and therefore the whole flock has been scattered.

XCVI. I know, O Lord, that his way is not man's, nor is it a man's to walk and to direct his steps. Let the new preachers blush, who say that each one is ruled by his own will, when this prophet too says: his way is not man's, and David sings in a lyric song: By the Lord shall a man's steps be directed, and he will greatly desire his way. Or else this is the sense: 'What we suffer from the Babylonians comes not from dread of them, but from our desert and from your indignation'.

XCVII. Chasten me, O Lord, but yet in judgment and not in your fury, lest perhaps you bring me to nothing! Pour out your indignation upon the nations which have not known you, and upon the provinces — or generations — which have not called upon your name, for they have devoured Jacob and consumed his place, and his beauty — or pastures — they have scattered or reduced to desolation. 2. We read this passage in the Psalm: O Lord, rebuke me not in your fury, nor chasten me in your wrath! And the sense is: 'We deserve indeed all that we endure, and we deserve much greater things than we suffer; yet this I beseech, that you chasten me as a father, not as an adversary, that you amend me as a son and do not punish me as an enemy; for you chastise every son whom you receive, and through every pain and scourge you amend Jerusalem. 3. But the enemies, who have not known you, and the provinces or regions, which have not called upon your name, ought by no means to feel your judgment, but your indignation. For you handed us over that we might be amended. But they made their yoke heavy; they did not spare the old man and they afflicted the little ones; they ate us up and utterly devoured us, and reduced Jerusalem to such desolation that they scattered everything of both public and private buildings and killed your people'.

Jeremiah 11

XCVIII.

The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord, saying: Hear the words of this pact — or covenant — and speak to the men of Judah and to the inhabitants of Jerusalem, and you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord, the God of Israel. It is not, indeed, set down in the heading at what time, or under which king and in which year of his, this prophecy was made; but we understand either that it is to be joined to the former prophecy and its time, or certainly that this word of the Lord came to the prophet some interval of time after the former prophecy. 2. It is to be noted, moreover, that the word 'berith' Aquila and Symmachus always translated 'pact', the Seventy and Theodotion 'covenant'. Properly, however, the word is now directed to Jerusalem and to the men of the tribe of Judah.

XCIX. Cursed be the man who will not hear the words of this pact, which I commanded your fathers in the day in which I brought them out of the land of Egypt, out of the iron furnace, saying: Hear my voice and do all that I command you, and you shall be my people and I will be your Lord. 2. Not because of the privileges of race, not because of the injury of circumcision and the idleness of the sabbath, but because of obedience does God become the God of the people of Israel, and Israel his people. And here, indeed, he speaks as to servants, that they may please God; but in the Gospel the Lord says to the disciples: You, he says, are my friends, if you do what I command you. No longer do I call you servants, because the servant does not know what his lord does. But I have called you friends, because all that I have heard from my Father I have made known to you. 3. And when they have become friends, from friends they pass over into sons; for as many as received him, to them he gave power to become sons of God. Hence he commands friends and sons: Be perfect, as your Father is perfect, enjoining likeness, not equality. And there it is obedience to the commandments, here likeness to God. And what he says: in the day in which I brought them out of the land of Egypt, out of the iron furnace, gives us to understand that the oven and the iron furnace, kindled, signify the greatness of tribulation and punishment, not some place of punishment made ready out of the material of iron.

C. That I may raise up the oath which I swore to your fathers, that I would give them a land flowing with milk and honey, as it is this day. The fathers are seen to receive when the sons receive, and the promise to Abraham is fulfilled in his posterity. 2. A land moreover streaming with milk and honey we ought to take hyperbolically, for an abundance of all things, as in that line: let honey flow for them, and let the rough bramble bear amomum, and again: and he held back the wine running everywhere in streams; or else, tropologically, let us understand the land streaming with milk and honey as the Church of Christ, in which, as little ones and sucklings, we are brought up through faith, so that we may be able to take solid food.

CI. And I answered and said: Amen, O Lord, for which LXX: So be it, O Lord — for this is what 'amen' signifies — is what they translated. The Lord had said: I swore to your fathers that I would give them a land streaming with milk and honey, as is proved today by the facts themselves. The prophet, loving his people, takes up the voice of the Lord and longs that what has been given may be true and may endure forever. Hence he says: 'Truly, O Lord, you have fulfilled what you promised; or So be it, O Lord, that is: may what you have given remain always!'

CII. And the Lord said to me: Cry out — or read — all these words in the cities of Judah and outside Jerusalem, saying: Hear the words of this pact and do them! The most merciful God frequently foretells what is to come, so that the mind that is hard to believe may at last be softened. And he foretells it both in the city of Jerusalem and outside in the countryside, so that those whose peril is common may have the same penitence.

CIII. For, testifying, I testified to your fathers in the day in which I brought them out of the land of Egypt, even to this day; rising early I testified and said: Hear my voice! And they did not hear nor incline their ear, but each one went away in the perversity of his evil heart; and I brought upon them all the words of this pact, which I commanded them to do. 2. Up to this point it is not found in the LXX, and what follows: and they did not do it, was placed by them and joined to the end of the preceding passage, in which it is written: Hear the words of this pact and do them! And they did not do it. But what it means to rise early and frequently press the words of God upon them through the prophets, and to bring them out of Egypt, and often to admonish them and to say: Hear my voice, and that they went away after the perversity of their heart, and that afterwards he renders to them what they deserve to receive, we have already said before.

CIV. And the Lord said to me: A conspiracy has been found among the men of Judah and among the inhabitants of Jerusalem. They have turned back to the former iniquities of their fathers, who would not hear my words; and these, therefore, have gone after foreign gods to serve them. The house of Israel and the house of Judah have made void my pact, which I made with their fathers. 2. For 'conspiracy', which we have translated following Symmachus, Aquila and the Seventy and Theodotion translated syndesmon, which we can call 'a binding-together'. Hence also Athaliah, when she discovered that a plot was being prepared against her in the temple, spoke with the same word: Conspiracy, conspiracy. Properly, however, Scripture employs this word when one strives toward wrongdoing not by a sudden and chance sin but by prepared plots and conspiracy, and when with like mind and one zeal they act so as to despise the commandments of God. 3. And he says that both fathers and sons, with one mind and an equal resolve, neglecting God, worshiped idols, namely both Israel and the house of Judah, that is, both the ten tribes and the two, whose rule was in Jerusalem, so that those who had one zeal in contempt of God may have an equal punishment in enduring captivity.

CV. Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will bring upon them evils from which they will not be able to go out; and they will cry to me and I will not hear them. And the cities of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem will go and cry to the gods to whom they pour libations; and these will not save them in the time of their affliction. 2. God does not hear in the time of need and distress, because they too would not hear the voice of the Lord. This Saul also suffered; for when, dreading the ranks of the Philistines, he had not deserved to receive the word of the Lord, he turned to the pythoness, so as to learn from idols what he ought to have wrung from the Lord by the insistence of prayer and of tears. 3. Through these things we learn that, even if the Lord does not hear, we must by no means cease, nor flee for refuge to demons. They cannot help their worshipers; but we must flee to the help of the Lord, who, though angered, is quickly turned and changes his sentence, if those too with whom he has been angry are changed. And all that is now said pertains to the tribe of Judah and the city of Jerusalem, upon whom captivity is pressing.

CVI. For according to the number of your cities were your gods, O Judah, and according to the number of the ways of Jerusalem you have set up altars of confusion for pouring libations to the Baals. Let us read the books of Kings and of Chronicles, and we shall find that Judah and Jerusalem did far worse things than Israel, so that as many cities as they had, so many kinds of idols they also had, and as many as were the streets and street-corners in the city of Jerusalem, so many altars they had, to their own confusion, on which they sacrificed victims to the idols of the Baals.

CVII. You, therefore, do not pray for this people, and do not take up praise and prayer for them, for I will not hear in the time of their crying to me, in the time of their affliction! Jeremiah is commanded not to wish to entreat the Lord for those against whom the sentence has already been completed, lest his prayer should seem weak and should not be heard because of their own crimes. 2. 'Do not', he says, 'pray for them, nor take up praise, striving, by recounting the clemency of the ancient history with which I have always had mercy on him, and by praising me, to change my sentence! For even if you do this, I will not hear those who are forced to ask me in the time of necessity'. From which we learn that it is in vain for anyone to ask on behalf of another, when the one for whom God is asked does not deserve to receive.

CVIII. What is it that my beloved has done many crimes in my house? Will holy flesh take away from you your wickednesses, in which you have gloried? A rich olive, beautiful, fruitful, comely, the Lord called your name; at the sound of a great speech a fire blazed up in it, and its thickets were burned up. 2. He calls beloved and most dear the people of Judah, which set up idols in his temple and worshiped them, and thinks that in it it appeases the wrath of God if it sacrifices many offerings, and glories in the multitude of sacrifices, which cannot take away the wickednesses of sins. 3. He compares Jerusalem, or the whole people of the Jews, to a beautiful and fruitful olive, which, exalted through pride, by no means acted humbly nor understood its creator and ruler, but, lifted up through pride and having spoken grandly, was set on fire by the fire of the Lord, so that its branches or thickets were burned up and reduced to nothing, that is, the whole people was destroyed by the sword of the adversaries. 4. This sense is found in another place too, where it is said to Jerusalem: I planted you a fruit-bearing vine, wholly true; how have you been turned into bitterness, O alien vine? When its walls are torn down and the boar from the forest lays it waste and all the beasts devour its fruits, let us say this passage to the princes of the churches: What is it that my beloved has done many crimes in my house? Or else to the rich, who, while they plunder what belongs to others and do not take away the wickednesses of their heart, think that they merit the clemency of God: Will holy flesh take away from you your wickednesses, in which you have gloried? 5. But now the names of those who make offerings are recited publicly, and the redemption of sins is turned into praise, nor do they remember that widow in the Gospel who, by casting two coins into the treasury, surpassed the gifts of all the rich.

CIX. And the Lord of hosts, who planted you, has spoken evil against you for the evils of the house of Israel and of the house of Judah, which they did to themselves to provoke me, pouring libations to the Baals. 2. 'A rich olive,' he says, 'beautiful, fruitful, your Lord called you, and he planted you; but because at the sound of the great speech of God a flame came down upon you and consumed all your branches, therefore he who had planted you has now spoken evil against you, not out of the injustice of his sentence nor out of a sudden perversity of speech, but for the evils which the house of Israel and the house of Judah did to themselves and zealously carried out, pouring libations to the Baals, so as to provoke me to anger'. 3. And although God can do what he wills, he gives his reasons, lest he should seem to act unjustly, according to that which is written: that you may be justified in your words and may overcome when you are judged.

CX. But you, O Lord, have shown me and I have known; then you showed me their pursuits. And I was like a gentle lamb that is carried to be a victim, and I did not know that they had devised counsels against me: Let us put wood into his bread and crush him from the land of the living, and let his name be remembered no more! But you, O Lord of Sabaoth, who judge justly and test the reins and the heart, let me see your vengeance on them! For to you I have revealed my cause. 2. This is the consensus of all the churches, that they understand these words to be spoken by Christ under the person of Jeremiah: that the Father showed him how he ought to speak, and showed him the pursuits of the Jews, and that he himself, like a lamb led to be a victim, did not open his mouth and did not know — 'sin' is understood, according to what is said by the Apostle: who, though he had not known sin, was made sin for us —, and that they said: Let us put wood into his bread — namely the cross into the body of the Savior; for he it is who says: I am the bread that came down from heaven, — and let us root him out — or crush him — from the land of the living! 3. For this crime they devised in their mind, to blot out his name forever. But on the contrary, according to the mystery of the body he had assumed, the Son speaks to the Father and calls down his judgment, while he praises his righteousness and invokes God as the inspector of reins and heart, that he himself may render to the people what it deserves, and he says: let me see your vengeance on them — namely on those who persist in crime, and not on those who turn to repentance; of these he says on the cross: Father, forgive them; for they do not know what they do —, and he reveals to the Father and lays open his cause, that he was crucified through no fault of his own but by the crime of the people, saying: Behold, the prince of this world comes and finds nothing in me. 4. The Jews and our Judaizers understand these words to be spoken in the person of Jeremiah, and they maintain that he endured these things from the people because of his prophecy of things to come and because of the impending evils of the captivity. But I do not know how they can prove that Jeremiah was crucified, since Scripture does not record it, unless perhaps they devised it and did not do it.

CXI. Therefore thus says the Lord to the men of Anathoth, who seek your life and say: You shall not prophesy in the name of the Lord, and you shall not die in our hands. Therefore thus says the Lord of hosts: Behold, I will visit upon them; the young men shall die by the sword, their sons and their daughters shall die by famine, and there shall be no remnant of them; for I will bring evil upon the men of Anathoth, the year of their visitation. 2. This seems to run counter to the former view, by which we wish to prove that the things that were said were said in the person of Christ, and not in that of Jeremiah, who properly dwelt in the little village of Anathoth, which is three miles distant from Jerusalem. But if we understand the etymologian [etymology] of the town Anathoth, which means 'obedience', it will be clearly shown that by the men of Anathoth, who once obeyed the precepts of the Lord, all the Jews are meant, and especially the inhabitants of the city of Jerusalem, upon whom came the final sentence, that they should perish by the evils of the siege, by sword and famine and pestilence. 3. But to free ourselves from all the trouble of interpretation, let us follow this rule: that all the prophets did most things as a type of the Lord the Savior, and that whatever was fulfilled in Jeremiah in the present time is prophesied of the Lord for the future.

Book Three

I. Fables relate that the Lernaean serpent sprouted many serpents from the middle of its head, and that Scylla, the monster of the Sicilian strait, with a maiden's face indeed, but girded with dogs, tore apart the wrecks of the wretched, while the deadly song of the Sirens was joined to her on the same shore; to escape these, Homer's Ulysses is said to have stopped his ears and to have escaped an insuperable evil by prudent counsel. 2. When I desired to do this, and to turn aside from the madness of the heretics, singing, after the manner of the poet Ismenias, to myself and to my own, and was pondering that prophetic word: when the sinner stood against me, I became mute and was silent about good things, the devil did not suffer me, content with the quiet I had longed for, to apply myself to the explanation of the holy Scriptures and to hand on to the men of my own tongue the learning of the Hebrews and of the Greeks; but he works at this day and night, both openly and through plots, mixing false things with true, nay rather smearing all his lies with treacherous honey, so that whoever hears the sweetness of the words may not fear the poisons of the heart; he promises peace, in order to wage heavier wars; he smiles, in order to bite; he offers his hand, in order to kill the guileless Abner unawares. 3. This, no doubt, is what the Apostle also was speaking of: for we are not ignorant of his wiles. Here he is silent, elsewhere he accuses; he sends papyrus letters into the whole world, once gold-bearing, now slanderous, and interprets our patience, which comes from the humility of Christ, as a sign of a bad conscience. And he himself, mute, barks through an Alpine dog, huge and corpulent, and one able to rage more with his heels than with his teeth. 4. For he has his stock from the Scottish race, from the neighborhood of the Britons, and, after the fables of the poets, like Cerberus he must be struck with a spiritual club, so that he may fall silent in eternal silence together with his master Pluto. But of these things elsewhere. Now I shall take up the third book on Jeremiah, in which I shall try, brother Eusebius, to compress the very broad fields of explanation into the narrow path of commentaries.

Jeremiah 12

II.

Righteous indeed are you, O Lord, if I dispute with you — or for I will satisfy you — ; yet I will speak judgments to you: Why is it that the way of the wicked prospers, and it is well with all who transgress and act wickedly? You planted them and they have put forth root; they prosper — or they have begotten sons — and they bear fruit; you are near to their mouth and far from their reins. 2. This disputation is indeed against all who act wickedly, and the meaning of Psalm 72 (73) is briefly summed up in it, in which the prophet says: How good is the God of Israel to those who are upright of heart! But my feet were almost moved, my steps had almost been poured out, because I was jealous of sinners, seeing the peace of sinners, and so on. 3. But properly it is said against the heretics: though they are wicked, their way prospers, and they beget as sons those whom they have deceived in heresy, and they transgress and act wickedly, so that they despoil the Church, and, while they persist in the perversity of their opinion, they boast that they have been planted by God and have put forth root, have begotten sons and have borne fruit; and though they repeat the name of Christ again and again, they do not have God dwelling in them, according to that word of Isaiah: This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me.

III. And you, O Lord, have known me, you have seen me and have tested my heart with you. Gather them like a flock to be victims, and sanctify them in the day of slaughter! 'It is no stumbling-block', he says, 'that the wicked, or all the heretics, flourish for a time; for you, O Lord, have known me and have seen me and have tested my heart with you'. 2. Whom does God the Father know as he knows his Son? For no one knows the Son except the Father, and no one knows the Father except the Son, and he to whom the Son wills to reveal him. 'Although', he says, 'the heretics prosper, although they beget sons and bear fruit, and you are near to their mouth and far from their reins, that is, from their conscience, yet it is no small consolation that like cattle they are being fattened to be victims. 3. You gather them into the city of Jerusalem, or into their own conventicles, so that like victims they may be slaughtered unto death, and may then be sanctified when they have been slain by the sword of the Church; for the slaying of the heretics is the salvation of those who had been deceived'.

IV. How long will the land mourn, and the grass of every region wither, because of the wickedness of those who dwell in it? Beast and bird are consumed, because they said: He will not see our last end or God will not see our ways. Whatever happens in the world, whether of good or of evil, does not happen without providence and by fortuitous chance, but by the judgment of God. 2. The land is now barren, the grass withers: do you wish to know the reason? The wickednesses of its inhabitants bring this about, so that both the animals upon the earth and the birds of the sky are consumed, since all these things were created for the uses of men. They rose up to such blasphemy as to say that God is ignorant of their ways and does not know what each one is going to suffer. And when he says 'how long', he signifies that the wrath of God remains, because the mind of those who sin is not changed.

V. If, running with footmen, you have grown weary, how will you be able to contend with horses? If in a land of peace you have been confident, what will you do in the pride — or roaring — of the Jordan? 'If', he says, 'the frequent captivity inflicted by the neighboring nations has worn you out, by the Moabites and Ammonites, the Philistines and Edomites, what will you do in the face of the long captivity which is to lead you all the way to Chaldea?' And he compares footmen with horsemen, because in truth and according to the history all Persia and all Chaldea and the army of those regions delight in cavalry. 2. But these nations which I have mentioned above, because of the difficulty of their terrain, are suited not so much to battle as to brigandage. And he keeps the metaphoran [metaphor] and says: 'If, running with footmen, you have failed from weariness, what will you do if you wish to match your running to horses? And if in your own land you had some confidence, what will you do when you have crossed the Jordan and endured its whirlpools?'

VI. For even your brothers and the house of your father, they too have fought against you and have cried out after you with full voice; do not believe them when they speak good things to you! 'So far', he says, 'will you be covered by the most grievous floods of the Jordan, and so far will the multitude of horsemen coming against you from afar lay you waste, that your brothers too, the Edomites, and the house of your father, who were begotten of the stock of Lot, Moab and Ammon, will themselves, in the time of necessity and distress, fight against you and insult you. 2. Therefore beware of being willing to believe them and of placing hope in kinship, on account of which they rage against you with greater hatred than enemies do'. This can also be taken of the Savior, that his brothers and the house of his father fought against him and cried out with full voice, saying: Crucify, crucify him; we have no king but Caesar.

VII. I have left my house, I have abandoned my inheritance, I have given my beloved soul into the hand of its enemies. My inheritance has become to me like a lion in the forest; it has given forth its voice against me, therefore I have hated it. He who said in the Gospel: Rise, let us go hence, and again: your house shall be left to you deserted, here too, in the prophet, threatens the same things, and says that he has done what he is going to do. For the inheritance of the Lord is Israel, and the cord of his inheritance. 2. And what he says: I have given my beloved soul into the hand of its enemies, is this: I have power to lay down my soul, and I have power to take it up again.
