You shall be left, he says, alone, bereft of your inheritance, which I gave you, and I will make you serve your enemies in a land which you do not know, either under the Babylonians or, as is truer, under the Romans. For they themselves kindled the fire and provoked the most merciful Lord to fury, and this fire of his fury will burn forever. 3. I am ashamed of the contentiousness of our own people, who find fault with the Hebrew truth. The Jews read against themselves, and the Church does not know what is in her favor. Hence let us, who are the sons of the apostles, remember the iniquities of the former people, and we testify that they have suffered justly. 4. But the 'high places', which in Hebrew are called 'bamoth', can also be taken against the heretics, who have set their mouth on high, and their tongue has passed over the earth, who have broken out into such madness that they alone have remained without the grace of the Holy Spirit and have lost the inheritance of the Lord, namely the former truth of the faith. Hence for them too an eternal burning is prepared, and servitude to the demons, who are their enemies and avengers.

LXXII. Thus says the Lord: cursed is the man who trusts — or has hope — in man and makes flesh his arm, and whose heart departs from the Lord. For he will be like a tamarisk in the desert, and he will not see when good comes, but he will dwell in dryness in the desert, in a land of saltness and uninhabitable. 2. If every man who trusts in man is cursed, and Paul of Samosata and Photinus, although they proclaim the Savior holy and exalted above all in virtues, nevertheless confess him to be a man, then they will be cursed, having hope in a man. But if it is objected to us that we too believe in him who says: but now you seek to kill me, a man who has spoken the truth to you, which I heard from God; this Abraham did not do. You do the works of your father, we shall answer with that saying of the Apostle: even if we once knew Christ according to the flesh, yet now we no longer know him. 3. Then the same Apostle, at the beginning of his epistle, writes to the Galatians: Paul an apostle, not from men nor through man, but through Jesus Christ and God the Father, who raised him from the dead, and all the brethren who are with me. For if death has been swallowed up in victory, why should not the lowliness of the flesh, which was assumed for the sake of human salvation, have passed into the majesty of the divinity, so that he has made both one, and we do not adore a creature but the Creator, who is blessed forever? 4. Cursed, therefore, is not only he who has hope in man, but he who makes flesh his arm, that is, his strength, and who thinks that whatever he has done belongs not to the mercy of the Lord but to his own power. For he who does this, his heart departs from the Lord, since he asserts that he can do what he cannot. And he will be like a tamarisk — which in Hebrew is called 'aroher', or, as Symmachus translated, an unfruitful tree — in the wilderness, and he will not see when good things come, which the multitude of the nations is to see, but he will dwell in dryness in the desert. 5. This is said of the people of the Jews, who dwell in the desert and bear no fruits and are in a land of saltness, which bears no fruits, and uninhabitable, which has neither God as its guest, nor the protection of angels, nor the grace of the Holy Spirit, nor the knowledge of teachers.

LXXIII. Blessed is the man who trusts in the Lord, and the Lord will be his confidence — or hope — ; and he will be like a tree that has been transplanted — or fruitful — upon the waters, that sends out its roots toward moisture; and it will not fear when the heat comes, and its leaf will be green — or its twigs in leaf — and in the time — or year — of dryness it will not be anxious — or will not fear — nor will it ever cease to bear fruit. 2. Let that be said of the Jews and of the heretics, who have hope in man, namely in their Christ, whom they think will come not as the Son of God but as a mere man. On the contrary, the man of the Church, who trusts in the Lord, hears this: and know that the Lord himself is God. He trusts in the Lord and will be compared to that tree of which it is sung also in the first Psalm: and he will be like a tree that has been planted beside the courses of waters, which will give its fruit in its season; and its leaf will not fall off. 3. Upon the waters, moreover: the grace of the Holy Spirit, its diverse gifts. That sends out its roots toward moisture: that it may receive fruitfulness from the Lord; but we can also say otherwise, that we have been transplanted from the Jewish dryness into the everlasting grace of baptism. 4. And it will not, he says, fear when the heat comes — either the time of persecution or the day of judgment — ; and its leaf will be green — or there will be twigs in leaf on it —, so that it never fears dryness, but buds with the grace of all the virtues. And when the time or the year of dryness comes, it will not be afraid, when the Lord in anger commands the clouds not to rain a shower upon Israel. 5. And what follows, nor will it cease to bear fruit, will be able to explain that passage which is written in Mark, that the Lord came to the fig tree and did not find fruit on it, because it was not yet the time, and cursed it, that it should not bear fruit forever. For he who trusts in the Lord, and whose confidence the Lord is, will not be afraid even in the time of the Jewish dryness, but will always bear fruits, believing in him who once died for us and dies no more and says: I am the life.

LXXIV. The heart of all is depraved and inscrutable; who will know it? I the Lord search the heart and prove the reins, who give to each one according to his way and according to the fruit of his devices. LXX: the heart is deep above all things, and it is man; who will know him? and so on in the same way. The Hebrew word 'enos' is written with four letters, 'aleph' and 'nun' and 'uau' and 'sin'; if, then, it is read 'enos', it means 'man'; but if 'anus', 'inscrutable' or 'desperate', because no one is able to find out the heart of men. 2. Symmachus, however, translated this passage thus: inscrutable is the heart of all; but who is the man that can find it out? And our people are wont, with a good desire indeed but not according to knowledge, to use this passage against the Jews, to show that the Lord and Savior is a man according to the dispensation of the flesh that he assumed, and that no one can know the mystery of his birth, according to what is written: who will declare his generation?, except God alone, who searches out secrets and renders to each one according to his works. 3. But it is better that we take it simply, that no one knows the secrets of the thoughts except God alone; for he had said above: cursed is the man who has hope in man, and on the contrary: blessed is the man who trusts in the Lord. 4. Hence, lest we should think the judgment of men certain, he added that the hearts of nearly all are perverse, as the psalmist says: cleanse me from my hidden faults, O Lord, and from those of others spare your servant — no doubt, from thoughts — and in Genesis: but God, seeing that the wickedness of men was great on the earth, and that every thought of the heart was intent upon evil at all times, and again: for the sense and thought of the human heart are prone to evil from its youth. 5. Through these we learn that God alone knows their thoughts. But if it is said of the Savior, but Jesus, seeing their thoughts, and no one can see thoughts except God alone, then Christ is God, who searches hearts and proves the reins and will render to each one according to his works.

LXXV. The partridge has brooded — or has gathered —, what it did not bring forth — and, as the Seventy translated: the partridge cried out, it gathered what it did not bring forth —, he makes his riches not with judgment; in the half of his days he will leave them — or in the half of his days they will leave him — and at his end he will be a fool. 2. The writers of natural history, of beasts and birds as well as of trees and plants, whose chief men among the Greeks are Aristotle and Theophrastus, and among us Pliny Secundus, say that this is the nature of the partridge: that it steals the eggs of another partridge, that is, eggs not its own, and sits on them and broods them, and that when the young have grown up, they fly away from it and leave their alien parent. 3. Of this kind are the rich who plunder what belongs to others and, without reflecting on the judgment of God, make riches not with judgment, which they leave behind in the middle of their time, snatched away by sudden death, when it is said to them: fool, this night they will demand your soul from you; and the things you have prepared, whose will they be? And nothing is more foolish than not to foresee the last things and to think brief things perpetual. 4. Others, however, both on account of the history given above and on account of another, by which they say that the partridge is most quarrelsome and unclean, so much so that it defiles the one it has vanquished, interpret the devil under its name, because he has gathered to himself riches not his own, saying to the Lord: all these things I will give you, if you will fall down and worship me. His riches, which had been wickedly gathered by him, will leave him, and through the apostles they will return to the Lord, and he who seemed to himself most prudent will be, in the judgment of all, a fool. 5. And what is said by the Seventy, the partridge cried out, is to be referred to the person of the heretics: that this partridge, the devil, has cried out through the chiefs of the heretics and has gathered what it did not bring forth, and has gathered to itself a multitude of the deceived, which afterwards will abandon it, and it will be proved by the judgment of all to be most foolish.

LXXVI. A throne of glory of the height from the beginning, the place of our sanctification, the expectation of Israel, O Lord, all who leave you will be confounded! Let those who depart be written in the earth, since they have forsaken the vein — or fountain — of living waters, the Lord. 2. The partridge being forsaken for its folly, the expectation of Israel, that is, of the people of God and of the one who believes in the Lord, is he himself who made all things, whose throne is glorious and exalted from the beginning, and who is the place of sanctification of all believers; so that the Lord is not in a place, but wherever he is, that place is sanctified. On the contrary, those who forsake the Lord will be confounded with perpetual confusion, and departing or turning aside from him they will be written in the earth, blotted out from the book of the living. 3. For just as he who can say with the Apostle, but our citizenship is in heaven, is written in the heavenly places, so he who leaves the Lord or turns aside from him will be written in the earth with those who mind earthly things. And the cause is plain why they are written in the earth: because they have left the fountain of life, the Lord, or the fountain of living waters, the Lord, who speaks in the Gospel: if anyone thirsts, let him come to me and drink! He who believes in me, as the scripture says, rivers of living water will flow from his belly. Now this he said of the Spirit, which those who believed were to receive.

LXXVII. Heal me, O Lord, and I shall be healed; save me, and I shall be saved, since you are my praise! Many physicians in the Gospel had treated the woman with the issue of blood, who had spent all her substance on them, and yet she could be cured by none except by him who is the true physician and in whose wings is healing. Hence now too the prophet, having suffered reproaches from the people and having been often beset by plots, desires to be healed and to be saved by him who is his true praise and his true medicine.

LXXVIII. Behold, they say to me: where is the word of the Lord? Let it come! But I was not troubled, following you as shepherd — or I have not labored following you — and I have not desired the day of man; you know. What went out from my lips was right in your sight — or is before your face —. Do not be a terror to me, my hope in the day of affliction, or do not become a stranger to me, sparing me in the worst day! 2. Those who do not think that what has been spoken will come say to the prophet: where is the word of the Lord? let it come!, judging the delay to be a setting aside of the sentence. But while they are saying these things, he says: 'I was not troubled, nor did I labor, following you as shepherd, or walking in your footsteps; nor was I content to stop there, but I have not desired the day of man, either a longer life or any of the prosperities of this world'. And he calls the same one as witness whom he calls as judge: you have known. 3. There follows: what went out from my lips was right in your sight, meaning that he never lied, that he said nothing contrary to the will of the Lord. Do not, he says, be a terror to me, my hope in the day of affliction! 4. This is clear according to the Hebrew; but according to what the Seventy translated, saying, do not become to me as a stranger, sparing me in the evil day, the sense is: 'do not spare me in the present world, which is evil, but render to me according to my sins, so that I may have everlasting rest! for I know that it is written: whom the Lord loves, he reproves; and he scourges every son whom he receives'. But the evil day is either the whole world or the day of judgment for those who are tormented on account of their sins.

LXXIX. Let those who persecute me be confounded, and let me not be confounded! Let them tremble, and let me not tremble! Bring upon them the day of affliction, and crush them with a double crushing! The prophet calls down imprecations against those who taunt him with the word of the Lord and say, where is the word of the Lord? let it come: that those who persecute him may be confounded, and may blush and return to salvation; that they, who lie, may tremble, and not he, who foretells true things; and that, when the day of vengeance has come, he may crush them with a double crushing, famine and sword.

LXXX. Thus says the Lord to me: go and stand in the gate — or in the gates — of the sons of the people, through which [gate] — or through which [gates] — the kings of Judah go in and go out, in all the gates of Jerusalem, and you shall say to them: hear the word of the Lord, kings of Judah and all Judah — or Judea — and all the inhabitants of Jerusalem, who go in through these gates! 2. 'Since', he says, 'they scorn to hear your words and do not come to you to inquire what the sentence of God may be, go you to the most frequented place, either of the gates of the temple or of those of the city, through which the kings and all the common people go in and go out, so that they may be compelled by necessity to hear, and you may preach the word of God out of season and in season, and no excuse may remain to them that they did not do it because they did not hear'.

LXXXI. Thus says the Lord: guard your souls, and do not carry weights on the sabbath day, nor bring them in through the gates of Jerusalem! And do not cast out loads from your houses on the sabbath day, and you shall do no work, and sanctify the sabbath day, as I commanded your fathers! And they did not hear, nor did they incline their ear, and they hardened their neck — and what is not found in the Hebrew: above their fathers —, that they might not hear me and might not receive instruction. 2. And it shall be, he says, if you hear me, says the Lord, so as not to bring in loads through the gates of this city on the sabbath day, and if you sanctify the sabbath day, so as not to do any work on it, there will enter through the gates of this city kings and princes sitting upon the throne of David and mounting in chariots and on horses, they and their princes, the men of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem, and this city will be inhabited forever. 3. And they will come from the cities of Judah and from round about Jerusalem and from the land of Benjamin and from the plains and from the hill country and from the south, bringing burnt offering and victims — or incense offerings — and sacrifice — or manna — and frankincense, and they will bring in an offering — or praise — into the house of the Lord. 4. But if you will not hear me, to sanctify the sabbath day and not to carry a load and not to bring it in through the gates of Jerusalem on the sabbath day, I will kindle a fire in its gates, and it will devour the houses of Jerusalem and will not be extinguished. 5. Lest I should tear in pieces the precept of the sabbath renewed through Jeremiah, I have chosen to set it out whole, so that we may know everything together. He guards his soul who does not carry the weights of sins on the day of 'rest' and of sabbath, nor bring them in through the gates of Jerusalem, which we ought to take as the virtues. And do not, he says, cast out loads from your houses — for they are not to be carried, but utterly cast away — and do no work — either servile work or that of which it is written: food for the belly and the belly for foods; but God will destroy both it and them —, but that work is to be worked of which the Savior speaks: 'work the work that does not perish!' 6. Sanctify, he says, the sabbath day, so that we may spend all the time of our life in sanctification, as our fathers Abraham, Isaac and Jacob did. And when God had commanded these things, they did not incline their ear — that of the mind, surely, not of the flesh —, but they hardened their neck, rejecting the yoke of the law and, by metaphoran [metaphor], bearing the likeness of untamed animals. 7. Let us see what is the reward of those who do not carry loads on the sabbath day and who sanctify it. There will enter, he says, through the gates of this city kings, whose heart is in the hand of God and who reign over their bodies, and princes sitting upon the throne of David, so that they imitate the example of Christ, and mounting in chariots and on horses, of which it is written: the chariot of God is ten thousandfold, manifold, thousands of those who rejoice; the Lord is among them in Sinai, in the holy place, and elsewhere: your riding is salvation: every man who 'confesses' God and who dwells in Jerusalem, of which it has been said: was made in Salem, that is, in peace, his place, and his dwelling in Zion; and the Church of God will be inhabited forever. 8. And they will come from the cities of Judah and from round about Jerusalem, of which we have already spoken, and from the land of Benjamin, who is the 'son' 'of power' and 'of the right hand', and from the plains, which in Hebrew is called 'sefela' and signifies the plain understanding of the history, and from the hill country, namely the lofty doctrines, and from the south, of which it is written, God will come from the south, whence comes heat and full light, and where all cold is driven out. 9. Bringing, he says, burnt offerings — consecrating themselves to God — and a victim or incense, so that they may say: a sacrifice to the Lord is a troubled spirit, and: we are the good odor of Christ in every place, and elsewhere: let my prayer be directed as incense in your sight; and sacrifice, for which the Seventy put the Hebrew word itself, 'manaa', which by a very bad custom, nay rather by the carelessness of scribes, is read in our books as 'manna'; and frankincense, of which it has been said, to what purpose do you bring me frankincense from Sheba?; and they will bring in an offering, which in Hebrew is called 'thoda' and can be rendered as 'thanksgiving', and which the Seventy translated as 'praise'. Into the house of David: no doubt, into the Church. 10. These are the rewards of those who sanctify the sabbath and are weighed down by no weight. 'But if', he says, 'you will not hear my precepts, and do the things which I commanded not to be done, I will kindle a fire in its gates' — that is, of Jerusalem: that fire of which it is said, all committing adultery, like an oven, their hearts —, 'which will devour the houses or streets of Jerusalem' — which the Seventy translated as amphoda, Aquila and Symmachus as bareis, that is, 'towered houses', and which are called in Hebrew 'armanoth' — 'and this burning will never be extinguished', as the Apostle says: the fire will test what sort each one's work is, and again: if anyone's work is burned, he will suffer loss, but he himself will be saved, yet so as through fire. 11. But if our Judaizers reject the figurative explanation, they will be compelled either to be Jews and, with the observance of the sabbath, to circumcise their foreskins, or else to find fault with the Savior, who on the sabbath commanded the paralytic to carry his bed, as the evangelist says: for this reason the Jews sought all the more to kill him, not only because he was breaking the sabbath, but also because he said that God was his own Father, making himself equal to God.

Book Four

I. The devil always threatens a good work; everywhere he sets snares for those who walk, as the prophet tells of the plots of his henchmen, beside the path they have set a stumbling block for me, and as the Gospel teaches this very thing more fully, that the birds of the sky snatch away and destroy the seeds thrown beside the way. 2. Why I have made use of this beginning, brother Eusebius, the following discourse will show. Occupied with the many crowds flowing together here from the whole world, and with the cares of the holy brothers and of the monastery, I was dictating commentaries on Jeremiah at intervals, so that what was lacking in leisure might be made up by diligence, when suddenly the heresy of Pythagoras and Zeno, of apatheias and anamartēsias, that is, of 'impassibility' and 'sinlessness', which was once slain in Origen and long since in his disciples Grunnius and Evagrius of Pontus and Jovinian, began to revive and to hiss, not only in the West but also in parts of the East, and in certain islands, especially Sicily and Rhodes, to stain very many and to grow day by day, while they teach in secret and deny in public. 3. Long silent, and swallowing my pain in silence, I was compelled by the frequent demands of the brothers to answer it; and yet I have not so far broken out as to set down the names of its authors, preferring that they be corrected rather than defamed; for I am the enemy not of men but of error. They, in order to dash against me a return in kind and the back tooth of their own resentment, patching together the old calumnies of their masters, have been shown to be so tongueless and wretched that they could not even revile in words of their own. 4. Their trifles were refuted at that time in books published against them; and those who are willing to read these will see clearly that they are, according to Isaiah, dumb dogs, which know not how to bark, having indeed the will and the rage to lie, but not having the art of feigning and barking. 5. To them I will speak briefly: either the things you teach are good, or they are evil. If good, defend them freely; if evil, why do you secretly slay the wretched with error, and flaunt an exposition of the right faith in order to deceive all the simple? If it is true, why is it hidden? If false, why is it written? I ask, what is this madness? Though the Apostle instructs that we be ready to give satisfaction to everyone who asks of us a reason for the hope that is in us, and the prophet cries out, I spoke of your testimonies in the sight of kings and was not confounded, these men flee from public view and whisper in the corners of the lost, and they grieve, as if for their own, over things which they fear to confess as their own. 6. And whenever we have said anything in general against vices and heretics, they complain that they are being attacked, and, their indignation long concealed, they proclaim the evil of their conscience. But if we must beware lest we seem to injure an old friendship, should we cut down a most proud heresy with the spiritual sword, then we shall have to endure the crosses of a betrayed faith and to say with the prophet: I was turned about in misery, while the thorn is fixed into me. 7. Rather let them hear that apostolic saying, one must obey God rather than men, and again: if I pleased men, I would not be a servant of Christ. But these things, unless they keep silent, we shall pursue more fully in a work of its own. Now let us take up the journey begun on Jeremiah, and, running briefly through the fourth volume on him, let us linger only on those things which prove obscure.

Jeremiah 18

II.

The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord, saying: Arise and go down into the potter's house, and there you shall hear my words! And I went down into the potter's house, and behold, he was doing a work upon the wheel — or stones —. And the vessel that he was making from clay with his hands was broken; and turning — or and again — he made it another vessel, as it had pleased in his eyes to make it. 2. And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: Can I not do with you as this potter, O house of Israel? says the Lord; behold, as clay in the hand of the potter, so are you in my hand, O house of Israel! Suddenly — or in sum — I will speak against a nation and against a kingdom, to root it out — or to take it away — and to destroy and to ruin it. 3. If that nation repents of its evil, which I have spoken against it, I too will repent of the evil which I thought to do to it. And suddenly — or in sum — I will speak concerning a nation and a kingdom, to build and to plant it. If it does evil in my eyes, so as not to hear my voice, I will repent of the good which I spoke of, to do to it. 4. Through all the senses, indeed, one arrives at judgment and understanding of mind, through hearing, smell, taste, touch; but what is seen with the eyes is retained more in the mind. Hence the prophet is ordered to go to the potter's house and there to hear the Lord's commands. 'And when,' he says, 'I had set out and had gone down into the potter's house, he was doing a work upon the wheel', which the Seventy, led astray by the ambiguity of the word, translated 'stones'; for 'abanim', according to the nature of the context and the difference of pronunciation, is used both for an 'instrument', that is, the potter's 'wheel', and for 'stones'. 5. 'And when,' he says, 'I saw a vessel being made from clay, it was suddenly broken', God's providence bringing this about, so that the craftsman's hand, without knowing it, should by its own mistake figure forth a parable. And again that potter, who had lost the vessel of clay as the wheel was running, made another for himself, as it seemed good to him. And at once the Lord says to the prophet: 'If a potter,' he says, 'has this power, that from the same clay he makes again what had been broken, shall I not be able to do this in you, who, as far as lies in you, seem to have perished?' 6. And to signify free will, he says that he announces both evils to this or that nation and kingdom and, again, good things; and yet that what he himself has foretold does not come about, but that the contrary happens, so that good things befall the evil, if they repent, and evils befall the good, if after the promises they have turned to sins. And we say this not because God does not know that this or that nation or kingdom will do this or that, but because he leaves man to his own will, so that he may receive either rewards or punishments by his own desert. 7. Nor will what befalls be wholly man's at once, but belongs to the grace of him who has bestowed all things; for the freedom of the will must be preserved in such a way that the grace of the giver excels in all things, according to that prophetic word: Unless the Lord builds the house, those who build it have labored in vain; unless the Lord guards the city, he who guards it has watched in vain. For it is not of him who wills, nor of him who runs, but of God who shows mercy.

III. Now therefore say to the man of Judah and to the inhabitant of Jerusalem, saying: These things — or thus — says the Lord: Behold, I am fashioning evil against you and devising a device against you; let each one turn back from his evil way, and make straight your ways and your pursuits! And they said: We have despaired — or we will be strong —; for we will walk after our own thoughts, and each one of us will do the depravity — or what has pleased his evil heart —. Therefore thus says the Lord: Ask among the nations: who has heard such horrible things as the virgin of Israel has done exceedingly? 2. The Lord fulfills the parable which he had taught both by sight and by words, and says: 'Behold, I am fashioning evil against you, as a potter fashions clay' — evil, however, in the sense of Isaiah, who says: making peace and creating evil, not that it is evil in itself, but that it seems to be evil to those who suffer it — 'and devising a device against you' — that is, to pass sentence according to desert —; 'change your works and make straight your ways, so that the punishment too may be changed into prosperity!' 3. 'But they, on the contrary,' he says, 'said: We will be strong' — in their evil works, that is, or, according to Aquila, we have despaired, and according to Symmachus, we have failed, both of which offend God, that one should either think that he can by no means be saved, or should have failed in spirit for appeasing God — 'and after our own thoughts,' he says, 'we will walk.' 4. Where, then, is the power of free will and the judgment of one's own will apart from the grace of God, when it is a grave offense against God to follow one's own thoughts and to do the will of a depraved heart? Therefore he adds, saying: 'Ask among the nations and all the peoples round about, who has done, who has heard, while serving idols, what the virgin of Israel has done exceedingly.' And he calls her a virgin because she had served the one God, as the prophet says: God is known in Judea, his name is great in Israel.

IV. Will the snow of Lebanon fail from the rock of the field? Or can the cold waters that burst forth and flow down be torn away? LXX: Will breasts fail from the rock, or snow from Lebanon? Or will water violently carried off by the wind turn aside? 2. Something of the same kind that line of Vergil also sounds: Sooner, therefore, shall the nimble stags graze in the air, and the seas leave the fishes bare upon the shore, than his face shall slip from our heart; and in another place: While rivers run into the seas, while shadows move over the slopes of the mountains, while the sky feeds the stars, ever shall your honor, your name and your praises abide. 3. 'Just as,' he says, 'the snow cannot fail from the summits of Lebanon, nor is it overcome by any heat of the sun so as to melt entirely, and the streams flowing down from the mountains are by no means dried up at their springs, so my name, which is in itself stable and everlasting, cannot be changed; and yet, while all other things keep the order of nature, my people has forgotten me.' For there follows:

V. For my people has forgotten me, making libations in vain and stumbling in their ways, in the paths of the age — or everlasting —, so that they walked in them on a road not trodden. He who forgets God and forsakes him who says: I am the way, and pours libations to strange gods, stumbles in his own ways — not God's at all, but his own — and forsakes the ancient and everlasting paths, which are trodden by the footprints of all the saints who worship God. These, however, have walked on a road not trodden and, having abandoned the worship of God, have venerated idols; on account of which the punishment that follows is brought upon them:

VI. So that their land should become a desolation and an everlasting hissing; everyone who passes by it shall be astonished and shall shake his head. 'And because they have forsaken,' he says, 'the worship of God and have followed the depraved paths of idolatry, therefore their land has been reduced to a wilderness and to a wonder and a hissing to all, so that those who saw the land and city, once most flourishing, collapsed into a desert and ashes should marvel and be astonished and show the confusion of their soul by the gesture of their body'; for this is what it is to shake the head and to show the astonishment of the mind by hissing. 2. And this we understand to have been fulfilled more fully and more correctly after the coming of the Lord, when none of the Jews is permitted by law to enter the land and the once holy city; but when they come to mourn, they marvel at and weep over the prophecies of the prophets fulfilled in deed.

VII. Like a burning wind I will scatter them before the enemy — or the enemies —; the back and not the face will I show them in the day of their perdition. To this day the sentence of God remains upon the Jews; they have been scattered throughout the whole world before the enemy, the devil, or the enemies, the demons, and, though day and night in the synagogues of Satan they call upon the name of God, God shows them his back and not his face, so that they may understand that he is always withdrawing and never coming to them. 2. Now the day of the perdition of the Jews is the whole time after the Savior's passion until the consummation of the age, so that, after the fullness of the nations has come in, then all Israel may be saved.

VIII. And they said: Come and let us devise devices against Jeremiah! For the law shall not perish from the priest, nor counsel from the wise, nor the word from the prophet; come and let us strike him with the tongue, and let us not heed all his words! 2. This was both then the thought of the Jews against Jeremiah, or against the Lord the Savior, and is today the thought of heretics against his servants: to contrive calumnies and to forestall holy men with accusation, and not to consider what truth those men speak, but what falsehood they themselves may contrive; for they boast that the law of God and counsel and utterance remain in their own priests and wise men and false prophets, though Scripture says: Into a perverse soul wisdom shall not enter.

IX. Attend to me, O Lord, and hear the voice of my adversaries! Is evil repaid for good, that they have dug a pit for my soul? Remember that I stood in your sight to speak good for them and to turn away your indignation from them! Therefore give their children up to famine and lead them into the hands of the sword; let their wives be childless and widows, and let their husbands be put to death, let their young men be pierced by the sword in battle, let an outcry be heard from their houses! 2. In the type of the Savior, indeed, Jeremiah suffers all these things from the people of the Jews, who were afterwards laid waste when the Babylonian came; but they are fulfilled more fully and more perfectly in Christ. And when the city was overthrown they were slaughtered by the Roman sword, not because of idolatry, which did not exist at that time, but because of the killing of the Son of God, when the whole people together cried out: Away, away with such a man; we have no king but Caesar! 3. And their imprecation has been fulfilled by everlasting damnation: His blood be upon us and upon our children! For they had dug a pit for Christ and had said: Let us take him away from the land of the living! — he who had such great mercy toward them, and stood in the sight of the Father to speak good for them and to turn away his indignation from them, that on the cross also he said: Father, forgive them! For they do not know what they are doing. 4. We pass quickly over what is plain, so as to linger on the more obscure passages, by no means following the ravings of certain people who interpret this even of a captivity of the heavenly Jerusalem, but pursuing the clear history and the most manifest prophecy with all confidence in the words and the senses.

X. For you will bring a robber upon them suddenly, because they have dug a pit to catch me and have hidden snares for my feet. But you, O Lord, know all their counsel against me unto death; do not be propitious to their iniquity, and let their sin not be blotted out from before your face; let them be falling — or stumbling — in your sight; in the time of your fury consume them or deal with them! 2. If we understand it of Jeremiah, let us refer the sudden robber to Nebuchadnezzar; if of the Savior, which is truer and better, to the Roman army. And lest the sentence of God should seem unjust, he sets out what they did against Christ, the Son of God, and what they suffered. 3. But what he adds: do not be propitious to their iniquity, and let their sin not be blotted out from before your face, is by no means contrary to the earlier sentence, in which he entreats the Father for the people; but after the time given for repentance has passed and they persevere in their crime, they are punished not so much for themselves as for others, lest a sin left unpunished should harm the rest by its example. 4. And what he adds: let them be stumbling — or falling — in your sight, is like that saying of Isaiah and of the apostle Peter: and you shall not stumble as on a stone of offense and a rock of scandal; which the prophet also mentions in the psalms: The stone which the builders rejected, this has become the head of the corner; this was done by the Lord.

Jeremiah 19

XI.

Thus says the Lord: Go and take a potter's earthen flask, and some of the elders of the people and of the elders of the priests, and go out to the valley of the son of Hinnom, which is by the entrance of the earthen gate — or Charsith —, and you shall preach — or you shall cry or you shall read — there the words which I shall speak to you, and you shall say: Hear the word of the Lord, O kings of Judah and inhabitants of Jerusalem! 2. For 'potter's flask', which in Hebrew is called 'bocboc', the Seventy translated 'little jar', and for 'earthen gate' Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion put the Hebrew word itself, 'Harsith', for which the Seventy, according to their custom, added the Greek 'chi' for the aspiration of the letter 'heth', so as to say 'Charsith' for 'Harsith', just as there is 'Chebron' for 'Hebron', and 'Hiericho' for 'Hieriho'. 3. Now divine Scripture wishes the people to be taught not only by the ears but also by the eyes; for, as we have already said, what reaches the mind by sight is retained in the mind more than what reaches it by hearing. Take, he says, a flask — or an earthen little jar — and some of the elders of the people and of the priests, and go out to the valley of the son of Hinnom, of which we spoke above, where there was the shrine of Baal and the grove and wood watered by the springs of Siloam. 'And this valley,' he says, 'is by the gate' which in the Hebrew tongue is called 'Harsith', that is, 'earthen'. 4. 'And you shall preach — or you shall read — there the words which I shall speak with you, so that what I am about to say may be heard there.' And the reason why, as we have already said, 'you shall preach' and 'you shall cry' and 'you shall read' are all set down, is that the Hebrew word 'carath' signifies three things. And he wishes both the kings of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem, that is, both the royal stock and the whole people, to hear what he is about to say, so that those who refuse to hear may be without excuse.

XII. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Behold, I will bring affliction — or evils — upon this place, so that the ears of everyone who hears of it — or of them — shall tingle, because they have forsaken me and have made this place strange and have poured libations in it to strange gods, whom neither they nor their fathers nor the kings of Judah knew; and they have filled this place with the blood of the innocent and have built the high places of the Baals to burn their sons with fire as a burnt offering to the Baals, which I did not command — or think of — nor speak, nor did they come up into my heart. 2. And some foolishly refer this place to the heavenly Jerusalem. So that I need not always give warning of this, let it suffice to have said this once: that an explanation of this kind, nay rather heresy, is to be avoided, which overturns what is plain and tries to bring certain conjuring tricks into the churches of Christ. But no one doubts that they set up the idol of Baal in the temple of God, or else in the shrine which was in the valley of the son of Hinnom, where there were both the grove of Baal and his altar, upon which they sacrificed and burned their sons. 3. These things the Lord neither thought of nor spoke, nor did they come up into his heart; not that God was ignorant of things to come, but that he says he does not know what is unworthy of his knowledge, according to that word of the Gospel: Depart from me, workers of iniquity, I do not know you! For the Lord knows those who are his, and he who does not know shall not be known. Or certainly these things too, like others, are to be taken of God anthrōpopathōs [in human terms]. 4. Now every heretic forsakes God and makes the place strange to God's habitation, the place which he has polluted by his deceit, and pours libations to strange gods, whom neither he himself nor his fathers knew, namely the apostles and apostolic men; and the kings of Judah, that is, the patriarchs of the heretics, fill the place that was once God's with the blood of the deceived and the innocent — for unless a man is foolish and simple, he is not quickly overturned — and they build the high places of the Baals when they claim to be disputing about sublime things, and they burn to the idol their sons, whom they have begotten in heresy. All of which the Lord says he does not know, and that it never came up into his thought.

XIII. Therefore, behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and this place shall no longer be called Topheth and the valley of the son of Hinnom, but the valley of slaughter. LXX: Therefore, behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and this place shall no longer be called ruin and polyandrion of the son of Hinnom, but polyandrion of slaughter. 2. What the valley of the son of Hinnom is, which in Hebrew is called 'gehennom', whence they think 'gehennam' [Gehenna] too was named, we have said above. But I wonder what the Seventy meant by putting diaptōsin, that is, 'ruin', for 'Topheth', and polyandrion, which signifies 'a multitude of men', for 'valley', unless perhaps because there the people fell and a multitude of men was slain, either spiritually in the error of idolatry or, according to the letter, by the Babylonian army, as is read more plainly in what follows.

XIV. And I will scatter the counsel of Judah and Jerusalem in this place, and I will overthrow them by the sword before their enemies and by the hand of those who seek their lives, and I will give their corpses as food to the birds of the heaven and to the beasts of the earth, and I will make this city an astonishment and a hissing; everyone who passes by it shall be astonished and shall hiss over all its wounds. 2. And I will feed them with the flesh of their sons and with the flesh of their daughters, and each one shall eat the flesh of his friend in the siege and in the distress in which their enemies, and those who seek their lives, shall shut them in. 3. Although we know that these things also befell the people in the Babylonian captivity, yet they refer more fully to the times of the Savior, when they were besieged by Vespasian and Titus and their city in the times of Hadrian collapsed into everlasting ashes, so that those who had offered their sons to idols should themselves afterwards, driven by the necessity of famine, turn them into use as food, and the flesh of all should be given to the birds of the heaven and to the beasts of the earth, so that those who had abused God's gifts for impiety and had sacrificed their own offspring to idols should make their bellies the tombs of their children.

XV. And you shall break the flask before the eyes of the men who will go — or will go out — with you, and you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord of hosts: So will I break this people and this city, as a potter's vessel is broken, which cannot be restored any more. 2. This is plainly said not of the Babylonian but of the Roman captivity; for after the Babylonians both the city was restored and the people was brought back into Judea and restored to its former abundance. But after the captivity which happened under Vespasian and Titus, and afterwards under Hadrian, the ruins of Jerusalem will remain until the consummation of the age, although the Jews think that a golden and jeweled Jerusalem is to be restored to them, and again victims and sacrifices and the marriages of the saints and the kingdom of the Lord the Savior on earth. 3. Although we do not follow these things, yet we cannot condemn them, because many churchmen and martyrs have said them, so that each one may abound in his own sense and all things may be reserved to the judgment of the Lord. 4. And just as an earthen vessel of clay, once it has been broken, cannot be re-formed into its old appearance, so too the people of the Jews and Jerusalem, overthrown, will not have their former state. Indeed, the very name of the city today is void, and it is called Aelia after Aelius Hadrian, and together with its former habitation it has also lost its former name, to strike down the pride of its inhabitants. 5. And the names of the holy Cross and of the Resurrection signify not a city but a place, and not the greatness of the riches of old, through which the people of the Jews perished, but the glory of holiness, which our poor little Bethlehem possesses, having not gold and jewels, but the bread which was born in it.

XVI. And they shall be buried — or they shall bury — in Topheth, because there is no other place for burying. So will I do to this place, says the Lord, and to its inhabitants, that I will make this city like Topheth — or like ruin —. And the houses of Jerusalem and the houses of the kings of Judah shall be like the place Topheth or of ruin. But what is added in the Seventy: all the houses of the kings of Judah like the place Topheth, is not found in the Hebrew. And there follows:

XVII. Unclean are all the houses on whose housetops — or roofs — they have sacrificed to all the host of heaven and have poured out libations to strange gods. What he had said above: this place shall no longer be called Topheth and the valley of the son of Hinnom, but the valley of slaughter, he now sets out more plainly: that so great a slaughter will take place in that very place that the people will be buried there in heaps and the place once of religion will become a tomb of the dead; that the city itself too, which overhangs this place, will become like Topheth — for which the Seventy translated 'ruin' —; and that the houses of Jerusalem also and the palaces of the kings will be turned into similar ruins. 2. And the reason is given: because they were unclean and polluted by the crime of idolatry, in that on their housetops and roofs they sacrificed to the sun and the moon and the stars of heaven and burned incense, and were not content with this error, but sacrificed to demons and poured out libations to strange gods.

XVIII. Then Jeremiah came from Topheth, where the Lord had sent him to prophesy, and he stood in the court of the house of the Lord and said to all the people: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Behold, I will bring upon this city and upon all its towns all the evils which I have spoken against it, because they have hardened their neck so as not to hear my words. 2. After Jeremiah, in Topheth, in the presence of the elders of the people whom he had brought with him, broke the flask, or jar, and prophesied with the words of the Lord the breaking of the people and of the city of Jerusalem, he returns and stands in the court of the house of the Lord and speaks to the whole multitude, which had refused to go to the place Topheth, that the Lord is about to bring upon the city of Jerusalem and upon all its towns the evils which he has spoken against it. 3. And lest we think the sentence cruel, he gives the reasons why he is about to bring evils: 'because,' he says, 'they have hardened their neck so as not to hear my words, not willing to repent even after many impieties.'

Jeremiah 20

XIX.

And Pashhur son of Immer, the priest, who had been appointed prince in the house of the Lord, heard Jeremiah prophesying these words. And Pashhur struck Jeremiah the prophet and put him in the stocks that were in the upper gate of Benjamin in the house of the Lord. 2. For 'Phassur' the Seventy translated 'Phaschor', which means 'blackness of mouth'; and for the 'stocks' which we have given, the Seventy and Theodotion translated 'cataracten' [a trapdoor], Symmachus basanistērion or streblōtērion, both of which signify 'tortures'; we, however, said 'neruum' [stocks] according to common usage, a kind of torture that we also read of in the Acts of the Apostles, when the apostles Paul and Silas were given into the custody of the prison. 3. Now this man was the high priest of the temple, and, the dignity of the priesthood having been given to him, he abuses it perversely, not to teach and to correct by word, but to terrify by torments. Hence both the Savior and the apostle Paul are beaten at the command of the high priest. Nor is it any wonder if even today the servants of God are beaten by Phaschor, are sent into prison, are kept in dreadful custody; for this power is given by God, so that the faith of the prophets may be shown. Yet he who beats is not the greater, but he who is beaten is the stronger. And the prophet patiently accepts the judgment of God and does not cry out against the blows, but considers the one who commands them. 4. 'Emmer' means 'word', from which 'blackness' is often begotten, not by the fault of the parent, but by the wickedness of the one who degenerates. And the 'torture' by which the prophet is tormented is seen in the 'right' part, which is what 'Beniamin' means, and in the 'lofty' gate, which indicates not truth but the unjust power of the high priest.

XX. And when it had dawned on the morrow, Pashhur brought Jeremiah out of the stocks, and Jeremiah said to him: Not Pashhur has the Lord called your name, but terror on every side. Here too all have likewise translated both the name of the high priest and the kind of torture as above. And the name of the high priest is changed so that from the name the punishment of things to come may be shown. 2. 'By no means,' he says, 'will you have blackness of mouth and the rule of unjust power, but you will be led captive to Babylon — for this is what 'terror on every side' or 'round about' signifies —, 'so that, trembling and uncertain of your own safety, you may look around this way and that and dread the adversaries coming against you.' For 'terror', which in the Hebrew is written 'magur', the Seventy and Theodotion translated metoikon, that is 'migrant'; Aquila's second edition 'stranger', the first 'looking around'; Symmachus 'carried off' or 'gathered' and 'compelled'.

XXI. For thus says the Lord: Behold, I will give you into terror, and all your friends, and they shall fall by the sword of their enemies, and your eyes shall see it; and all Judah I will give into the hand of the king of Babylon, and he shall carry them over into Babylon and shall strike them with the sword. 2. And I will give all the substance — or strength — of this city, and all its labor, and every price — or glory — and all the treasures of the kings of Judah I will give into the hand of their enemies, and they shall plunder them and take them and lead them into Babylon. But you, Pashhur, and all the inhabitants of your house shall go into captivity, and you shall come into Babylon, and there you shall die and there you shall be buried, and all your friends, to whom you prophesied a lie. 3. According to the earlier interpretation, by which the name 'Phassur' had been changed so that he should be called 'Magur', all likewise translated so as to signify either 'terror' or 'sojourning' or 'removal' and 'transfer' and 'migration'. 4. And he threatens that he, with his friends, is now at any moment to be taken and handed over into the hands of the enemy; and that the whole people of the tribe of Judah is to be held in the hands of the Babylonian king, some to be killed by the sword and others to be led into captivity; and that all the riches, both of the city and of the royal treasuries, are to be seized by the enemies; and that Pashhur himself, with all his kindred and household, is to be led into captivity and will die in Babylon, because he deceived the people by his lie, promising them by no means things true and sad, but prosperous things through a lie. 5. At the same time the patience and the prudence of the prophet must be noted: sent into prison, he is silent and conquers the injury by silence, and yet he does not conceal what he knows is to come, so that at least in this way the false-prophet high priest may cease to sin and may entreat the mercy of God.

XXII. You have seduced me, O Lord, and I was seduced — or you have deceived me, O Lord, and I was deceived —; you were stronger than I and you prevailed — or you held fast and you were able —; I have become a derision all the day, all mock me, for long since I speak crying out iniquity, and I keep proclaiming devastation or for with my bitter word I shall laugh, I shall call upon transgression and misery. 2. The prophet says that he was deceived by the Lord because, hearing at the beginning: I have given you as a prophet to the nations, and again: behold, I have set you this day over nations and over kingdoms, to uproot and to destroy and to ruin and to scatter and to build and to plant, he supposed that he would say nothing against the people of the Jews, but against the various nations round about, and therefore he willingly took up prophecy; and that the contrary came about, so that, preaching the captivity of Jerusalem, he endured persecutions and straits. 3. And as for what he added: I have become a derision all the day, all mock me: it is because they think that he lied about everything, and that all the things he foretold were to come were lies; for the prophet had thought that what the Lord threatened would come at once, and the people supposed that what had not come at once would never come at all. 'And I cry out the Babylonian devastation and the iniquity of the enemies, by which my people is to be oppressed.' 4. But if we follow the Seventy in what they said: for with my bitter word I shall laugh, I shall call upon transgression and misery, this is the sense: 'I know that the present sadness is to be exchanged for future joy, according to what is written: blessed are those who weep, for they shall laugh; and therefore I willingly endure misery and iniquity and affliction, so that I even desire them and call upon them, and make up for the brevity of the injury with the eternity of happiness'.

XXIII. For the word of the Lord has become to me a reproach and a derision all the day. And I said: I will not remember him — or I will not name the Lord — nor will I speak any more in his name. And there came to be in my heart as it were a burning fire, shut up in my bones, and I fainted — or I was dissolved — not enduring to bear it; for I heard the insults — or the reproach — of many, and terror round about — or of those gathered from every side —: Persecute, and let us persecute him! 2. 'When I cried out and said that the Babylonian army was already coming and that the sword of the enemy would plunder everything, the word of the Lord was turned for me into a reproach and a derision, since they think the delay of the prophecy is a lie; for which reason I resolved in my mind that I would no longer speak words to the people of God nor name his name. 3. When, overcome by shame, I had resolved this modestly indeed, but foolishly, there came to be', he says, 'in my heart as it were a burning fire, shut up in my bones, and I fainted or I was dissolved on every side, so that I cannot bear it.' 4. For the divine word, conceived in the mind and not brought forth by the mouth, burns in the breast; hence Paul too says: if I preach the Gospel, there is no glory for me; for necessity lies upon me; for woe is me if I do not preach the Gospel. For if I do this willingly, I have a reward; but if unwillingly, a stewardship has been entrusted to me. And at Athens, seeing the city given over to idolatry, he was stirred in spirit and burned with his whole mind; and a little later in the same book we read: and when Silas and Timothy had come from Macedonia, Paul was pressing on with the word, testifying to the Jews that Jesus is the Christ. 5. But many of the teachers in the Church even today endure similar things, hearing the reproach of many gathered round about against them and who say: Persecute, and let us persecute them!

XXIV. All the men who were peaceable toward me and guarding my side: if somehow he may be deceived, and we may prevail against him and take our revenge on him! But the Lord is with me as a mighty warrior. When adversaries have risen up, and those who were once friends and peaceable have turned to war and have wished to lay snares for us, let us not be greatly concerned, but let us choose to say this, which the prophet too speaks: But the Lord is with me as a mighty warrior.

XXV. Therefore those who persecute me shall fall and shall be weak — or therefore they persecuted and could not understand —, they shall be greatly confounded, because they did not understand the reproach or the everlasting ignominy, which will never be blotted out. Because they persecuted, therefore they could not understand the prophetic word, and vehement confusion followed upon their ignorance; nor did they understand the everlasting ignominy which will hold them fast and will be blotted out by no forgetting. 2. Let them say, then, what they will, the men once peaceable and those who guarded my side, and let them desire to deceive, provided that so great a vindication and such great rewards attend the righteous man and teacher of the Church after persecution.

XXVI. And you, Lord of hosts, tester of the righteous — or who test righteous things —, who see the reins and the heart, let me see, I pray, your vengeance on them! for to you I have revealed my cause. The Lord alone is the one who knows how to test righteousness, just as he alone is the one who beholds the inward parts of the heart; hence Jesus too, knowing the thoughts of men, is God not by advancement, as some think, but by nature. Something of the kind sounds in the Psalms as well: no one living shall be justified in your sight. If one who lives in virtues is not justified, how much more one who is dead in sins! 2. And though the righteous man knows that he has God as his champion, yet through the impatience of human frailty he desires to see even now what he knows is to come. And he revealed his cause to him who says in another place: vengeance is mine, I will repay, says the Lord. Happy, then, the conscience whose cause is revealed to the Lord, as the Apostle says: everything that is made manifest is light.

XXVII. Sing to the Lord, praise the Lord, for he has delivered the soul of the poor man from the hand of the most wicked! He who is poor in spirit and has not the riches which have received their consolation in this world, and of which Paul speaks: only that we should be mindful of the poor, when he has obtained vengeance from the Lord, praises the Lord in spirit and glories that he has been rescued from the hand of the most wicked. Yet all this comes about not by our merit, but by the grace of him who delivered the poor man; and he has not the riches of a pride that falls to ruin, but the humility of a poor man set free.

XXVIII. Cursed be the day on which I was born; the day on which my mother bore me, let it not be blessed! Cursed be the man — or the person —, who brought the news to my father, saying: A son, a male child, is born to you, and as it were made him glad with joy! Let that person be as the cities are which the Lord overthrew and did not repent! Let him hear a cry in the morning and wailing at the time of noon, who — or because he — did not kill me from the womb, so that my mother might become my grave, and her womb an everlasting conception! Why did I come forth from the womb, to see labor and sorrow, and that my days should be consumed in confusion? 2. Those who think that souls were in the heavenly places and were cast down from a better state into a worse use this and testimonies of this kind, namely that it would have been better to dwell in the heavenly places than to dwell in the earthly ones and take on the body of humiliation, seeking certain new, nay rather already old, arguments for their heresy. 3. We, however, reading that saying of blessed Job: cursed be the day on which I was born, and that night in which they said: behold, a male, and: cursed be the person who brought the news to my father, saying: a boy is born to you, fit them to this testimony, namely that it is better not to be than to live in torments, according to what is written: death is rest to a man for whom God has shut his way, and again: why is light given to the wretched, and life to those who are in bitterness of soul? 4. And in the Gospel we read it said simply: it would have been better for him if he had not been born, not that one who has not been born exists, but that it is better not to be than to exist wretchedly; for it is one thing not to be at all, another, while one exists, to be tormented without any intermission, just as we prefer a quiet death to a miserable life. Hence Amos too calls the day a day of darkness, a day of affliction; and Jacob, because he lived in toil and distress, calls the days of his life few and evil; and the apostle Paul says: that he might rescue us from the present evil age, and again: redeeming the time, because the days are evil. 5. The Hebrews reckon the fifth month, in which Jerusalem was taken and the temple overthrown, as that of Jeremiah's birth, by certain inextricable and incredible arguments. But if they were able to prove this, how they could interpret the testimony of Job I do not know; unless perhaps they claim that that day too, by a certain prefiguration and prophecy of things to come, belonged to the overthrow of the temple. 6. And as for his imprecating the likeness of the overthrown cities, I think it is said of Sodom and Gomorrah; and all time in mourning, so that in the morning there should be a cry and at noon wailing. And as for what he adds: who did not kill me in the womb, they think that God is meant. 7. 'That there might be for me,' he says, 'an everlasting conception'; all of which is said hyperbolically. Finally he sets out the reasons why he prefers death to life, and not being at all to existing wretchedly, adding: why did I come forth from the womb, to see labor and sorrow, and that my days should be consumed in confusion?

Jeremiah 21

XXIX.

The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord, when King Zedekiah sent to him Pashhur son of Malchiah, and Zephaniah son of Maaseiah, the priest, saying: Inquire of the Lord for us, for Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, is fighting against us; if perhaps the Lord will do for us according to all his wonders, and he will withdraw from us. 2. Above, the high priest Phassur or Phaschor, who struck Jeremiah, had Immer as his father, but this Phassur is the son of Malchiah; this is said so that no one may think him the same man. Now King Zedekiah sends to the prophet without wishing either the people or the princes to know, so that by a secret message through envoys he may learn what sentence the Lord holds concerning the city of Jerusalem and the people of the Jews. And what he adds: for Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, is fighting against us, shows that Zedekiah asks this of the prophet when Jerusalem was already besieged. 3. And it must be noted that in the prophets, and above all in Ezekiel and Jeremiah, the order of kings and of times is by no means kept, but, in reverse order, what according to the history happened later is related first, and what was done first is related later; for it is one thing to write history, another to write prophecy. Thus in the present place Zedekiah, who was captured with the city of Jerusalem, is written of as sending to Jeremiah, and that at the time when Jerusalem was being besieged; and afterward is narrated the history of Jehoiakim his brother, who was king before him, and of Jehoiachin, that is, Jeconiah, who was the son of Jehoiakim, concerning whom we shall speak in what follows.

XXX. And Jeremiah said to them: Thus shall you say to Zedekiah: Thus says the Lord God of Israel: Behold, I will turn back the vessels of war — or the weapons of war —, which are in your hands and with which you fight against the king of Babylon and the Chaldeans, who besiege you round about the walls, and I will gather those things — or those men — into the midst of this city, and I will vanquish you with an outstretched hand and with a strong arm — or a lofty one — and in fury and in indignation and in great wrath, and I will strike the inhabitants of this city; men and beasts shall die of a great pestilence. 2. 'In vain', he says, 'do you wish to resist the besieging Chaldeans and prepare weapons of war, of which you will have the use only in the midst of the city, so that you may seem to be armed. For the rest, while they besiege you, I will vanquish you with an outstretched hand and with a strong arm and will strike you with my fury and indignation, so that both you and everything that breathes may die of famine and pestilence in the midst of the city.' And this indeed we read was done; for without any ring of assault or din of combatants the city was taken by siege alone, so that they had none to conquer, but only those to capture; better off, he says, were those wounded by the sword than those slain by famine.

XXXI. And after this, says the Lord, I will give Zedekiah, king of Judah, and his servants and his people, and those who have been left in this city from the plague and the sword and the famine, into the hand of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, and into the hand of their enemies, and into the hand of those who seek their life; and he shall strike them with the edge of the sword, and he shall not be moved, nor spare, nor have mercy. 2. This, which we translated: into the hand of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, and into the hand of their enemies, is not found in the Seventy; and for what we said: he shall not be moved, nor spare, nor have mercy, the Seventy translated: I will not spare, nor have mercy. 3. And it is better according to the Hebrew, so that the cruel and inflexible sentence may seem to belong to the Babylonian king rather than to the Lord. First, then, prophecy was made concerning the whole city; now it is foretold particularly to Zedekiah and to his people, those who would be left after the plague, the sword and the famine, that he is to be captured by Nebuchadnezzar, the Babylonian king, and to be slain with his friends by the sword, and that he should hope for no mercy from him whose covenant and bond he had abandoned by perjury.

XXXII. And to this people you shall say: Thus says the Lord: Behold, I set before you the way of life and the way of death. He who dwells in this city shall die by the sword and by famine and by plague; but he who goes out and deserts to the Chaldeans, who besiege you, shall live, and his soul shall be to him as a spoil; for I have set my face against this city for evil and not for good, says the Lord; into the hand of the king of Babylon it shall be given, and he shall burn it with fire. 2. To those who had been sent by the king and had come to beseech the prophet to inquire of the Lord for them, he had given the answer above, and what they should report to King Zedekiah; now he urges them to answer the people, and gives counsel that they desert to the Chaldeans against the royal will; and no one is unaware that this was dangerous for the prophet. 3. Hence he even calls down death upon himself and says: cursed be the day on which I was born, and: why did I come forth from the womb, to see labor and sorrow?; not that it is a light thing to counsel those not yet captured to hand themselves over to captivity of their own accord — as if men about to suffer shipwreck were ordered, before the shipwreck and the breaking up of the ship, to seize oars and planks and commit themselves to the waves — but that it is more tolerable to live as captives in whatever way than to be consumed by the sword, famine and plague. 4. There are those who expound this passage according to tropology thus: that it is better to give oneself to secular studies, and above all to philosophy, than to remain in that church in which there is a famine of the word of God and the whole people dies by the sword of the heretics, by the dearth of doctrine and by the heretical plague.

XXXIII. And to the house of the king of Judah: Hear the word of the Lord, house of David! Thus says the Lord: Judge judgment in the morning, and rescue the one oppressed by violence from the hand of the one falsely accusing, lest perhaps my indignation go forth — or so that my indignation may not go forth — like fire, and be kindled, and there be none to quench it; and what follows: because of the wickedness of your pursuits, is not found in the Seventy. 2. Because he had said above: and to this people you shall say: thus says the Lord, he now accordingly adds: and to the house of the king of Judah, so that there is understood: 'you shall say: thus says the Lord'; for by apo koinou [in common, i.e. shared by both clauses] both what precedes and what follows are joined, so that the sense is: 'and to this people you shall say: thus says the Lord, and to the house of the king of Judah you shall say: thus says the Lord'. Properly, however, the word is addressed to the royal house, chiefly on account of whose guilt the city is besieged, so that it may amend its error by repentance and obtain the mercy of the Lord. 3. 'Judge', he says, 'judgment in the morning' — not in the darkness of iniquity, but in the light of righteousness — 'and rescue the one oppressed by violence from the hand of the false accuser, so that you do not respect persons in judgment, but, when the rich oppress the poor, the command of God may prevail more with you than the power of the persecutor! And if', he says, 'you do this, the fire of my fury will by no means be kindled among you, nor will it find material to consume.' 4. And in this place an incredible mercy of God is shown: those of whom he had said above: for I have set my face against this city for evil and not for good; into the hand of the king of Babylon it shall be handed over, and fire shall burn it, he now, while the Lord's sentence is already bearing down upon them, calls to salvation; not that he does not know that the city of Jerusalem is to be captured, but so that free will may be preserved for man, and they may be seen to perish not through ignorance of the future but by their own will; 5. just as the Savior too knew that the people would deny him and that he was to be crucified, inasmuch as he had often foretold this to the apostles, and nevertheless he admonished them, wishing to correct them toward repentance, so that whatever they afterward suffered befell them by their own fault, not by the harshness of the one who threatened.

XXXIV. Behold, I am against you, inhabitant of the solid and level valley, says the Lord, you who say: Who shall strike — or terrify — us, and who shall enter our houses? And I will visit upon you; and what follows: according to the fruit of your pursuits, says the Lord, is not found in the Seventy. And I will kindle, he says, a fire in its forest, and it shall devour everything round about it. 2. For 'inhabitant of the solid and level valley' the Seventy translated: behold, I am against you who dwell in the level valley of Sor, for which Symmachus translated 'rock', Theodotion 'besieged', Aquila's first edition 'solid', the second 'Tyre'; for 'Sor' or 'Sur' in the Hebrew language means 'Tyre' and 'flint' and 'narrowed'. 3. Now he speaks against Jerusalem, which was walled in by siege; or which, in the likeness of Tyre, is so surrounded by the Babylonian army, as by a great sea, that it cannot escape; or else which, like the hardest rock, thinks itself impregnable and strong because of the solidity and size of its walls, and says: 'who will be able to terrify us and who shall enter our houses?', whereas God, on the contrary, says: 'I will visit you; you will not be able to escape my eye. 4. And I will visit you unto destruction and render to you the fruit of your wickedness, and I will kindle a fire in your forest: not the Babylonian, as you think, not the king of the Chaldeans, but my wrath will accomplish all these things.' And he calls Jerusalem and all the region around it a 'forest', because, having no fruit-bearing trees of good works, it has been made ready for burning. And fittingly he calls the 'valley' 'level', because it lies open to the enemies, and not a 'lofty mountain', which can be climbed only with difficulty; in accordance with which it is also said in Isaiah: the vision of the valley of Zion. 5. Whatever is prophesied concerning the royal house and the mother city, let us refer to the ecclesiastical order and to the princes of the churches, those only, that is, who have given themselves over to pride and riches and wantonness. Nor will it be delivered from destruction at once simply because it is the royal house; just as among those who were of the stock of David very few were found who pleased the Lord, such as David himself, Hezekiah and Josiah, and a great part of the princes and of the royal stock provoked the wrath of the Lord against the whole people.

Jeremiah 22

XXXV.

Thus says the Lord: Go down into the house of the king of Judah, and there you shall speak this word and say: Hear the word of the Lord, O king of Judah, who sit upon the royal seat of David, you and your servants and your people, who enter through these gates! Thus says the Lord: Do judgment and righteousness, and deliver him who is oppressed by force from the hand of the slanderer, and do not grieve the stranger and the orphan and the widow, nor oppress them unjustly — or impiously — and do not shed innocent blood in this place! 2. For if you indeed do this word, there shall enter through the gates of this house kings of the race of David sitting upon his throne, and mounting chariots and horses, they and their servants and their people. But if you do not hear these words, by my own self I have sworn, says the Lord, that this house shall become a desolation. 3. This vision, nay rather this word of the Lord, comes to the prophet either before Zedekiah sent messengers to him, or certainly after he had instructed the messengers what they should report to the king; Jeremiah is commanded not to speak to the king through messengers at all, but to go himself to the palace and speak to him there. 4. And at the same time let us note the divine wisdom, in that through the messengers he orders sad tidings to be announced, mixed with prosperous ones too, should the king be willing to do repentance. Here, however, because he himself is commanded to go, he does not announce sad tidings and a captivity now on the very point of coming, but he advises what the king ought to do in order to escape the impending sentence of God. Now it is the proper work of kings to do judgment and righteousness, and to deliver from the hand of slanderers those oppressed by force, and to give help to the stranger and the orphan and the widow, who are more easily oppressed by the powerful. 5. And to instill in them a greater care for the precepts of God, he added: 'Do not grieve, so that you not only rescue them, but do not even allow them, through your connivance, to be grieved by others, and do not shed innocent blood in this place!' For to punish murderers and the sacrilegious and poisoners is not the shedding of blood, but the ministry of the laws. 6. 'If', he says, 'you do these things, O kings of Judah, you will hold your former power and will enter the gates of Jerusalem with pomp; but if you are unwilling to do them, O royal house, it will be not so much the Lord's cruelty as your own will that the whole city is reduced to desolation.' 7. Whatever was said to the royal house, let the bishops and their associates, the presbyters and deacons, and the whole ecclesiastical order understand: that if they do what has been commanded, and among other things do not shed innocent blood by causing even the least to stumble and by wounding the consciences of individuals, they will retain the dignity handed down to them by the Lord; but if they are unwilling to do it and despise it, they themselves will reduce the Church to desolation. 8. Now those of the race of David enter through the gates of Jerusalem and sit upon the throne of him whose name means 'strong of hand', and they mount chariots and horses, when they rein in both their own disturbances and those of the people, and walk in the Church with composed step and with a choir of many virtues singing in harmony with them on every side. And that we may believe this to be true, he swears by himself, who, according to the Apostle, has no other greater by whom he could swear.

XXXVI. For thus says the Lord concerning the house — or to the house — of the king of Judah: You are Gilead to me, the head — or the beginning — of Lebanon; if I do not make you a desolation, cities uninhabitable. And I will sanctify against you a man who slays, and his weapons, and they shall cut down your choice cedars and hurl — or cast — into the fire. And many nations shall pass through this city, and each shall say to his neighbor: Why has the Lord done thus to this great city? And they shall answer: Because they forsook the covenant of the Lord their God and worshiped strange gods and served them. 2. Scripture records that Gilead, which the half-tribe of Manasseh possessed, lies beyond the Jordan; on this mountain Laban overtook Jacob as he fled, and the mountain, according to Genesis, received the name sōros, that is, 'mound of witness', because Jacob and Laban swore there, after stones had been gathered into a heap. 3. Now it is the head or beginning of Lebanon, a lofty mountain which is planted all over with cedars, according to that word of David as he sings: and the Lord will shake the cedars of Lebanon, and elsewhere: I saw the wicked man exalted and lifted up like the cedars of Lebanon; and in Zechariah we read: Open your gates, O Lebanon, and let fire devour your cedars! 4. Therefore in the present passage, since he was speaking to the royal house, he speaks by metaphoran [metaphor] to the temple, or to the house of the stock of Judah, because it is itself on a height, or because remedies for all sins were sought from the temple and the sanctuary; whence this same prophet also records: Is there no resin in Gilead, or is there no physician there? Why has the healing of the daughter of my people not gone up? 5.
