He threatens, therefore, the royal house and the city of Jerusalem and the temple, which he calls the head of Lebanon, that it is to be reduced to desolation together with all its cities, not by the power of the Babylonian king but by the command of the Lord, who says: I will sanctify against you one who slays. And Nebuchadnezzar and all his army are called 'holy' because he carries out the sentence of God. 6. 'And they shall cut down', he says, 'your choice cedars' — all the powerful men and princes of the city — 'and cast into the fire, so that the flame devouring all things may consume them. And when everything has been destroyed, many nations will pass through the city and the temple, which before they were not permitted to enter, and each will say to his neighbor, why the Lord has wrought so sudden and so great an overthrow upon a city so renowned and great. And those who are asked', he says, 'will answer and set forth the causes of the ruins, saying: because they forsook the covenant of the Lord their God and venerated idols in place of God.' 7. Let the royal house of our city, too, hear this, and its princes and cedars, which lift their crown up to the clouds and say in their pride: 'Who will not see us?', that they will quickly be consumed by the flame of the Lord if they are unwilling to submit to his precepts. But the sanctification of the slayer and of his weapons is one thing, that of the priests and of those who minister to the Lord another.

XXXVII. Do not weep for the dead, nor mourn over him! Lament with weeping him who goes out, for he shall not return again, nor see the land of his birth! For thus says the Lord to Shallum son of Josiah, king of Judah, who reigned in place of Josiah his father, who went out from this place: He shall not return here any more, but in the place to which I have carried him away, there he shall die, and he shall not see this land any more. 2. Josiah, a righteous king, had three sons, Jehoahaz and Jehoiakim and Zedekiah. The first of them Pharaoh Neco, king of Egypt, led captive into Egypt, and there he died; and he set up in his place as king his brother Eliakim, and changed his name to Jehoiakim. When he died, his son Jeconiah reigned, who was led into captivity by Nebuchadnezzar, the Babylonian king, together with his mother and the princes; in his place his uncle Zedekiah reigned, who, when Jerusalem was taken, was led into Babylon. 3. And so it is asked who this is who ought not to be wept for, and who is to be led into captivity and will never return, since three were taken and carried away. The Hebrews think that this fits all of them, that is, Jehoahaz and Jeconiah and Zedekiah, and that all the sons of Josiah are called 'Sellem' or 'Sellum', which means 'consummation' or 'completion', because the kingdom of Judah came to its end in them. To me, however, this seems to be said properly of Zedekiah, about whom the prophecy of both the present and the previous chapter is, in whom the kingdom of Judah truly came to its end, under whom the city also was taken, and who, it is written, was led into Babylon and died there. He is 'Sellum', that is, 'consummation' and 'completion', son of Josiah, king of Judah, who reigned in place of Josiah his father. 4. Jeconiah, on the other hand, was not the son but the grandson of Josiah, the son of Jehoiakim, so that from the beginning of the vision, when there sent to Jeremiah King Zedekiah Pashhur son of Malchiah, and Zephaniah son of Maaseiah, the priest, down to this chapter, we are to understand that everything is said to the king and about King Zedekiah.

XXXVIII. Woe to him who builds his house in unrighteousness and his upper rooms not in judgment, who oppresses his friend for nothing and does not pay him his wage; who says: I will build myself a broad house and spacious upper rooms, who opens windows for himself and makes ceilings of cedar and paints them with red ochre! 2. Will you reign, since you compare yourself to the cedar? Did not your father eat and drink, and do judgment and righteousness then, when it was well with him? He judged the cause of the poor and needy to his own good; was it not therefore, because he knew me?, says the Lord. But your eyes and heart are for avarice, and for shedding innocent blood, and for slander, and for the course of evil work. 3. LXX: O you who build your house not with righteousness and your upper rooms not in judgment! With him his neighbor works for nothing, and he will not pay him his wage. You have built yourself a little house, upper rooms open to the wind, set off with windows and raftered with cedar and daubed with red ochre. 4. Will you reign, because you contend against Achaz, your father? They shall not eat and they shall not drink; it was better for you to do judgment and good righteousness. They did not know, they did not judge judgment for the lowly, nor judgment for the poor. Is not this your not knowing me?, says the Lord. Behold, your eyes are not upright nor your heart good, but they are for your avarice and for shedding innocent blood, and for iniquity and murder, to do them. 5. I have set down both editions in full, so that both the Hebrew truth and the difficulty of the common edition may be more easily recognized. Now the word is against Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah, of whom we spoke above, whom Pharaoh Neco, king of Egypt, set up in place of Jehoahaz his brother, whom he led bound into Egypt. And we read, and the history both of Kings and of Chronicles relates, that Jehoiakim son of Josiah reigned eleven years in Jerusalem, and reigned cruelly, and proved impious, and afterwards died; and yet his burial is not related, although holy Scripture has this custom, that it reports of all the kings both that they died and that they were buried. This one, however, it properly relates to have died and not been buried; of which we shall speak later. 6. He laments, then, the aforesaid king, because he trusts in unrighteousness and thinks his royal dignity perpetual, and makes himself upper rooms, and oppresses his friends, and does not pay those who work for him their wage, and supposes the building of his palace to be everlasting. 7. 'Will you', says the divine word, 'be able to reign forever, since you desire to compare yourself to the lofty cedar, that is, to your father Josiah, a righteous king? Your father', he says, 'both ate and drank and enjoyed royal wealth; and yet he did not offend God in this, that he had riches, but pleased him in this, that he did judgment and righteousness, and therefore it was well with him, and will be, both in the present age and in the one to come. 8. He judged', he says, 'the cause of the poor and needy, and to the refreshment of those whom he heard, and properly to his own good. And all these things turned out prosperously for him because he knew me, says the Lord. But your eyes, O Jehoiakim, turn aside to avarice, and, so that you may shed innocent blood, to slander and to the course of evil work.' 9. But what sense the words have according to the Seventy I cannot understand; for while the rest agree with one another to some extent, as for what is added: Will you reign, since you contend with Achaz your father?, in place of which in the Hebrew is written 'araz', and this word means 'cedar', it is plain that it has no sense; and so too what follows: They shall not eat and they shall not drink, and the rest, which are so scattered and confused among themselves that without the truth of the Hebrew reading they have no intelligible meaning. 10. We can, however, take this passage according to anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense] against the heretics, who build for themselves a house not great and most broad with the abundance of the Church, but a little one. And they build not with righteousness and judgment, desiring to plunder what belongs to others; whence it is said: You have built yourself a little house, upper rooms open to the wind, which are carried about by every wind of doctrine, and set off with windows; for they have no lasting building and no solid firmness. 11. And raftered, he says, with cedar: they seem indeed to have the most beautiful rafters, but in the rains and the storm of persecutions they quickly rot and collapse. And daubed with red ochre: for they too promise the passion and the blood of the Lord, but they do not reign forever, because they contend with and provoke to anger 'araz', that is, 'cedar', their father. For every heretic is born in the Church, but is cast out of the Church, and contends and fights against his parent. 12. And as for what he adds: They shall not eat and they shall not drink, 'the body and blood of the Savior' is understood; and the rest that is like these. And he says that all their error comes down from this, that they have not known God and do not have upright eyes, but their heart is bent on avarice, to plunder what belongs to others and to shed the blood of those they have deceived; for this is to commit murder. Obscure matters must be discussed more fully.

XXXIX. Therefore thus says the Lord to Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah: They shall not lament for him: Woe, brother, and woe, sister! They shall not cry out together for him: Woe, lord, and woe, renowned one! But with the burial of an ass he shall be buried, rotted and cast out beyond the gates of Jerusalem. This, which we have set down from the Hebrew: They shall not lament for him: Woe, brother, and woe, sister, is not found in the Seventy. 2. And it is said properly against Jehoiakim, king of Judah, and the riddle which before seemed hidden is opened, and the ambiguity among the three brothers is resolved, so that the word is not about Jehoahaz nor about Zedekiah, but properly about Jehoiakim, who, the Hebrew history relates, was killed by raiding bands of the Chaldeans, of Syria, of the Ammonites and of the Moabites; whence also in Malachim [Kings] it is written that he died, and his burial is passed over in silence. In the book of Days we read that he was bound with chains and led into Babylon, and nothing further is reported of him. 3. And finely he says that he is to be buried with 'the burial of an ass', so as to signify in other words that he would be unburied, that is, torn apart by beasts and birds. For this is the burial of an ass.

XL. Go up to Lebanon and cry out, and in Bashan give your voice, and cry to those passing by, for all your lovers are crushed! I spoke to you; in your abundance you said: I will not hear! This is your way from your youth, that you have not heard my voice. The wind shall feed all your shepherds — or lovers — and your lovers — or friends — shall go into captivity; and then you shall be confounded and shall blush for all your wickedness. You who sit in Lebanon and nest in the cedars, how you groaned when pains came upon you like the pains of a woman in labor! 2. By metaphoran of Lebanon and Bashan, regions and mountains beyond the Jordan, the word is directed to Jerusalem, because she trusted in Egypt in vain; or to Jehoiakim himself, who at that time was reigning in Jerusalem and had been set up as king by the Egyptians, because he hopes in vain for the help of the Egyptians, and because they too are to be overcome by the Babylonian king and led into captivity. 3. And in saying: 'I spoke to you' — that is, God himself through the prophets — or: 'They spoke to you' — so that 'my prophets' is understood — 'and you in your abundance said: I will not hear', he convicts her of pride, and of having abused the greatness of her riches to despise God, and he relates that not only at this time but from the beginning, when she was led out of Egypt, she did not hear the voice of God; whence all her shepherds and princes too have been scattered hither and thither and have bowed their necks to the Babylonian captivity. 4. And in adding: You who sit in Lebanon and nest in the cedars, he taunts her arrogance, which had grown out of the abundance of all things, and says that like a woman in childbirth sudden pain and an unexpected captivity will come upon her. And where we said: cry to those passing by, and in the Hebrew is written 'meabarim', the Seventy and Theodotion translated 'across the sea', Symmachus 'from opposite', to signify that the voice of the prophet must reach from Jerusalem as far as Mount Lebanon and Bashan.

XLI. As I live, says the Lord: though Jeconiah son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, were a ring on my right hand, I will tear him — or you — from there, and I will give you into the hand of those who seek your life, and into the hand of those whose face you dread, and into the hand of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, — which is not found in the Seventy — and into the hand of the Chaldeans. And I will send you — or I will cast you — and your mother who bore you into a strange land, in which you were not born, and there you shall die; but to the land to which they lift up their soul, to return, there they shall not return. 2. He had said above: 'To the house of the king of Judah you shall speak these things', and then: Go down into the house of the king of Judah, and again: Thus says the Lord concerning the house of the king of Judah; and in reversed order, after he has spoken about Zedekiah, who was the last of the kings in Jerusalem, he returns to Jehoiakim his brother, who reigned before him. This prophecy completed, he now speaks to the son of Jehoiakim, the grandson of Josiah, the king of Jerusalem, Jeconiah, who is also called by another name Jehoiachin, who was taken by Nebuchadnezzar and led into Chaldea with his mother and the princes and the craftsmen and many nobles, and died there. 3. This, then, is what is said: 'If, as a ring does not leave the hand of the one who wears it and slips from the finger only with difficulty, Jeconiah were on my hand, yet I will tear him away and hand him over to the Babylonian king, and there he will die with his mother and all his companions, and he will no more see the land of Judea, which he longs for.' 4. Wretched Grunnius, who opened his mouth to slander holy men and taught his tongue a lie, translated into Latin one book of Sextus the Pythagorean, a thoroughly pagan man, and divided it into two volumes, and dared to publish it under the name of the holy martyr Xystus, bishop of the city of Rome; in these volumes there is no mention of Christ, none of the Holy Spirit, none of God the Father, none of the patriarchs and prophets and apostles; and with his usual rashness and madness he named this book 'The Ring', and it is read through many provinces, and above all by those who preach apatheian [freedom from passion] and sinlessness. 5. As, then, the Lord threatens that he will cast Jeconiah from his hand and finger like a ring, so I beseech the reader to throw away this wicked book, and, if he wishes, to read it as he reads the other books of the philosophers, not as a church volume. 6. In commentaries and expositions, whose custom it is to set down the differing opinions of interpreters, I am accustomed to insert language of this kind: 'Some say this, others assert that, certain ones think thus.' For this both wretched Grunnius himself and, many years later, the disciple of Jovinian and of him have slandered and still slander me, saying that I set down my own opinions under others' names, which I do for the sake of kindness, so as not to seem to tear anyone apart by name. 7. Since, then, kindness has been turned into slander, I now say both to him who is dead and to this one who lives and tries to restore his heresy, that their master Origen refers this passage to Christ: that he was torn like a ring from the hand of God the Father and sent into the land of captivity, into the valley of tears, and handed over to the cross; and that his mother too — no doubt the synagogue — was taken and cast out. 8. And in recalling these things he is not afraid to understand what follows also: Land, land, land, hear the word of the Lord! Thus says the Lord: write this man abominated or barren, and the rest, of the Lord of majesty. And he writes this, lest his disciples dare deny it, in the fifth book of the Stromateis.

XLII. Is this man Coniah an earthen vessel, and broken? Is he a vessel without any pleasure — or usefulness —? Why are he and his seed cast away and cast forth into a land which they did not know? For what we said: Is he an earthen vessel, and broken, Symmachus translated: Is he refuse or worthless sweepings, and cast away?; in place of which there is nothing in the Seventy, but they translated only this: Jeconiah has been dishonored like a vessel in which there is no usefulness. 2. And when this is said of Jeconiah, son of Jehoiakim, does anyone dare to refer it to the type of Christ, and, from the fact that the Apostle says that the Lord Savior is the image of the invisible God, the firstborn of all creation, that is, wisdom, word, truth, life and righteousness, to say that he is called the ring which is cast off or torn away from the hand of the Lord and handed over to King Nebuchadnezzar? 3. They are cast away, he says, he and his seed, and cast forth into a land which they did not know, which no one doubts happened to Jeconiah. 'Iechonias' means 'the preparation of the Lord'; in the present passage the first syllable, that is, the name of the Lord, is taken away from him, and he is called 'Chonias', so that 'prepared for perdition and destruction' is understood.

XLIII. Land, land, land, hear the speech of the Lord! Thus says the Lord: Write this man barren, a man who shall not prosper in his days; for there shall not be of his seed a man who sits upon the royal seat of David and has power any longer in Judah. 2. If I should wish to note, place by place, how much the Seventy have either passed over or changed, it would become long, especially since the diligent reader can consider from each edition what has been changed, what added, what taken away. For 'barren' in the Hebrew is written 'ariri', which Aquila's first edition translated 'barren', the second anauxēton, that is, 'not growing', Symmachus 'empty', the Seventy and Theodotion 'abominable' and 'disowned'. 3. And the question arises how the prophecy can stand that no one was begotten of his stock who sat upon the throne of David or was prince any longer in Judah, when our Lord and Savior was born of his seed, about whose birth Gabriel speaks to Mary: Behold, you will conceive in the womb and bear a son, and you will call his name Jesus. He will be great and will be called the Son of the Most High, and the Lord God will give him the seat of David his father, and he will reign over the house of Jacob forever, and of his kingdom there will be no end. 4. We can therefore say this: that what is lacking in the Seventy, namely in his days he shall not prosper or shall not grow, has created the problem for those who do not know. For the Seventy translated: Write this man a disowned man, for there shall not grow from his seed a man who sits upon the throne of David, a prince any longer in Judah, which in the Hebrew is put twice; and those who wrote from the beginning, thinking this had been added in the Greek books, took it away. 5. Let us answer, then, that in the days of Jeconiah no man succeeded him who sat upon his throne, but long afterward there was born of his seed one who obtained his royal seat. It can also be resolved thus: there will indeed not sit upon the throne of David a man and a human being, but God will sit, and his kingdom will not be earthly and brief, as David's was, but perpetual and heavenly, as Scripture says: He will reign over the house of Jacob forever, and of his kingdom there will be no end. 6. Of Jehoiakim, then, was born Jeconiah, of Jeconiah Shealtiel, of Shealtiel Zerubbabel, and so in order one comes to Christ. But in the days of Jehoiachin no king, his son, succeeded him, as he himself had succeeded his father; but he was in captivity, and neither Shealtiel nor Zerubbabel nor anyone down to Christ obtained royal power. And this is so because in the Hebrew is written: in his days, and in the very time in which he lived, not shall there be a man who sits upon the royal seat of David. For all were captives, and of the stock of David no one thereafter held rule in the land of Judea. 7. Whence Josephus too reports that princes of the priestly race and of the tribe of Levi ruled, whom Herod, son of Antipater the proselyte, succeeded, and that afterwards, under Vespasian, the kingdom of this race, nay rather the semblance of rule, was utterly destroyed.

Jeremiah 23

XLIV.

Woe to the shepherds who destroy and tear the flock of my pasture — or O shepherds, who destroy and scatter the sheep of my pasture —, says the Lord. Therefore thus says the Lord, the God of Israel, to the shepherds who feed my people: You have scattered my flock, you have driven them out and have not visited them; behold, I will visit upon you the wickedness of your pursuits, says the Lord. 2. And I will gather the remnant of my flock from all the lands to which I shall have driven them out there, and I will bring them back to their fields — or I will restore them to their pastures — and they shall grow and be multiplied. And I will raise up shepherds over them and they shall feed them; they shall fear no more and shall not be dismayed, and none shall be sought from the number, says the Lord. 3. The prophetic discourse is addressed to the shepherds, or concerns the shepherds; and since we read it written of Jeconiah, the next-to-last king of Judea, who was of the stock of David: O land, land, land, hear the words of the Lord! Write this man down as rejected — or barren — and there shall not be of his seed a man who shall sit upon the throne of David, and all hope of the Jewish kingdom had been cut off, the discourse passes over to the princes of the Church, and, the synagogue with its shepherds being abandoned and condemned, it is addressed to the apostles, of whom it is said: and I will raise up shepherds over them and they shall feed them; they shall fear no more and shall not be dismayed, and none shall be sought from the number, says the Lord. 4. For the apostles will feed the flock of the Church confidently and without any fear, and the remnant of the people of Israel will be saved from all the lands and will be brought back to their fields or to their pastures, and they will grow and be multiplied. But upon the evil shepherds, the scribes and Pharisees, the Lord will visit because of the wickedness of their pursuits. 5. We can also understand this according to tropology of the princes of the Church who nevertheless do not rule the sheep of the Lord worthily: that, these being cast off and condemned, the people may be saved, handed over to others who have proved worthy, and the remnant may be saved. Those shepherds destroy the sheep who teach heresy; those tear and scatter them who make schisms; those drive them out who separate them from the Church against righteousness; those do not visit them who withhold their hand from the penitent. On all these the Lord will have mercy, restoring to them their former pastures and taking away the evil shepherds.

XLV. Behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and I will raise up for David a righteous branch — or a righteous Rising — and a king shall reign and shall be wise — or shall understand — and shall do judgment and righteousness in the land. In those days Judah shall be saved and Israel shall dwell confidently, and this is the name by which they shall call him — or he shall call him — : the Lord our righteous one or our righteousness, which in Hebrew is said 'sadecenu'. 2. This, which has been wrongly added in the Seventy: in the prophets, must be cut away entirely; for another chapter follows, which has been passed over by them, and when it is finished the title is: to the prophets or against the prophets, of which we shall speak in its own place. 3. So, the shepherds of the synagogue having been cast off, namely the scribes and Pharisees, and the remnant out of Israel having been saved, and the apostles of the Gospel having been set in the place of the former princes, there is brought in the shepherd of shepherds and prince of princes and king of kings and lord of lords, namely Christ our Savior, who is properly the 'righteous branch' or 'righteous Rising', of whom we read: in his days righteousness shall rise and in another place: behold a man, the Rising is his name, and beneath him it shall rise, and he shall build a temple to the Lord, so that, as in Isaiah he is called 'Emmanuhel', that is, 'God with us', so in Jeremiah he receives the name 'our righteousness'; whence the Apostle too says: who was made for us wisdom from God, and righteousness and sanctification and redemption. Opposed to him, the Antichrist and the devil who dwells in him is called in Zechariah the 'foolish shepherd'. 4. And he shall do, he says, judgment and righteousness in the land; for the Father judges no one, but has given all judgment to the Son. In those days both the two and the ten tribes, Judah and Israel, shall be saved alike, and out of two rods, according to Ezekiel, one rod shall be made; and its name, if according to the Seventy the Lord shall have called him, shall be Iosedec, that is, the righteous Lord; if according to the Hebrew, who said: they shall call his name, it shall be said: the Lord our righteousness; for this is what adonai sadecenu means, for which Symmachus translated: O Lord, justify us!

XLVI. Therefore, behold, the days are coming, says the Lord, and they shall no longer say: The Lord lives, who brought the children of Israel out of the land of Egypt, but: The Lord lives, who brought out and brought back the seed of the house of Israel from the land of the north and from all the lands to which I had driven them out there, and they shall dwell in their own land. 2. This whole chapter is not found in the Seventy; and its sense is this: that the people of God is no longer to be freed from Egypt through Moses, but through Jesus Christ from the whole circle of the earth, into which it had been scattered. This is fulfilled in part even now in the world, and will be fulfilled wholly when from the east and the west, the north and the south they shall come and recline with Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, so that, after the fullness of the nations has come in, then all Israel may be saved.

XLVII. To the prophets or in the prophets or against the prophets. This title, as we said above, has been placed in the edition of the Seventy as the end of the preceding chapter, where we read it written: and this is the name which the Lord shall call him, Iosedec, in the prophets. Not knowing this, very many invent various ravings of explanation; and it would have been much better simply to confess ignorance than to make others heirs of their own unskilfulness. 2. The discourse, moreover, is against the prophets, nay rather the false prophets of Jerusalem and Samaria, whom he now calls by the common and popular name 'prophets', of whom it is written in what follows: and in the prophets of Samaria I saw iniquity and immediately: and in the prophets of Jerusalem I saw horrible things. But when he puts the conjunction 'and', he shows that the things above were said of the prophets of the Lord, who themselves too proved like the false prophets.

XLVIII. My heart is broken in the midst of me — or in me —, all my bones have trembled — or have been shaken —; I have become like a drunken man and like a man soaked — or overcome — with wine before the face of the Lord and before the face of his holy words or and before the face of the comeliness of his glory. 2. Having considered the countenance of almighty God, that is, of the Father, and having considered the countenance of the Son, who according to the Apostle is called the 'radiance of his glory' and the 'form of the substance of God', the prophet shudders in both mind and body and understands that he is nothing, according to what is said in another place too: I have become like a beast of burden before you; or else he offers to God the victim of his conscience and humility, according to that which is written in the psalms: a sacrifice to God is a troubled spirit; a contrite and humbled heart God does not despise. 3. But the bones which trembled or were shaken, let us understand to be those of which the same David sings: all my bones shall say: Lord, who is like you? He became as one drunk and like a man soaked — or overcome — with wine, having no understanding and no wisdom; for the Lord knows the thoughts of men, that they are vain. 4. But if this is so, where are those who preach perfect righteousness in man? And if they answer that they say this of the saints, not of themselves, certainly I think no one is holier than Jeremiah, who, a virgin, a prophet, and sanctified in the womb, by his very name prefigures the Lord Savior. For Jeremiah means 'the exalted one of the Lord'.

XLIX. Because the land is filled with adulterers, because before the face of cursing — or of the oath — the land has mourned, the fields — or pastures — of the desert have dried up; their course has become evil and their strength unlike. This, which we have set down from the Hebrew: because the land is filled with adulterers, is not found in the Seventy, who said 'oath' for 'cursing'. 2. And he gives the causes: that because of adulteries and curses, or superfluous swearing, nay rather perjuries, barrenness of the crops followed. Whatever you understand of the land of Judea according to the letter, refer to the congregation of believers, since because of adulteries and lies or perjuries there is in the churches a barrenness of the virtues and gifts of God.

L. For prophet and priest are polluted, and in my house I have found their evil, says the Lord. Therefore their way shall be like a slippery place in darkness; they shall be driven on and shall fall in it, for I will bring — or I will inflict — evils upon them, the year of their visitation, says the Lord. When evils are found in the Church of God, and especially in its princes, let us know that this has been fulfilled: prophet and priest are polluted; in my house I have found their evils, says the Lord. 2. Now the house of Christ is the Church, of which Paul too writes to Timothy: that you may know how you ought to conduct yourself in the house of God, which is the Church of the living God, the pillar and foundation of the truth. In the prophet understand the teacher, in the priest the dignity of the ministry; and if they have consented with a depraved mind, their way will be into a slippery place and into darkness, and they will not have the Lord saying: I have come as light into the world, that everyone who believes in me may not remain in darkness. Whence the holy man, fleeing all darkness, says: the light of your countenance, O Lord, has been signed upon us; you have given gladness in my heart. 3. But when they are in darkness and on the slippery way, that is, in heretical error, at every motion they will be driven on and will fall. And the Lord inflicts evils upon them: not that they are evils in such a way that the Lord inflicts evils, but they are evils to those who undergo the punishments; otherwise the same things are both evils and goods: evils with respect to those whom they torment, goods with respect to those whom they amend. 4. And this must be noted, that the year of the Lord's visitation is called the correction and torment of sinners, according to that which is written: I will visit their iniquities with the rod and their sins with stripes; but my mercy I will not scatter from him.

LI. And in the prophets of Samaria I saw folly — or iniquity —; they prophesied in Baal and deceived my people Israel. I think that by the prophets of Samaria, according to the mystical senses, the heretics are properly meant, and all who boast of a knowledge falsely so named. 2. And as the prophets of Samaria, whatever they spoke, prophesied in Baal, namely an idol consecrated to demons, so the heretics, whatever they speak inside the Church or outside the Church, speak in demons, in order to trip up the people 'Israhel', which formerly 'saw God'; whence too he says significantly: and in the prophets of Samaria I saw folly; for they do not have him of whom it is said: 'Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God'.

LII. And in the prophets of Jerusalem I saw a likeness — or horrible things —, adultery and the path of falsehood; and they strengthened the hands of the most wicked, so that no one turned back from his wickedness — or from his most wicked way —; they have all become to me as Sodom, and its inhabitants as Gomorrah. 2. 'Not only', he says, 'have these things been found in the conventicles of the heretics, but in the prophets of Jerusalem too, that is, in the teachers of the Church, I have seen like things, or horrible things: men adulterating the word of God and walking along the way of falsehood, so that they acquiesced in the deceits of the heretics and strengthened the hands of the most wicked and joined their evils to their own crimes, and led to destruction those whom they ought to have corrected. 3. Let those who have done this not think themselves unpunished! For both they themselves and those whom they favor will be as Sodom, and all who dwell with them and do not withdraw from such men, as Gomorrah'. Let the most wicked doctrine exult, then, as much as it will, and let the prophets of Jerusalem glory that they have prevailed through falsehood and strengthened the hands of the most wicked: their end will be as Sodom and Gomorrah.

LIII. Therefore thus says the Lord of hosts to the prophets: Behold, I will feed them with wormwood — or with pains, and according to Symmachus with bitterness — and I will give them gall to drink — or bitter water —, for from the prophets of Jerusalem pollution has gone forth into all the land. 2. Let us use this testimony against those who send out into the world letters full of lies and fraud and perjury, so as both to pollute the ears of the hearers and to injure the reputation of the simple: that what is written is fulfilled in them: from the prophets of Jerusalem pollution has gone forth into all the land; for it is not enough for them to devour their own iniquity and to injure their neighbors, but those whom they have once hated they try to defame throughout the whole world, and to sow blasphemies everywhere.

LIV. Thus says the Lord of hosts: Do not listen to the words of the prophets who prophesy to you and deceive you! They speak the vision of their own heart and not from the mouth of the Lord. They say to those who blaspheme me — or cast away my word — : The Lord has spoken: peace shall be to you; and to everyone who walks in the perversity of his heart they have said: Evils shall not come upon you. 2. Lest the people should think itself free of guilt if it acquiesced in perverse teachers, 'Do not', he says, 'listen to the words of the prophets who prophesy to you a lie and deceive you', — for the punishment will be equal for master and disciple — 'they do not speak from the mouth of the Lord, but the things they have feigned in their own heart. They say to those who blaspheme me' — namely to heretics and perverse men — 'or who cast away my speech, what do they say?: The Lord has spoken: peace shall be to you; do not fear harsh punishments nor be terrified by empty threats; peace shall be to you, and tranquillity, and whatever we say and announce to you, the Lord has spoken; nor shall evil come upon you, which you fear because of a bad conscience, but good, which the Lord has spoken to you.'

LV. For who has been present in the counsel of the Lord and seen and heard his speech? who has considered his word and heard? Where we translated 'in the counsel of the Lord' and in the Hebrew is written 'bassod', Aquila translated 'secret', Symmachus 'speech', the Seventy and Theodotion 'substance' or 'subsistence'. 2. And the sense is: 'Do not believe, O untaught crowd, the prophets who announce false things to you, who say this: The Lord has spoken: peace shall be to you; evil shall not come upon you! For whence can they know the secrets of God, or at whose announcing have they learned the counsel of the Lord? How has the word of the divine dispensation reached them?' Some of our own think that in this place they have found where the substance of God is written of.

LVI. Behold, the whirlwind — or tempest and commotion — of the Lord's indignation goes forth, and a tempest bursting forth shall come upon the head of the ungodly, and the fury of the Lord shall not turn back until he does and until he fulfills the thought of his heart; in the last days you shall understand his counsel. 2. He who had said above: 'It has been spoken to us by the Lord: peace shall be to you', and had rebuked them because they could not know the things to come nor know the sentence of God, now shows by contraries that they know nothing at all: for instead of peace and security a Babylonian tempest is coming, and coming not upon just anyone, but upon the heads of the ungodly, whether of the whole people or of those who announced false things to the people. Nor is the wrath and fury of God to be appeased as in times past, but what he foretold and very often threatened is to be fulfilled in deed, and his thought and sentence are to be proved by the punishments of the ungodly. 3. 'When', he says, 'the last time of the captivity has come, and the exulting victor has taken hold of you and bound your hands with the grating of chains, then you shall understand his counsel, which you now vainly boast that you know'.

LVII. I did not send the prophets, and they ran; I did not speak to them, and they prophesied. If they had stood in my counsel and made my words known to my people, I would surely have turned them from their evil way and from their most wicked thoughts. 2. This sense the Apostle too unfolds to the Romans: and as they did not see fit to have God in knowledge, God handed them over to a reprobate sense, so that they do the things that are not fitting, filled with all iniquity, malice, fornication, wickedness, avarice and the rest. For when false teachers have once handed themselves over to lies and perjuries and to the deaths of those they have deceived, they run, not step by step nor with considered pace, but headlong, to the destruction both of themselves and of those whom they have deceived. 3. Nor does the Lord speak to them, but they themselves speak as if from the mouth of the Lord, of whom it is also said: 'if they had stood in my counsel — that is: if they had been willing to acquiesce in my will — and had made my words known to my people, not flattering them and ruining them with adulation, so as to say: You have no sins, you possess perfect righteousness, holiness and chastity and righteousness are found in you alone; then I would not have handed them over to uncleanness and ignominy, to do the things that are not fitting and to follow their most wicked thoughts'. 4. Let us contemplate the heretics: how, once despairing of salvation, they hand themselves over to gluttony and delights, eat meats, frequent the baths, are fragrant with musk, and, anointed with various unguents, seek beauty of body. For once and for all they do not hope for the things to come, nor do they believe in the resurrection. What they do not disclose in speech, they show in deeds; for if they believed, they would not do these things. And in this place, where it is written: if they had stood in my counsel, Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion and the Seventy translated similarly, as above.

LVIII. Am I a God from near at hand, says the Lord, and not a God from afar? If a man is hidden in secret places, shall I not see him?, says the Lord; do I not fill heaven and earth?, says the Lord. LXX: I am a God drawing near, says the Lord, and not a God from afar. If a man shall hide in secret places, shall I not see him?, says the Lord; do I not fill heaven and earth?, says the Lord. 2. Aquila and Symmachus translated similarly: Am I a God from near — or nearby — and not a God from afar? But the Seventy and Theodotion translated in a contrary sense, so as to say: I am a God drawing near, says the Lord, and not a God from afar: namely, the former assert that God knows not only things nearby but also those which are far off, and sees not only present things but future ones; while these think that God is present everywhere and that there is no place where God is not present. For God draws near to all, and especially to the saints, as a garment clings to the skin; but sinners, withdrawing far from him, will perish. 3. This sense we read in the psalms too: Whither shall I go from your spirit, and whither shall I flee from your face? If I ascend into heaven, you are there; if I descend into hell, you are present; if I take my wings at dawn and dwell in the uttermost parts of the sea, even there your hand shall lead me and your right hand shall hold me. 4. Amos too agrees with these words, saying: if they go down even to hell, from there my hand shall bring them out, and if they go up even to heaven, from there I will drag them down; and if they are hidden on the summit of Carmel, searching I will take them away from there, and if they have hidden themselves from my eyes in the depth of the sea, there I will command the serpent and it shall bite them. And again in the psalm above: For darkness, he says, shall not be darkened to you, and night shall be lit up as day; as its darkness, so also its light. 5. And as for its being said frequently in the prophets: 'thus says the Lord', it is always added for this reason: that they should not be despised as words of the prophets, but that people should be continually reminded that the words they speak are God's.

LIX. I have heard what the prophets said who prophesy a lie in my name and say: I have dreamed, I have dreamed — or I have seen a dream —. How long is this in the heart of the prophets who prophesy a lie and prophesy the seductions — or the wishes — of their own heart? Who wish — or think — to make my people forget my name because of their dreams, which each one tells to his neighbor, as their fathers forgot my name because of Baal or in Baal. 2. Since the preceding title is against the prophets or to the prophets, whom we clearly understand to be false prophets — and there are many kinds of prophesying, one of which is by dreams, such as was in Daniel —, therefore the prophetic discourse is directed also at those who believe in dreams and think that all they see is divine revelation, which is properly opened to the saints and servants of God. And if we read that Pharaoh and Nebuchadnezzar, ungodly kings, saw dreams which were true, it was not of the merit of those who saw them, but so that on their occasion the holy men Joseph and Daniel might become renowned, and the hard and untamable hearts of the tyrants might feel the majesty of the Lord by their own conscience. 3. There are even today dreamers in the Church, and especially in our flock, who vaunt their errors as the prophecy of the Lord and frequently thrust upon us: I have dreamed, I have dreamed; whom the Lord rebukes, saying: 'How long is this in the heart of the prophets who prophesy a lie and prophesy the seductions of their own heart? 4. And they do this in order that, as the ancient people which went out of Egypt forgot the name of God, so their descendants too may forget me'. But prophesying of this kind is not in the name of the Lord, but in the name of Baal, which is properly the idol of the Sidonians, or the Babylonian one, and is called by a kindred name Bel.

LX. Let the prophet who has a dream tell the dream, and let him who has my word speak my word truly! what has chaff to do with wheat?, says the Lord; are not my words like fire, says the Lord, and like a hammer — or an axe — breaking the rock? It belongs to those to expound a dream who merit to have the word of God and to say: 'thus says the Lord', to whom the Lord has spoken, in whom there is truth and not fraudulent falsehood. 2. What has the chaff of the heretics to do with the wheat of the Church? Of this John the Baptist speaks more fully: that Christ will purge his threshing floor and with the winnowing fan leave the chaff to be scattered by the blasts of the winds and burned with fire, but will store the wheat in the barns, that it may become heavenly bread and each of the believers may say: taste and see that the Lord is sweet! And beautifully is perverse doctrine compared to chaff, which has no marrow and cannot nourish the peoples of believers, but is threshed out of empty stalks. 3. And because the heretics are always wont to promise prosperity and to open the kingdoms of heaven to sinners, so as to say: 'The kingdoms of heaven are prepared for you; you can imitate the majesty of God, so as to be without sin; for you have received the power of free will and the knowledge of the law, through which you may attain what you will', and they deceive the wretched with blandishments, and especially silly women laden with sins, who are carried about by every wind of doctrine, always learning and never coming to the knowledge of the truth, and they deceive all their hearers with adulation, therefore the Lord, comparing his own words with the chaff of the heretics, says: 'are not my words like fire, says the Lord, and like a hammer breaking the rock? 4. For my word', he says, 'announces future punishments, to deter men from sinning', and he threatens the chaff of sinners with burning, so that the hearts of the heretics, hard and untamable like flint, may be broken by the hammer of his word, as he takes away the heart of stone to put in its place a heart of flesh, namely a soft one, and one that can receive and feel the precepts of God. 5. Something of the kind the Lord says through Ezekiel too: that the false prophets with their blandishments daub a wall without mortar, which afterward is overthrown by a violent rain and by the truth of judgment, and the false prophetesses sew pillows under every elbow of the hand, to make sinners rest and never soften the wrath of God with tears. 6. For 'hammer' the Seventy translated 'axe', namely the one of which John the Baptist speaks: already the axe is laid at the roots of the trees. This axe cuts down the unfruitful trees and this hammer breaks the hardest stones. Whence too Nahum the prophet says: his fury — no doubt God's — shall consume principalities, and the rocks shall be broken by him. This against the heretics. But of churchmen it is written that hammer and axe were not heard in the house of God.

LXI. Therefore, behold, I am against the prophets, says the Lord, who steal my words each one from his neighbor; behold, I am against the prophets, says the Lord, who take up their tongues and say: He says — or they slumber a slumber — ; behold, I am against the prophets who dream a lie, says the Lord, who tell them and seduce my people in their lies and in their wonders — or astonishments and terrors — of their own, when I had not sent them nor commanded them, who have been of no profit to this people, says the Lord. 2. Falsehood always imitates truth, and unless it has some likeness of what is right, it cannot deceive the innocent. As, then, among the former people the prophets lied and said: 'thus says the Lord' and: 'I saw the Lord' and: 'the word of the Lord which came to this man or that', so the heretics take up testimonies of the Scriptures from the Old and New Instrument and steal the words of the Savior each one from his neighbor, from prophets and apostles and evangelists, and take up their tongues, to utter with the mouth the poisons of the heart, and slumber a slumber — of whom it was truly said: they have slumbered their sleep and found nothing — or, according to the Hebrew: and they say: He says, so that 'the Lord' is understood, or certainly 'the divine word'. 3. The Lord therefore threatens that he himself will come against masters of this kind, who seduce his people in their lies and in their astonishments and wonders. For they promise great and incredible and immense things in order to seduce the wretched; who have been of no profit to the people of God, and fulfill that apostolic word: teaching what they ought not, for the sake of base gain; who are wont to announce to the wicked prosperity, and the contrary.

LXII. If therefore this people or a prophet or a priest asks you, saying: What is the burden of the Lord — or what is the taking up of the Lord — ?, you shall say to them: You are the burden — or the taking up — ; for I will cast you away — or I will dash you down, — says the Lord. And the prophet and the priest and the people who says: The burden — or the taking up — of the Lord, I will visit — or I will avenge — upon that man and upon his house. 2. These things you shall say each one to his friend and to his brother: What has the Lord answered and what has the Lord spoken? and the burden — or the taking up — of the Lord shall be remembered no more, for the burden — or the taking up — shall be to each one his own word. 3. The Hebrew word 'massa' Aquila translates arma, that is, 'burden' and 'weight'; Symmachus, the Seventy and Theodotion 'taking up'. Wherever, then, what the Lord threatens is heavy and full of weight and toil and unbearable, in the title too it is called arma, that is, 'weight'; but wherever the Lord promises prosperity, or after a threat promises better things, there it is called 'vision' or certainly 'word of the Lord'; and it was clear from the very title of the prophecy, 'of weight' or 'of vision' and 'of the word of God', what kind of prophecy would follow. 4. Since, therefore, the prophets were wont to announce sad things to the sinful people and to threaten punishments, in order to draw them back to repentance, while the Lord, merciful and compassionate, long deferred the sentence, the people, deceived and seduced by the fraud of the false prophets, thought that the things the Lord threatened would not come, and they had turned a serious matter into sport and jest, and when the prophets prophesied they would say as if mocking: 'Here again he sees a weight and burden of the Lord'. And so it came about that it was no longer called 'vision', but in jest and derision 'burden' and 'weight'. 5. The Lord therefore commands that, whether the people or a prophet or a priest should ask Jeremiah what the weight or what the taking up of the Lord is, he should answer them and say: 'You are the burden and you the taking up; for I will take you up and cast you away and dash you down and make you perish. But if anyone hereafter, whether of the prophets or of the priests or of the people, dares to name the burden and weight of the Lord, I will visit', he says, 'upon that man and upon his house, and I will blot him out forever'. 6. Let each one therefore say to his neighbor and friend by no means: What is the burden of the Lord?, but: 'What has the Lord answered and what has the Lord spoken? Forget the old word; let burden and weight or taking up no longer sound in your mouth, for to each man his own word and his own work will be reckoned as a burden and weight, according to that which is written: by your mouth you shall be justified and by your mouth you shall be condemned'.

LXIII. And you pervert the words of the living God, the Lord of hosts, our God. Thus shalt thou say — or shall you say — to the prophet: What has the Lord answered you and what has the Lord spoken? 2. But if you say: The burden of the Lord — up to here it is not found in the Seventy; and there follows: because of this, thus says the Lord: Because you have said this word: The burden — or the taking up — of the Lord, and I sent to you saying: Do not say the burden — or the taking up — of the Lord, therefore, behold, I will lift — or I will take up — you, carrying you, and I will forsake you and the city which I gave to you and to your fathers, from before my face, and I will give you over to everlasting reproach and eternal ignominy, which shall never be blotted out by oblivion. 3. The Lord commanded that the word of 'burden' and 'weight' and 'taking up' should by no means be spoken among the people, but that of 'answer' and 'word of God'. And because the people had disdained to fulfill this, he interprets the very word upon them and says: 'Since you have said what I did not wish, and this when I often sent prophets and ordered you not to say it, therefore I will fulfill in deed upon you your word of taking up, burden and weight; for I will take you up and lift you and carry you, and dash you down, and make you fall from on high to the ground — and not only you, but your city too, which I gave to your fathers — and I will give', he says, 'over to reproach and everlasting ignominy, which shall never be blotted out by oblivion'. 4. We know that this was done at the time of the Babylonian captivity too; but it is fulfilled more fully and more perfectly after the passion and resurrection of the Savior, when the Lord said: your house shall be left to you desolate, and the sentence will remain until its end. 5. Let us also say it otherwise, at least according to the Seventy: lēmma means not only 'taking up' but also 'gift and present'. Since, therefore, the people always promised itself prosperity, he says that they ought by no means to say this any longer; for they are unworthy of the gifts and presents of God, and are rather to be cast away and wholly abandoned by the help of God. 6. And moreover, things are often shown both by words and by the interpretation of names, as the changed names of Abraham, of Sarah, and of Peter and the sons of Zebedee signify a change of things, and in this same prophet 'Phaschor' is called 'fear' or 'removal' and 'settler' or 'stranger'. It must be noted that the words: of the living God, the Lord of hosts, our God the Latin and Greek codices do not have, and the Hebrews read them in their volumes against themselves, which properly signifies the mystery of the Trinity.

Book Five

I. The fifth book of the commentaries on Jeremiah, brother Eusebius, will take its beginning from two baskets, of which the one will show the sweetness of the right faith, the other the bitterness of the faithlessness of the heretics. Although Hananiah son of Azzur resists Jeremiah, and Shemaiah the Nehelamite desires that the prophet be sent into prison, and Zephaniah the priest swears himself to the words of the false prophets, yet truth can be shut up and bound, but it cannot be conquered; it is content with the fewness of its own and is not terrified by the multitude of its enemies. 2. Therefore raise your hands to heaven with Moses and hang up the ancient serpent in the desert, and at once Amalek will be destroyed and the poisoned bites will not prevail, and the people of the Lord, secure, will cross the streams of the Jordan with Jesus [Joshua], and after the vast wilderness will eat the bread that was born in our little village of Bethlehem.

Jeremiah 24

II.

The Lord showed me, and behold, two baskets — or, according to Symmachus, hampers — full of figs, placed before the temple of the Lord, after Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, had carried away Jeconiah, son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, and his princes and the smith and the setter from Jerusalem, and had brought them to Babylon. One basket had very good figs, as figs of the first season usually are, and one basket had very bad figs, which could not be eaten because they were bad. 2. And the Lord said to me: What do you see, Jeremiah? And I said: Figs, good ones, very good, and bad ones, very bad, which cannot be eaten because they are bad. And the word of the Lord came to me, saying: Thus says the Lord God of Israel: As these figs are good, so will I know the exile of Judah, which I have sent out from this place into the land of the Chaldeans, for good. 3. And I will set my eyes upon them for good, and I will bring them back into this land, and I will build them and not destroy them, and I will plant them and not pluck them up. And I will give them a heart, that they may know me, that I am the Lord, and they shall be to me for a people, and I will be to them for a God, because they will return to me with their whole heart. 4. For as the very bad figs, which cannot be eaten because they are bad, thus says the Lord, so will I give — or hand over — Zedekiah, king of Judah, and his princes and the rest of Jerusalem, who have remained in this city and who dwell in the land of Egypt. 5. And I will give them to vexation and affliction — or dispersion — to all the kingdoms of the earth, for a reproach and for a parable and for a proverb and for a curse in all the places to which I have cast them out. And I will send among them the sword and famine and pestilence, until they are consumed from the land which I gave to them and to their fathers. 6. Some interpret the two hampers or baskets of good and bad figs as the law and the Gospel, the synagogue and the Church, the people of the Jews and that of the Christians, Gehenna and the kingdom of heaven, of which the one pertains to the punishment of sinners, the other to the dwelling-place of the saints. 7. But we, knowing according to the Apostle Paul that the law is good and holy and the commandment good and holy, and that there is one God of both Testaments, should rather refer them to those who at the coming of the Lord the Savior believed and did not believe: so that those who, stirred up by the scribes and priests, cried out, saying: Crucify, crucify such a one, are the basket of bad figs; while those of the people who believed after his ascension are referred to the good figs and to the best basket and hamper. 8. But let us follow the simple and true history: that he called Jeconiah the basket of good figs, who by the counsel of Jeremiah and the command of God had handed himself over to the Babylonian king, and to whom the Lord also promises prosperity; and the basket of bad figs Zedekiah, who, contradicting the sentence of God, was captured, and with his eyes blinded was led to Babylon and died there. 9. And to such an extent did God set his eyes upon them for good, those who acquiesced in his command, and bring them back into their land, and build them and not destroy them, and plant them and not pluck them up, and give them a heart, that they might know him, that he himself was the Lord, and that they might become his people and he himself might be their God, that even in the captivity he set his eyes upon them and permitted them to work the land, to build houses and to plant orchards in the region of Babylonia; Daniel, through miracles of signs, was suddenly made a prince from a captive; the three youths were gloriously delivered from the fire of the furnace; and when the seventy years were completed, under Zerubbabel and Jeshua the great priest, and Ezra and Nehemiah, the greatest part returned to Jerusalem, whose number is contained in the volume of that same Ezra. 10. It must also be noted that this vision came to the prophet in the times of Zedekiah, after Jeconiah had been led into 'exile'; for he did not say 'captivity', because Jeconiah had handed himself over of his own accord. And by the smiths and setters we ought to understand either interpreters and teachers of the law, or craftsmen and enclosers of gold and gems, an art that is most precious among the barbarian nations. For 'setters' the Seventy translated 'bound men', to signify the evil of captivity, and of their own they added 'rich men', which is not found in the Hebrew. 11. And he compares the basket that had good figs, and very good ones, to the figs of the first season — which in Greek are called proima —, namely to Abraham and Isaac and Jacob and Moses and Aaron and Job and the other holy men, of whom one of the twelve prophets speaks: Like a grape in the desert I found Israel, and like figs on the fig tree I found their fathers; whence we too are called sons of Abraham, and on the contrary it is said to the Jews: If Abraham were your father, you would do his works. But these baskets, which had good and bad figs, were not outside and beyond the Church, but before the temple of the Lord, because all things lie open to his knowledge. 12. Nor do these figs that are outside have as great a bitterness as those that after the confession of faith have been changed by transgression; nor do the good figs that are not in sight of the temple of God — such as were the philosophers of the world, who, by natural goodness and by the understanding of the Creator, seemed not so much to follow the virtues as to praise them — have as great a sweetness as the figs have that are in the temple of God, among which were the prophets and apostles, of whom one fig said: I gave you milk, not solid food, and: My little children, with whom I am in labor again until Christ is formed in you. Hence it is said that in sight of the temple of God the good figs were very good and the bad figs were very bad. 13. And lest we be thought to be setting down our own sense, Scripture itself lays itself open. As these figs, it says, are good, so will I know the exile of Judah, which I have sent out from this place into the land of the Chaldeans, for good, signifying Jeconiah and the princes who were captured with him. 14. And on the contrary, of the basket that had bad figs: And as the very bad figs, he says, which cannot be eaten because they are bad, so will I give Zedekiah, king of Judah, and his princes and those who fled over into Egypt and who remained in this city, to vexation and affliction to all the kingdoms of the earth, when they were captured in Egypt too, and Nebuchadnezzar set his throne in Tahpanhes, and the Lord sent upon them the sword, famine and pestilence, until they were consumed from the land which he gave to their fathers. 15. But what he said of the good figs: I will give them a heart, that they may know me, that I am the Lord, is like the apostolic saying: It is God who works in you both to will and to accomplish, in that not only our works but our will too rests on the help of God. 16. In this place the ever-allegorical interpreter raves, and, wishing to do violence to the truth of the history, relates that some were captured from the heavenly Jerusalem and carried over into the land of the Chaldeans and will return again to their former place, such as Jeremiah and the other holy prophets, while others, who were sinners, will die in this land and in the valley of tears. I have set out the whole perikopēn [passage] of this vision together, so as not to divide the sense in the exposition.

Jeremiah 25

III.

The word that came to Jeremiah concerning all the people of Judea — or upon all the people of Judah — in the fourth year of Jehoiakim son of Josiah, king of Judah. These things are earlier than the preceding vision; for that one took place under Zedekiah, after Jeconiah had been carried off to Babylon, but these under Jehoiakim, the son of Josiah and father of Jeconiah. 2. For it was not their concern, as I have already said before, to preserve the chronological order of prophecy, which the laws of history require, but to write in whatever way they knew to be useful to those who would hear and read; hence also in the Psalter some wrongly look for the order of the psalms according to the sequence of history, which is not observed in lyric song.

IV. This is the first year of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, which is not found in the Seventy; and there follows: which Jeremiah the prophet spoke to all the people of Judah and to all the inhabitants of Jerusalem, saying. In the fourth year of King Jehoiakim son of Josiah, father of Jeconiah, Nebuchadnezzar took up the rule in Babylon, and so it came about that the year which was the first of Nebuchadnezzar in Babylon was the fourth of King Jehoiakim in Jerusalem. 2. Then, in the eighth year of his reign, he led into captivity Jeconiah with his mother and the princes, who had reigned only three months after the slaying of his father Jehoiakim. Now Jehoiakim was slain in the eleventh year of his reign in Jerusalem.

V. From the thirteenth year of Josiah son of Amon, king of Judah, up to this day, this is the twenty-third year, the word of the Lord has come to me, and I have spoken to you, rising by night and speaking; and you have not listened. 2. In the thirteenth year of the reign of Josiah, who reigned in Jerusalem thirty-one years, Jeremiah began to prophesy, and he prophesied under him for nineteen years. His son Jehoahaz succeeded him in the kingdom; and when he was at once led into captivity by Neco, the Egyptian king, his brother Jehoiakim obtained the kingdom, in the fourth year of whose reign this word of the Lord comes to Jeremiah. And so it was the twenty-third year of Jeremiah the prophet since he began to speak to the people, and he never ceased to preach, but every day, rising at dawn and by night, he spoke to the people; and you have not, he says, listened. 3. But if, as the new heretics sprung from the old would have it, the law was given once for all as a help, and precepts were established which we either do or do not do by the volition of our own free will, how is it that the prophet always presses himself upon them and daily repeats the commandments of God, which, once received, would surely have sufficed for those to whom they had been given, unless it is to show that we always need God's help, and that what has been given once can never suffice unless it is renewed daily by the Lord's admonition?

VI. And the Lord sent to you all his servants, the prophets, rising at dawn and sending; and you have not listened. Not through one prophet but through all of them God always admonished his people, and as though he himself were stationed on watch and on guard, he rose at dawn to warn his people. And you have not, he says, listened, so that the more frequent the admonition, the greater were the sins of those who despised it.

VII. Nor did you incline your ears to hear, when I said: Return, each one, from his evil way and from your very wicked thoughts, and you will dwell in the land which the Lord gave to you and to your fathers from age and even unto age. 2. So great was the hardness of the people that it would not even take on the bearing of one who listens and incline its ear, above all when the Lord was warning it that each one should turn back from his evil way and from his very wicked thoughts. And O infinite clemency: not to inflict punishment for the crime, but to call to repentance both for evil works, which the evil way signifies, and for very wicked thoughts, which even by themselves, without the deed, are reckoned as sin! 3. And he promises a reward if they do what is commanded: that they should dwell in the land which was given to their fathers and taken away because of the vices of the sons. And his saying: from age and even unto age, or: from eternity even unto eternity, shows that God's gifts are perpetual, if those to whom they were given prove worthy.

VIII. And do not go after strange gods, to serve them and worship them, nor provoke me to anger in the works of your hands, and I will not afflict you; and you have not listened to me. 'And these things', he says, 'I was urging together with the earlier ones: that you should not serve strange gods and worship them, and that you should not, by doing these things, provoke me to anger because of the works of your hands, so that I should do what I did not wish and afflict you; and you have not listened to me'. 2. And what follows: says the Lord, so that you provoked me to anger in the works of your hands, to your own harm, is not found in the Seventy.

IX. Therefore thus says the Lord of hosts: Because you have not listened to my words, behold, I will send and take all the families of the north, says the Lord, and Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, my servant. 'Because you have provoked me to anger and done evil to your own destruction, and have trampled with a proud foot him who called you to repentance, I will send', he says, 'to all the families, or nations, of the north, and I will bring their prince Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, my servant'. 2. Now the Lord sends either angels to stir up the nations, or else he moves thoughts of such a kind that they do the will of the Lord. And as for his calling Nebuchadnezzar 'his servant', he is not called 'servant' in the way the prophets and all the saints are, who truly serve the Lord, but in that in the overthrow of Jerusalem he serves the will of the Lord, in accordance with what the Apostle too says: whom I have delivered to Satan, that they may learn not to blaspheme. And that the Chaldeans lie in the northern quarter with respect to the position of Jerusalem is doubtful to no one.

X. And I will bring them upon this land and upon its inhabitants and upon all the nations that are round about it, and I will slay them and make them an astonishment and a hissing and everlasting desolations. 2. These are the rewards of those who despise the words of God and refuse to hear them; whatever evil, therefore, is brought upon us, our sins have brought about. And just as all the nations were then brought against Jerusalem, so today they are brought against the negligent Church, that he may slay them and make them an astonishment and a hissing and everlasting desolations, so that they may be an example and a marvel to all. Now hissing is a sign of marvel and astonishment, and everlasting desolation is found in those who do not have God as their protector.

XI. And I will destroy from them the voice of joy and the voice of gladness, the voice of the bride and the voice of the bridegroom, the voice of the mill and the light of the lamp. And all its land shall become a desolation and an astonishment. 2. This is proved even today in the assembly of the malignant, in that their teachers do not teach the word of God but hiss like a serpent. And there perishes in them the voice of joy and the voice of gladness, so that they never hear that saying of the Apostle: rejoice, again I say, rejoice!; also the voice of the bride, that is, of the Church's faith, and the voice of the bridegroom, that is, of the Lord the Savior, for he who has the bride is the bridegroom; the voice of the mill, so that grain is not ground in it and given to the peoples to eat; and the light of the lamp, namely the teaching and knowledge of the prophets. Indeed, of John the Baptist too it is said: he was a shining lamp, and another prophet says: a lamp to my feet, he says, is your word, O Lord, and a light to my paths. 3. 'All', he says, 'the land of the heretics shall become a desolation and an astonishment, when its last end has been shown to be foolish'.

XII. And all these nations shall serve the king of Babylon seventy years. And when the seventy years are fulfilled, I will visit upon the king of Babylon and upon that nation, says the Lord, their iniquities, and upon the land of the Chaldeans, and I will make it everlasting desolations. And I will bring upon that land all my words which I have spoken against it, all that is written in this book, whatever Jeremiah prophesied against all the nations. 2. Just as Jerusalem after seventy years receives back its former inhabitants and, its punishments completed, because it obeyed the sentence of God, enjoys its former happiness, so the king of Babylon, because he was lifted up into pride and judged that his reigning over the nations was the work of his own strength and not of the Lord's will, will be destroyed when the Medes and Persians come. 3. Indeed, to this day only the remains of the city of Babylon survive, and the Lord has made it an everlasting desolation and has fulfilled all the words that are contained in the book of this very prophet. For in what follows the discourse of Jeremiah describes what evils Babylon is to suffer.

XIII. Because they served them, though they were many nations and great kings; and I will repay them according to their works and according to the deeds of their hands. This is not found in the Seventy. And it signifies that Jeremiah prophesied not only against Babylon but also against the other nations which were in the Babylonian army and fought against the people of the Lord. 2. Indeed, in what follows words are spoken against Egypt and the Philistines and Moab and Ammon and Idumea and Damascus and Kedar and the kingdoms of Hazor and Elam, and at the last against Babylon and the land of Chaldea.

XIV. For thus says the Lord God of Israel to me: Take this cup of the wine of fury — or of pure wine — from my hand, and you shall give drink from it to all the nations to whom I shall send you; and they shall drink and be drunk — or vomit — and go mad before the sword which I shall send among them. And I took the cup from the hand of the Lord and gave drink to all the nations to whom the Lord sent me. 2. The giving to drink of the cup, and of the cup 'of pure wine' or 'unmixed', which in Greek is called akratou, is a sign of the Lord's fury, so that all the nations which made war on the people of God drink of the cup of the Lord's fury, of which Isaiah too writes against Jerusalem: the bowl of fury, the cup of ruin you have drunk up and emptied; therefore arise! And this cup is drunk by all the nations for this reason: that they may vomit and go mad. 3. This, indeed, a perverse interpreter takes in a good sense, as though, like a cathartic potion, it forces out whatever bile and phlegm and harmful humor there is in the breast, and restores former health. He also takes the Savior in place of Jeremiah, because he himself gave drink to all the nations, to whom he was also sent, so that, casting off idolatry, they might dedicate themselves to the worship of God. That this is contrary to holy Scripture what follows will show; for not as a remedy, as he would have it, but as a punishment are they given to drink from the undiluted cup.

XV. To Jerusalem and to the cities of Judah and to its kings and to its princes, to make them a desolation and an astonishment and a hissing and a curse, as it is this day. 2. 'I thought', he says, 'that I was giving drink to the nations alone, and therefore I had gladly offered myself for this ministry; but among the other nations, nay rather before the other nations, I gave drink to Jerusalem and to the cities of Judah and to its kings and princes, to make them an astonishment and a desolation and a hissing and a curse, as is also proved by the present example'. Hence he also says above: You have deceived me, O Lord, and I was deceived; you were stronger, and you prevailed!

XVI. To Pharaoh, king of Egypt, and to his servants and to his princes and to all his people and to all in general. After Jerusalem, Pharaoh, king of Egypt, drinks, and his allies; the princes drank, and the whole people, and everyone who is indeed not an Egyptian but dwells in its regions, whom the Seventy translated symmiktous, that is, 'people of mixed stock', a populace not of the Egyptian region but foreign and immigrant.

XVII. To all the kings of the land of Ausitis. It is called 'Vs' in Hebrew, and the history relates that Job too was from it: there was a certain man in the region of Ausitis, named Job. And yet it should be known that this short verse is not found in the Seventy, and that Theodotion translated 'island' for 'Vs'.

XVIII. And to all the kings of the land of the Philistines and of Ashkelon and of Gaza and of Ekron and to the remnants of Ashdod. In general he set down the Philistines, that is, the land of the Palestinians, and in particular their cities Ashkelon, Gaza, Ekron, Ashdod; Gath alone he passed over in silence, which is contained in the 'remnants'. For this is what is written: and to the remnants of Ashdod; for the region of the city of Gath is neighboring and bordering on Ashdod, which in Hebrew is called Esdod. And Isaiah too writes that the Palestinians were captured and laid waste by the Babylonians.

XIX. To Idumea and Moab and the sons of Ammon and to all the kings of Tyre and to all the kings of Sidon. Idumea is the land in which are the mountains of Seir, and which in Hebrew is called Edom; Moab and Ammon are themselves the sons of Lot, neighbors of the Dead Sea. Tyre and Sidon are the chief cities on the coast of Phoenicia, which themselves too were overcome when the Babylonian came; Carthage is their colony, whence also the Poeni are so called, by a corruption of the word, as though Phoeni, and their language is to a great extent akin to the Hebrew language.

XX. And to the kings of the islands which are beyond the sea. He passes over to Cyprus and Rhodes and the islands which are called Kyklades [the Cyclades]; for these too were occupied by the Babylonians.

XXI. And Dedan and Teman and Buz and all who are shorn round the hair. These nations are in the wilderness, neighboring on and intermingled with the regions of the Ishmaelites, whom they now call Saracens, and of whom it is said: who are shorn round the hair.

XXII. And to all the kings of Arabia. This is not found in the Seventy. After Dedan, then, and Teman and Buz and the Ishmaelites, the order of the regions follows.

XXIII. And to all the kings of the west, who dwell in the desert, and to all the kings of Zimri. This too is not found in the Seventy.

XXIV. And to all the kings of Elam and to all the kings of the Medes. From the fact that Elam and the kings of the Medes follow Zimri, we judge that Zimri too is a region of Persia, unless perhaps, from the fact that Arabia came before, these too are to be understood as kings of the desert. Now Elam is a region of Persia beyond Babylon; hence also the Elamites, and the Medes too and the Persians, by whom Babylon was captured and destroyed; and they themselves also drank the undiluted cup when Alexander, king of the Macedonians, gave it them to drink.

XXV. And to all the kings of the north, near and far. Because he had enumerated the regions of Persia and Babylon, of Elam and of the Medes, he now sets down in general all the kings of the north, who are near and far. For 'north', which in Hebrew is called 'safun', the Seventy translated aphēliōtēn, which we can call 'the east wind'.

XXVI. Each one against his brother, and to all the kingdoms of the earth which are upon its face. So that it should not become lengthy to enumerate one by one all the regions of the eastern province, he set down in general all the kingdoms of the earth which dwell on the earth. And as for his saying: against his brother, there is understood 'I gave the undiluted drink', so that all should rage, vomit, go mad and fight one another in mutual battles. And what he added:

XXVII. King Sheshach shall drink after them, and is not found in the Seventy, has this sense: 'All the nations round about', he says, 'will be subject to the Babylonian empire, and he will subject all things to his power, so that all the nations which the preceding discourse has recounted serve him and drink of his cup'; hence also in the vision against Babylon it is written: Babylon is a golden cup that makes all the earth drunk. But last of all the king of Babylon will drink this potion, given him to drink by the rider of the chariot, of the camel and of the ass, Cyrus, king of the Medes and Persians. 2. But how Babylon, which in Hebrew is called 'Babel', is to be understood as 'Sesach' will not greatly trouble anyone who has even a little knowledge of the Hebrew language.
