Just as among us the Greek alphabet is read in order up to the last letter, that is, 'alpha, beta' and so on up to 'omega', and again, for the sake of children's memory, we are accustomed to reverse the order of reading and mix the last letters with the first, so that we say 'alpha omega, beta psi', so too among the Hebrews the first letter is 'aleph', the second 'beth', the third 'gimel', up to the twenty-second and last letter 'thau', of which the next-to-last is 'sin'. 3. And so we read 'aleph thau, beth sin', and when we come to the middle, the letter 'lamed' meets 'chaph'; and just as, if it is read straight, we read 'Babel', so with the order reversed we read 'Sesach'. But the vowel letters between 'beth and beth and lamed' and 'sin and sin and chaph' are not set down in this name, according to the idiōma [idiom] of the Hebrew language; and I think it was prudently concealed by the holy prophet, so that he should not openly stir up against himself the madness of those who were besieging Jerusalem and were on the very point of taking it. 4. We read that the Apostle too did this with regard to the Roman Empire, when he wrote of Antichrist: do you not remember that, when I was still with you, I used to tell you these things? And now you know what restrains, that he may be revealed in his own time, Antichrist being understood. For the mystery of iniquity is already at work; only he who holds now, until he be taken out of the midst. And then the wicked one will be revealed, whom the Lord Jesus will slay with the spirit of his mouth and destroy with the illumination of his coming. By 'he who holds' he indicates the Roman Empire; for unless it has been destroyed and taken out of the midst, according to the prophecy of Daniel, Antichrist will not come before. If he had chosen to say this openly, he would foolishly have stirred up the rage of persecution against the Christians and the Church then coming to birth. 5. We have spoken of this passage at greater length than the brevity of commentaries allows, which the Greeks and Latins will perhaps disdain, because it is not found in their codices. But what will that profit them, when in what follows this same prophet says: how Sheshach has been taken, and the renowned one of the whole earth seized; how Babylon has become an astonishment among the nations? 6. Allegorical interpreters refer this whole passage to all the nations which the devil has made drunk with the most undiluted cup of sins; for he himself too, they say, will at the last drink punishments and torments, he of whom the Apostle writes: whom the Lord Jesus will slay with the spirit of his mouth, whence also in another place he says: but the last enemy will be destroyed, death. It requires great powers to be able to carry the names of different nations over into their etymologiais [etymologies] and to fit individual vices to individual names.

XXVIII. And you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Drink and be drunk and vomit and fall and do not rise, before the sword which I will send upon you! 'After', he says, 'you have given drink to all the nations and fulfilled the command of the Lord, you will again command these things with the words of the Lord and say: Drink and be drunk and vomit and fall and do not rise!' 2. If 'to drink, to be drunk, to vomit and to fall' is a sign of salvation, so that, like a cathartic potion, everything harmful is expelled, how does there follow: and do not rise? And what the drink is that makes them fall forever he sets down more plainly, that is: before the sword which I will send among you.

XXIX. And when they refuse to take the cup from the hand, to drink, you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord of hosts: Drinking you shall drink; for behold, in the city in which my name has been invoked, I am beginning to afflict; and will you, as though innocent, be exempt — or and will you be clean with cleanness — ? you shall not be exempt, or you shall not be clean. 2. He covertly shows that those who have refused to do the command of God willingly will undergo it by necessity, and will hear: drinking you shall drink. 'Whether you will it', he says, 'or not, the sentence of God must be fulfilled. For if the city of Jerusalem, in which the name of God was known, as the prophet says: God is known in Judea; his name is great in Israel, drank the undiluted cup, the cup of fury, how much more will you not be clean, who worship idols in place of the name of God!'

XXX. For I am calling a sword upon all the inhabitants of the earth, says the Lord of hosts. This is the most undiluted drink, this the cup of the Lord's fury, which is called not upon Jerusalem alone but upon the whole earth and all the nations round about; of which he had said above: 'and I will send to Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, my servant, and I will bring them upon this land and upon its inhabitants and upon all the nations that are round about it.'

XXXI. And you shall prophesy to them all these words and say to them: The Lord will roar from on high — or will give answer — and from his holy dwelling he will utter his voice; roaring he will roar over its beauty — or he will answer over his place —, a chant as of those who tread will be sung together against all the inhabitants of the earth. The sound — or destruction — has come to the ends — or upon a part — of the earth, for the Lord has judgment with the nations; he himself is judged with all flesh; the wicked he has given over to the sword, says the Lord. 2. First let us say how the truth of the translation stands. 'To all the nations', he says, 'you shall prophesy and say that the Lord roars from on high, that is, gives a sign of his fury, according to what is written: the lion will roar, and who will not fear? the Lord has spoken, and who will not prophesy?' and from his holy dwelling he will utter his voice, so as to terrify all who hear. 3. And he will roar over its beauty, that is, over the temple. And when he has roared, a chant will be sung like that of those who tread in the winepresses, and at the shedding of one another's blood a mournful song will be sung together, which Symmachus calls katalegma, Aquila iasmon; and the sound of this chant and song will reach to the ends of the earth, for the judgment of the Lord is with the nations. 4. But if the judgment of the Lord is with the nations, the merits of the nations too are diverse. This serves to expound that passage, as some would have it: he who does not believe is already judged. He is judged indeed in that he did not believe; but those very ones who do not believe will be afflicted with punishments that differ among themselves. He himself is judged with all flesh, so that no one goes away unjudged. But the wicked — that is, those who do not believe in the Lord — he gives over to the everlasting sword. 5. Allegorical interpreters expound this passage according to the Seventy in such a way as to refer the Lord's open threat to a good sense. 'The Lord', they say, 'will give answer from on high, namely to those whom he is going to save, and from his holy place he will utter his voice, and he will answer a word in his place. But they, like vintagers, full of the fruits of that sentence, will answer. Upon all the inhabitants of the earth destruction will come, not upon the whole earth, but upon a part of the earth, namely, of those who do not believe; for the Lord will have judgment among the nations, and he himself will be judged with all flesh, but the wicked will be given over to the sword'.

XXXII. Thus says the Lord of hosts: Behold, affliction — or evil — shall go forth from nation to nation, and a great whirlwind — or tempest — shall go forth from the heights — or the ends — of the earth. And the slain of the Lord — or those wounded by the Lord — shall be on that day from the height of the earth to its height; they shall not be lamented, and they shall not be gathered up nor buried; they shall lie as dung upon the face of the earth. 2. Let them blush who try to do violence to holy Scripture by expounding in a good sense things that are full of threatening. Now of this passage I think the Lord too spoke: nation will rise against nation and kingdom against kingdom, and the rest that is contained in that same saying of the Gospel. 3. And let us understand that these things were done according to the history, when all the nations round about were subjugated by the Babylonian king and felt his cruel rule, or according to prophecy, as things that are to come long afterward at the consummation of the world. And they are called slain by the Lord, not because the Lord himself strikes them, but in that in the destruction of the most wicked the will and command of the Lord is fulfilled.

XXXIII. Howl — or shout for joy —, you shepherds, and cry out, and sprinkle yourselves with ashes, you nobles of the flock — or lament, you rams of the sheep —, for your days are fulfilled, that you should be slain, and your scatterings, and you shall fall like precious vessels — or like choice rams —. And flight shall perish from the shepherds, and deliverance from the nobles — or rams — of the flock. 2. It should be noted that only in this place have the Seventy put 'jubilation' in a bad sense, for which the other translators rendered 'howling', as it is written in the Hebrew. Between the shepherds and the rams, or nobles, of the flock there is this difference: the shepherds pertain to rational beings, while the rams and nobles refer to the rich, who themselves too are called part of the flock. 3. And as for what he adds: your days are fulfilled, that you should be slain, their days will be fulfilled then, when their sins have been fulfilled, and they will be scattered and will fall like precious vessels, so that once broken they cannot be restored, and the more precious they were before, the greater the loss in their breaking; or like choice rams, so that they may be a fat victim for those eager to devour them. 4. Flight, he says, shall perish from the shepherds, when they have not shown repentance. Indeed, it is said to the Pharisees: brood of vipers, who showed you how to flee from the wrath to come? And in the Psalms we read: flight has perished from me. And deliverance, he says, from the nobles of the flock, or rams; apo koinou [in common, i.e. shared by both clauses] 'shall perish' is understood.

XXXIV. The voice of the cry of the shepherds and the howling of the nobles of the flock — or the jubilation of the rams —, for the Lord has laid waste their pastures. And the fields — or the beautiful things — of peace have fallen silent before the wrath of the fury of the Lord. Here too it should be noted that in the Seventy 'jubilation' is put for 'howling'. 2. Now between the shepherds and the rams there is this difference: the shepherds are taken to be those in the Church who preside over the flock with wisdom and learning and teaching, but the rams those who seem indeed to be leaders among the people, but have in themselves nothing of teaching and wisdom, and through excessive simplicity are almost neighbors of folly. 3. But when we have peace and do not understand either the good things or the beautiful things of peace, but give ourselves over to luxury and idleness and pleasures, then the good things of peace will grow quiet, or fall silent, and will be taken away from us because of the wrath of the Lord's fury, and that which is written will be fulfilled: when they say: peace and security, then sudden destruction will come upon them, at whose coming all things will fall silent.

XXXV. He has forsaken, like a lion, the shade — or the lair — that is his — and, as is more correct, the tabernacle; for this is what the Hebrew 'soccho' means —, for their land has become a desolation — or trackless — before the wrath of the dove — or before the great sword — and before the wrath of the fury of the Lord. 2. The Lord, of whom he had said above: from on high he will roar, and from his holy dwelling he will utter his voice; roaring he will roar over its beauty, will himself forsake his tabernacle, of which it is written: his place has been made in peace — or in Salem — and his dwelling in Zion; he himself will desert his seat and will fulfill what he spoke through this same prophet: I have left my house, I have forsaken my inheritance. 3. And he has left, like a lion, his lair, so that all the beasts have power to lay waste his land. For while the lion is its guardian and protector, no one dares to approach it. Their land, he says, has become — without doubt either that of the people of the Jews, or else that of all the nations — a desolation and trackless before the wrath of the dove. 4. He who has read above that Nebuchadnezzar is called the Lord's 'servant' will not be surprised that he is understood as the Lord's 'dove' — for which the Seventy translated 'great sword' —, although we can also take the 'dove' in the person of Jerusalem, because she is angry and sad that she has lost the guardianship of her lion and that her land has come into desolation.

Jeremiah 26

XXXVI.

In the beginning of King Jehoiakim, son of Josiah, king of Judah, this word came from the Lord, saying: Thus says the Lord: Stand in the court of the house of the Lord, and you shall speak to all the cities of Judah, from which they come to worship in the house of the Lord, all the words that I have commanded you to speak to them; do not withhold a word! If perhaps they will hear and turn, each one from his evil way, and I may repent of the evil — or I may rest from the evil —, which I am thinking to do to them because of the wickedness of their pursuits. 2. This prophecy is earlier than the former one, although it was made under the same king. For that one was made in the fourth year of Jehoiakim, son of Josiah king of Judah, but this one in the beginning of the same king, as Scripture says: in the beginning of King Jehoiakim, son of Josiah, king of Judah, this word came from the Lord, saying. Therefore, as we have often said already, the order of history is not to be woven together in the prophets, since in the present case, under the same king, earlier things are spoken afterwards and later things before. 3. But he who is about to speak the word of the Lord ought to stand with Moses and to hear with the psalmist: you who stand in the house of the Lord, in the courts of the house of our God. And he is commanded to speak to all the cities of Judah, although the Seventy did not translate 'cities', lest perhaps it should seem unfitting to speak, in the court of the house of the Lord, to cities which were not present; but when he speaks to the peoples and to the citizens, he speaks to the cities themselves. And he fittingly stands in the court and vestibule of the temple of the Lord, so that, on the occasion of prayer to the Lord and of worshiping him, they may be compelled to hear the words of the prophet. 4. 'Do not', he says, 'withhold a word; although it be sad, although the rage of those who hear be stirred up against you, nevertheless say what has been commanded you, fearing not the persecutions of those who are to be stirred up against you, but the command of the Lord who orders it!' 5. Perhaps, he says, they will hear and turn. The ambiguous word 'perhaps' cannot befit the majesty of the Lord, but he speaks according to our feeling, so that free will may be preserved for man, lest by his foreknowledge man be compelled, as if by necessity, either to do something or not to do it; for it is not because God knows that something is going to be that it is therefore going to be, but because it is going to be, God knows it, as one who foreknows things to come. 6. And yet it must be known, according to this same Jeremiah, that if the Lord has foretold evils and the people has repented, he himself too will repent over the things which he threatened to do; and if he has promised prosperity and the people has acted negligently, God changes his sentence and brings evils instead of good things. Something of this kind is also that saying in the Gospel: I will send my son; perhaps they will reverence him; which is surely said in the person of almighty God. 7. Finally, in the present passage too he says: 'if perhaps they will hear and turn, each one from his evil way, so that, when they have turned, I too may repent of my sentence and not do what I am thinking to do to them. But I am thinking to do it because of the wickedness of their pursuits; and if these have been changed, my sentence too will be changed.' Let us read the history of Jonah and Nineveh.

XXXVII. And you shall say to them: Thus says the Lord: If you will not hear me, to walk in my law which I gave you, to hear the words of my servants, the prophets, whom I sent to you, rising up by night and sending, and you did not hear, I will make this house like Shiloh, and this city I will make a curse to all the nations of the earth. 2. It is therefore placed in our power either to do something or not to do it, only in such a way that whatever good work we will, seek and accomplish, we refer to the grace of God, who, according to the Apostle, has given us both to will and to accomplish. But if it suffices to walk once in the law which was given to us through Moses, as the foolish heresy supposes, how is it that he added: to hear the words of my servants, the prophets? who surely were sent after the law, and not once but frequently, nor idly and as from one at ease, but always and as from one who is anxious. 3. 'I sent', he says, 'to you my servants daily and rising up by night; and if you will not hear them, I will make this house' — that is, the temple of God — 'like Shiloh', where the tabernacle was. And when the temple has been destroyed, consequently the city too will be a curse to all the nations of the earth. 4. And just as, when the temple had been built on the threshing floor of Ornan and on Mount 'Moria', that is, 'of vision', on which Abraham is related to have offered his son Isaac, the worship of Shiloh ceased and no sacrifices were afterwards celebrated there, so, when the Church had been built and spiritual victims had been offered in it, the ceremonies of the law ceased, and the city of the Jews was made a curse to all the nations of the earth, from which the Lord has freed us, as the Apostle says: Christ has redeemed us from the curse of the law, having been made a curse for us.

XXXVIII. And the priests and the prophets — or false prophets — and all the people heard Jeremiah speaking these words in the house of the Lord. And when Jeremiah had finished speaking all that the Lord had commanded him to speak to all the people, the priests and the prophets — or false prophets — and all the people seized him, saying: Let him die the death, because he has prophesied in the name of the Lord, saying: This house shall be like Shiloh, and this city shall be made desolate, so that there is no inhabitant. 2. The priests and the prophets, whom the Seventy more plainly translated as 'false prophets', are angry with Jeremiah because he preaches true things, and because, with the temple overthrown and the city deserted, their religion would perish, and the profits coming from religion; therefore they seize him and, with the people consenting with them, destine him for death, because he had said in the name of the Lord: This house shall be like Shiloh, and the city shall be made desolate, so that there is no inhabitant. 3. If, then, at any time, because of the commandments of the Lord and the truth of the faith, either priests or false prophets or a deceived people are angry with us, let us not greatly care, but let us carry out the sentence of God, pondering in our mind by no means present evils, but future goods.

XXXIX. And all the people was gathered against Jeremiah in the house of the Lord. And the princes of Judah heard these words, and they went up from the king's house to the house of the Lord and sat in the entrance of the new gate of the Lord. 2. Jeremiah was prophesying the words of the Lord in the temple and had said: I will make this house like Shiloh, and this city a curse to all the nations of the earth, and at once, a riot having been stirred up by the priests and prophets and people, the whole crowd is gathered against the prophet into the temple, where the prophet was and was being held by the hands of the priests and prophets and crowd. 3. When the princes of the city, who were staying in the royal house, had heard this, they crossed over, or went up from the king's house to the house of the Lord. And it is to be noted that to go to the house of the Lord is always an ascent. And they sat in the entrance of the new gate of the Lord. For it was the duty of the princes to sit in the gate of the house of the Lord and there to learn the truth of the business and of the riot. But the gate is called 'new' because those who sat in it and presided over the judgment resisted the slanders of the priests and false prophets.

XL. And the priests and the prophets spoke to the princes and to all the people, saying: There is a judgment of death for this man, because he has prophesied against this city, as you have heard with your ears. 2. While the princes of the city, who had run from the king's palace to the temple so that the riot of the people might be calmed, were sitting in the gate of the temple and in the new gate, and an assembly of the people had been gathered, the priests and false prophets accuse him; and the prophet would have perished, as far as it lay with the priests and prophets, if the accusers themselves had had the power of judgment. From this we understand that those who seemed devoted to religion were crueler toward the prophet, through envy of his holiness, than those who presided over public needs.

XLI. And Jeremiah said to all the princes and to all the people, saying: The Lord sent me to prophesy to this house — or upon this house — and to this city — or upon this city — all the words which you have heard. Now therefore make good your ways and your pursuits, and hear the voice of the Lord your God, and the Lord will repent of the evil which he has spoken against you — or and the Lord will rest from the evils which he has spoken against you —. 2. As for me, behold, I am in your hands; do to me as is good and right in your eyes — or as is expedient for you — ! Nevertheless, know and understand that, if you kill me, you will bring innocent blood against your very selves and against this city and its inhabitants; for in truth the Lord sent me to you, to speak in your ears all these words. 3. When, with the people present, the princes of the city were sitting in the gate, and the priests and prophets were accusing Jeremiah the prophet and pressing a capital charge, Jeremiah speaks to the princes and to all the people, whom the faction of the priests and false prophets had stirred up, prudently and at the same time humbly and steadfastly. 4. Prudently, in that he said he had been sent by the Lord to speak against the temple and the city, and gave the counsel that, if they were willing to hear his counsel and to repent, the Lord too would change his sentence. Humbly, in what he says: behold, I am in your hands; do to me as is good and right in your eyes! And steadfastly: in truth the Lord sent me to you, to speak in your ears all these words. 5. And in other words he says: 'If you are angry because I have spoken against the temple and the city of the Lord, and the safety of the city and of the temple is your concern, why do you add sins to sins and make both the city and its inhabitants guilty of my blood?' 6. If, then, at any time we too have need of humility because of the straits of necessity, let us so take it up that we do not desert truth and steadfastness; for it is one thing proudly to insult the one who judges, which is a sign of folly, another so to avoid the impending danger that you subtract nothing from the truth.

XLII. And the princes and all the people said to the priests and the prophets: There is no judgment of death for this man, because he has spoken to us in the name of the Lord our God. 2. The people, which had earlier been tripped up by the priests and false prophets, joins the princes of the city and speaks for Jeremiah, saying that he is by no means guilty of death, but has prophesied in the name of the Lord and from his mouth; for the untaught crowd, once it has received a reason, quickly changes its opinion. But the resentment of the accusers, especially of the priests and false prophets, cannot be changed; and therefore, while they accuse and persevere in the accusation, the people is changed, because he had given them hope of the Lord's pardon, if they made their ways good and heard the voice of the Lord their God, so that the Lord too would bend his sentence.

XLIII. And there rose up men of the elders of the land and said to the whole assembly of the people, speaking: Micah of Moresheth was a prophet in the days of Hezekiah, king of Judah, and he spoke to all the people of Judah, saying: Thus says the Lord of hosts: Zion shall be plowed like a field, and Jerusalem shall become a heap of stones, and the mountain of the house the heights of the forests. 2. Did Hezekiah, king of Judah, and all Judah condemn him to death? Did they not fear the Lord and entreat the face of the Lord, and the Lord repented of the evil which he had spoken against them? So we are doing a great evil against our own souls. 3. The princes of the city and the people understand the truth of the judgment; but the elders, whose proper task it was to know old things, unfold the history and prophecy of Micah of Moresheth, who prophesied under King Hezekiah, compare it with the prophecy of Jeremiah, for which a capital charge is being pressed against him, and show that Micah had said graver things and yet had suffered nothing from the righteous king Hezekiah, but that they, converted to repentance, had turned the sentence of the Lord in a good direction. 4. For Micah said: Zion shall be plowed like a field, and Jerusalem shall become a heap of stones, and the mountain of the house the heights of the forests; and Jeremiah: I will make, he says, this house like Shiloh, and this city I will make a curse to all the nations of the earth. 5. And they give counsel, thinking that what Micah had foretold would by no means come to pass, because for a long time it had not been done on account of the repentance of the people, and that this too, which Jeremiah spoke, would by no means come to be, if, according to his counsel, they made good their ways and their pursuits and heard the voice of the Lord their God, so that the Lord would not bring the evil with which he had threatened them. 6. And at the same time they break the rage of the accusers and mingle themselves with them, saying: So we are doing a great evil against our own souls, not that they ought to do it, but because, if they did it, they would by no means harm the accused, but their own souls, which they could have freed by a change of their sentence.

XLIV. There was also a man prophesying in the name of the Lord, Uriah, son of Shemaiah of Kiriath-jearim, and he prophesied against this city and against this land according to all the words of Jeremiah. And King Jehoiakim and all his mighty men and his princes heard these words, and the king sought to kill him; and Uriah heard and was afraid and fled and entered Egypt. 2. And what follows: and King Jehoiakim sent men into Egypt, Elnathan, son of Achbor, and men with him into Egypt, is not found in the Seventy. Then is joined: and they brought Uriah out of Egypt and led him to King Jehoiakim; and he struck him with the sword and cast his corpse into the graves of the ignoble common people. Nevertheless the hand of Ahikam, son of Shaphan, was with Jeremiah, so that he should not be delivered into the hand of the people and they kill him. 3. It is asked why, when Uriah, son of Shemaiah, from the town of Kiriath-jearim, had prophesied the same things as Jeremiah, and, struck with fear, fled to Egypt and, dragged back from there, was killed, Jeremiah was able to escape, who certainly had not fled, but, boldly persevering in his former statement, was freed both by the judgment of the crowd and of the princes and by the counsel of the elders against the accusers, the priests and false prophets. 4. To this one must briefly answer that the judgment of God can by no means be known, when, in the same cause and for the same statement, one is punished and another is freed; unless perhaps we can answer this, that Uriah was slaughtered to the condemnation of both the accusers and the people, while Jeremiah was preserved by the judgment of God, so that he might preach to the remnant of the unhappy people and draw them back to repentance. This indeed we also read in the Acts of the Apostles: that the apostle James at once suffered the sentence of Herod and was crowned with martyrdom, but Peter and the other apostles were preserved for the teaching of the Lord. 5. And the prophetic steadfastness is to be noticed, in that not even when dragged back from Egypt did Uriah change his statement, but, seeing death threatened against him, nonetheless spoke what the Lord had commanded. And that he feared and fled and entered Egypt is a sign not of unbelief but of prudence, lest we offer ourselves to dangers in vain. Otherwise we read that the Lord and Savior too slipped out of the hands of his persecutors, and that he commanded the apostles: when they persecute you in this city, flee to another! 6. It is also asked how Jehoiakim, king of Judah, a king of small dominion, weakened, and about to perish at any moment, had the power of sending into Egypt and of bringing Uriah out from there. This is easily solved if we consider that he had been appointed prince by Neco, king of Egypt, and that this prophecy was made in the beginning of his reign. 7. But although Jeremiah was freed by the help of the Lord, yet it is reckoned as a reward to him also through whom the Lord freed his prophet, namely to Ahikam, son of Shaphan, as we shall read later, when Jeremiah is freed from the danger of death out of the cistern of mud by the counsel and protection of Ebed-melech the eunuch.

Jeremiah 27

XLV.

In the beginning of the reign of Jehoiakim, son of Josiah, king of Judah, this word came to Jeremiah from the Lord, saying. This is not found in the edition of the Seventy, and many think it is the beginning of the following chapter, which is by no means so; rather, it is to be joined to the preceding one, so that whatever is reported as said and done we should believe to have been done in the beginning of the reign of Jehoiakim; whence too he had the power of sending into Egypt, as to a friendly king. 2. But the Seventy seem to me to have passed over this title in silence for this reason, lest they should seem to say it a second time; for they had already set down at the beginning: in the beginning of King Jehoiakim, son of Josiah, king of Judah, this word came from the Lord, saying.

XLVI. Thus says the Lord to me: Make for yourself bonds and chains — or kloious [collars], which in Hebrew are called 'mutoth', and in common speech they call them 'boias' [neck-shackles] — and you shall put them on your neck and you shall send to the king of Edom and to the king of Moab and to the king of the sons of Ammon and to the king of Tyre and to the king of Sidon by the hand of the messengers who have come to Jerusalem to Zedekiah, king of Judah. And you shall command them to speak to their masters: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Thus shall you say to your masters. 2. The previous vision came to the prophet in the beginning of the reign of Jehoiakim, son of Josiah, king of Judah, but this one under Zedekiah, who reigned last in Jerusalem and under whom the city was taken and overthrown. 3. And Jeremiah is commanded to put chains, or wooden forks, which, as we have said, are called 'mutoth' in Hebrew, on his neck, and to send them to the kings of Edom, Moab, the sons of Ammon, Tyre and Sidon by the envoys who had come to Zedekiah, and to command them to announce to their masters that they must serve King Nebuchadnezzar and hear the things which the prophet's following discourse goes on to set out. And lest perhaps the envoys and the kings of these nations should answer: 'Why do you not command this to your own people?', he speaks similar things to King Zedekiah too, and to the priests and prophets. 4. This passage the ever-allegorical interpreter, who flees from the truth of history, interprets of the heavenly Jerusalem: that its inhabitants must voluntarily take on bodies and descend into 'Babylon', that is, into the 'confusion' of this world, which is set in the evil one, and serve the Babylonian king, without doubt the devil; and that, if they will not do this, they will by no means bear heavy bodies, but will perish by sword and famine and pestilence, and will by no means become men, but demons. 5. Let this stand as what he said, lest his defenders bring a false charge against us. But as for us, let us follow the simple and true history, lest we be wrapped in certain clouds and illusions.

XLVII. I made the earth and man and the beasts that are upon the face of the earth, by my great strength and by my outstretched arm, and I gave it to him who pleased in my eyes. 2. Although this scripture speaks anthrōpopathōs [in human terms], in the way that we men can speak and understand, yet the 'strength' of God and his 'arm' is he of whom the Apostle also speaks: 'Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God', and Isaiah: Lord, who has believed our hearing, and to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed? John the evangelist also writes: all things were made through him, and without him nothing was made. David too speaks in his song: by the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and all their power by the spirit of his mouth. 3. And as for his saying: I gave it to him who pleased in my eyes, this signifies that all things have been granted to the human race through the grace of God. I, he says, made the earth and man and the beasts. The order is reversed; for in Genesis the living creatures are made first and man last, but here he names man first and afterwards the things that are subject to man.

XLVIII. Now therefore I have given all these lands into the hand of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, my servant; and moreover I have also given him the beasts of the field, that they may serve him. And all the nations shall serve him and his son and his son's son. 2. Of what great unhappiness is Israel, when in comparison with it Nebuchadnezzar is called 'servant of God'! It is written in the Gospel: the world was made through him, and the world did not know him; he came to his own, and his own did not receive him. Rightly therefore does the Creator hand over his creation to whom he will. Finally the devil too, as whose type Nebuchadnezzar went before, confesses: all these things have been handed over to me. 3. And as for what he added: moreover I have also given him the beasts of the field, that they may serve him, either we ought simply to understand every kind of living creature — for together with man the things subject to him are also handed over — or certainly let us take the 'beasts' as the savage nations, because even those serve which before had not known how to serve. And his 'son' and 'son's son' — according to the Hebrew — he calls Belshazzar and Evil-merodach, of whom Daniel writes.

XLIX. Until the time of his land and of himself comes. Fittingly, lest the empire of Nebuchadnezzar be thought perpetual, he says that he himself too is to be taken by the Medes and Persians; for this is what it signifies: until the time of his land and of himself comes. But this too is not found in the Seventy.

L. And many nations and great kings shall serve him. He did not say 'all' — for this is properly due to the empire of Christ —, although according to Symmachus one does not read: many nations and great kings shall serve him, but: many nations and great kings shall subject him to servitude, so that he himself too, whom all the nations had served before, serves the Medes and Persians. 2. This, which we have set down from the Hebrew: I have given him, that they may serve him, and all the nations shall serve him and his son and his son's son, until the time of his land and of himself comes and many nations and great kings subject him to servitude, is not read, as we have already said, in the edition of the Seventy.

LI. But the nation and kingdom which will not serve Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, and whoever will not bend his neck under the yoke of the king of Babylon, with sword and famine and pestilence I will visit upon that nation, says the Lord, until I consume them by his hand. 2. Not only does the Lord subject sinful nations to Nebuchadnezzar, but the Apostle too speaks of sinners: whom I have handed over to Satan, that they may learn not to blaspheme; and in another place: I have handed over such a one to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, that the spirit may be saved. He also warns that the powers must be obeyed not only because of wrath, but also because of conscience, lest we be condemned by them.

LII. You therefore, do not listen to your prophets and diviners and dreamers and augurs and sorcerers, who say to you: You shall not serve the king of Babylon; for they prophesy a lie to you, to remove you far from your land and to drive you out, and you perish. But the nation that puts its neck under the yoke of the king of Babylon and serves him, I will leave it in its own land, says the Lord, and it shall till it and dwell in it. 2. In this place too the allegorical interpreter raves, and exhorts those set in the heavenly Jerusalem not to hear their prophets and diviners, dreamers and augurs and sorcerers, but rather to serve Nebuchadnezzar and take on the body of humility, infancy, the wailing and the cradles of little ones; for if they do this, when their servitude and the condition of human mortality have been fulfilled, they will return to their land and dwell in it and work the works which they worked before. And he says that he suspects that those who have despised the commands of God to be weighed down with human bodies will become demons and unclean spirits and will by no means regain their former seat. 3. But let us expound simply: that there are among the nations prophets, who pretend that they foretell things to come by a divine spirit; and diviners, of whom there is also the common proverb: 'the wise, they say, divine'; and dreamers, who imitate Joseph and Daniel; and augurs, who by the flight of birds and the voices of omen-birds announce what is to be done or not to be done; and sorcerers, whom we can call either poisoners or those who serve the phantasms of demons, who in Hebrew are called 'chassane'. 4. 'All these', he says, 'deceive you and trip you up, so that you may not serve the king of Babylon'. For it is much better to take up servitude voluntarily and to have as a friend the one you serve, and to till your native land, than to serve as captives by force and necessity.

LIII. And to Zedekiah, king of Judah, I spoke according to all these words, saying: Put your necks under the yoke of the king of Babylon and serve him and his people, and you shall live. Why will you die, you and your people, by sword, famine and pestilence, as the Lord has spoken to the nation that will not serve the king of Babylon? 2. Do not listen to the words of the prophets who say to you: Do not serve the king of Babylon; for they themselves speak a lie to you, for I did not send them, says the Lord, and they prophesy in my name falsely, so that I may drive you out and you perish, both you and the prophets who prophesy to you. 3. After all the nations he passes to Zedekiah, king of Judah, and speaks with the same words with which he threatened the nations; for he who has sinned sins either like or greater than the other nations does not merit the privilege of the Israelite nation. Finally, because the rebellious people disdained to hear, it was consumed by sword, famine and pestilence. 4. And it is to be observed in holy Scripture that instead of 'false prophets' it calls 'prophets' those who prophesy falsely in the name of the Lord. 'And they do this, so that I', he says, 'may drive you out and you perish, both you and the prophets who prophesy to you'; therefore the destruction both of those who are deceived and of those who deceive is alike. 5. This, which we have set down from the Hebrew: under the yoke of the king of Babylon and serve him and his people, and you shall live. Why will you die, you and your people, by sword, famine and pestilence, as the Lord has spoken to the nation that will not serve the king of Babylon? do not listen to the words of the prophets who say to you, is not found in the Seventy; and therefore I admonish the prudent reader to understand how much is lacking, point by point, in the Greek and Latin codices.

LIV. And to the priests and to this people I spoke, saying: Thus says the Lord: Do not listen to the words of your prophets who prophesy to you, saying: Behold, the vessels of the Lord will return from Babylon now quickly; for they prophesy a lie to you. — This, which we have set down, 'now quickly', is not found in the Seventy, and likewise that which follows: — Do not listen to them, but serve the king of Babylon, that you may live! Why is this city given over to desolation? 2. And after the nations and the king he speaks to the priests and to the people, because he had already announced destruction to the prophets above, saying: so that I may drive you out and you perish, both you and the prophets who prophesy to you. And he speaks the same things which he had spoken to the king and to the nations: that they should not hear the words of their prophets and say that the vessels which had been carried away with Jeconiah and the princes and his mother were to be brought back to the temple of the Lord right now; and he warns them to serve the king of Babylon and live, and that the city, voluntarily subjected, should by no means be handed over to burning. And in this is the clemency of the Lord: to hand them over to a lighter punishment, lest they endure a heavier one.

LV. And if they are prophets and the word of the Lord is in them, let them meet — and what follows, which we are about to subjoin up to the end of this chapter, is not found in the Seventy — the Lord of hosts, that the vessels which had been left in the house of the Lord and in the house of the king of Judah and in Jerusalem may not come into Babylon. 2. For thus says the Lord of hosts to the pillars and to the sea and to the bases — for which in the Hebrew is written 'mechonoth' — and to the rest of the vessels which remained in this city, which Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, did not take when he carried Jeconiah, son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, from Jerusalem into Babylon, and all the nobles of Judah and Jerusalem. 3. For thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel, to the vessels which have been left in the house of the Lord and in the house of the king of Judah and in Jerusalem: They shall be carried into Babylon and shall be there until the day of their visitation, says the Lord, and I will cause them to be brought and restored to this place. 4. These words, as we have said, are not found in the Seventy, but have been translated from the Hebrew truth; in place of which they set down something else, which was not written, saying: to me, because thus said the Lord: and the rest of the vessels which the king of Babylon did not take, when he carried Jeconiah from Jerusalem, shall enter Babylon, says the Lord, setting down the sense rather than the words, perhaps thinking it irrational that God should speak to the pillars and to the sea and to the bases and to the rest of the vessels which had remained in Jerusalem — as if we did not read both that the Lord rebuked the morning worm and that he spoke to the sea: be silent and be still! 5. And what he says: let them meet me or the Lord of hosts, shows that a true prophet can withstand the Lord with prayers, in accordance with which Moses too stood in the smiting against the Lord, to turn away the wrath of his fury; Samuel also did the same. And the Lord to Moses: let me alone, he says, I will strike this people! But when he says: let me alone, he shows that he can be held back by the prayers of the saints. 6. 'Let the prophets meet', he says, 'and let them show whatever they foretell fulfilled in deed, and then the prophecy will be proved by its truth'. Now the pillars and the sea and the bases and the rest of the vessels we read of in Malachim [Kings] and at the end of this prophet's volume; and there are enumerated the vessels that were carried into Babylon when Zedekiah was taken and the city burned and the temple overthrown.

Jeremiah 28

LVI.

And it came to pass in that year, in the beginning of the reign of Zedekiah, king of Judah, in the fourth year, in the fifth month, Hananiah son of Azzur, the prophet from Gibeon, said to me in the house of the Lord before the priests and all the people, saying: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: I have broken the yoke of the king of Babylon; yet two years of days, and I will cause all the vessels of the Lord to be brought back to this place. 2. Those whom the Hebrew calls 'prophets', that is 'nebeim', the Seventy translated 'false prophets', in order to make the understanding plainer. Indeed, in the present passage too he is called 'prophet', that is 'nebia', and not 'false prophet'. Now the word of the Lord comes under King Zedekiah, in the fourth year of his reign and in the fifth month — while the prophet Ezekiel was not yet prophesying in Babylon to those who had gone into exile with Jeconiah — and he has the confidence to speak in the temple of the Lord against the prophet, because he promises prosperity to the people, and lies are gladly heard, especially those that promise joyful things. 3. Jeremiah had said that the rest of the vessels too, whether of the temple or of the royal house and of the whole people, which Nebuchadnezzar had left behind, were to be carried over to Babylon; but this man, on the contrary, promises that even those which had been carried over are to be brought back.

LVII. Which Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, took from this place and carried over into Babylon. Jeconiah, son of Jehoiakim, king of Judah, and all the exile of Judah, who went into Babylon, I will bring back to this place, says the Lord; for I will break the yoke of the king of Babylon. 2. For which the Seventy translated: Jeconiah and the exile of Judah, because I will break the yoke of the king of Babylon, briefly setting down the sense of the Hebrew truth rather than its words. Now Hananiah the 'prophet' — as he then seemed to the people — promises that not only the vessels but also King Jeconiah is to be brought back to Jerusalem, and that the yoke of the Babylonian king is to be broken, that is, his empire destroyed, and that this would come about before two years were yet completed, so that the nearness of the time of the promise might increase the greatness of the joy.

LVIII. And Jeremiah the prophet said to Hananiah the prophet in the eyes of the priests and in the eyes of all the people who stood in the house of the Lord, and Jeremiah the prophet said: Amen, so may the Lord do; may the Lord raise up your words which you have prophesied, that the vessels may be brought back from the house of the Lord, and all the exile of Babylon, to this place! 2. He wishes 'to be done' what the false prophet lyingly tells — for this is what 'amen' means, a word the Lord often uses in the Gospel: amen, amen I say to you —, and for the sake of the prosperity of affairs he desires that the other rather than himself be speaking the truth. Hence another prophet too testifies, saying: would that I were not a man having the spirit, and spoke a lie rather! Jonah, on the contrary, is grieved that he has lied, and is rebuked by the Lord, being shown that a lie on the prophet's part is more useful than the ruin of so great a multitude. And lest he should seem to approve the false prophet's prophecy, he adds the truth under the example of others, without insulting the liar:

LIX. Nevertheless hear this word which I speak in your ears and in the ears of all the people: the prophets who were before me and you from the beginning, and prophesied over many lands and over great kingdoms of battle and of affliction and of famine; the prophet who prophesied peace, when his word comes, the prophet will be known whom the Lord has sent in truth. 2. Jeremiah could have said to Hananiah: 'You speak falsely, you deceive the people, you are not a prophet but a false prophet.' But had he said that, the false prophet too could have turned the same words back upon Jeremiah; therefore he does him no insult and speaks as though to a 'prophet'. 3. 'Not only,' he says, 'am I a prophet, and you, but before me and you there were many others, among whom were Isaiah, Hosea, Joel and Amos and the rest. They prophesied,' he says, 'against many lands and kingdoms not small but great, announcing to them war and adversity and want of all things; and on the contrary there were others who promised peace and prosperity. The pronouncement of both was proved not by the flattery of a lie but by the outcome of events.' 4. Under the example of others, then, he speaks of himself and of Hananiah: that when the end of events has come, then the truth of the prophets is shown. The Lord also said this same thing through Moses, that a prophet is shown by the fulfilment of his prophecy. And this must be noted, that not threateningly, not savagely, but with the confidence of truth he rebukes the liar and defers the matter to the future, so that those who hear may await the outcome of events.

LX. And Hananiah the prophet took the chain — or the fork, which in Hebrew is called 'muthoth', — from the neck of Jeremiah the prophet and broke it. And Hananiah spoke in the sight of all the people, saying: Thus says the Lord: so will I break the yoke of Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon, in two years of days, from the neck of all the nations. And Jeremiah went on his way. 2. The Seventy did not translate 'two years'; nor did they call Hananiah 'prophet', lest, that is, they should seem to call a prophet one who was not a prophet — as if many things in the holy Scriptures were not said according to the opinion of the time to which the events reported belong, and not according to what the truth of the matter held; thus Joseph too is called in the Gospel the 'father' of the Lord, and Mary herself, who knew that she had conceived of the Holy Spirit and had answered the angel: how will this be, since I have not known a man?, says to her son: son, why have you done so to us? behold, your father and I, sorrowing, were seeking you. 3. And at the same time the prudence of Jeremiah, and his humility and patience, are to be considered. The false prophet does him injury by deed and, snatching the fork from his neck, breaks it — which he certainly could not have done with an iron one —; this man is silent and conceals his pain; for it had not yet been revealed to him by the Lord what he should say, so that holy Scripture might tacitly show that the prophets by no means speak by their own will alone, but also by the will of the Lord, above all concerning things to come, the knowledge of which belongs to God alone. 4. He went, he says, and withdrew on his way, as though defeated, and fulfilling that prophetic word: I became as a man who does not hear and has no reproofs in his mouth.

LXI. And the word of the Lord came to Jeremiah, after Hananiah the prophet had broken the chain — or fork — from the neck of Jeremiah the prophet, saying: Go, and you shall say to Hananiah: Thus says the Lord: the chain — or fork — of wood you have broken, and you will make — or I will make — in their place forks — or chains — of iron. 2. For thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: I have put a yoke of iron upon the neck of all these nations, that they may serve Nebuchadnezzar, king of Babylon. And what follows: and they will serve him; moreover I have also given him the beasts of the earth, is not found in the Seventy. And in the present passage, according to the Seventy, Hananiah is not written 'prophet', nor in what follows, lest, that is, as I said before, they should seem to call a 'false prophet' a 'prophet'; but what is that to the Hebrew truth? 3. After Jeremiah the prophet had gone on his way and swallowed the injury in silence, the word of the Lord came to him, so that the prophet should not speak in his own words to the false prophet glorying in his lie, but should say: thus says the Lord, although Hananiah too, in breaking the wooden fork, had spoken with the same authority in the sight of the Lord: thus says the Lord; for falsehood always imitates truth. 4. And what he added: you have broken forks of wood and you will make — or you have made — in their place chains of iron, shows this: that by refusing a lesser penalty he became the cause of a greater punishment among the people. 5. In this passage too the allegorical interpreter raves, calling the wooden forks and chains ethereal and aerial bodies, namely those of demons and of the hostile powers, but the iron forks or chains our grosser bodies, which are woven together of sinews and bones, flesh and veins, so that those from the heavenly Jerusalem who refused to undergo lesser torments according to the quality of their sin are condemned into the chains of our bodies, and endure the wailing of infancy, the bonds and filth of swaddling clothes, and serve the devil, the king of Babylon, that is, of this world — Scripture saying: the world is set in the evil one — together with the beasts of the earth, which are bound fast in the bodies of brute animals. 6. That unlearned commentator and follower of the Grunnian slander has compelled me to set forth openly the vices of others, which before I used to speak of with reserve, leaving them to the reader's prudence.

LXII. And Jeremiah the prophet said to Hananiah the prophet: Hear, Hananiah: the Lord has not sent you, and you have made this people trust in a lie. Therefore thus says the Lord: behold, I will send away — or cast out — you from the face of the earth; this year you will die. And what follows: because you have spoken against the Lord. And Hananiah the prophet died in that year, in the seventh month, is not found in the Seventy, in place of which they put only: and he died in the seventh month. 2. Here too in the Seventy Hananiah is not called 'prophet', though — according to the Hebrew — holy Scripture calls him 'prophet', even if, where Jeremiah rebukes him, saying: hear, Hananiah, the Lord has not sent you, he left out 'prophet'; for how could he call him 'prophet' whom he denied had been sent by the Lord? But the truth and order of the history are kept, as we said above, not according to what was, but according to what was thought at that time. 'You have deceived,' he says, 'the people with a lie, so that they would not acquiesce in God's sentence; know, therefore, that you will die this year!' 3. If, when we die, we are freed from the prisons of our bodies, according to that testimony which the heretics interpret wrongly: bring my soul out of prison, how is death now inflicted on the false prophet as a punishment? And this too must be noted, that Jeremiah, having suffered injury from the false prophet, is silent while the word of the Lord had not yet come to him, but afterwards, sent by the Lord, boldly rebukes the liar and announces his approaching death. 4. And as to his dying in the 'seventh' month: those who are always accustomed to interpret this number as showing 'rest' will perhaps falsely claim that he died in the 'seventh' month for this reason, that he might be freed from the evils of the body, according to the saying they bring forward as written: 'death is rest to a man'. But we know that the bodies of believers are temples of God, if indeed the Holy Spirit dwells in them.

Jeremiah 29

LXIII.

And these are the words of the book which Jeremiah the prophet sent from Jerusalem to the remnant of the elders of the exile, and to the priests, and to the prophets, and to all the people whom Nebuchadnezzar had carried over from Jerusalem into Babylon, after Jeconiah the king went out, and the lady — or the queen — and the eunuchs and the princes of Judah and Jerusalem — or from Jerusalem or the nobles — and the craftsmen from Jerusalem, by the hand of Elasah son of Shaphan and of Gemariah son of Hilkiah, whom Zedekiah king of Judah sent to Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon, into Babylon, saying: 2. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel, to all the exile which I carried over from Jerusalem into Babylon: Build houses and dwell, and plant gardens — or orchards — and eat their fruit! Take wives and beget sons and daughters; give wives to your sons and give your daughters to husbands, and let them bear sons and daughters; and multiply, and do not be few in number! And seek the peace of the city — or of the land —, to which I have carried you over, and pray for it to the Lord, for in its peace there will be peace for you! 3. This letter, nay rather little book, of Jeremiah the prophet is sent into Babylon through Zedekiah's envoys, Elasah and Gemariah, to those who had been carried over by Nebuchadnezzar with Jeconiah and his mother, so that, taking the occasion of the royal embassy, the prophet might also accomplish his own work and admonish the exiled people of the things that had been commanded him by the Lord. 4. And he said finely: Jeconiah the king went out, and the lady, the eunuchs and the princes of Judah and the rest, and: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel, to all the exile which I carried over from Jerusalem into Babylon, so that they may be seen to have been carried over not by the power of the king of Babylon but by the will of the Lord. And the word of God is directed first to the elders, then to the priests, third to the prophets, fourth to all the people, so that the prophet's letter too might reach those who were being admonished according to the order of age. 5. But he admonishes them not with his own words but with the Lord's, that they should build houses and dwell in them, and plant gardens or orchards and eat their fruit, take wives and beget sons and daughters, and multiply in the place of exile and not be few in number, and seek the peace of the city or land to which the Lord had carried them over, and pray for it to the Lord. And giving the reasons he says: 'for in the peace of that land there will be peace for you'. 6. To Jeremiah, because captivity was to overtake Jerusalem after a short time, it is commanded that he not take a wife, that he not beget sons. Hence to us also it is said through the Apostle: the time has been shortened; it remains that those who have wives be as though they had none. But if, because of the narrowness of the time, the use of wives is taken away from those who have them, how much more is it commanded to those who do not have them that they not take them! 7. Now the prophet's prophecy commands all this so that they may not give in to the false prophets, who were promising them a return to Jerusalem after a short time, but that they may know that they will stay a long time in Babylon, so that they ought to take wives, plant orchards and sow gardens, build houses and beget sons. 8. And in what he added: seek the peace of the city or of the land, and again: for in its peace there will be peace for you, that saying of the Apostle is confirmed in which he enjoins: I beseech therefore, first of all, that supplications, prayers, intercessions, thanksgivings be made for all men, for kings and all who are in high station, that we may lead a quiet and tranquil life in all piety and chastity. 9. Further, according to the mystical senses: after we have been cast out of Jerusalem, that is, the Church, because of our sins, and handed over to Nebuchadnezzar — of whom the same Apostle says: 'I have handed over such a one to Satan for the destruction of the flesh, that the spirit may be saved in the day of the Lord' and again: whom I have handed over to Satan, that they may learn not to blaspheme —, we ought not to be secure, nor, sluggish with idleness, to despair utterly of salvation, but first to build houses — not upon sand, but upon rock — and such houses as the midwives too built in Exodus, because they feared the Lord; 10. Then to plant gardens or orchards, such a paradise as the Lord too planted in Eden, and set in it the tree of life, of which it is written: it is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her; and he who holds her fast, blessed; third, to take wives, of whom one is wisdom, of whom Solomon writes: love her, and she will keep you; encircle her, and she will exalt you, and in another place: her I sought to take as a bride for myself, and I became a lover of her beauty. Nor does one spouse, wisdom, suffice us, unless we have the rest also: fortitude, temperance and righteousness, so that from them we may beget more sons. 11. Let us also give our daughters to husbands, so that the truth of faith, which is understood in the sons, may be coupled with good works, which are referred to the daughters, and good works may be joined to the soundness of faith. And begetting such sons and daughters, let us be multiplied in number, so that, putting away the things of a little child and growing up to the perfect man, we may deserve to hear: I write to you, fathers, because you have known him who is from the beginning, and may say with the Apostle to our children: for in Christ Jesus through the Gospel I have begotten you. 12. Let us also seek peace for the Church, our city and our land, so that we may deserve to return to it, from which by the Lord's judgment we have been carried over to dwell in the error of 'confusion'; for if it receives us, we shall have peace. And at the same time the clemency of the Lord is to be considered: he commands us to pray for our enemies and to do good to those who persecute us, so that we may not be content with our own salvation only, but may seek the salvation of our enemies also.

LXIV. For thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Let not your prophets who are in your midst, and your diviners, lead you astray, and do not attend to your dreams which you dream, for these prophesy falsehoods to you in my name, and I did not send them, says the Lord. 2. That there were prophets, nay rather false prophets, and diviners and dreamers in Babylon among those whom Nebuchadnezzar had led away with Jeconiah and his mother, the prophet Ezekiel testifies, writing against those whom Jeremiah too commands are not to be believed. But at the time when this letter was being sent, Ezekiel had not yet begun to prophesy in Babylon; for this word is sent at the beginning of King Zedekiah, while Ezekiel began to prophesy in the fifth year of the exile of Jeconiah, which was the same year of Zedekiah's reign. 3. Further, according to tropology, we ought to take as false prophets those who take the words of the Scriptures otherwise than the Holy Spirit sounds them, and as diviners those who pronounce the conjectures of their own mind and the uncertainties of things to come as though true, without the authority of the divine words; and as dreamers, those who do not hear that which is written: give no sleep to your eyes nor slumber to your eyelids, of whom Jude the Apostle too speaks: likewise these also, dreaming, defile the flesh, but despise dominion, whose mind is by no means awake, but, weighed down by the slumber of arrogance and error, is surrounded by the horror of night; to whom the Apostle Paul also speaks: rise, you who sleep, and arise from the dead, and Christ will enlighten you.

LXV. For thus says the Lord: When seventy years begin to be fulfilled in Babylon, I will visit you, and I will raise up over you my good word, to bring you back to this place. For I know the thoughts which I think over you, says the Lord, thoughts of peace and not of affliction, to give you an end and patience — or hope —. And you will call upon me and you will go — or, according to Symmachus, you will find — and you will pray to me, and I will hear you. You will seek me and you will find, when you seek me with your whole heart, and I will be found by you or I will appear to you. 2. 'Do not,' he says, 'believe your false prophets, diviners and dreamers, who promise you a near return to Jerusalem! For unless the seventy years have been completed — Cyrus, king of the Persians, releasing the captives —, you will by no means return to your fatherland; and then I will fulfill my promises, to bring you back to this place; for I know the thoughts which I think over you, says the Lord'. He says that he knows what he thinks, but that they, with their prophets, diviners and dreamers, do not know; the knowledge of things to come therefore belongs to God alone. 3. 'To give you,' he says, 'an end and patience: an end of the captivity and patience in the present labors, or hope of things to come. Then you will call upon me and you will go to Jerusalem and you will pray, and I will hear you.' Certainly the Lord could have fulfilled what he had promised without the invocation and prayer of the captives, but he exhorts them to prayers, so that they may deserve to receive what has been promised. You will seek and you will find me, when you seek with your whole heart, in accordance with that saying of the Gospel: ask, and you will receive; seek, and you will find; knock, and it will be opened to you. 4. According to anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense], we dwell in the 'confusion' of this world for as long as it takes until we deserve to receive the 'rest' of the number seven, and, repentance having been received, God fulfills what he promised, and we are brought back to our place, the Church; for this is why the Lord seemed to strike, that he might heal, and he will give us an end of our labor and of our patience. And we shall call upon him and shall return to the Church and shall pray, and we shall be heard. We shall seek and shall find him, when we have sought him with our whole heart, and then he will appear to us. 5. A certain one interprets the 'seventy years' according to that which is written: the days of our years, in them seventy years; and when these have been completed, then we may return to the Lord with our whole heart and be heard, and there may be an end of our labor and patience; for now we possess all things in shadow and image.

LXVI. ※ And I will be found by you, says the Lord, and I will bring back your captivity and gather you from all the nations and from all the places to which I drove you out, says the Lord, and I will make you return from the place to which I made you go into exile, because you have said: The Lord has raised up prophets for us in Babylon. 2. For thus says the Lord to the king who sits upon the throne of David, and to all the people, the inhabitant of this city, and to your brothers who did not go out with you into exile; thus says the Lord of hosts: Behold, I will send upon them the sword, famine and plague, and I will make them like bad figs, which cannot be eaten because they are very bad. 3. And I will pursue them with the sword, with famine and with pestilence, and I will give them over to vexation to all the kingdoms of the earth, for a curse and for astonishment and for hissing and for a reproach to all the nations to which I am casting them out, because they did not hear my words, says the Lord, which I sent to them through my servants, the prophets, rising up by night and sending; and you did not hear, says the Lord. You, therefore, hear the word of the Lord, all the exile, which I sent out from Jerusalem into Babylon! 4. ※ Up to this point, what I have marked beforehand with asterisks is not found in the Seventy; for the rest, in which either single verses or a few words have been passed over by them, I was unwilling, overcome by weariness, to note them, lest I should cause the reader disgust. Now the Lord promises those who were in exile that after seventy years of captivity he will make them return from all the nations and from all the places to which he had driven them out, and that, the captivity being released, they would recover their former state and fatherland. 5. 'And since I,' he says, 'am going to do these things of my own accord and to restore you at a fixed time, you are deceived in vain and think that you have prophets in Babylon who promise you false things; so know, then, that you ought by no means to hope for a return now, but to build houses, plant orchards, take wives and beget sons and multiply in number and await the promised time. 6. Hear what the Lord says to Zedekiah, who now reigns in Jerusalem, and to all the inhabitants of his city, that is, to your brothers, who were unwilling to obey my sentence and to migrate to Babylon with you: that they can by no means escape captivity, but will die by sword and famine and plague, and that he will make them like bad figs', which Theodotion and Aquila's first edition translated 'sudrinas', the second 'very bad', Symmachus 'last'; in Hebrew they are called 'suarim' [vile], but by the fault of copyists, in place of the middle syllable or letter 'alpha', the Greek 'delta' has crept in, so that 'sudrim' is read in place of 'suarim'. 7. And just as the basket or hamper which had good figs is said to have had 'first-ripe' ones, so the other basket, which had bad figs, is written to have had 'last' figs. 8. 'And I will pursue,' he says, 'them, who now dwell in the city of Jerusalem, with sword, famine and pestilence, so that, first by the siege, and then at once, those who have been able to remain and escape may be scattered into all lands and be an example of curse, astonishment and hissing and reproach to all among whom I shall cast them out, because they did not hear my words, says the Lord, which I spoke to them through my servants, rising up by night and sending, and I never ceased admonishing them to imitate you, who now in exile enjoy a secure leisure until the Lord's promise is fulfilled. But you, who obeyed my sentence and handed yourselves over to the Babylonian king, hear what I am about to say'. 9. And in this place a delirious interpreter dreams of the ruin of the heavenly Jerusalem and supposes that the prophecy is directed to those who dwell in the Babylonian region of this world, that they did well to descend of their own accord into these bodies and in the land of the Chaldeans to build houses, plant orchards, take wives, beget sons, and by good works to be restored after seventy years to their former place and to the heavenly Jerusalem; 10. but that those who were unwilling by their own will to descend to earthly things would suffer these things which the Lord threatens against Zedekiah and his people, who were unwilling to imitate their brothers or to come into Babylon: that he sends upon them the sword and famine and plague, that is, the want of all things, and makes them like very bad figs, which utterly cannot be eaten, and pursues them with an everlasting sword and gives them over to vexation to all the kingdoms of the earth, so that, namely, they become not men at all but demons, aerial powers, and are, before all the angels who preside over the several provinces, for a curse and for astonishment and for hissing and for a reproach to all the nations. 11. And that they would suffer this for the reason that they were unwilling to hear, in the heavenly Jerusalem, the words of the prophets, who exhorted them to descend to earthly things and take up the body of humiliation, and, having done penance, after the true sabbath-rest to possess their former place. 12. So much he may say; and when his disciples and the dung of the Grunnian household hear it, they think they are hearing divine mysteries, and us, who despise these things, they hold as brute animals and call pēlousiōtas [men of mud], because, set in the mud of this body, we cannot perceive heavenly things.

LXVII. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel, to Ahab son of Kolaiah and to Zedekiah son of Maaseiah, who prophesy to you in my name falsely: Behold, I will deliver them into the hand of Nebuchadnezzar king of Babylon, and he will strike them before your eyes. 2. And from them a curse will be taken up by all the exile of Judah that is in Babylon, saying: May the Lord make you like Zedekiah and like Ahab, whom the king of Babylon fried in the fire, because they did folly — or iniquity — in Israel and committed adultery with the wives of their friends — or fellow citizens — and spoke a word in my name falsely, which I did not command them; I am judge and witness, says the Lord. 3. The Hebrews say that these are the elders who did folly in Israel and committed adultery with the wives of their fellow citizens, to one of whom Daniel says: you who have grown old in evil days and to the other: seed of Canaan and not of Judah, beauty has deceived you and lust has perverted your heart! Thus you did to the daughters of Israel, and they in fear spoke with you; but a daughter of Judah did not endure your iniquity. 4. And what the prophet now says: and they spoke a word in my name falsely, which I did not command them, they think signifies this: that they so deceived wretched silly women, who are carried about by every wind of doctrine, as to tell them that, since they were of the tribe of Judah, Christ was to be begotten of their seed; and these women, enticed by desire, offered their bodies as if they would be the mothers of Christ. 5. But what is said in the present passage: whom the king of Babylon fried in the fire, seems to contradict the history of Daniel. For that asserts that they were stoned by the people at Daniel's sentence; but here it is written that the king of Babylon fried them in the fire. Hence by many, indeed by almost all the Hebrews, that very story is not received, being taken as a fable, nor is it read in their synagogues. 6. 'For how,' he says, 'could it be that captives had the power of stoning their princes and prophets?' And they affirm that this is rather the truth, which Jeremiah writes: that the elders were indeed convicted by Daniel, but that sentence was pronounced against them by the king of Babylon, who as victor and lord had authority over the captives. 7. How many from our own flock too, like Ahab and Zedekiah, prophesy falsehood in the name of the Lord and do folly in Israel and commit adultery with the wives of their fellow citizens, who were begotten in the same city of the Church! These the true Nebuchadnezzar fries in the fire of sin, as the prophet Hosea says: all who commit adultery are like an oven kindled by the one who bakes. 8. Happy he who takes up the yoke from his youth and sits alone, because he is filled with bitterness, and can say with David: I have not sat in the council of the malignant, and with those who do iniquity I will not go in! 9. And what the prophet has now added: I am judge and witness, says the Lord, has this sense: 'these things which I say about the two false prophets, who speak a word in my name falsely which I did not command them, I have by no means come to know by opinion, but I myself know them to be true, I from whom no one can hide nor escape the truth of my judgment'.

LXVIII. And to Shemaiah the Nehelamite you shall say; and what follows: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Because you have sent books in your own name to all the people that is in Jerusalem, is not found in the Seventy; and again they added of their own: I did not send you in my name, and consequently, in order: and to Zephaniah son of Maaseiah, the priest, and again from the Hebrew: and to all the priests; 2. and then the story is woven on: The Lord has made you priest in place of Jehoiada the priest, that you may be leader — or instructor and overseer — in the house of the Lord over every man who is possessed and prophesies, that you may put him in the stocks and in prison — or in custody and in the cataract, for which Symmachus put mochlon [a bar], and Aquila put the Hebrew word itself, 'sinac' —. 3. And now why have you not rebuked Jeremiah the Anathothite, who prophesies to you? For on this account he sent to us in Babylon, saying: It is long; build houses and dwell, and plant gardens and eat their fruit! So Zephaniah the priest read this book in the ears of Jeremiah the prophet. 4. Shemaiah, from the place 'Nehellami', which means 'torrent', had been led to Babylon with King Jeconiah and was prophesying falsely to the people that they would quickly return to Jerusalem. That he was a false prophet the following words of Jeremiah show: thus says the Lord to Shemaiah the Nehelamite: because Shemaiah has prophesied to you and I did not send him. 5. Since, therefore, Jeremiah had sent a letter to those who were in Babylon, saying: build houses and dwell; plant gardens and eat their fruit; take wives and beget sons! And after other things had added: let not your prophets who are in your midst, and your diviners, lead you on! And then: for these prophesy falsely to you in my name, and I did not send them, says the Lord, Shemaiah, understanding that it had been written against him under the common name of the false prophets, sends a letter to Jerusalem, to Zephaniah son of Maaseiah, the priest, and to the rest of the priests, against Jeremiah, asking why he was not rebuked by Zephaniah the priest, whose office it is to discern between prophets, which speak by the Holy Spirit and which by the contrary one, and why he was not ordered to be shut up in prison, that he might pay the penalty of his lie and cease to trip up the people any further. 6. Jehoiada was the priest who handed the rule to Joash after the killing of Athaliah and killed the priest of Baal. This, then, is what he writes: 'why do you not imitate Jehoiada the priest and kill Jeremiah the false prophet? For the Lord has appointed you in place of Jehoiada, that you may have care of the temple and especially discern who speak by the Holy Spirit and who by a demonic one' — and the Apostle too records that the discernings of spirits belong to the Lord's grace —; 'why,' he says, 'have you not rebuked Jeremiah the Anathothite?' 7. What he himself deserved as a false prophet he turns back upon the prophet, and he forestalls the truth with a lie. Hence too the sons of darkness are thought more prudent than the sons of light in this generation, and while we act patiently and wait for the salvation of the wretched, the heretics forestall us and call us by their own name, the blind leading the blind into the pit. 8. He sent, he says, to us in Babylon, saying: It is far off. This is all that grieves him: that against his lie Jeremiah wrote the truth, that the return was far off and that they would return to Jerusalem after seventy years; and that therefore they ought to build houses, plant orchards and eat their fruit, take wives and beget sons, as the preceding discourse related. When Zephaniah the priest had received this letter, which was written specifically in his name, he read it to Jeremiah, in a way insulting him and rebuking him by the very reading, as to why he dared to write such things to Babylon.

LXIX.
