And the word of the Lord came to Jeremiah, saying: Send to all the exile, saying: Thus says the Lord to Shemaiah the Nehelamite: Because Shemaiah has prophesied to you and I did not send him, and he has made you trust in a lie, therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will visit upon Shemaiah the Nehelamite and upon his seed; there shall not be a man sitting in the midst of this people, and he shall not see the good which I will do to my people, says the Lord, because he has spoken transgression against the Lord. 2. The false prophet Shemaiah, truly a 'Nehellamites', who had taken up foreign and troubled waters from the 'torrent', is angry that Jeremiah has written true things against his lie, and he sends letters to Zephaniah the priest, asking why the prophet dared to write the truth, and he desires that he be shut up in prison so that he may not speak. Zephaniah too secretly accuses the prophet, while he reads the letter of the liar and boasts that he holds his accusation. 3. How much more guilty of crime are those who defend false prophets and cherish liars and make the evil inventions of others their own sins! Let the false prophet therefore hear, let the priest understand what he himself also deserves to hear through him: thus says the Lord: Behold, I will visit upon Shemaiah. This the Lord speaks, not the prophet: that he will visit upon Shemaiah the Nehelamite, not for remedy but for the punishment of the liar, according to that which is written: I will visit their iniquities with the rod, and their sins with scourges. 4. Nor does he visit upon the false prophet alone, but also upon his seed, all the disciples whom he deceived by his error. There shall not be, he says, a man sitting in the midst of this people. Let the worst stock be blotted out of the assembly of the saints, and let him not sit in the midst of those at rest, who could not stand with those who stand, nor heard that saying: if he had stood in my substance. And whereas the Lord promises the perfection of all virtues when the sevenfold time is ended, he will not see the good which he claimed for himself in the present time. 5. Now all this will come about because he has spoken transgression against the Lord, in saying that, the captivity of sins being already loosed, they would return to Jerusalem; such men the Apostle threatens: already you are filled, already you have become rich, without us you have reigned; and would that you did reign, that we also might reign with you!

Book Six

I. The length of the book of Jeremiah the prophet overcomes our purpose, so that, however briefly, we nonetheless say much. Hence the present sixth book of the commentaries on Jeremiah will also contain the mystical promises which the Jews, and our Judaizers, think are to be fulfilled at the consummation of the world; for they cannot prove that they were already fulfilled under Zerubbabel. 2. We, however, following the authority of the apostles and evangelists, and above all of the Apostle Paul, show that whatever is promised carnally to the people of Israel has been fulfilled spiritually in us and is being fulfilled today, and that there is no other contest between Jews and Christians but this: that, while they and we both believe that Christ the Son of God has been promised, the things that are to be under Christ are said by us to have been fulfilled, by them to be yet fulfilled. 3. We, therefore, who believe that Christ has already come, must teach that the things which are said to be going to take place under Christ have been fulfilled, and that we are the sons of Abraham, of whom it is written: God is able from these stones to raise up sons to Abraham, to whom the promise was made: and in your seed all the nations shall be blessed. Teaching that this blessing was fulfilled in Christ, the vessel of election says: he did not say, he says, in seeds, but in seed, which is Christ. 4. Pray, therefore, brother Eusebius, to the Lord Jesus Christ, that with the same labor and grace of the Spirit with which we have interpreted the promises of the other prophets, and especially of Isaiah, we may be able to explain those of this prophet also, who, as much as he seems simple and easy in his words, is in the same measure most profound in the majesty of his meanings.

Jeremiah 30

II.

This word, which came to Jeremiah from the Lord, saying: Thus says the Lord God of Israel, saying: Write for yourself in a book all the words which I have spoken to you! For behold, days will come, says the Lord, and I will turn back the turning of my people Israel and Judah, says the Lord, and I will turn them back — or I will make them settle — into the land which I gave to their fathers, and they shall possess it. 2. While the false prophets in Babylon were promising that the people who had been taken captive with Jeconiah would soon return, and while Hananiah son of Azzur was preaching the same things in Jerusalem, Jeremiah the prophet both asserted that this would indeed come to pass, not within two years, as they lied, but when the seventy years were finished, and now he is ordered to write in a book and to hand down to remembrance the things which the Lord foretells are to come. 3. From this it is plain that the time of the prophecy is by no means near, but that these things are to be fulfilled after a long time, when Israel and Judah are to return to their land, and that what Ezekiel prophesied is to be fulfilled: that the two rods are to be joined together with each other, and that David the king will rule, of whom he writes: and my servant David shall be king over them, and there shall be one shepherd of them all. 4. Whatever, therefore, we said on that prophecy must be understood in the present passage as well, especially since Ezekiel in Babylon and Jeremiah in Jerusalem prophesied the same things, and at the same time.

III. And these are the words which the Lord spoke to Israel and to Judah, for thus says the Lord: We have heard a voice of terror — or of fear —; dread, and there is no peace. Ask and see whether a male gives birth! Why then have I seen the hand of every man upon his loins, as of a woman giving birth, and all faces have been turned into jaundice? 2. First sad things are announced, so that after the greatness of the evils glad things may follow; for health is more welcome when sickness has been driven off, and the greatness of pain is turned into a greatness of joys. What he says, then, is this: so great will be the fear and so great the dread that, peace having been put to flight, all things will be filled with wars and blood, and that a womanish terror will take hold even of the men, whose proper task is to fight against adversaries, and they will put their hands by no means to arms but to holding their kidneys, as if a woman giving birth were clutching her flanks and loins. Hence also the faces of all will be turned into jaundice, testifying by the pallor of the countenance to the terror of the heart. 3. Some interpret this passage according to tropology in such a way that they set down that testimony: From your fear, O Lord, we have conceived and travailed and brought forth; we have made the spirit of your salvation upon the earth, and they compare to this example that saying of the Apostle in which he says: My little children, with whom I am again in travail until Christ be formed in you; which plainly pertains not to terror but to joy, whereas the present Scripture signifies the time of the devastation and ruin of Israel.

IV. Woe, for great is that day, nor is there any like it, and it is the time of the tribulation of Jacob! and from it he shall be saved. He foretells the time of misery in order to bring in the time of joy. 'Although,' he says, 'such great evils have gone before that the pain of all the men is compared to the pain of a woman giving birth, yet the time of the tribulation of Jacob, that is, of the people of God, will be changed into prosperity, and from it too he shall be saved' — 'time' is understood —, 'of which the discourse had spoken'. But by 'Jacob' understand the twelve tribes, which were saved by no means under Zerubbabel, as some falsely suppose, but by the calling of the Gospel.

V. And it shall be in that day, says the Lord of hosts, I will break his yoke from off your neck and I will burst his bonds, and strangers shall no longer rule over him, but they shall serve — or work for — the Lord their God and David their king, whom I will raise up for them. 2. This is the David whom the Gospel also mentions: that he would grant us that, without fear, delivered from the hand of our enemies, we might serve him in holiness and righteousness before him all our days. For just as a first Adam and a second Adam are written of according to the truth of the body, so also the Lord and Savior is David, because according to the flesh he is from David, holy Mary contributing to him wholly whatever was of the stock of David, while she had the origin and conception from the Holy Spirit. 3. And as for his saying: I will break his yoke from off your neck and I will burst his bonds, there is no doubt that under the type of Nebuchadnezzar it must be understood of the devil.

VI. You therefore, do not fear, my servant Jacob, says the Lord, nor be afraid, Israel, for behold, I will save you from a far-off land, and your seed from the land of their captivity, and Jacob shall return and be at rest and abound in all things, and there shall be none whom he fears, since I am with you, says the Lord, to save you. For I will make a full end among all the nations among which I have scattered you; but you I will not bring to a full end, but I will chastise — or instruct — you in judgment, so that you may not seem to yourself guiltless or and cleansing, I will not cleanse you. 2. This perikopē [passage] is not found in the Seventy, and in very many manuscripts of the common edition it has been added under asterisks from Theodotion. The divine word makes a promise and, more familiarly, calls Jacob and Israel 'his servant', just as Abraham, Isaac and Jacob are called 'servants of God' — Moses too and the other prophets, and the Apostle Paul at the beginning of his epistles glories in this title —, so that both the two and the ten tribes, that is, the twelve, may know that they are to be saved from a far-off land, that the captivity is to be loosed and peace restored, and that they are to be filled with all abundance, according to what is said in the psalm: Let there be peace in your strength and abundance in your towers! 3. And this will come about because they will enjoy the presence of the Lord, when the hostile nations which had captured them will perish and these will be set free from among the nations. And he teaches that they were handed over by no means to punishment but to instruction, so that they might be judged as his own, not destroyed as strangers; for he who does not believe has already been judged, that is, judged beforehand unto destruction. 4. And what he added: so that you may not seem to yourself guiltless, or according to Symmachus: and cleansing, I will not cleanse you, or according to Aquila: when I have instructed you through judgment, I will by no means make you innocent, signifies that the whole world stands in need of the mercy of God, and that no one, however holy he may be, goes to the judge secure; against the new heresy sprung from the old, which supposes that in this age and in this mortal flesh, before this corruptible puts on incorruption and this mortal puts on immortality, there is perfection in anyone, and that the righteous man can fulfill all the virtues at once.

VII. For thus says the Lord: Incurable is your fracture, most grievous your wound. There is none who judges your judgment for binding up; there is no benefit of healings for you. All your lovers have forgotten you, they will not seek you; for I have struck you with the blow of an enemy, with cruel chastisement — or strong —. 2. Because of the multitude of your iniquity have become hard — or have been multiplied — your sins. Why do you cry out over your crushing? Incurable is your pain; because of the multitude of your iniquity and because of your hard sins I have done these things to you. 3. He speaks as to a beautiful woman, to whom he had said above: I will chastise you in judgment, so that you may not seem to yourself guiltless or innoxia [guiltless, in the feminine]; and by metaphoran [metaphor] he speaks to Jerusalem, that she has been most grievously wounded by the judgment of God and can be healed by no other curing her except him who had struck her — there is none, he says, who judges your judgment — nor draw the skin of a scar over the deepest wound. 'Wherever you turn, there is no benefit for you, because you have offended him who is the true and only physician. 4. All your lovers have forgotten you, whether the priests or the princes, or at any rate the protection of the angels by which, before you offended the Lord, you were walled about. They will not seek you, acting contrary to the Apostle, who sought the believers and not the things which were the believers'; for I have struck you with the blow of an enemy, with cruel chastisement.' 5. A friend strikes in one way, an enemy in another; a father in one way, a foe in another. The one beats in order to correct, the other strikes in order to kill; hence the prophet too says tearfully: O Lord, do not rebuke me in your fury, nor chasten me in your wrath! But this has happened because because of the multitude of your iniquity your sins have become hard. 6. And what follows: Why do you cry out over your crushing? Incurable is your pain because of the multitude of your iniquity, is not found in the Seventy, evidently because it is said a second time: because of the multitude of your iniquity and your hard sins, and those who copied from the beginning thought it had been added. 7. And the sense is: 'That I struck you with the blow of an enemy, that I beat you with cruel chastisement, the multitude of your iniquity and your hard sins brought about, which could not be healed except by the most biting powder and the burning cautery and the sharpest steel, with which I might cut away the putrid and incurable flesh. And yet because of the multitude of your iniquity and your hard sins I have done this to you, not by my own will, but because the method of medicine compelled me'.

VIII. Therefore all who devour you shall be devoured, and all your enemies shall be led into captivity, and those who lay you waste shall be laid waste, and all your plunderers I will give up as plunder. For I will draw a scar over you and from your wounds I will heal you, says the Lord, because they have called you cast out — or scattered —, Zion: this is she who had no one seeking her. 2. And we know that these things were done under Zerubbabel too. When the Babylonians and Chaldeans laid waste the Assyrians, that is, Nineveh, and the Medes and Persians in turn captured the Babylonians and Chaldeans and Babylon was destroyed, then Zion began to have the Lord seeking her, and a scar was drawn over her wound and she was healed of her blows; which is fulfilled more fully and more perfectly in Christ.

IX. Thus says the Lord: Behold, I will turn back the turning of the tents of Jacob, and on the dwellings — or captivity — of them I will have mercy, and the city shall be built on its height, and the temple shall be founded according to its order. And from them shall go forth praise and the voice of those playing, and I will multiply them and they shall not be diminished; and what follows: and I will glorify them and I will not make them few, is not found in the Seventy. 2. And his sons, he says, shall be as from the beginning, and his assembly shall abide before me, and I will visit against all who afflict him. And his leader shall be from himself, and a prince shall be brought forth from his midst, and I will draw him close and he shall approach me; for who is this who draws his heart close so as to come near to me?, says the Lord; and again, what follows in the Hebrew is not found in the Greek: and you shall be, he says, my people, and I will be your God. 3. A type of these things went before in Zerubbabel and Ezra, when the people returned and the city began to be built on its height and the worship of the temple to be observed, and the other things which are contained in the book of Ezra himself; but it was fulfilled more fully and more perfectly in the Lord and Savior and the apostles, when there was built the city on its height — of which it is written: a city that is set on a mountain cannot be hidden — and the temple according to its order and ceremonies was founded, so that whatever was done carnally among the former people might be fulfilled spiritually in the Church. 4. Then there went forth praise or thanksgiving — for this is what 'thoda' signifies —, so that all the apostles said: grace to you and peace, and the voice of those playing, not with that play with which the people ate and drank and rose up to play, but with that with which David played before the ark of the Lord. And they were multiplied and not diminished, so that the whole world believed in God the Savior, and they were glorified, so that what is written was fulfilled: Glorious things have been spoken of you, city of God! And his sons, that is, the apostles, were as Abraham, Isaac and Jacob, the princes of the race of Israel, were from the beginning. 5. Then the Lord visited against all who afflicted the people of God, namely the adverse powers. And there was his leader from himself — no doubt the Lord and Savior, according to the flesh from the race of Israel — and a prince from his midst was brought forth. The Father drew him close to himself, and he approached him, so that the Son might say: I in the Father and the Father in me, because no one can so draw his heart close to the Lord, nor be joined to him as the Son is to the Father. 6. And what he says according to the Hebrew only, but not according to the Seventy: and you shall be my people, and I will be your God, we see fulfilled in deed, in part in Israel and wholly in the multitude of the nations.

X. Behold, the whirlwind of the Lord, fury going forth, a storm rushing down, shall come to rest upon the head of the wicked. The Lord will not turn back the anger of his indignation until he does and fulfills the purpose of his heart; in the last of days you will understand these things. 2. The whirlwind of the Lord's fury and the rushing storm and tempest will rest upon the head of the wicked, whether of the demons or of those who blasphemed the Son of God; and he will not turn back the anger of his indignation until he does and fulfills the purpose of his heart, and until Jerusalem is surrounded by an army and utterly destroyed. Now just as a craftsman cannot be understood except when the work is completed, nor the diligence of medicine except after health has been attained, so, when the overthrow of Jerusalem and the casting off of the former people have come about, then believers will understand that the rejection of the Jews is the occasion of our salvation.

Jeremiah 31

XI.

At that time, says the Lord, I will be God to all the families of Israel — or to the race of Israel — and they shall be to me for a people. Unless the thought of the Lord has been fulfilled and his fury has come to rest upon the head of the ungodly, the Lord of the universe cannot be God to the families of Israel; but this he says to the remnant, which has been saved. 2. But if what is said is set against us: I will be God to the race of Israel or to all the families of Israel, let us take up an example: 'if you were children of Abraham, you would do the works of your father'; and the Apostle writes: behold Israel according to the flesh. From this he teaches that there is another Israel according to the spirit. That one, then, is Israel who discerns God with the mind, or is the most upright one of the Lord, and an Israel of this kind will be the people of God.

XII. Thus says the Lord: the people that had remained from the sword found grace in the desert; Israel shall go to its rest. LXX: thus said the Lord: I found warm in the desert with those who had perished by the sword; go, and do not kill Israel! 2. Ridiculously, in this place the Latin codices, through the ambiguity of the Greek word, translated 'lupines' for 'warm'; for the Greek THERMON signifies both, and it is not itself found in the Hebrew. For there is written 'hen', which Aquila, Symmachus and Theodotion translated as charin, that is, 'grace'; the Seventy alone put 'warm', thinking that the last letter was 'm'. For if we read 'hen' with the letter 'n', it means 'grace'; if with 'm', it is interpreted 'heat'. 3. Now the sense according to the Hebrew is this: the people of the Jews, which had remained from the Roman sword, or at any rate had been able to escape the wrath of the Lord's fury, found grace in the desert of the nations, so that amid the crowd of the nations it might be saved in the Church; whence Israel shall go and shall find its rest, which it had always hoped for, which the prophecies of the prophets had promised it. 4. Further, according to the Seventy this is the understanding: the Lord found the apostles and their companions warm and living in the desert of the nations, among those who had been killed by their own unbelief and did not have the warmth of life. Hence it is commanded to the angels and to those who are in the ministry of God that they should not kill all, that Israel should not be utterly destroyed, and it is said to them: 'go, and do not kill Israel; let there be some who live, let there be those who are warm with the ardor of faith, let there be those who escape the cold of unbelief and of death, whom the Lord may find in the desert!'

XIII. From afar the Lord appeared to me — or to him — : and with an everlasting love I have loved you; therefore I have drawn you, having pity. And again I will build you and you shall be built, virgin Israel. Again you shall be adorned with your timbrels — or you shall take up your timbrels — and you shall go forth in the dance of those who make merry. Again you shall plant vineyards on the mountains of Samaria; plant plantings and gather the vintage! for there shall be a day on which the watchmen on Mount Ephraim shall cry: arise, and let us go up into Zion to the Lord our God! 2. Because Israel had offended the Lord and had said: we have no king but Caesar, and: come, let us kill him, and the inheritance will be ours, and had withdrawn far from God, therefore the Lord after a long time appeared to him — not in the time of Zerubbabel and Ezra, after they were taken captive again —, but with an everlasting love, which will be blotted out by no end, he loved him, and drew him, having pity; for he was saved not at all by merit, but by clemency. 3. And again, he says, I will build you and you shall be built, virgin Israel. Let us understand this properly of the Church; for they rave who sigh for a golden and jeweled Jerusalem, consecrating their own avarice in the mystery of the Lord's city. 'Again you shall be adorned with your timbrels, so that you may sing to the Lord in the churches, all the flesh of evil works in you having been consumed, and you shall go forth in the dance of those who make merry with the crowds of the nations, you shall plant vineyards on the mountains of Samaria', not at all in the valleys and low places, but on the mountains of Samaria, which after the captivity of the people of Israel were possessed by foreigners, to whom it is said: plant plantings and gather the vintage! 4. Then was the day of the Lord, on which the watchmen — namely the apostles and apostolic men — cried on mount 'Samaria' and on mount 'Ephraim', of which the one sounds 'guard', the other 'fruitfulness'; but what do the watchmen of Samaria say, nay rather, what do they cry on Mount Ephraim? 'Arise, you who lie down, leave lowly things, spurn the offerings of victims' — a sacrifice to the Lord is a troubled spirit —, 'let us go up into Zion, that is, into the Church, where there is 'contemplation' and the beholding of God; and when we are in Zion, nay rather have gone up to it, we shall go up likewise to the Lord our God'.

XIV. For thus says the Lord: exult with gladness, Jacob, and neigh against the head of the nations; sound aloud, sing and say: save, O Lord, your people, the remnant of Israel! Significantly, not the whole of Israel is saved, but the remnant of Israel, the Lord commanding and saying: 'exult with gladness, you who are of Jacob, and neigh toward the head of the nations, referring all things to it, because the tail was once turned into the head; sound aloud and sing and say!' What is it that they are bidden to say? 2. Save, O Lord, your people. Which people? Surely the remnant of Israel, which has been saved according to election, of whom Paul too speaks, taking up the testimony of Isaiah: unless the Lord of Sabaoth had left us seed, we should have become as Sodom and we should have been made like Gomorrah.

XV. Behold, I will bring them from the land of the north and will gather them from the ends of the earth; and what follows: at the solemnity of the Passover; and it shall beget many sons, is not found in the Hebrew, but is read only in the Seventy, in place of which among the Hebrews is written: among whom shall be the blind and the lame and the woman with child and the woman giving birth together, a great church of those returning hither. 2. The remnant of the people of Israel is gathered through the apostles and apostolic men, of whom we read above: the watchmen shall cry on the mount, and to whom it is commanded: sound aloud and sing and say, so that the remnant of Israel may be saved. 3. And the Lord himself promises that he will bring them back from the land of the north, which is a most harsh wind, but by name is called right, from unbelief, from the cold of the Lord's love, and will gather them from the ends of the earth, not at another time, but at the solemnity of the Passover, that is, on the festal days of the Lord's passion, when the Lord was crucified and that was fulfilled which he himself promised in the Gospel: when I am lifted up, I will draw all things to myself. Then it begot a great people, so that that saying of Isaiah might be fulfilled: for a nation is born at once; for in one day three thousand and five thousand men believed. 4. And what is written in the Hebrew: among whom shall be the blind and the lame and the woman with child and the woman giving birth together, a great church of those returning hither, although it has also been fulfilled according to the letter, in that the blind saw and the lame walked, yet it can better be understood according to anagōgēn [the higher, spiritual sense]: that those who before were blind with faithlessness afterwards believed in the Savior, and those who were lame, to whom Elijah once spoke: how long do you limp on both feet?, afterwards walked, and the people who sat in darkness and the shadow of death saw a great light, and the lame ran, and the woman with child bore sons, a great church of those returning to the faith. 5. The Jews think that this was fulfilled when under Ezra, after the day of the Passover, they went out from Babylon to return to Jerusalem, in which there was a type and not the truth; for they will not be able to prove that at that time all the things which we have read and are going to read were fulfilled.

XVI. With weeping they shall come — or they shall go out — and in mercy I will lead them back, and I will bring them through torrents of waters in a straight way, and they shall not stumble — or they shall not go astray — in it, for I have become a father to Israel, and Ephraim is my firstborn. 2. If we read according to the Hebrew: with weeping they shall come — for this is what 'iabu' indicates —, we shall say that sometimes weeping is also a sign of excessive joy, according to that saying: I weep for joy. But if according to the Seventy, who said: with weeping they shall go out and in mercy — or in consolation — I will lead them back, we shall set down that sense which is also spoken in the Psalms: going they went and wept, carrying their seeds; but coming they shall come with exultation, carrying their sheaves. 3. For they wept when they were led away captive, and they received the greatest consolation when they were led back by the mercy of the Lord. And the Lord brought them, through the apostles and apostolic men, full of waters and of a most abundant river, in a straight way, namely that of faith, not in the faithlessness of the Jews. And they shall not, he says, stumble in it, because they have ceased to be blind, to whom it was once said: if you were blind, you would not have sin; but now you say, We see; your sin remains. 4. We can also understand the 'straight way' as Christ, in whom whoever walks will not stumble. I have become, he says, a father to Israel, who has been led back, and Ephraim is my firstborn; for where once sin abounded, grace abounded the more. And Scripture testifies that Ephraim is in the type of the people gathered from the nations. 5. For he was the younger son of Joseph and stole away the birthright of Manasseh, who by nature was the firstborn; but in the mystery of the cross, the hands being crossed, he who stood at Jacob's left received the blessing of his right hand, and he who had stood at the right, blessed with the left, was reduced to the second rank. And as Jacob from Esau, so Ephraim snatched the birthright from Manasseh, and the whole people of the ten tribes was called Ephraim, because Jeroboam son of Nebat, from this tribe, was the first to obtain the kingdom in Samaria.

XVII. Hear the word of the Lord, O nations, and declare it to the islands that are far off, and say: he who scattered Israel will gather him and will keep him as a shepherd his flock, for the Lord has redeemed Jacob and has delivered him from the hand of one more powerful — or stronger —. And they shall come and shall praise on Mount Zion and shall flow together to the good things of the Lord, over grain and wine and oil and the young of flocks and of herds. Their soul shall be like a watered garden — or like a fruit-bearing tree — and they shall hunger no more. 2. Then the virgin shall rejoice in the dance — or virgins — and young men and old together, and I will turn their mourning into joy and will console them and make them glad from their sorrow, and I will inebriate the soul of the priests with fatness — or of the sons of Levi — and my people shall be filled with my good things, says the Lord. 3. The calling of the nations is clearly shown, Scripture saying: hear the word of the Lord, O nations, and declare it to the islands that are far off, and say! What do they declare to the islands that are situated far off? That the Lord, who scattered Israel, himself will gather him — it was not at all, then, of the power of the adversaries that they were scattered, but of the Lord's will — and will keep him as a shepherd his flock — for the good shepherd lays down his life for his sheep —, for the Lord has redeemed Jacob at the price of his own blood and has delivered him from the hand of one more powerful or stronger. 4. By this it is shown that the adversary powers are stronger than the nature of human frailty, and, as to the strength that is in each, that they are stronger by nature, but we stronger by faith, if only we deserve to be delivered by him who can bind the strong man and plunder his house. 5. And they shall come, he says, — no doubt delivered from the hand of the powerful — and shall praise their deliverer on Mount Zion, that is, in the Church, and shall flow together to the good things of the Lord, the abundance of all things, which is perceived not in the fruits and foods of this flesh, but in the variety of the virtues. 6. Over grain, he says, and wine and oil — from which the bread of the Lord is made, and the type of his blood is fulfilled, and the blessing of sanctification is shown, Scripture saying: God, your God, has anointed you with the oil of gladness above your fellows — and the young of flocks, who are the simple in the Church, and of herds, which are horned and toss the adversaries. 7. But that we may know that these blessings do not pertain at all to the body but to the soul, there follows: and their soul shall be like a watered garden or like a fruit-bearing tree, which is planted beside the streams of waters, and the paradise of the Lord in delights. 8. And no more, he says, shall they hunger; not at all with that hunger of which it is written: blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, but with that which is changed by satiety and shuts out the want of all things. Then the virgin shall rejoice in the dance — of whom the Apostle writes: for I have betrothed you to one husband, to present a chaste virgin to Christ — and the young men — to whom John speaks: I write to you, young men, because you have overcome the evil one — and the old, to whom the same testifies in mystical speech: I write to you, fathers, because you have known him who is from the beginning. 9. And I will turn, he says, their mourning into joy — so that the resurrection may gladden those whom the cross had terrified — and I will console them and make them glad from their sorrow — according to what the Lord says: blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be consoled — and I will inebriate the soul of the priests, who have the knowledge of God, from whose mouth they ask the law of the Lord, who believe in him to whom the prophet sings: you are a priest forever according to the order of Melchizedek. 10. But what follows according to the Seventy: of the sons of Levi, is not found in the Hebrew, and it is clear that here it is not at all said of those priests who are sons of Levi, but of those in whose type Melchizedek went before. And the 'inebriation' of the priests is proved in the apostles too, when they were fervent in faith and were said to be full of new wine; whence also the place in which the Lord was seized is called 'Gessemani', which in our language sounds 'valley of fatness'. 11. And when the priests have been made fat with the teaching of the Lord and, inebriated at the banquet of Joseph, have said: your cup that inebriates, how excellent it is!, then this too will come about which the Lord now promises: and my people shall be filled with my good things. All these things are now granted in part; but then they will be given in full, when we see face to face and the body of our lowliness has been changed by the glory of the resurrection.

XVIII. Thus says the Lord: a voice of lamentation was heard on high, of weeping and mourning, of Rachel bewailing her sons, and she would not be consoled over her sons, because they are not. LXX: thus said the Lord: a voice was heard in Ramah, lamentation and weeping and mourning, of Rachel bewailing her sons, and she would not be quieted, because they are not. 2. Matthew took the testimony neither according to the Hebrew nor according to the Seventy; for we read in him, after the description of the slaughter of the infants: then was fulfilled what was spoken through Jeremiah the prophet, saying: a voice was heard in Ramah, wailing and much howling, of Rachel weeping for her sons, and she would not be consoled, because they are not. From this it is clear that the evangelists and apostles did not at all follow anyone's translation from the Hebrew, but, as Hebrews from Hebrews, expressed in their own words what they read in Hebrew. 3. Rachel, the mother of Joseph, when she was coming to Bethlehem, was suddenly seized with the pain of childbirth and bore a son, whom the midwife, because she was dying in giving birth, called Benoni, that is, son of my sorrow; but his father Jacob changed the name and called him Benjamin, that is, son of the right hand. 4. It is asked, then, how Matthew the evangelist transferred the testimony of the prophet to the killing of the little ones, when it is clearly written of the ten tribes, whose chief was of Ephrathah, and it is not at all in the tribe of Ephraim, but in the tribe of Judah. For this same place is also Bethlehem, diōnymos [double-named], whence also the names of both agree; 'Bethleem' is called 'house of bread', 'Ephratha' karpophoria, which we can call 'fruitfulness'. 5. Since, then, Rachel was buried in Ephrathah, that is, in Bethlehem — as both holy Scripture and the inscription of her tomb testify to this day —, she is said to weep for the children who were killed near her and in her own regions. 6. Some of the Jews interpret this place thus: that, when Jerusalem was taken under Vespasian, countless thousands of captives were sent to Rome by this road, by Gaza and Alexandria; others, that in the last captivity under Hadrian, when the city of Jerusalem too was overthrown, a numberless people of various ages and of both sexes was sold at the market of the Terebinth; and that for this reason it is abhorrent to the Jews to visit that most frequented market. 7. Let them say what they will; we shall say that the evangelist Matthew rightly took the testimony for the place in which Rachel was buried and wept for the sons of the neighboring villages round about as if they were her own.

XIX. Thus says the Lord: let your voice cease from wailing and your eyes from tears, for there is a reward for your work, says the Lord, and they shall return from the land of the enemy — or of the enemies — and there shall be hope for your last end, says the Lord, and the sons shall return to their borders. 2. This has not yet happened according to the letter — for we do not read that the ten tribes, which are in exile in the cities of the Medes and of the Persians, have returned to the land of Judea —, but according to the spirit it was fulfilled in the passion of the Lord and is being fulfilled up to now, when Israel is saved from the whole world and it is said to Rachel: let your voice cease from wailing and your eyes from tears!, and the sense is: 'cease to wail — for the Lord has looked upon your former works — and your sons shall return from the land of the enemy, and do not be held by present sorrow! for there is hope in your last end, says the Lord, and your sons shall return to their borders, which their fathers had, Abraham, Isaac and Jacob'. 3. But we understand it better of the little ones: that they have the reward of their blood shed for Christ, and in place of the land of Herod the enemy they hold the kingdoms of heaven, and they will return to their former seat when, in place of the body of lowliness, they receive a glorious body. This is the last hope, when the righteous shall shine like the sun, and the infants, once little ones and sucklings, rise again without the increase of years and without the injuries and toil of the body. Into the perfect man, into the measure of the fullness of Christ.

XX. Hearing I heard Ephraim going into exile — or lamenting — : you have chastised me and I was instructed like an untamed bullock — or like a calf, and I did not learn — ; convert me, and I shall be converted, for you are the Lord my God. For after you converted me — or I was taken captive —, I repented, and after I knew — or you showed me —, I struck my thigh — or I groaned — ; I was confounded and blushed — or from the day of confusion and I showed you —, because I bore the reproach of my youth. 2. God says what he heard Ephraim saying and lamenting. Now no one doubts that it signifies the ten tribes, over which Jeroboam son of Nebat first ruled, who also made the golden calves in Dan and Bethel, so that the people, seduced by this error, might cease to worship and adore the God of Israel. You have chastised me, he says, and I was chastised. Every correction, which for the present seems to be of sadness, profits for salvation and afterwards brings peaceable fruits. 3. And what he says: like an untamed bullock or a calf, and I did not learn, signifies this: that he was instructed with much toil and with blows, so that he might be turned to repentance, and he did not profit. 'Convert', he says, 'me, and I shall be converted' — therefore even this very thing, that we repent, we cannot accomplish unless we rely on the help of God — ; 'for after you have converted me and I have been converted to you, then I shall know that you are the Lord my God, and not at all my errors and sins. And after you converted me, I repented'. 4. See how great is the help of God and how frail the human condition, that this very thing, that we repent, we are not at all able to accomplish unless the Lord has first converted us. 'And after', he says, 'you showed me either repentance itself or the knowledge of you, or I knew you, I struck my thigh.' This is the sign of one grieving and lamenting and bewailing his former error: that he strikes his thigh with his hand and confesses that he was foolish before. 5. I was confounded, he says, and blushed or from the day of confusion. For what time is not one of our confusion, if we recall our ancient sins and call to memory all the things we have done ill? And what the Seventy said: and I showed you, signifies that, after he groaned and knew his own offenses, he then came to such progress that he even showed God to others who did not know him, according to what David too says in repentance: I will teach the wicked your ways, and the ungodly shall be converted to you. 6. Because I bore, he says, the reproach of my youth. He says that he sinned through the ignorance of his age, so that he may more easily obtain pardon, according to what David too sings: the offenses of my youth and my ignorances do not remember! Whence also in what follows the Lord calls him 'a little one' and 'abounding in delights'; and this he says on account of the greatness of its riches and the fertility of the land, in which the tribe of Ephraim luxuriates to this day.

XXI. Is Ephraim an honorable son — or beloved — to me, is he a delicate child? for, since I spoke of him — or my words in him —, I will still remember him; therefore my bowels are troubled over him; pitying I will have pity on him, says the Lord. 2. Since Ephraim repents and says at the beginning: you have instructed me and I was instructed like an untamed bullock, and at the end: because I bore the reproach of my youth, the Lord answers, and sustains him, turned to him with his whole mind, with such an oracle: 'Ephraim is a beloved son to me, whom I loved so much from the beginning that I preferred him to his brother Manasseh.' 3. An honorable son, who against the order of nature received the honor of the firstborn by the condescension of the Lord; a delicate child, of whom he writes: the sons of Ephraim, bending and shooting the bow, turned back in the day of battle, against whom and to whom the whole book of the prophet Hosea is addressed, whom Jacob blesses. And let us take 'delights' in this place according to what is said in the psalm: delight in the Lord, and he will give you the petitions of your heart, for which in the Greek and in the Hebrew it has: abound in delights; whence also the paradise in Eden is called the paradise 'of delights'. 4. 'For', he says, 'there were my words in him, with remembrance I will still be mindful of him.' Lest the blessing be thought gratuitous, and to have been conferred out of the indulgence of the giver rather than by the merit of him on whom it was bestowed, therefore he says: 'I will remember him, for my words were in him, not in his mouth, not on his lips, but in the innermost affection of the heart; for which cause also my bowels are troubled over him'. 5. To him he speaks through Hosea too: what shall I do to you, Ephraim? what shall I do to you, Israel? shall I make you as Admah and as Zeboiim? my heart is turned within me, my bowels are troubled. I will not execute the fury of my wrath and I will not destroy Ephraim. Pitying I will have pity on him, says the Lord. 'My words indeed were in him, and he received all my commandments with an eager mind and kept them in his heart; but nevertheless pitying I will have pity on him', to show that all the righteousness of men needs the mercy of God.

XXII. Set up for yourself watchtowers, put for yourself bitternesses, direct your heart into the straight way in which you walked; return, virgin Israel, return to these your cities! How long will you be dissolved in delights, wandering daughter? for the Lord has created a new thing upon the earth: a woman shall encompass a man. 2. LXX: set up for yourself lookouts, make punishment, give your heart to your shoulders; the way in which you walked, return, virgin Israel, return to your cities mourning! How long will you turn away, despised daughter? for the Lord has created you as salvation for a new planting; in your salvation men shall go about. 3. Where we said: how long will you be dissolved in delights?, Symmachus put: how long will you sink into the deep? Now I have set out each edition in full, in order to show that a most obscure passage, one containing the mysteries of the Church, was either unknown to or omitted by the Seventy — or whoever else translated this prophet. 4. The Hebrew word 'sionim' is rendered either as 'lookouts' or as 'watchtowers', as Aquila and Symmachus translated. Hence I wonder what the common edition meant by putting 'Sion' in place of 'sionim', that is, 'lookouts', and so confusing the reader's understanding that he would think that after Ephraim the word of God had suddenly been addressed to Zion and to the tribe of Judah, although the discourse is continuously to Ephraim, to whom he also spoke above: hearing I heard Ephraim, and: Ephraim is an honorable son to me or a delicate child, and to whom he now also speaks: 'set up for yourself watchtowers or lookouts, to announce to you beforehand that so great a happiness of all things is coming.' 5. And what follows, 'bitternesses', which in Hebrew are called 'themrurim', for which Symmachus translated 'changes', indicates this: that she ought to weep, either for her ancient sins or from the greatness of her joy, and to be converted to God with her whole mind, and to set or direct her heart into the way by which she set out; for by that way she will return. 6. And what the Seventy said in place of this: give your heart to your shoulders, signifies that she ought to join her thoughts to works, or to contemplate the shoulders of those carrying her, bringing her back from captivity; which Isaiah sets out more fully in the camels and chariots and litters, asserting that they are to be brought back. 7. 'Return', he says, 'virgin Israel, return to your cities, which you deserted when they were captive! How long will you be dissolved in negligence and wander in deep error? Look at what I am about to say, and attend diligently to whence so great a blessedness is to be hoped for by you; hear what you had never known before! The Lord has created a new thing upon the earth: without the seed of a man, without any intercourse and conception, a woman shall encompass a man in the bosom of her womb, who, according to the increments of age indeed, through wailings and infancy will be seen to advance in wisdom and age, but a perfect man will be contained in a woman's belly for the usual months.' 8. Hence both Symmachus and Aquila translated in accordance with our edition. But what the common edition meant in this place I could say, and could find some sense, were it not sacrilege to argue about the words of God with human sense. Theodotion, however, himself also agreeing with the common edition, translated: the Lord has created a new salvation, in salvation man shall go about, putting the singular for the plural; and at the same time this too is to be noted, that the nativity of the Savior and the conception of God is called 'creation'.

XXIII. Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: they shall still say this word in the land of Judah and in its cities, when I turn back their captivity: the Lord bless you, beauty of righteousness, holy mountain! And Judah shall dwell in it, and all its cities together, husbandmen and those who drive flocks. 2. LXX: thus said the Lord of powers, the God of Israel: they shall still say this speech in the land of Judah and in its cities, when I bring back its exile: blessed be the Lord upon his righteous holy mountain, and those who dwell in the land of Judea and in every city of it with the husbandman; and he shall be lifted up in the flock. 3. According to the Hebrew it is plain that at the return of Israel, its captivity having been turned back into its own land, the cities of Judah dwell, and it is said to them one by one: the Lord bless you, who is truly the beauty of righteousness and the holy mountain, in which whoever dwells will fear no snares; and Judah will dwell without iniquity in its cities, and there will be husbandmen and a multitude of cattle. This seems to have been fulfilled in part under Zerubbabel and Ezra, but let the fullness of the prophecy be referred to the times of Christ: either to the first coming, when these things were done spiritually, or to the second, when all things are fulfilled — according to us spiritually, according to the Jews and our Judaizers carnally. 4. Further, according to the Seventy this is the sense: 'this speech shall still be spoken in the land of Judah and in its cities, when I turn back its captivity'. What shall be spoken? Blessed be the Lord upon his righteous holy mountain. The mountain which deserves to receive the name 'of righteousness and of holiness' is no other than the Savior; for the rest, it is foolish, with Jewish error, to believe an irrational and insensible mountain 'righteous and holy'. 5. He it is of whom it is also written in what follows: and in every city of it — understood: of the Savior — with the husbandman; no doubt it signifies God, of whom it is written in the Gospel too: I am the vine, you the branches, and my Father is the husbandman; whence also the Apostle says: you are God's husbandry, God's building. And what follows: and he shall be lifted up in the flock, shows this: that in the several flocks the righteous and holy one and husbandman, the Lord himself, is lifted up, and in his servants and believers ascends to the heights.

XXIV. For I have inebriated the weary soul — or for I have inebriated every thirsting soul — and every hungering soul I have satisfied — or I have filled —, therefore I awoke and saw, and my sleep was sweet to me. 2. The change of persons makes the understanding of the prophets obscure. The Lord had said: they shall still say this word in the land of Judah and in its cities, when I turn back their captivity. And what shall they say? No doubt this, which follows: the Lord bless you, beauty of righteousness, holy mountain, and the rest. And again, while they say these things, the Lord answers: for I have inebriated the weary soul — or thirsting — and every hungering soul I have satisfied. 3. And while he says these things, the people that had come from captivity answers: therefore I awoke and saw, and my sleep was sweet to me. For the Lord inebriates the weary or thirsting soul, and says in the Gospel: whoever thirsts, let him come to me and drink, and: whoever believes in me, as the Scripture says, out of his belly shall flow rivers of living water; and he satisfies every hungering and thirsting soul, of which thirst and hunger the same testifies in the Gospel: blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied. 4. And it is to be noted that 'inebriation' in this place is put in a good sense, of which in the Canticle too it is said: eat, friends, drink and be inebriated, dearest ones! With this inebriation Joseph too was inebriated with his brothers at midday. And those who had been weary and had hungered, inebriated and satisfied, give thanks, answering: 'I awoke and saw the Lord', namely raising them up and saying: arise, you who sleep, and rise from the dead, and Christ will enlighten you. 5. 'And my sleep', he says, 'was sweet to me, so that I might imitate the words of my Lord, who says: I slept and was put to rest; and I rose up, because the Lord sustained me'.

XXV. Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will sow the house of Israel and the house of Judah with the seed of men and the seed of beasts. And as I watched over them to pluck up and to demolish and to scatter and to destroy and to afflict, so I will watch over them to build and to plant, says the Lord. In those days they shall no longer say: the fathers have eaten a sour grape and the teeth of the sons are set on edge; but each one shall die in his own iniquity; every man who eats the sour grape, his teeth shall be set on edge. 2. 'House' and 'house', that is, the house of Israel and the house of Judah, is not in the Seventy, but only: Israel and Judah, so that it reads: and I will sow Israel and Judah. And what he added: with the seed of men and the seed of beasts, we ought to refer to the rational and the simple. And as at the beginning of Jeremiah it was said to him: 'behold, I have set you this day over nations and over kingdoms, to pluck up and pull down and destroy and scatter and build and plant, — and I watched over them, to do what I threatened, — so', he says, 'now I will watch over them to build and to plant; God's indeed husbandry, God's building you are.' 3. All promises of this kind are thought, according to the Jews and our Judaizers, to be fulfilled in a kingdom of a thousand years. But we, since the Apostle says: I planted, Apollos watered, but God gave the increase, and the prophet Isaiah records that the Savior is the builder of hedges and of houses, maintain that they were fulfilled spiritually at the first coming of Christ — and fulfilled in part, not in whole, because now we see in a mirror and in an enigma, and we do not know as we ought to know; but when that which is perfect has come, then that which is in part will be done away — or at any rate we believe that they are to be fulfilled at the second coming, when the Lord will appear in his majesty and the fullness of the nations has come in, so that all Israel may be saved, and not at all in part, through individuals, but that God may be all in all. 4. And what is brought in: in those days they shall no longer say: the fathers have eaten a sour grape and the teeth of the sons are set on edge, and the rest, we discussed more fully in the explanations of Ezekiel, when we interpreted that place: son of man, what is this parable to you among the sons of Israel, who say: the fathers have eaten a sour grape and the teeth of the sons are set on edge? As I live, says the Lord, if this parable shall be any longer in Israel! for all souls are mine; as the soul of the father, so also the soul of the son; the soul that sins, it shall die. By this we learn that it is not the Lord who makes death, but sin; for the soul that sins, it shall die. 5. And this is said in the present place: that Israel will not at all stumble forever because of the sins of the fathers, but will be saved by its own merits and by faith in Christ after many ages. And it is to be observed that vices and sins are called 'a sour grape', so that the teeth of those who eat are set on edge and cannot perceive the sweetness of him of whom it is said: taste and see that the Lord is sweet! 6. Whoever does not understand the Scriptures as the truth of the matter holds eats a sour grape; whence also all heretics, believing perverse things, cannot eat the bread that comes down from heaven, but their teeth are set on edge, not by the harshness of the foods, but by the fault of the teeth.

XXVI. Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and I will strike — or I will dispose — for the house of Israel and the house of Judah a new covenant — or testament — not according to the covenant — or testament — which I made with your fathers on the day when I took their hand to lead them out of the land of Egypt, a covenant — or testament — which they made void; and I ruled over them — or I neglected them —, says the Lord. 2. And this will be the covenant — or testament — which I will strike with the house of Israel after those days, says the Lord: I will put my law in their inward parts — or in the mind — and in their heart I will write it, and I will be to them for God and they shall be to me for a people. 3. And they shall no longer teach, each one his neighbor and each one his brother, saying: know the Lord, for all shall know me, from the least of them to the greatest, says the Lord, for I will be propitious to their iniquity and of their sin I will be mindful no more. 4. The apostle Paul — or whoever else wrote the epistle — used this testimony to the Hebrews, and all churchmen after him say that all things were fulfilled at the first coming of the Savior, and that the New Testament, that is, the Gospel, succeeded to the Old Testament, and that from it the law of the letter has been exchanged for the law of the spirit, so that all the sacrifices too, and circumcision, and the sabbath, might be fulfilled spiritually. But that we put 'pactum' [covenant] for 'testamento' [testament] belongs to the Hebrew truth, although a testament too is rightly called a covenant, because in it is contained the will and the attestation of those who enter into a covenant. 5. When Israel was led out of the land of Egypt, so great was God's familiarity with that people that he says he took it by the hand and gave a covenant, which they made void; and therefore the Lord neglected them. 6. But now in the Gospel, after the cross, the resurrection and the ascension, he promises that he gives a covenant not on tablets of stone, but on fleshly tablets of the heart, and that, when the testament of the Lord has been written in the mind of believers, he himself is to them for God and they are to him for a people, so that they do not at all seek Jewish teachers and the traditions and commandments of men, but are taught by the Holy Spirit, if only they deserve to hear: you are the temple of God and the Spirit of God dwells in you. The Spirit, however, breathes where he wills, and has diverse graces, and the knowledge of the one God is the possession of all the virtues. 7. 'And this', he says, 'will come about because I will be propitious to their iniquity and their sin I will remember no more.' From this it is clear that, according to the understanding of this perikopēs [passage], the foregoing is to be understood of the first coming of the Savior, when both peoples, Israel and Judah, were joined to each other. 8. But if it raises a scruple for anyone why he said: I will dispose for the house of Israel and the house of Judah a new covenant — or testament — not according to the covenant which I made with your fathers, let him understand that the first Church of Christ was from the Jews, and that the Lord Savior came to them and said: I have not come except to the lost sheep of the house of Israel, and that the apostles confirmed this very thing: it was necessary that the word of God be spoken first to you; but since you reject it and judge yourselves unworthy of eternal life, behold, we turn to the nations. 9. For it was not right to give the children's bread to dogs; but because the sons were unwilling to receive the father coming into his own, he gave to all the power that those who received him should become sons of God.

XXVII. Thus says the Lord, who gives the sun for the light of the day, the order of the moon and of the stars for the light of the night, who troubles the sea and its waves sound; the Lord of hosts is his name. If these laws — or these statutes — cease before me, says the Lord, then the seed of Israel too shall fail, so as not to be before me all the days. 2. And at the beginning of Genesis we read that the sun was set in the heaven for the light of the day, and the moon too and the stars for the light of the night, and in the psalm: day to day utters the word and night to night shows knowledge, in that day and night succeed one another in turn. 3. 'As', he says, 'the order of things, and especially of the heavenly stars, cannot be changed, and the waves of the sounding sea roll to the shores, and the terrible roar of the gulfs and of the swelling waves is heard, and it cannot advance further than has been prescribed by the command of God, according to that saying: you have set a bound which they shall not pass over, nor shall they turn back to cover the earth, so', he says, 'the seed and race of Israel will by the will of the Lord be perpetual and will never fail.' But 'laws' here are not to be understood as the Mosaic ones, but as the constitution and order of nature. 4. Let us ask the Jews: 'if the heavens shall perish and all shall grow old like a garment, and it is said to God: but you yourself are, and your years do not fail, how can the seed of Israel be perpetual?' For either, when the heavens perish, the seed of Israel too will perish, or, if it is perpetual, then neither will the heavens perish. But if Scripture cannot lie and the heavens are going to perish, then the seed of Israel too will perish, especially since Jacob too says to his sons: come, and I will declare to you what is to come in the last days; and when it is said: in the last days, then the world will cease to be and another dispensation of things will come about. This against them. 5. For the rest, for our own people the Gospel too shows that this world is not perpetual, saying: heaven and earth shall pass away, and: behold, I am with you all days, even to the consummation of the age. Let us also say it otherwise: that as long as this world lasts, the seed of Israel and the Jewish nation will remain, not in those who are now unbelieving, but in those who believed with the apostles and through the apostles, so that a remnant might be saved.

XXVIII. Thus says the Lord: if the heavens above can be measured and the foundations of the earth beneath searched out, I too will cast off the whole seed of Israel for all that they have done, says the Lord. LXX: thus says the Lord: if the heaven is raised higher and if the floor of the earth is lowered beneath, I too will not reject the race of Israel, says the Lord, for all that they have done. 2. In this place the Hebrew differs much from the common edition. Let us speak first according to the Hebrew: 'if the heavens above can be measured and their height known, or the foundations of the earth searched out and their extremities comprehended by reason, I too', he says, 'will cast off the whole seed of Israel for all that they have done, says the Lord. But as that is impossible, that we should know the summits of the heavens and the foundations of the earth, so this too will be impossible, that I should cast off the whole seed of Israel. But if I cast off the whole seed of Israel, then the summit of the heavens and the extremities of the lands will be measured'. 3. This syllogism is woven in the Gospel too, when an impossible thing is compared with an impossible one: a camel will more easily enter through the eye of a needle than a rich man into the kingdoms of heaven. For as that cannot happen, so neither will this be able to happen; but if this happens, then that too will happen which was thought impossible. Those err, then, who expound this place otherwise, citing also that testimony, that the Son could have asked the Father and brought twelve legions of angels to his aid. 4. The Seventy translated the contrary of this sense, saying: if the heaven is exalted higher and the floor of the earth lowered beneath, I too, he says, will not reject the seed of Israel, says the Lord, for all that they have done. If this is so, the nation of Israel will be rejected; for as the heaven cannot be higher than what it is, nor the earth lower than what it is, so too the race of Israel will by no means be able to be approved. 5. If we see the Jews glorying in this testimony according to the Hebrew, let us agree with them that the whole seed of Israel has not been cast off; for not all have been cast off, but only those who were unbelieving.

XXIX. Behold, days are coming, says the Lord, and the city shall be built for the Lord from the tower of Hananel to the gate of the corner. And the rule — or, according to Symmachus, the line — of its measure shall go out further, over against it, upon the hill Gareb, and it shall go round Goah — or, according to the LXX, of chosen stones — and all the valley of ruins — for which Theodotion put the Hebrew word itself, Phagarim — and the ashes and all Asaremoth — which we better read Asademoth, for which Aquila translated suburbs — to the brook Kidron and to the corner of the eastern gate of the horses, the holy place of the Lord; it shall not be plucked up and shall not be pulled down for ever. 2. Those who accept a thousand-year reign of Christ in the land of Judea, namely the Jews and our Judaizers, try to point out the tower of Hananel and the gate of the corner and the hill Gareb and Goah and the valley Phagarim and all Asademoth and the brook Kidron and the place of the corner of the eastern gate of the horses, and there, they say, the sanctuary of the Lord, that is, the temple, is to be founded and is to remain in perpetuity. 3. And because they cannot show it fulfilled after the captivity, in the times of Zerubbabel and Ezra, they pass to the times of Christ, who they say will come at the consummation of the world, so that a golden and jeweled Jerusalem, according to the Revelation of John, may descend and be built round about in this space of land, that is, from that place to that place. 4. And they catch at this little breeze for their supposition: that from the tower of Anathoth, which today is called Jeremiah's, three miles distant from Jerusalem, as far as the brook Kidron — which is recorded in the Gospel and is in the valley of Jehoshaphat, where there was a garden in which Judas the betrayer handed over the Savior — the foundations of the city are to be laid. 5. 'We are going to read', they say, 'in what follows that Anamehel, son of Shallum, was the cousin of Jeremiah, whose field he bought; and this is the tower of Ananehel', ignorant of the Hebrew truth; for here according to the Hebrew is written: from the tower of Ananehel, namely with 'nun' as the middle letter, but there: behold, Anamehel, son of Shallum, your cousin, will come to you, with 'm' as the middle letter. 6. Calling, therefore, upon the Lord Savior, who has the key of David, who opens, and no one shuts, and who shuts, and no one opens, who also opened the sealed book of Isaiah and of all the prophets, and whom the twenty-four elders holding harps adored, because he alone could unlock the divine mysteries, let us approach the building of the city to which the prophetic word is directed: glorious things are spoken of you, city of God, and: the rush of the river gladdens the city of God. 7. The Church, then, is built from the tower 'of obedience' or 'of grace' and 'of the gifts of God' — for this is what Ananehel means — to the gate of the corner; which, though it seems to have a lofty beginning, yet as long as we abide in this flesh we cannot possess the straight line of truth, but we stand in the corner and in broken lines; and the line of measure goes out further, over against it, that is, against the gate of the corner, upon the hill Gareb, which in our language is rendered either 'sojourning' or 'scab', so that they may teach us that we are strangers and pilgrims, and itching in our ears, and readily giving consent to the novelty of the worst doctrines. 8. And it shall go round, he says, Goah, for which the Seventy translated: round about with chosen stones, which are rolled upon the face of the earth and are bound together by the corner stone, as the apostle Peter says: and you yourselves, as living stones, be built up, a spiritual house, a holy priesthood, offering spiritual sacrifices acceptable to God through Jesus Christ. 9. And all, he says, the valley Phagarim — which means of ruins — and the ashes — 'it shall go round' is understood —, so that, although we seem to ourselves to be on hills, we may yet always fear ruins and consider ashes and, repenting, say with David: for I have eaten ashes like bread and mingled my drink with weeping. Whence also it is said to those who lie fallen: does not he who falls rise again?, says the Lord. 10. And the whole, he says, Sademoth, which we render as the region of death, dividing one name into two words: 'sade', which is called 'region', and 'moth', which means 'of death', for which Aquila rendered 'suburbs' or 'fields' and 'countryside'. But the 'region' 'of death' is the region of sinners, and the 'suburbs' the region of pleasures, which goes on to the brook Kidron — where the Lord was betrayed —, which means 'darkness'. 11. See how many places the Church has, and that that apostolic saying: 'that it be without spot and wrinkle', is reserved for the future and for the heavenly places. You hear of corners, you hear of scab, you hear of ruins and ashes and the region of death and darkness, and you boast of your virtue and sinlessness! 12. Then follows: and to the corner of the gate. Here too is a corner, so that no true righteousness, no sure victory may be shown in this world. The corner of the gate itself too, though it is 'eastern', whence the light comes forth, is yet called 'of the horses', to teach us that we need running and contest, and that at last we may deserve to hear with the Lord: you mounted your horses, and your riding is salvation. 13. In the eastern gate, in the gate of the four-horse chariots, is the sanctification of the Lord, so that we may think ourselves perfect then, when we have said to the Lord: the chariot of God is ten-thousandfold, thousands of those rejoicing; the Lord is among them. A building of this kind, which is set upon the foundation of Christ, of which the Apostle too speaks: as a wise architect I laid the foundation, is never pulled down, but will remain in perpetuity. Obscure and difficult things must be discussed more broadly, so that we may run through plain things more briefly.

Jeremiah 32

XXX.

The word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord in the tenth year of Zedekiah, king of Judah; that is the eighteenth year of Nebuchadnezzar. Then the army of the king of Babylon was besieging Jerusalem, and Jeremiah the prophet was shut up in the court of the prison, which was in the house of the king of Judah. For Zedekiah, king of Judah, had shut him up, saying: Why do you prophesy, saying: Thus says the Lord? 2. Not only the words but also the deeds of the prophets are an example to us for virtue. Jeremiah could have announced prosperity and enjoyed the friendship of King Zedekiah, but he preferred to obey God rather than men, and him who can kill both soul and body in Gehenna rather than him who could have power only over the body. 3. And this must be considered: it was the tenth year of Zedekiah's reign, Jerusalem was already besieged and consumed by sword, famine and pestilence, captivity was near, and yet Zedekiah persists in his opinion; and something of his clemency is shown in that he ordered him to be shut up not in the prison but in the vestibule of the prison, in free custody, that is, so that he could not escape, as though all Jerusalem, enclosed by its fortifications, were not a common prison for its inhabitants. 4. That is the eighteenth year of the reign of Nebuchadnezzar, who began to rule in the fourth year of King Jehoiakim. And the whole cause of the king's anger is this: that he speaks in the name of the Lord the things that had been commanded him.

XXXI. Behold, I will give this city into the hand of the king of Babylon, and he shall take it. And Zedekiah, king of Judah, shall not escape from the hand of the Chaldeans, but shall be delivered into the hand of the king of Babylon, and his mouth shall speak with his mouth, and his eyes shall see his eyes. And he shall lead Zedekiah into Babylon, and there he shall be until I visit him, says the Lord. But if you fight against the Chaldeans, you shall have nothing prosperous. 2. This was the cause of the king's anger: that he preferred truth to a lie, and said that both the city of Jerusalem and King Zedekiah were to be taken; and, what is more grievous, that he would see the face of the Babylonian king and, humbled and captive, would speak with the madness of a most violent king. For it is a heavier terror to see the one you fear, and to undergo the rebuke of words before the torment of punishments. 3. And into Babylon, he says, he shall lead Zedekiah, and there he shall be, for which the Seventy translated: and he shall enter Babylon; of which the one signifies being dragged unwilling, the other going of one's own will. And there, he says, he shall be: he puts an ambiguous word, so as not to seem to prophesy tortures and miseries. 4. And what follows: until I visit him, says the Lord. And if you fight against the Chaldeans, you shall have nothing prosperous, is not in the Seventy. And he prudently tempered the sentence, which can be referred to a good and to a bad sense; for 'visitation', as I have often said, signifies both 'consolation' and 'punishment'.

XXXII. And Jeremiah said: The word of the Lord came to me, saying: Behold, Hanamel, the son of Shallum — or Salom —, your cousin — which in Hebrew is called 'dodach' —, is coming to you, saying: Buy for yourself my field, which is in Anathoth; for the purchase belongs to you by kinship. 2. That the word of God had come secretly to Jeremiah no one could know unless he himself, to whom it had come, disclosed it. And it is foretold to him that Hanamel, his cousin, will come to him and offer him the possession of the field that had been his; and that the place itself is in Anathoth, among the suburban lands which were given by the law to the priests throughout the several tribes and cities; nor was it lawful for a possession to pass from tribe to tribe, nor from one family to another family — whence also the daughters of Zelophehad receive their lot among their brothers — and especially the suburban lands of the priests could be sold to no one else until the year of release, except to him whom nearness of blood required. 3. So his cousin comes to him and offers the purchase, which is owed to him by kinship. Hilkiah and Shallum were full brothers; Jeremiah was the son of Hilkiah, Hanamel the son of Shallum. 'Chelchias' means 'portion of the Lord', 'Hieremias' 'loftiness of the Lord'; and rightly the 'height of the Lord' is born of the 'portion of the Lord'. 'Sellum', again, is rendered into our language as 'peace' or 'peaceable', 'Anamehel' as 'gift' or 'grace of God'; nor shall we wonder that 'peace' and 'grace' are joined to each other, since this is also the opening of the apostolic letters: grace to you and peace. First, then, we merit the peace of God, and after peace grace is born for us, which lies not in the choice of the one who possesses, but of the one who gives. 4. And 'the grace of God' offers the purchase to him who is set in 'lofty places', so that, although he seems exalted, he nevertheless needs the grace of God. That which is often sung by the bride in the Song: my cousin, that is ho adelphidos mou, is called 'dodi' in the Hebrew; therefore he should be called not 'fratruelis' [cousin through a brother] but 'patradelfus', that is 'patruelis'. That Jeremiah was the son of Hilkiah, of the priests who were in Anathoth, in the land of Benjamin, the opening of this book also testifies.

XXXIII. And Hanamel, the son of my uncle, came to me according to the word of the Lord, to the vestibule — or court — of the prison, and said to me: Possess — or buy — my field, which is in Anathoth, in the land of Benjamin, because the inheritance belongs to you and you are the kinsman, so that you may possess or buy. 2. What the word of the Lord had announced to the prophet as future was at once fulfilled in deed. 'There came', he says, 'to me Hanamel' — 'the grace of God' —, 'the son of my uncle' — that is, 'the son of peace' —; 'and he came into the vestibule of the prison and said to me the things which the Lord had foretold would be said by him'. 3. And this priestly field, whose purchase and possession was being offered to Jeremiah, is in 'Anathoth', in the land of 'Beniamin', of which the first means 'obedience', the second 'son of the right hand'; and consequently he seeks the purchase of that place in which the 'obedience' and the 'power' of the Lord dwelt. For 'kinsman' the Seventy translated presbyteron, that is 'elder', which does not suit this passage.

XXXIV. And I understood that it was the word of the Lord, and I bought the field from Hanamel, the son of my uncle, which is in Anathoth, and I weighed out the silver to him, seven and ten silver staters. And I wrote it in a book and sealed it and called witnesses and weighed the silver in the balance. And I took the book of possession, sealed, the stipulations and the ratifications and the seals on the outside. 2. It was indeed hard, almost inconsistent and worthy of laughter, that he who was prophesying that Jerusalem was on the very point of being taken and that all were to be led captive or to perish by sword, famine and pestilence, should buy a field in Anathoth which he was not going to possess. 'But I understood', he says, 'that it was the word of the Lord, and that my purchase was to be fitted to the argument and prophecy of the Lord; and therefore I acquiesced in his command to buy, knowing that the word of God had not come to me in vain about a matter of this kind, and I weighed out ten and seven shekels of silver', which we have rendered as 'staters'. Now a shekel has twenty obols, as is written at the end of the book of Ezekiel. 3. And the prophet bought it for 'ten and seven' shekels, the number in which the servant of the Lord, David, sang on the day on which the Lord delivered him from the hand of all his enemies and from the hand of Saul, and he said: I will love you, O Lord, my strength! The Lord is my firmament and my refuge and my deliverer. My God is my helper, and I will hope in him; my protector and the horn of my salvation. 4. That 'ten' is a mystic number is shown by the decalogue, which was written on tablets of stone by the finger of God, and by the day of fasting and propitiation in the seventh month; that 'seven' too, in which the true sabbath-keeping and rest are found, is holy we prove by many testimonies of the Scriptures, of which I would have set down at least a few, were it not idle to teach what is known. 5. In this number, then, the possession is bought by the prophet and priest, and it is written in a book and sealed, and witnesses are called and the silver is carefully weighed, so that all the rights of sale and purchase may be preserved and the possession may be certain, strengthened by stipulations and responses. Let them at least hear this who try to claim for themselves, by calling witnesses, wills that are false and sometimes not even wills at all!

XXXV. And I gave the book of possession to Baruch, the son of Neriah, the son of Maaseiah, in the eyes of Hanamel, my cousin, and in the eyes of the witnesses who were written in the book of purchase, in the eyes of all the Jews who were sitting in the court of the prison. 2.
