Although the possession was at once to be abandoned — nay rather, it was being bought for posterity, and bought by one who had no sons, for he had not taken a wife —, yet, obedient to the command of the Lord, he performs everything duly and gives the sealed book of possession to Baruch, the son of Neriah, the son of Maaseiah. 'Baruch' in our language is called 'blessed'; he was the son of 'Neri', which means 'my lamp', as the prophet says: your word is a lamp to my feet and a burning light to my paths; and Neriah, Baruch's father, was in turn the son of 'Maasiae', that is, of 'making' and of 'the work of the Lord'. 3. Let us notice, then, with what great privileges of virtue the disciple Baruch ministered to Jeremiah, as David says: he who walked in the undefiled way, he ministered to me. Hence also Elisha, the minister of Elijah, so pleased God that after the translation of his master he merited to receive even a double spirit. I say this for the admonition of those who use the ministry of wicked men and do not dare to cast off those whom they know to be bound to them by the glue of a bad conscience. 4. And the book is handed to Baruch, a man so great and of such quality, in the sight of Hanamel, who had sold, and of the witnesses, who had signed and whose names were contained in the volume of the purchase, and in the presence, he says, of all the Jews who were sitting in the court of the prison, who, that is, had either come to console the prophet or, out of zeal for the fear of God, desired to hear the words of the Lord.

XXXVI. And I commanded Baruch before them, saying: Thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Take these books, this sealed book of purchase and this book which is open, and put them in an earthen vessel, that they may last many days. For thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: Houses and fields and vineyards shall yet be possessed in this land. 2. With all those whom the preceding discourse has named present and looking on, Baruch, the minister and disciple, is commanded — not by the words of his teacher, but by the order of God who commands — to take the books, one sealed, the other open — a custom of purchases still kept to this day, so that what the seals hold shut up within, the open volume displays to those who wish to read it —, both of them in an earthen vessel, that they may last many days. Firm, therefore, and to endure long afterwards was the possession that was guarded with such great care, lest the books of the purchase, if placed outside, should lie open to plunder, or, if buried in the ground, should be dissolved by the moisture of the earth. 3. And all this is done so that those who saw it might understand that Jerusalem would again be inhabited and the fields possessed; and although they ought to have understood this by themselves without Jeremiah's word, yet they are admonished by the words of the Lord, and it is said to them: thus says the Lord of hosts, the God of Israel: houses and fields and vineyards shall yet be possessed in this land. This is what Jeremiah had said a little before: I understood that it was the word of the Lord; and for that reason he bought a field which he was not going to possess.

XXXVII. And I prayed to the Lord, after I had delivered the book of possession to Baruch, the son of Neriah, saying: Alas, alas, alas, Lord God — or you who are, Lord God —, behold, you made heaven and earth in your great strength and in your arm outstretched — or lofty —; not difficult for you — or impossible — shall be any word — or according to the Seventy nothing is hidden with you —, you who show mercy to thousands and repay the iniquity of the fathers into the bosom of their sons after them, most mighty, great, powerful — which in Hebrew is called 'gibbor' —, the Lord of hosts — or of powers — is your name! 2. Great in counsel and incomprehensible in thought, whose eyes are open upon all the ways of the sons of Adam — or of men —, to render to each according to his ways and according to the fruit of his devices. 3. After the purchase of the field had been duly performed, and after the sentence of the Lord in which he promised that houses, fields and vineyards would be possessed thereafter, the prophet prays to the Lord and expresses the grief of his heart with groans, saying: alas, alas, alas, Lord God, for which the Seventy translated ho ōn, that is, you who are, Lord God, according to what is said to Moses: go, say to the people of Israel: He who is has sent me; not that others do not exist, but it is one thing to subsist by the gift of the Creator, another by the eternity of one's nature. 4. He praises God and from the creatures proclaims the Creator; and first he extols with his voice his power and mercy and righteousness toward the whole race of men, then he passes to Israel and describes in solemn speech how much he had bestowed on it. And after such great benefits he says that they, unmindful of his good gifts, provoked his clemency to bitterness, so that the city is besieged and, before the enemy's irruption into it takes place, has been consumed by sword and famine and pestilence. 5. All this he set forth beforehand in order to bring in, as a consequence, that which seemed to be said in reproach of the divine sentence: and you say to me, Lord God: Buy the field for silver and call witnesses, when the city has been given into the hands of the Chaldeans? This, for the present, is what the whole perikopē [passage] of this place contains; now let us return to the particulars. 6. You made heaven and earth in your great strength. And John says of the Son: all things were made through him, and without him nothing was made that was made; for this is the 'strength' of the Lord, as the Apostle confirms: 'Christ the power of God and the wisdom of God'. 7. And in your outstretched arm or lofty, both of which are the mark of one who strikes; and this is the 'arm' of which Isaiah also speaks: and to whom has the arm of the Lord been revealed? No word shall be difficult for you — for the things that are impossible with men are possible with God — or, for whom nothing is hidden, according to the word of the psalmist, who says: for darkness shall not be dark to you, and the night shall be lit up as the day. 8. You who show mercy to thousands and repay the iniquity of the fathers in the bosom of their sons after them. Great is the clemency of the Creator, to extend his mercy to a thousand generations and to show righteousness at once in the next generation, which itself, however, is mixed with mercy; for he does not punish the offender at once, but waits for repentance, so that, if the children have imitated the vices of their parents, the long-deferred punishment is repaid. 9. Most mighty, great, powerful, the Lord of hosts — or of powers — is your name! These names indicate the power of the Creator; but properly the name of God is 'Father', which is revealed in the Gospel through the Lord, who says: Father, I have manifested your name to men. Great in counsel: and does anyone dare to thrust himself into the secret of the Lord and to pass judgment on his judgments! And incomprehensible in thought: him whom thought does not comprehend, how can speech comprehend? Whose eyes are open upon all the ways of the sons of Adam: in vain, then, does man think he can hide from the knowledge of God. 10. And what he adds: to render to each according to his ways and according to the fruit of his devices, indicates this, that at times, because of his exceeding wisdom, his judgments seem unjust; a passage which the Apostle explains more fully to the Romans: do you not know that the goodness of God exhorts you to repentance? But according to your hardness and impenitent heart you treasure up for yourself wrath on the day of wrath and of the revelation of the righteous judgment of God. The later, therefore, the punishment of sinners, the more righteous it is, after the likeness of Pharaoh, who, warned by ten plagues, not punished, and persisting in his hardness, was at last overwhelmed by the waves of the Red Sea.

XXXVIII. You who set signs and portents in the land of Egypt even to this day, and in Israel and among men — or the earthborn — and made for yourself a name as this day. And you brought your people Israel out of the land of Egypt with signs and with portents and with a strong hand and with an outstretched arm and with great terror. 2. And you gave them this land, which you swore to their fathers that you would give them, a land flowing with milk and honey. And they entered and possessed it; and they did not obey your voice and did not walk in your law. All that you commanded them to do they did not do; and all these evils befell them. 3. From the general he passes to the particular and runs through in brief speech what he had bestowed specially on Israel. You who set, he says, signs and portents in the land of Egypt, by which Egypt was afflicted, even to this day, and in Israel and among men or the earthborn. 4. This which is said, 'even to this day', must be joined to what follows, so that we read: 'both in Israel and among all mortals your signs are accomplished daily'; or otherwise: 'you performed signs and portents not only in Egypt, but even to this day the same strength of your mercy has saved your people, and you succor the whole human race with the power of the Creator'. And this is to be noted, that he separates 'Israel' from 'men' and 'the earthborn', according to that saying: Israel is my firstborn son. 5. And made for yourself a name as this day: 'your praises', he says, 'are celebrated in the speech of the whole world.' And you brought your people Israel out of the land of Egypt: he rightly said 'your people'; for at the time when it was brought out, it was serving the commands of the Lord. 'And you brought them out with signs and portents, by which Egypt was struck, and with a strong hand and with an outstretched arm and with great terror', when the Red Sea gave a way to the people of Israel as they crossed and overwhelmed the army of Egypt. 6. And you gave them this land, which you swore to their fathers that you would give them, namely, to Abraham and Isaac and Jacob. So they received the land of promise not by their own merit but by the virtues of their fathers, a land flowing with milk and honey; for they could not yet take solid food, but were nourished with the honey and milk of infancy, or at any rate with honey and milk, the fruitfulness and abundance of all things. And they entered and possessed it. And at once there was nothing between possession and disobedience; for plenty begets security, security negligence, negligence contempt. 7. And they did not obey, he says, your voice, and did not walk in your law. In vain, then, did they promise in the wilderness, saying: all that the Lord commands we will do; for the reward lies not in the pledge but in the deed; so as to beat back the impudence of those who think that a man can fulfill all that he has promised he will do. 8. All that you commanded them to do they did not do; and certainly they had promised that they would do it. And all these evils befell them; 'evils' to those who suffered them, but 'goods' according to the sentence of the Lord, which renders to each according to his ways.

XXXIX. Behold, siegeworks have been built against the city, that it may be taken, and the city has been given into the hands of the Chaldeans who fight against it, before the face of the sword and of famine and of pestilence; and whatever you have spoken has come to pass, as you yourself see; and you say to me, Lord God: Buy the field for silver and call witnesses, when the city has been given into the hand of the Chaldeans? 2. It was the tenth year of King Zedekiah; for so it is written: the word that came to Jeremiah from the Lord in the tenth year of Zedekiah, king of Judah; then the army of the king of Babylon was besieging Jerusalem, and Jeremiah the prophet was shut up in the court of the prison. And rightly it is now said: 'behold, siegeworks have been built against the city, that it may be taken, and the city has been delivered into the hand of the Chaldeans, and they have no one to conquer, only people to capture; for they have already been consumed by sword, famine and pestilence, and whatever you said we see fulfilled; how then do you say to me, Lord: buy the field for silver and call witnesses, when the city has been given into the hand of the Chaldeans?' 3. Therefore he does not reproach but asks, and wishes to learn not so much for himself as for the others who were sitting in the court of the prison and perhaps were silently reproaching him, how the same prophet, whom they believed to announce the truth, both says that the city is to be taken and buys a field as though he were going to possess it.

XL. And the word of the Lord came to Jeremiah, saying: Behold, I am the Lord, the God of all flesh; shall for me difficult — or impossible — be — or be hidden from me — any word? Therefore thus says the Lord: Behold, I will deliver this city into the hand of the Chaldeans and into the hand of the king of Babylon, and he shall take it. 2. And the Chaldeans shall come — and shall enter — fighting — or the Chaldeans shall come — against this city, and they shall set it on fire and burn it, and the houses on whose roofs they sacrificed to Baal and poured out libations to strange gods to provoke me. 3. To sad things he joins glad ones, and after the overthrow of Jerusalem he promises a return to the captive people. And first he sets out the causes of the offense and of the righteous fury of God, so that the greater the guilt of the sinners, the more ample the clemency of the Creator toward the sinners. 4. I, he says, am the Lord, the God of all flesh; not of all the nations, nor of the people of Israel, nor indeed, as he is often accustomed to say of the saints, 'the God of Abraham, the God of Isaac, the God of Jacob,' but the God, he says, of all flesh, so that he may be believed to have made both the rational and the brute animals himself. For there are those who acknowledge the providence of the Creator as far as rational creatures, but assert that the brutes either perish or live by chance happenings; and the prophetic word declares that there is nothing that escapes the providence and knowledge of God, because some things were created for their own sake, others for the use of men. 5. Shall it be difficult for me — or impossible, or at any rate shall it be hidden from me — any word? We have also said above: the things that are impossible with men are possible with God; and here and in many other places we must take 'word' for 'things'. But what does it mean that the word came: therefore thus says the Lord? For what had gone before, that he should put a causal conjunction, saying: therefore thus says the Lord? 6. 'Because', he says, 'it is my care to rule all things, to order everything and to render to each according to his ways, therefore I will deliver this city into the hand of the Chaldeans and into the hand of the king of Babylon, and they shall take it'; for first it is surrounded by an army and is taken in Nebuchadnezzar's absence, and Zedekiah is led to Riblah and there is handed over to the king. 7. And the Chaldeans, he says, shall come, fighting against this city. Aquila did better, who for what is written, 'shall come', translated eiseleusontai, that is, 'shall enter' the city; for they were not absent, so as to 'come', since they had surrounded Jerusalem, as Scripture testifies: then the army of the king of Babylon was besieging Jerusalem, and then: behold, siegeworks have been built against the city, that it may be taken, and the city has been given into the hands of the Chaldeans. How then shall they 'come' who were present? 8. 'But those who were besieging the city shall enter', he says, 'and shall take it and set it on fire and burn it to the ground' — for the Hebrew word 'bau', by its ambiguity, means both 'shall come' and 'shall enter' — 'and the houses on whose roofs they sacrificed to Baal, the idol of the Sidonians, and poured out libations to strange gods to provoke me, so that they seemed to be heaping insults on the Creator, not through an error of religion but out of a kind of contentiousness'. 9. And just as the world is written to be destined to perish, according to what is written: heaven and earth shall pass away, because it is set in the evil one, so too the very houses and places in which crimes were committed lie subject to the wrath of God. There are those who contentiously refer to the places themselves what is written: 'the place in which the Lord and Savior was crucified is spiritually called Gomorrah and Egypt'. But others think that the whole world is signified under the name of Egypt and Gomorrah; for as Gomorrah was destroyed by divine fire, so the world too is to be burned up by the judgment of God.

XLI. For the sons of Israel and the sons of Judah were continually — or alone — doing evil in my eyes from their youth. The Hebrew word 'ach' Aquila translated plēn, which signifies the conjunction 'nevertheless'; the first edition of Symmachus and the Seventy and Theodotion translated 'alone'; but the second edition of Symmachus rendered diolou, which we too have followed for the present, so as to say 'continually'. 2. Let us then speak first according to the Hebrew: the sons of Israel and the sons of Judah continually doing evil. 'Both the ten', he says, 'and the two tribes did evil without ceasing, and their perseverance in the worst works was continual'. But if it was continual and always in the whole people, where is everlasting righteousness? 3. But according to the Seventy, who said: alone doing evil, the question arises: had the other nations not done evil at the time when Israel and Judah were sinning? It is resolved thus: he who has the knowledge of God and departs from him sins alone in the eyes of God; but those who remained unbelieving offend as though he did not see and did not care. Hence also David, a holy man, because he had fallen into sin with Bathsheba, the wife of Uriah, afterwards in repentance says: against you alone have I sinned and done evil before you, that is, 'in your sight'. 4. Then it is added: alone doing evil in the eyes and in my sight from their youth. And what follows: the sons of Israel, who until now embitter me by the work of their hands, says the Lord, is not in the Seventy and has been added from the Hebrew. But because they offended continually from their youth up to the present day, the sentence of God is therefore just, and Scripture rightly goes on:

XLII. In my fury and in my indignation this city has become to me from the day on which they built it until this day, on which it is taken away from my sight. If from the time when the foundations of the city were laid until this day, when it was taken and set on fire and removed from the sight of the Lord, it was always in vice and provoked the indignation of the Lord against itself, where is — as we have often said already — the respite from sins?

XLIII. Because of the wickedness, he says, of the sons of Israel and of the sons of Judah, which they did, provoking me to anger, they and their kings, their princes and their priests and their prophets, the men of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem. Because he had said above: in my fury and in my indignation this city has become to me from the day on which they built it until this day, on which it is taken away from my sight, and had shown that in general no one had been without sin, he now enumerates them part by part, both their kings and princes and priests and prophets, and, embracing everything in one word: the men, he says, of Judah and the inhabitants of Jerusalem. 2. And he rightly did not say: 'my kings and my princes and my priests and my prophets', but, because they had sinned: their kings and their princes and their priests and their prophets.

XLIV. And they turned to me their backs and not their faces. According to what is written elsewhere: and they turned against me a withdrawing shoulder. For he who prays, with neck bowed, is cast prone upon the ground; but he who turns his back shows by the very gesture of his body that he disregards the one who threatens. 'And this', he says, 'they were doing'.

XLV. When I taught them at dawn and instructed them, and they would not listen, so as to receive discipline. 'When the darkness of error and all worship of idols had been put to flight, I desired daily to enlighten their hearts, confuted by my sentence, and to teach them what is right'. And to preserve free will, he joins on and says: and they would not listen, so as to receive discipline. There follows:

XLVI. And they set their idols in the house in which my name is invoked, to pollute it. Not only at that time did Judah set up in the temple of God the statue of an idol of which we read at the beginning of Ezekiel, but to this day in the house of God, which is interpreted as the Church, or in the heart and soul of believers, an idol is set up when a new dogma is constructed and, according to Deuteronomy, is worshiped in secret; do you not know, he says, that you are the temple of God and the Spirit of God dwells in you?

XLVII. And they built the high places — or altars — of Baal, which are in the valley of the son of Hinnom, to initiate their sons and their daughters to Moloch. For 'initiate' in the Hebrew is written 'ebir', which Aquila and Symmachus translated 'lead across', the Seventy and Theodotion 'offer'. Of the 'valley of the sons of Hinnom', which in Hebrew is called 'Gehenna', we have spoken more fully above: that it lies below the springs of Siloam and, by its pleasantness, because it is a well-watered place, provoked the people to luxury, which the worship of idols follows. 2. And it is to be noted that 'altars' and 'high places' are called 'bamoth' in the Hebrew language; this for the sake of those who are in doubt what this word means in the book of Samuel and of Kings. Moloch is the idol of the Ammonites, whose name is rendered as 'king'; and divine Scripture signifies that in that very place the people served not only the idol Baal but also Moab and all the demons.

XLVIII. Which I did not command them, nor did it come up into my heart, that they should do this abomination and lead Judah into sin. Properly, the tribes of Judah and Benjamin worshiped the images of the demons in the shrine of Baal and of Moloch; but it is evident that the ten tribes, which were called Samaria, Joseph and Ephraim, worshiped the golden calves in Bethel and Dan. And so great was the evil that was done by the people that God testifies that he never thought, nor did it come up into his heart, what they were going to do. But all these things are said anthrōpopathōs [in human terms].

XLIX. And now, because of these things, thus says the Lord, the God of Israel, to this city, of which you say that it is delivered into the hand of the king of Babylon by the sword and by famine and by pestilence. Just as to those who hoped for help and trusted in the strength of the walls it is prophesied that Jerusalem is to be overthrown and the people on the very point of being captured, and before the captivity to perish by sword and famine and pestilence, so to those who despair and, after the overthrow of the city, expect no salvation, he promises his help, so that confidence and pride may merit a just sentence, and despair and humility the help of God.

L. Behold, I will gather them from all the lands to which I drove them out in my fury and in my wrath and in great indignation, and I will bring them back to this place and make them dwell confidently, and they shall be to me a people and I will be to them a God. And I will give them one heart and one way, that they may fear me all their days, and that it may be well with them and with their sons after them. 2. And I will strike with them an everlasting covenant — or I will appoint an eternal testament — and I will not cease to do them good, and I will put my fear in their heart, that they may not depart from me. And I will rejoice over them when I have done them good — or and I will visit them, to do them good —, and I will plant them in this land in truth — or in faith — with my whole heart and with my whole soul. 3. Many think that this was fulfilled in the time of Zerubbabel, son of Shealtiel, and of Jeshua, son of Jehozadak, the great priest, when Haggai and Zechariah prophesied under Ezra the priest, when the temple too was built and the walls were raised round about under Nehemiah, so that those whom he had previously cast out of Jerusalem in fury and wrath and in great indignation and scattered over the whole world, he afterwards brought back and made to dwell confidently, and they were the people of God and the Lord was their God, and the rest that Scripture goes on to relate. 4. But how this can be fitted to that time: I will make them dwell confidently, and I will strike with them an everlasting covenant or I will appoint for them an eternal testament, cannot be proved at all, since we have read, and the sacred history relates, that they were often taken captive not only by the neighboring nations but also by the Persians and Macedonians and Egyptians and Romans, and are in servitude to this day. 5. Everything, therefore, that we see fulfilled in our time, the time of faith, must be referred to the coming of the Savior; and the election — according to the Apostle — of the remnant has been saved, and they dwell confidently in Christ, and one heart has been given them, according to what is written: and of the multitude of believers there was one heart and soul. 6. And one, he says, way — him who says: I am the way and the life and the truth —, that they may fear me all their days; for the beginning of wisdom is the fear of the Lord. All, he says, their days: if this does not apply to the Jews, it must be taken of our people, with whom it has been and is and will be well, not only with themselves but also with their sons after them; for he has struck an everlasting covenant with us and will not cease henceforth to do us good. 7. And what follows: and I will put my fear in their heart, that they may not depart from me, so grants free will that the very fear which is bestowed nevertheless remains the grace of the giver. 'And when I have done them good', he says, 'I will rejoice and be glad', namely, that his creature has been saved; whence also there is joy among the angels in heaven over one sinner who repents. 8. And I will plant them, he says, in this land in truth or — as the Seventy translated — in faith, so as to signify properly the Christian people, whose religion is faith. With my whole heart and with my whole soul: if these are the words of the Lord the Savior, the heart and soul are rightly believed to be his, who says in the Gospel: 'I have power to lay down my soul, and I have power to take it up again'; but if we take it as spoken in the person of God the Father, it must be understood according to that saying: your new moons and sabbaths and feast days my soul hates.

LI. For thus says the Lord: As I have brought upon this people all this great evil, so I will bring upon them all the good that I speak to them. And fields shall be possessed in this land, of which you say that it is desolate because neither man nor beast has remained, and that it has been given into the hand of the Chaldeans. 2. Fields shall be bought with money and shall be written in a book, a seal shall be impressed, and a witness shall be called in the land of Benjamin and round about Jerusalem, in the cities of Judah and in the cities of the hill country and in the cities of the plain — or sephela — and in the cities of the nageb — that is: which are toward the south —, because I will turn their captivity, says the Lord. 3. Although according to the letter these things went before in type after the return from the Chaldeans, when at the command of King Cyrus the people returned to Judea, yet spiritually they are fulfilled more truly and more fully in Christ and in the apostles. Then both men and beasts were brought back into the Church, according to what is written: men and beasts you will save, O Lord, all who are rational and all who are simple. Then fields were bought with money, that we might make for ourselves friends of the mammon of iniquity, who might receive us into the eternal tabernacles; and they were written in a book — doubtless the book of the living —, and the sign of the standard of the Lord's cross and victory was impressed, and witnesses were called, the martyrs and the whole choir of the saints. 4. In the land of Benjamin, where the 'strength' of the Lord is; and round about Jerusalem, in which is the 'vision of peace' and eternal security; in the cities of Judah, in which is the true 'confession' of Christ; and in the cities of the hill country, one of which is that of which he says: a city set on a mountain cannot be hidden; and in the cities of the plain — which in Hebrew are called 'sephela' —, so that from the deep and low places we may advance across the level of the plains to the heights; and in the cities which are toward the south — for which the Seventy rendered 'nageb' —, where there is 'midday' and the full light of truth. 5. And when all these things have been done, what is written will be fulfilled: I will turn their captivity, says the Lord, of whom it is written: ascending on high he led captivity captive; he received or — as the Apostle says — he gave gifts to men.
